MIUEES E GOOgle POTAGER MODERNE TRAITÉ COMPLET DE LA CULTURE DES LÉGUMES APPROPRIÉE AUX JARDINS DU PROPRIÉTAIRE, DU LOCATAIRE, DU FERMIER, DU PRESBYTÈRE, DES COMMUNAUTÉS ET DES PENSIONS, DE L'INSTITUTEUR PRIMAIRE, DES GARES ET DES CAMPS, comprenant La préparation du sol, la fabrication des engrais, l'assolement, la culture et l'indication des variétés de légumes convenant à chacun de ces jardins, PAR GRESSENT, Professeur d'Arboriculture à l'INSTITUT AGRICOLE DE BEAUVAIS, Chargé des Cours des villes d'Orléans, Beauvais, Étampes, Noyon, etc. FIRST EDITION. Disuises t (O -.-- bigt COogle "12.41* tie.-~. 3 ------.---- THE MODERN VEGETABLE GARDEN. FOREWORD. For a long time, landowners who attend my arboriculture courses, struck by the quantity of vegetables I obtain from very-small spaces, have been urging me to publish a book dealing with the cultivation of the vegetable garden; but a practical book in the genre of Fruit Arboriculture, with which one can, even without knowledge of gardening, obtain vegetables in sufficient quantity and at an advantageous cost price. DISRESO GOC I resisted for a long time; but soon, the owners were joined by tenants of small gardens, for whom the supply of vegetables is both a distraction and a resource; farmers, who, most of the time, lack the vegetables necessary for their numerous and hard-working staff; country priests, for whom the cultivation of their garden offers a resource for themselves and their parishioners; community superiors, spending large sums on labor and fertilizers, etc., etc., and not reaping the benefits of the harvest, etc., and not reaping, on average, half of their expenditure; schoolteachers eager to learn about useful crops and to teach them to their pupils; railway employees occupying their leisure time, and finding a product on plots of land bordering the stations; finally the army, which, in these times of progress, has also started to create gardens in the camps. During my classes, everyone insisted on the need for a practical book teaching vegetable cultivation, so useful and profitable for all classes of society. It would be ungracious to hesitate in the face of a desire so generally expressed in all the towns where I teach. Consequently, I am publishing the Potager moderne. The modern kitchen garden is destined to bring about the same revolution in the old kitchen garden that fruit growing has accomplished in the cultivation of fruit trees. Simplification of cultivation, savings in labor and fertilizer, considerable increase in produce - these are the goals that will be easily achieved with Le Potager moderne, a book that puts forward new ideas, teaches new but easy cultivation, and whose effects are sanctioned by several years' experience. Le Potager moderne does not deal with vegetable growing from a speculative point of view. You have to be a born vegetable grower to sell vegetables and make money. Those destined to become market gardeners can do no better than to spend a few years working in Paris. The Parisian market gardener is the world's leading producer, but he can only grow vegetables in Paris, where manure abounds and is sold for nothing, with the arms of market gardeners and for the Paris market. Vegetables grown too fast and too wet lack flavor. The marshes of Paris are the first school for the market gardeners of the provinces, even if it means modifying their cultivation according to the resources and consumption of the towns where they settle. My aim in publishing Le Potager Moderne is not to train vegetable merchants, but to teach everyone how to obtain from their gardens an abundant harvest of excellent produce at a price lower than the market price, when costs have not yet been reduced by selling surplus produce. When this goal is reached, which will be easy for all those who follow the instructions of Le Potager moderne to the letter, this book will have accomplished a useful work; it is in this hope that I am writing it. May it find the same welcome at the ministry, among the clergy, among prefects, mayors and among all my listeners, as Arboriculture fruitière and Les Lecons élémentaires d'arboriculture. Orléans, May 25 1863. SISEESSGO 26 WHAT IS AND WHAT SHOULD BE. Before proceeding with an examination of vegetable growing in most of France, let's leave out: 1° The crops grown by market gardeners in Paris and other major French cities. 2° The vegetable gardens of castles. The Parisian market gardener is a type found only in Paris. He is the personification of intelligence and hard work. These two qualities, taken to extremes, turn his crops into production phenomena admired the world over. The Parisian market gardener has two resources inherent to Paris: mountains of excellent manure for next to nothing, and the Paris market hall, a yawning abyss that swallows everything. Add to these two resources a great understanding of cultivation, an upright, unprejudiced mind that loves progress, and an incredible amount of work, and we have the key to the wealth of Parisian market gardeners, who annually produce a gross income double the value of the soil; but, let's not forget, these prodigies can only be achieved in Paris and with Parisian resources. Second in line is the market gardener from the big cities, who more often than not apprenticed with the Parisian market gardener. He has learned to farm and has acquired the habit of incessant work. His resources are limited, he pays more for manure and sometimes finds it difficult to obtain; what's more, his production is limited to the needs and habits of the country. The provincial market gardener, by dint of hard work and industry, earns an annual income from his land equal to the land value. Then there's the château gardener, who has conquered a good position by dint of hard work; he's a skilled florist, a good market gardener, and sometimes an arborist. The château gardener has a large staff of helpers, for the greenhouses, the greengrocers, the park, etc. There can be no question of cost price at the chateau, and yet all his crops, well directed and skilfully understood, are most of the time less expensive than those of owners with average fortunes and an ignorant gardener. We bow our heads before the results obtained by these three classes of growers; we can only admire the first two and thank them on behalf of all for possessing the intelligence and arms likely to make the soil produce an income exceeding its land value. What's more, the market gardeners of Paris, like those in the provinces, all set an example of morality and probity, and prove to the lazy that to want is to be able. Let's thank them again for the example they so modestly set, which is rare and salutary in these times. Let's not forget the castle gardener; he is often the expression of horticultural science and proves abundantly to those who visit his crops that the word impossible is no longer French in horticulture. Let us thank him sincerely for the brilliant results he has achieved, and urge him, in the general interest, to make many more pupils. Let's also say that, within a radius of a few leagues of Paris, there are skilful gardeners who have learned and know; their well-tended crops bear witness to this, and always produce a product equivalent to the expenses incurred. But as soon as you move away from Paris, the center of enlightenment and learning, what do you find in the way of gardeners? Very good people, it's true, of irreproachable probity, we're happy to say, full of good will, but ignorant of the first elements of cultivation. This is where the modern kitchen garden begins its useful work, to be continued in the gardens of tenants, farmers, presbyteries, communities, schoolteachers at stations and camps. Let's take a look at what most gardens at a certain distance from Paris look like. In terms of appearance: a general jumble of high-stemmed, pyramid-shaped fruit trees, entangled with vines, currants and raspberries, and in the middle of this hideous mess a few withered flowers and impossible vegetables. There are many very large gardens that have been cultivated in this way for centuries, and for as long as they have been cultivated, the result has always been the same: no fruit, no vegetables and hardly any flowers. If the owners of these gardens take the trouble to count what they spend on fertilizers, labor and all kinds of expenses, and if they are willing to estimate their harvest at the market price, they will give up cultivating their garden the next day, so eloquent will the figure be. Let's now take a look at what we call a vegetable garden, and what it achieves. A vegetable garden is a necessity for the homeowner who spends a large part of the year on an estate far from the city. He needs vegetables, whatever the cost. Here's how this owner sees things: he hires a gardener for 1,200 fr. and a boy for 800 fr. for a total of 2,000 fr. and spends around 1,000 fr. on frames, cloches, seedlings, seeds, mats, etc. for a total of 3,000 fr. for the vegetable garden. What does the owner get for 1,000 écus? A kitchen garden sometimes adorned with high-stemmed fruit trees, but always planted with pyramid-shaped pear trees on either side of the long avenues, accompanied by flowers. It's essential to water the flowers and, by extension, the trees. The latter grow with extreme vigor until the roots rot, and, because of their vigor and often the pruning applied to them, don't bear fruit; during their short existence, it's said: "They're still too young to yield!" and when they're dead: "The land is worthless for trees!" As a result, the owner sends the fruit to the market! Let's move on to vegetables. If, as is all too often the case, the gardener grows vegetables in the local style, he doesn't know which varieties to introduce into the owner's garden; he plants the local varieties, very rustic indeed, but good for carters. Cabbages as acrid as they are monstrous, peas as big and hard as rifle bullets, carrots excellent for cows, woody radishes, and so on. This same gardener often ignores the needs of the plants he cultivates, and acts according to this learned axiom: "You have to grease the soil to harvest," and then he scatters what fertilizer he has all over his garden, and invariably obtains this result: leafy vegetables, which require abundant manuring, languish for lack of sufficient nourishment and give a quarter of a harvest three months too late, and what a harvest!... filanders everywhere and in everything. Roots, which require humus-filled soil but no fresh fertilizer, grow as stems and don't form roots; lettuces climb before they can peel. Excellent herbaceous harvest for rabbits! Dried-fruit vegetables, which require soil that has been free of fertilizer for at least two years and potassium silicates, grow in stalks; you have two-meter-high peas with no pods, giant beans with no fruit. We see this every day, and yet in such a garden perhaps more work and as much fertilizer have been expended as would have been needed to obtain, not only a quantity of excellent vegetables, but also an abundant harvest of melons and early fruit if the fertilizer had been well spent. The melon, such a precious fruit, exists only in the leafy state in most gardens. However, nothing is so easy to grow, and without any expense, when you know how to cultivate it. Sometimes the gardener obtains abundant produce, but all at once; the owner finds it necessary to feed the vegetables to his animals so as not to let them go to waste, and then runs out for months on end. So he returns to the market. Now, let the owner estimate his harvest and say how much the few bad vegetables he got cost him. Many have counted and all say that vegetable gardening is a ruinous necessity. This is true when growing in poor conditions. Most gardeners, at some distance from Paris and far from educational centers, are simply former manual laborers who are more intelligent and dexterous than their colleagues, and who have devoted themselves to garden cultivation, having only good will in terms of horticultural science. We have no right to reproach them for their ignorance in cultivation, since they have not been able to learn; but we can guide them with a practical book and help them to achieve fruitful results; this is the aim of the modern Potager. We have just examined what is and dwelt on the disadvantages of the present state; now let's look at what should be, to avoid falling back into the errors of the past. WHAT SHOULD BE. Let's start by saying that growing vegetables is incompatible with growing fruit trees and flowers. Vegetables require soil with a high humus content and constant moisture; such soil is fatal to trees and harmful to flowers, which grow in stems, flower little and give small blossoms. In addition, vegetables and trees require a great deal of air and light to grow and bear fruit. When the vegetable garden is planted with trees, their shade is very detrimental to vegetables, which, deprived of light, grow upwards, producing etiolated stems that become entangled in the trees and prevent any fruiting on the branches at the base. Frequent watering of vegetables first makes the trees grow very vigorously and then leads to their death by rotting the roots, before they have produced a serious harvest - an almost negative result for both fruit and vegetables! I refer the reader to Arboriculture fruitière for fruit trees. In this book, I have given all the indications needed to obtain, with certainty and even without any knowledge of arboriculture, a large quantity of the finest fruit from the smallest of spaces. The only planting possible in the kitchen garden is on the walls, in front of which a 1.5 m wide flowerbed is set aside for planting a double cordon, independently of the espalier. (See Arboriculture fruitière for advice on how to do this properly, and obtain fruit in the first year after planting). As a matter of principle, we believe that the kitchen garden should be devoted exclusively to vegetables, and that no trees or flowers should ever be planted there, if we want to get the produce it is likely to yield. I know that this will seem impossible at first sight to people with old gardens where they cultivate flowers, trees and vegetables all at the same time. I know how strong the force of habit is, and how difficult it will be to get old gardeners to change their cultivation. A modification such as the one I'm asking for would have been impossible twenty years ago, when the gardener's dictum was the supreme law in horticulture, but it's easy today, when everyone is busy cultivating, and everyone has learned that with a little study you can get a high income from a plot of land that was only an object of heavy expense as long as it was abandoned to carelessness and routine. I'm not asking for a Saint-Barthelemy of old gardens; on the contrary, I'd like to see what's left to serve as a point of comparison. So I would say to an owner with a 50-acre garden of mixed crops: you employ a man and his wife all year round, plus men for the day to maintain your garden. In addition, you need enormous quantities of fertilizer to fertilize just a quarter of this large space, i.e. to obtain a quarter of a harvest, further diminished by the lack of air and light, and most of the time, eight years out of ten, you send part of your supply of fruit and vegetables to the market. Keep 35 ares of overgrown garden. Completely shave 15 ares and distribute them as follows: 5 ares in the fruit garden and 10 ares in the vegetable garden; give these 15 ares the same quantity of fertilizer that you uselessly scatter in your large garden; devote, on average, one man's day and one woman's day per week to these two specimen gardens; the whole will be perfectly tended and will produce with a saving of half the expense: The fruit garden, well organized, from five to six thousand fruits per year; the vegetable garden, cultivated for four years, a large supply of vegetables of all kinds, and even small early fruits: melons, etc.., for at least ten people, something you've never had, or even tried, because the cost price seemed so exorbitant compared to that of vegetables grown in the garden. When the owner has tried this for two or three years, and compared the cost and produce of the two crops; when he is convinced by experience that 15 ares of well-cultivated land cost half as much and produce ten times as much as the 50-acre overgrown garden, he will refuse to cultivate the latter at all, and will be the first to hasten its destruction, as that of something hideous to behold, costing a lot and producing nothing. We rightly assume that owners have a sense of beauty, and above all of improving their properties. We haven't mentioned flowers in our crops. The owner usually likes them. If he doesn't, his wife, daughter or daughter-in-law will love flowers and ask for them. What will the homeowner do, even if he despises flowers, with a comfortable dwelling surrounded by 50 ares of cluttered garden, of which he will have devoted 15 ares to a fruit and vegetable garden supplying him beyond his needs? Will he convert the remaining 35 ares into fruit and vegetable gardens? Certainly not! Unless he wanted to become a fruit and vegetable merchant! The 35 ares thus cultivated would undoubtedly bring him a high income, but he would be obliged to employ more staff and keep a closer watch on them, and what's more, since he wouldn't be selling his produce himself, he'd be constantly fooled either by the greed or the nonchalance of his servants. The owner won't want to create a real job for himself, a burden, and he'll be right. If his family asks him for flowers, he'll make them all happy by adding lawns and clumps of trees and shrubs to the baskets on his 35 ares, and the maintenance costs will be almost nil. Let's now look at the result of this garden conversion in terms of enjoyment, expense, income and increase in property value. I have assumed a comfortable or at least habitable house, surrounded by a cluttered garden of 50 ares. It's a city dwelling with a farmer's garden. Nothing is less appealing to the walker, and especially to ladies, than the sight of fruit trees, twisted and stunted; stalks of crabapple, beans, cabbage, etc., etc.; add to this the perfume of onions, garlic, etc., etc., and we have a fair idea of the repulsion such a garden inspires: no shade, no flowers; stiles, oars and branches snagging dresses and hats everywhere! If a property in this condition comes up for sale, buyers will value it for little more than its size, at the price of plain land. Most will back out, not so much because of the expense of creating a garden, but because of the time it will take. When you buy a country house, you're looking for a well-created garden, shade and flowers! The cluttered garden, which produces little or nothing, requires a high level of expenditure, a man and a woman all year round, plus men for the day at various times. Our fruit and vegetable gardens, together containing 15 ares and providing ample supplies for the house, require one man's day and one woman's day a week for their perfect upkeep. What will it cost the owner to convert 35 ares of overgrown garden into parkland? No cost at all! If he spends, in the year of creation, in three months, the same number of days needed each year for the overgrown garden, the park will be created. There's still the cost of trees to plant in the beds and a few flowers! This expense will be covered in less than two years by the savings on days spent without any profit in the clutter garden. A year-round gardener, even assuming average activity, will maintain 35 ares of lawns, beds and baskets; 10 ares of vegetable garden and 5 ares of fruit garden in an irreproachable manner. And all this, not only without expense, but also with profit for the owner, who will have in his expenses the reduction in workmen's days and purchases of fruit and vegetables at the market, which, calculated over a period of ten years, would make a considerable saving. What's more, a property restored and managed in this way would become an object of curiosity, the envy of all and cited as a model in the surrounding area. Let's say the owner wants to sell his property after restoration. Purchasers who would not have dared to buy in the original state will pay double for a property of pleasure and product, in which there is nothing to spend, and there will be twenty purchasers for one. What will the owner have spent to achieve this goal? Nothing, less than nothing, since he will have made a notable saving in expenditure and increased income. He will have spent a little intelligence and, let's face it, this last expenditure will have provided him with a pleasant and healthy distraction and a useful use of his time. Having said that, let's return to the creation of a typical vegetable garden, and examine the conditions under which we need to create it in order to bring together all the elements for success: situation, exposure, nature of the soil, its preparation; ripping, amendments; the crop rotation to which the vegetable garden must be subjected, the distribution of fertilizers, the organization of beds, frames and cloches; the main cultivation operations: plowing, sowing, transplanting, topdressing, watering, hoeing, mulching, etc., etc., etc. LOCATION, EXPOSURE, SOIL TYPE. It's not always easy to choose the most favorable location and exposure for the vegetable garden; the park has conformation and perspective requirements that are impossible to violate, and let's face it, the vegetable garden, for all its usefulness, is far from picturesque, so it has to be relegated to a corner and hidden from view. Fortunately, an absolutely useful vegetable garden is much less demanding than a fruit garden in terms of location, exposure and, above all, soil quality. With a little thought and imagination, you'll always find a way to create it under the right conditions, and to conceal it skilfully enough so that it doesn't clash with the landscape. To achieve the best results, the vegetable garden must be created under the following conditions: 1° Be naturally sheltered from the north and west winds; be closed to these two exposures, and open to those from the east and south. North winds are icy, bringing frost, hail, etc., etc., and a garden cannot be too well protected from these winds, which are pernicious to all crops because of their harshness, the havoc they wreak, and the delay they cause in vegetation. West winds, less cold than those from the north, are no less to be feared: when their violent gusts don't break plants, they torment them enough to considerably delay their vegetation. A good garden should be hermetically sealed to the north and west, by natural shelters, or failing that, by artificial shrubs. If the same garden is open to the east and south, it will be remarkably early, sheltered from cold winds and storms, and exposed to the first rays of the sun and the warmth of the south. Everything can be done and everything can be hoped for in such a garden. 2° The vegetable garden must be as even as a table; if it has a slope, it must be levelled with the greatest care. A sloping vegetable garden, even one that faces south, is always in an unfavorable situation. Vegetable gardening is a very intensive activity; each bed' must produce an average of at least three harvests a year, and this can only be achieved through active cultivation and frequent watering. A slope, however slight, is an obstacle to cultivation, and almost an impossibility for good watering. 3° As water is essential to the vegetable garden, if not near a stream or spring, then at least in a place where you can be sure of drilling a shallow well, providing abundant water that never runs dry. The surrounding slopes form a natural shelter from the winds; the soil is rich and fresh, and there are often springs or fountains providing large quantities of water. In the absence of surface springs, you can be sure of finding abundant ones at shallow depths. h° The vegetable garden should be created in soil of medium consistency, rather light than compact, but still substantial enough. Soil is one of the most powerful natural agents of vegetation; it counts for a great deal in the success of cultivation, but we must not exaggerate its importance, and blame its poor quality for the many disappointments caused by ignorance, laziness and carelessness. Vegetable cultivation is not only possible but profitable in all soils, except pure silica and limestone, where it would still give passable results if the soil were properly amended. So, let's state the principle: VEGETABLE GROWING IS POSSIBLE IN ALL SOILS. With this in mind, let's take a brief look at the soil's constitution and the amendments that need to be made to it, in order to create, if not an artificial soil, then at least one that is suitably amended to come close to the type I've described and give certain results. Let's begin by examining the three main elements that serve as the basis of fertility, and are present in greater or lesser quantities in the constitution of all soils: clay, silica and limestone. CLAY, composed of 52 parts silica, 33 parts alumina and 15 parts water, is plastic, tenacious and difficult to divide; it retains a considerable quantity of water, about 70% of its weight; it has the ability to seize ammonia gases and retain them between its particles. Wet clay forms a soft paste that adheres to tools; dry, it acquires the hardness of stone. In both cases, it is impermeable to air. In addition, the clay must be completely saturated with fertilizer for the plants grown on it to feel the effects of manuring. Clay soils are cold, damp and, consequently, late, and they add to these disadvantages the double drawback of giving way to frost and splitting in very hot weather. These soils are suitable for artichokes, cabbages, leeks, broad beans, etc., etc., but most root crops and dried fruit vegetables do not do well in them. Despite these drawbacks, excellent vegetable gardens can be created in clay soils, if suitably amended. Amendments that are impossible in large-scale cultivation 2 can easily be applied to the vegetable garden where, as in the fruit garden, we will be cultivating intensively in the full sense of the word, i.e. spending a great deal of capital and labor to obtain an abundant harvest in a very limited space. The 10-acre vegetable garden I mentioned in the previous chapter is intensive cultivation; the clutter garden is extensive cultivation. We're dealing with very clayey soil, destined to form a vegetable garden with a soil plastic enough to make bricks or pottery. If we leave the soil in this condition, a few leafy vegetables, such as artichokes, cabbages, etc., will give good results, while all the others will languish. The first thing to obtain from the amendment is a friable, air-permeable soil, which doesn't stick to feet or tools when wet, and doesn't crack when dry. On the contrary, it's one of the most favorable conditions for vegetable gardening, saving on watering and ensuring fertility. It would be a serious mistake to drain the garden, unless you find stagnant water in the soil at a depth of less than 60 centimetres. Watering is the most expensive part of growing vegetables, and we must do everything we can to reduce the cost. So, to keep the soil fresh and healthy, we need to find ways of making it divisible and permeable at the lowest possible cost, according to local resources. If the soil to be amended consists entirely of brick or pottery soil, and it is possible to obtain some kind of fuel very cheaply, the most energetic method, giving the fullest and quickest results, is to burn it. It doesn't matter what kind of fuel you use: in Sologne, it's fir bunches that you don't know what to do with; in Picardy, it's peat; in Brittany, it's gorse and broom; elsewhere, it's coal or coke, or brushwood. The earth is opened up to form ridges 60 to 80 centimetres high; enough fuel is placed at the bottom of these ridges to make a fire that can last about two hours; the fuel is covered with large clods of earth to form a kind of furnace and allow the flames to penetrate between the clods; the fire is lit, then the holes are plugged with new clods of earth wherever the flames emerge. When the furnaces have cooled down, the clods are broken up with the head of the pickaxe; a single blow reduces them to dust. All that's then needed is to mix the product of the burning with the soil, add a generous amount of water and a good manuring, and the soil is immediately brought to the highest degree of strength and fertility. Whenever burning is not more expensive than transporting sand, it should be preferred. Not only does heavily heated clay never become plastic again and act like sand, but in this state it greedily absorbs atmospheric gases, and contributes powerfully to increasing the action of manures; in addition, burning radically purges the soil of all the insects that devastate the vegetable garden. For less clayey soils, known as "terres fortes", well-chosen amendments and good cultivation will soon make them remarkably fertile. Let's not forget that our vegetable garden will be abundantly smoked, ploughed three times a year, and hoeed eight or ten times, with each bed producing an average of at least three harvests. We'll add a few amendments to increase its fertility and hasten its loosening, and we'll only resort to burning for soils deemed impossible. First of all, let's assume that limestone amendments add an enormous amount of fertility to clay soils. If the soil is too compact, sand should be introduced, mixed well with the soil, and then limestone amendments18 should be used. Depending on their cost price, the following can be used indiscriminately: quicklime, in the proportion of two hectolitres per acre. It is first mixed with three to four times its volume of soil, to let it melt, and then the whole is mixed well together and added to the fertilizers to be spread evenly over the soil; MARN, in the proportion of two hectoliters per are, and mixed with the fertilizers; PLATRAS and old lime mortars, coming from the demolition of houses: they only cost the trouble of removing them in the vicinity of towns, and are as effective as lime and marl. They should be pulverized and mixed with fertilizers; ASH, in the absence of lime, marl or platras. Wood ashes are the best, but often very expensive. Charrée, the ash used to make laundry detergent, can be used successfully. Ashes from lime kilns and brickworks, less pure than those from fireplaces, are excellent and not very expensive. Coal ashes from factories are also a valuable soil improver, costing next to nothing; locomotive ashes, which clutter up all railway stations, are always very happy to be disposed of for a small gratuity. Locomotive ashes are a veritable treasure trove for vegetable gardens, field crops and vineyards, and almost everywhere they are dumped in public refuse dumps, just as black was once dumped in refineries. I have had large quantities removed from several stations, and all those who have used them following my advice have obtained excellent results, as I did. I cannot urge landowners, growers and winegrowers enough to remove them, and not to let a precious product intended to increase the richness of harvests go to waste, especially in clay and silica soils. Finally, if the landowner has no lime, marl or platras, and cannot obtain ashes, it will be easy to make them in thickly wooded countries such as Brittany and Sologne, etc., etc., with brambles, thorns, fir thinnings, heather, gorse, broom, seaweed, varecs, etc., etc.. The labor used to make ashes will be paid a hundredfold in Brittany shales and Sologne sands, which do not contain an atoma of limestone. A vegetable garden created in clay soil thus amended will give the most brilliant results; it will not be very early, but after four or five years of cultivation no garden will equal it for the richness and quantity of its produce. Well-cultivated clay soils are the most fertile, but also the most demanding; the owner must always remember that they need to be carefully amended, and the gardener must not forget that these soils require more work, and must be constantly loosened by good ploughing and frequent, deep hoeing. SILICE or sand is present in greater or lesser quantities in the constitution of all soils. It is found in several forms: as a rock crystal, insoluble in water and acids; as a very fine white powder, soluble in water and acids; and in combination with other substances, forming salts, where it plays the role of an acid, such as alumina, potash, etc. Siliceous soils, which vary from white to red depending on the quantity of iron oxide they contain, have the opposite characteristics to clay; they are friable, easy to work, permeable to air, but always exposed to drought, retaining only about 25% water. These soils are hasty, but very scorching in summer, and unless they can be watered constantly and copiously, it's hard to get a decent harvest. Vegetable gardens created in the sand are a ruin for the owner; he can only hope for good results once they have been completely saturated with fertilizer, and if he is able to water them at least twice a day from May to September. A vegetable garden of this kind is impossible; it uses twice as much fertilizer and ten times as much water (watering is the most expensive part of the job), as the harvest is worth, and if you neglect watering you get nothing. In that case, you have to abandon a ruinous crop in order to amend it. The most effective amendment is clay, which gives consistency to the soil, increases its fertility and enables it to retain a greater quantity of water. But clay doesn't mix well with sand; when used wet, it stays in clumps and doesn't work. To get good results, you need to do the following: obtain clay during the summer, lay it out in the sun, in a yard or in the alleys, until it is completely dry; then pulverize it completely, and cover the soil with a layer of this powder, about a centimetre thick, then plough it in during very dry weather. It's a long and painstaking operation, but it's the only and most economical way to improve soil that's too siliceous. Thanks to the perfect mixing of the clay, the soil takes on consistency and immediately becomes creamy. The result is a twofold increase in produce, and half the reduction in the twofold expense of fertilizing and watering. If the owner has an absolute need for a vegetable garden, and can only establish it in very siliceous soil, his first operation should be to amend; this will cause him an initial expense, but this expense will be an enormous saving of time and money, and the amendment, which is not very expensive, can be done in several stages. You can amend a quarter of the vegetable garden every year, and in any case, it would be better and more profitable to amend half or even a quarter of the garden completely, than to do the whole thing imperfectly. Cultivation is a work of knowledge, time and experience, and is as profitable when carried out under the right conditions as it is ruinous when directed by ignorance, routine and haste. If the necessary quantity of clay cannot be obtained cheaply, it can be replaced by the sludge from ponds, ditches, sewers and public laundries, which can be used in the same desiccated state as clay. Ashes also produce the best effects in siliceous soils, making them unctuous and increasing their fertility through the addition of potash. The best way to use them is to mix them with fertilizers. While essentially siliceous soils have many disadvantages, let's hasten to point out that there are some very fertile sandy soils, those containing a certain amount of clay, commonly known as greasy sands, and strongly colored red. This is the best soil of all for vegetable gardening. Its earliness and extreme fertility are due to the presence of iron oxide, which attracts and fixes atmospheric nitrogen in the form of ammonia. CALCIUM SOILS, made up of lime in the carbonate state, and containing varying amounts of clay and silica, are the least favorable for growing vegetables. Their white color repels the sun's rays, they heat up very poorly, absorb a considerable quantity of water very quickly, and dry out quickly. As a result, they are cold, late and always too wet or too dry. Calcareous soils, depending on their consistency, are amended with greater or lesser quantities of clay and silica. Strongly colored materials should always be chosen to mix with calcareous soils, either for amending or for smoking, so as to allow the action of the sun's rays to take hold and make their white color disappear. Brown clays, red sands and blacksmith's ash are the best amendments to introduce into limestone soils, along with animal black, soot and topsoil for fertilizers. With care and reasoning, these ungrateful soils can be improved enough, in just a few years, to produce excellent and abundant products. The first, most urgent and most profitable of all actions is that of amending the soil and placing it in good conditions of fertility; by giving it sufficient water, fertilizer and the necessary cultivation care, good results can still be obtained. It follows from the examination we have just made of the three main elements making up all soils: clay, silica and limestone, that not only does each of these elements separately provide an imperfect soil, unfit for most crops, but also that when they are mixed, the dominant element is detrimental to the quality of the soil and requires amendments. The model soil is Loam, known as Terre Franche in some countries. Loams contain 33 p. °l. of clay; 33 p. silica and 33 p. of limestone. All crops are possible in these soils, everything thrives and yields abundant produce with less fertilizer and labor. It's up to the grower to try to get as close as possible to this type of soil, with judiciously applied amendments; let him study, examine scrupulously, and operate wisely, without parsimony or prodigality, and he will soon be convinced of this great truth: there are no bad soils, only bad growers. Nature is more generous than the nonchalant suppose; she possesses treasures that only the industrious and enterprising man knows how to discover. It is not uncommon to find a clay soil on a siliceous subsoil, and a layer of limestone at a shallow depth. In such cases, all that needs to be done is to mix the separate layers, an operation made easy by breaking up the soil. As a general rule for all fruit and vegetable gardens, never choose a site, and never start any improvement work until you have sounded the soil in several places to a depth of one metre, and are fully aware of its chemical composition, and the resources of amendments it contains, if you don't want to expose yourself to making a bad operation, starting your work all over again, and making a double expenditure in pure loss. Before concluding on the subject of soil, let's say that the best soil will be almost infertile unless it contains a certain amount of humus. Humus is the basis of soil fertility; it comes from the decomposition of plant and animal matter. Humus supplies plants with nitrogen from the plants from which it is formed, and with carbonic acid gas, which permeates the water in the soil, forming an atmosphere overloaded with this acid at the foot of the plant and under the shelter of its leaves (plants absorb carbonic acid through their roots and leaves). Humus is all the more effective on vegetation because, like porous bodies, it has the ability to capture and condense the gases surrounding it. These gases are released when the temperature rises, or when humidity drives them out of the pores. Humus is a sort of reservoir of nutrients placed at the foot of the plant. We can easily introduce a greater or lesser quantity of humus or loam into the soil, and consequently direct vegetation almost as we please, if we know how to choose and manufacture our fertilizers properly and use them with care. Now that we've chosen our plot of land and recognized that it's suitable for establishing a vegetable garden, let's look at its design, shape, extent, distribution and layout. 10 SHAPE, EXTENT, FENCING, DISTRIBUTION, LAYOUT. I said earlier that the modern kitchen garden would bring about a revolution in vegetable cultivation as complete as that of fruit growing in the cultivation of fruit trees. I could say that this revolution has already taken place; my fruit gardens gave birth to it, with the first attack on the cluttered garden; all my followers are the instigators. It had to be after the results obtained in the fruit garden. Like arboriculture, the cultivation methods taught by the Potager Moderne are based on immutable principles rooted in the fundamental laws of vegetation; this cultivation has been sanctioned by many years of experience, and consecrated by success. It is enough to say that the exact application of the principles of the modern kitchen garden will be as fruitful in terms of results as the cultivation of fruit gardens; but we must not conceal the fact that this new culture, like all useful reforms, will meet with opposition from certain gardeners, because it opposes knowledge and logic to routine and prejudice, and modifies contracted habits through reasoning. The modern kitchen garden will eventually lead to the destruction of the cluttered garden. This destruction, so much to be desired from the point of view of the general interest, and above all the increase in public wealth, will undoubtedly stir up the camp of the princes of routine and the knights of the beaten rut. It will be up to the innovators to raise the flag of progress by producing comparative trials everywhere. This is the only way to convince the stubborn and even the furious. The result is everything for the masses; it's the supreme law before which those who don't want to take the trouble to study or reason bow. Nothing is so difficult to do as good! Nothing provokes so much anger and hatred as a just and sound idea, put forward to replace a program dictated by stupidity and routine; this is a sad and indisputable truth, but it is the duty of people with a good heart to have the courage to do good, and to brave stupid outcries and hatred, when they work to improve the lot of all and increase public wealth. I am starting a work of improvement with the publication of this book; I shall pursue it with the energy and perseverance that my listeners know me for, and I appeal to the most zealous among them, to assist me by creating everywhere the greatest possible number of specimen vegetable gardens, as small as they wish, but conforming to the indications of the modern Potager; they will not only work for their personal satisfaction, but will also contribute powerfully to a work all of utility and prosperity, here is how: The cultivation of vegetables is as backward as that of fruit trees. Everyone grows vegetables: only market gardeners cultivate them profitably, and yet vegetables are an object of primary necessity for all classes of society; their production can easily be increased tenfold on a third of the space occupied by the cluttered garden. Not only would this abundant production bring great well-being, especially to the poorer classes, but it would also create excellent positions for new producers, and work for a large number of workers, who, finding a high wage in their own country, would be less tempted to come to Paris and the big cities in search of misery. They occupy no less than a thousand hectares of the best land per department, i.e. 84 thousand hectares, not counting Corsica, Algeria and our new Savoy possessions. One third of this well-cultivated area, while increasing fruit and vegetable production tenfold, would make 56,000 hectares of elite land available for all crops, all of which would be a pure loss for the landowner, since these gardens are nothing but a burden for him, and for the public purse to which they contribute nothing. The destruction of overgrown gardens, to replace them with good crops, is therefore a humanitarian and national task, to which everyone is called upon to contribute by setting an example of more productive cultivation. Having said that, let me return to my subject: the first thing to do, after surveying the soil and making sure there's water in it, is to take a plan of the plot and study it on paper. The kitchen garden is not a luxury garden, still less a place for strolling. It must above all be a garden of produce, laid out and distributed for production. Fertility is the coquetry of the vegetable garden. Landscape architects have created many round vegetable gardens; the circular shape favors the exposure of walls, and is easier to conceal with flowerbeds; therein lie all its advantages, because on the other hand, it wastes the gardeners' time and hinders them from organizing the beds. The square shape is preferable and more convenient for cultivation. Secondly, a square is rarely perfect, and the tips often become a valuable resource for layout, and kitchen gardening. The abundant production we demand from the vegetable garden does not exclude order and cleanliness. 18 Nothing is more disgusting than to see piles of manure and debris of all kinds in a garden; tools, mats, bells, frames, or pottery lying around in every corner; whatever the shape of the vegetable garden, it's essential to establish everything close by, better still in a well-hidden corner: 1° a small yard for making manure, potting soil and mulch; 2° a greenhouse for tools, in which they will be carefully hung; 3° another greenhouse for frames, bells, useless pottery, doormats, etc., etc.; 4° An attic or small room of some sort to hold oars, stilts, stakes, etc., etc., etc. The main flaw in today's vegetable gardens is that they are always too big: this is more than a flaw, it's a vice. A vegetable garden that's too big requires very expensive labor, which always leaves something to be desired, and there's never enough fertilizer. The expense is high and the harvest mediocre. The size of the vegetable garden should be calculated as follows: two acres of land for each inhabitant, master or servant, and one acre for each stranger who comes to stay for a few days. Let's assume what happens very often: a house where the husband and wife live alone with a maid; a vegetable garden of 6 ares will suffice to provide for all their needs. 2 If the master of the house has a taste for gardening, his small garden will cost him 4 cars of manure at 6 fr. per year. . . . . 2h fr. 8 man-days at 2 fr. at different times, for plowing and bedding... ... ... ... ... ... ... . 16 hOfr. If the owner likes gardening, he'll do all the rest, which is not at all tiring, and with an annual expense of L0 fr. he'll supply his house abundantly with vegetables. 20 melons will totally reimburse him for his expenses. Who's to stop him from sending 20 melons to market, in addition to his supply? If this same owner doesn't want to touch his garden, and is content to direct the gardener, following the indications in Potager moderne, his garden will cost him per year: h cars of manure at 6 fr. . . .. 2hfr. 20 gardener days at 2 fr. 50C. . 50 10 of woman at 1 fr. . . . . 10 Seeds ... ... ... ... ... ... ... . 4 88 fr. At this price, there's still a big profit to be made from harvesting all your vegetables, not to mention the fruit on the walls. For an average house, with four masters, two servants, a gardener and his wife, and where four foreigners are received each year, a vegetable garden of 20 ares is sufficient to be richly supplied all year round. If instead of four people, eight are received, the vegetable garden will be increased by h ares. A gardener with his wife will perfectly look after a vegetable garden of 25 to 30 ares, and will only need a helper if he also has a somewhat extensive park. I insist on the figure of two ares per person because it is more than sufficient, if we apply to the letter the culture of the modern vegetable garden. The vegetable garden must be enclosed, to defend it from the attacks of marauders and rabbits, and to further increase its earliness, with the help of artificial shelters; the best of all fences is a wall; it is also the most expensive, but the interest on the capital employed for the construction of the walls is very largely paid for by the production of espaliers. If the vegetable garden is round, we don't need to worry about the orientation of the walls; the circular shape is favourable. But if it's square, we'll have to place the corners at the four cardinal points, contrary to what's usually done. Here's why: if we orient one wall to the clear south, an exposure at which the trees roast and the fruit burns nine times out of ten, the wall facing it is exposed to the north, and remains bare most of the time because nothing comes to it; the side walls offer the east exposure, which is too dry, and the west exposure, which is too humid. In short: four walls with four bad exposures! However, if these walls are to be built, they should be used as they are - Fruit Growing provides the means to do this - but if they are to be built, you should proceed as follows: place the four corners of the garden, to the north, south, east and west. In order to have, on the walls, the mixed exposures of south-east and south-west, north-east and north-west, all excellent and likely to give a product double that of the previous ones. If the kitchen garden has to be set up in the middle of a park, and needs to be hidden from view, only two walls can be built: one to the north, giving the southern exposure; the other to the west, giving the eastern exposure. These two walls can be trimmed with espaliers, and the other two faces, those to the south and east, can be enclosed with a dry hedge, or better still, with four or six lines of horizontal wire, placed 20 centimetres apart, and against which a hedge of Bengal roses is planted, having the double advantage of perfectly masking the vegetable garden, and giving flowers all year round. Here's how it's done: (see Arboriculture fruitière for how to lay the wires.) Once the wires have been laid, the Bengal roses are planted 30 centimetres apart; they are left to root well for the first year, and in the following spring only one stem is kept, the most vigorous, which is itself received 25 centimetres from the ground. Only two buds are kept on each stem, one on the right, the other on the left; they are planted on the wires, inclined at an angle of 60 degrees, and when they have reached a length of around 60 centimetres, they are intertwined with their neighbors. The hedge formed from regular lozanges is very attractive, and remarkably strong, in that all the branches naturally graft together at all points where they come into contact. A hedge formed in this way provides an impenetrable enclosure and a magnificent blooming appearance. Large trees can be planted in front of the two walls built to the north and west, firstly to hide the walls, then to double the artificial shelter with a higher natural shelter, but the large trees must be planted at least six meters from the walls so as not to harm the espaliers, either by their roots or by their shade. Finally, with a little imagination and practice, it's always easy to create a fertile vegetable garden, even in the middle of a park, and to hide it completely by clumps of trees, shrubs or rose hedges. Once you've chosen a location and fencing system, it's time to think about the distribution of the vegetable garden. The four-year rotation is the one we'll generally adopt as the most profitable; we'll therefore need four squares of equal size, in the center of which we'll set up a well or basin, so that we can water these squares copiously with a small pump. This is the most economical method, and I'll talk more about it in the chapter on watering. You should never be frightened by the irregularity of the terrain when creating a vegetable garden; the most irregular sites are those from which the best advantage can be taken, and which can be laid out most conveniently. In addition to the four squares, you need to think about creating a plan for asparagus outside the crop rotation, and sometimes small reserves for raising flowers destined for the beds, a small yard for manure and potting soil, and sheds, in the absence of buildings to hold tools, frames, etc., etc., etc... It's urgent to have all this on hand, and you need to have been a grower to know how important it is. Let's assume a 30 to 40 ares vegetable garden, well laid out, with all its outbuildings to hand. This vegetable garden will be perfectly cared for by the gardener and his wife, but if the manures, potting soils, frames, bells, stilts, oars and stakes are squeezed into a remote place, the gardener and his wife will have to go out of their way twenty times a day, to fetch a hundred items they'll need every moment. One of two things: either the work will be neglected, and the produce reduced with the same expense, or a helper will be needed who will increase the expense, to obtain the same result as if the vegetable garden had been well laid out. Cultivation is only as profitable as the work is well organized, and this is what is lacking most of the time in the creation of gardens, because most often those who create them are strangers to cultivation; they make very pretty drawings, it's true, and very elegant fantasia, but they know neither the needs nor the requirements. Hence the incessant to-ing and fro-ing, wasting half the time of an intelligent man paid according to his merit. Where the hard-working gardener will inevitably lose a third of his day in coming and going, the non-hard-working gardener will lose most of it. It's impossible to give a specimen plan that can be used for grounds of different conformations. Nevertheless, I've included plans of two vegetable gardens I've created, which may be of use to homeowners who wish to design their own gardens. If they don't want to take the trouble, or are afraid of making a mistake, they'll have to ask a special man (pl. 1 and 2). In any case, anyone designing a vegetable garden must never forget that manure, soil, water and all the necessary shelter and tools must be at hand. If the vegetable garden is large enough, look for a place outside the crop rotation to create an asparagus plan; divide the four squares subject to crop rotation into equal parts, and orient the square beds from north to south, and make alleys around and in the middle of the garden wide enough for wheelbarrows to circulate easily. When the plan has been sufficiently worked out on paper, the layout is laid out on the ground as follows: first, the beds and squares are marked out with a chalk line; at each corner, a sturdy stake is driven in at least 60 centimetres to find the reference points; all the measurements are checked; the paths are then emptied to a depth of about 20 centimetres, with the soil thrown back onto the beds and squares so that the layout does not fade, and the soil is prepared. DISEESOE GO LEVELLING, AMENDMENT AND RIPPING. Once the layout has been laid out on the ground, and the stakes placed at each corner for easy retrieval, we proceed to levelling. But first, the ground must be carefully examined to level it, if it is not well levelled, which is the first condition to make watering profitable for the plants. Levelling should be carried out at the same time as ripping, by the diggers who perform it, and without any great increase in cost. It's easy for them to do when their work is well prepared, and costs them little more trouble, which is a great saving for the owner. Levelling by the day, before or after ripping, is often more expensive than ripping, and has the disadvantage of damaging the soil, especially after ripping. However, if the ground is covered with mounds of earth, or has a very steep slope, it should be roughly levelled before scribing. In all other cases, proceed as follows: Take three levelers (pl. 3, fig. 1), or better still four, for each side of the square to be levelled, i.e. twelve for a square we assume to have 18 metres on each side. The first leveller is placed not quite at the corner of the square, but 20 centimetres from this corner, in the driveway, so as to drive levelling pegs all around the square, so that the diggers don't disturb them while digging and use them for levelling. With the first leveller placed at a height of around 80 centimetres, a second is driven in 6 metres from the first, and at the same height; then the third is placed 6 metres from the second, all three are aligned one on top of the other, and the fourth is placed at the other corner. Then, as we've just done, we place three levelers, 6 meters apart, on each of the two faces adjoining the one already levelled, and two on the fourth, to determine a perfectly horizontal line all around the square. Once this has been done, a levelling rod (pl. 3, fig. 2) is adjusted to the length of a leveller of average height, which is the height the ground should have, and a peg is driven in between each leveller with a mallet, adjusting its height with the levelling rod. In addition, when placing the levelling pegs, you must take into account the overrun of the excavated earth, which is about one tenth: thus, when excavating to a depth of one metre, you must count on ten centimetres of overrun, and place the pegs ten centimetres higher than the actual level. Once the stakes have been driven in, the levellers are removed one by one, and a stake adjusted with the levelling rod is placed in their hole, and if necessary with two rods when there are not enough levellers left. The head of each stake represents the exact height the ground should be; these stakes, placed three meters apart at the edge of the path, are a sure guide for the diggers, and make it easy to check the levelling at any time, with a rod placed on the stakes at each end, and a third placed on the ground. A bonus of five or ten francs, depending on the size of the garden, will sufficiently compensate the diggers for the time they have wasted checking their levelling with a levelling rod. The same stakes will be used to level the paths once they have been levelled. It is useful to give them a general slope, almost imperceptible to the eye, but necessary to facilitate water run-off during rainstorms. A slope of two millimeters per meter is sufficient; let's take as an example our 18-meter square, with seven stakes on each side. These stakes are placed three meters apart, and the overall slope is 36 millimeters. The stakes must therefore be driven in as follows: the first will not be touched; the second will be driven in by 6 millimeters; the third by 12; the fourth by 18; the fifth by 24; the sixth by 30, and the seventh by 36. All that remains to be done is to put soil up to the head of the stakes, and the slope will be adjusted with great accuracy, without pain or unnecessary expense. Alleys should not be levelled until at least three weeks after breaking, when the earth has regained its plumbness, and the levelling has been checked and found to be accurate; but, I cannot recommend it strongly enough, the levelling stakes should not be disturbed until the broken ground has been completely settled. Once the levelling stakes have been placed, we proceed with the general ripping. There are three ways to break up the soil, depending on its quality and depth: 1° WITH OPEN GAUGE. If the soil is of the same quality down to a depth of one meter, and if there are no amendments to be introduced, all that needs to be done is to break up the soil, at the following depths: 80 centimeters in clayey soils, 1 meter in medium-consistency soils, and 1 meter 10 centimeters in sandy soils. This work is paid by the job: 25 to 30 centimes per metre in clay soils, depending on their tenacity, and 20 centimes in medium-consistency soils and in sands. In all cases, whether for planting trees or for vegetable garden beds, smoking must never be allowed while ripping. This is what is most often done: in this case, manure buried very deeply, and therefore placed beyond the reach of the roots, is entirely lost. Ripping must always be done by the company or by the job: only then can it be done at the prices I have indicated. Whenever an owner tries to have them done by the day, no matter how cheaply he pays the workers, the work is poorly done and comes back at an exorbitant price. A good digger does an average of 20 meters of digging in a day; he always asks to work by the job; he prefers to take the trouble and earn the money. A poor day laborer, on the other hand, does barely four meters a day, and constantly repeats that ripping is an impossible job. In this case, if the owner lives in a country where the workers are too soft to undertake the task, i.e. are not willing to go to a little trouble to earn h to 5 francs a day, he will have a great advantage in bringing in diggers and paying them for their trip there and back. I sent some to Berry and Touraine, the owners were delighted, and the local workers considered them fantastic workers. Ripping should never be done with a spade, but always with a pick and shovel. Breaking up with a spade is disastrous for the landowner, in that the soil is turned upside down, with the good soil at the bottom and the bad soil on top, and can remain completely infertile for several years, and ruinous for the workman, because he makes no progress, and earns pitiful days with great difficulty. Both would do well to keep quiet. Raking with a pickaxe is the only way to benefit the owners, and the only way to benefit the worker, especially if he knows how to avoid transporting earth: Let's say we have to break up four vegetable garden squares separated by a cross-shaped path (pl. 3, fig. 3). We'll have all four squares dug without rolling a single wheelbarrow of earth. We'll open the trench at the corner of the first square facing the middle alley (A pl. 3, fig. 3). This trench will be four meters wide and two meters long, so that three men can work in it abreast without getting in each other's way. The earth from the trench opening will be thrown into the alley next to its opening (B pl. 3, fig. 3). Six men will go down into the trench and work in threes abreast: three to pick and three to shovel, taking turns. The first three will cut the soil at the bottom of the trench, in 30 to h0 centimetre thick slices, to the required depth; the second three will pick up the soil with their shovels as it falls under the pickaxe, and mix it again by throwing it behind them, up to the height of the levelling stakes. In this way, the soil will be perfectly mixed, giving one meter of ford of excellent soil of the same consistency. This soil will always be fresh, remarkably fertile and, thanks to its natural freshness, will save half on watering. The trench has been opened at the left-hand corner of the first square; the trenching will be carried out in such a way as to end at the opposite corner, the right-hand corner at the bottom of the square, where there will be a void equal to the soil removed and left in reserve in the alley (C pl. 3, fig. 3); this void will be filled with the soil from the opening of the trench at the right-hand corner of the second square; this soil will be shovelled over the alley (D pl. 3, fig. 3). The second square will be ripped out like the first and finished with the left-hand corner at the bottom (E pl. 3, fig. 3); this new void will be filled by opening the trench of the third square at the corner facing the void (Fpl. 3, fig. 3); this square will be ripped out like the previous two and finished with one of the corners bordering the transverse aisle (Gpl. 3, fig. 3); the void will be filled by opening the trench of the fourth square (H pl. 3, fig. 3), which the diggers will end at the right angle, at the top, where the earth reserve from the opening of the first trench is located (I pl. 3, fig. 3). All that's left to do is shovel it in to fill the final void. In all trenching operations, it is essential to mix the top, middle and bottom layers of soil together; to remove stones and roots with the utmost care, to break up any clods of soil, and to bury quackgrass very deeply if there is any; it never grows back when buried more than 60 centimetres deep. The soil in a vegetable garden that has been broken up must be completely cleaned. This is a one-off operation, but it must be done properly if it is to be profitable. 2° DEFONCEMENT WITH AMENDMENT is done at the same depth as the previous operation, and is carried out in the same way, with a shovel and a pickaxe, so that the mixture of soil and amendment is perfect. If the soil is clayey, it's best to use as much topsoil as possible. Soil from the surface of the alleys should be removed to the depth of a spade shoe, and thrown into the flowerbeds, to be replaced in the alleys by soil from the bottom of the trench. Surface soil, exposed to the influence of atmospheric agents, is always loose, whereas bottom soil is excessively plastic. When an amendment is added to the soil, such as platras, sand or coal ash, it is spread evenly over the square to be trenched; the soil from the alleys is thrown on top; the whole is mixed well while trenching, and the soil from the bottom is thrown into the alleys to replace that removed. The price for this breaking down is the same as for the previous one, but with the difference that the earth taken from the paths is paid to the earthworkers at 10 cents per metre of surface area. When the amendments are applied to siliceous soils, and it is not necessary to take 8 soil from the alleys, ripping is paid for at the same price as open-gauge ripping. Only the spreading of the amendment is paid for separately, by the day. 3° DEFONCEMENTS WITH CHANGED SOIL are the longest and most expensive, and are carried out in shallow soils whose subsoil is unsuitable for cultivation, when it is composed of clay, marl or tuff. We start by taking all the topsoil from the alleys, and throwing it onto the squares; we open the trench as I mentioned earlier, mix in all the good soil, and throw the subsoil back into the alleys. When there are 50 or 60 centimetres of good soil, the work isn't enormous, and it's quite easy to make a metre of excellent soil, without too much ploughing. In this case, you pay an extra five centimes for breaking up the soil; the paths count as breaking up the soil; the diggers graze the earth and level the paths. But when there is only 20 or 40 centimetres of good soil, the operation becomes more difficult and more expensive. In this case, there's no room for hesitation: create deep, rich soil where none exists. Despite the apparent difficulties involved, it is no more costly, when done properly, than a poorly executed rip in good soil. The first thing to do is to take all the topsoil that can be found in the alleys and throw it onto the squares. Once the trench has been opened, all the good earth is used; the bad earth is then extracted and used to fill the voids in the alleys, and the excess is taken away, to be replaced by good earth, strong or light, depending on the consistency of the soil, taken from elsewhere, and used lastly to recharge the trench and complete the levelling. These extremes are rare and exceptional. Most of the time, the subsoil can be partially mixed with the soil from the squares and alleys, without having to resort to costly ploughing. However, when it comes down to it, and a vegetable garden is essential, it should be created as small as possible, but done properly: such an operation, if poorly executed, would be quite costly and give only poor results. In this case, the cost of clearing is ten centimes more per metre than the current price; the driveways count as clearing; the earthworkers level them and load the cars with bad earth extracted from the ground, and they are paid extra, by the day, for spreading the earth brought in. Before concluding on the subject of ripping, it should always be done in advance, and in dry weather, especially in clay soils. As far as possible, they should be done in autumn or early winter, so that the garden can be cultivated in spring. Creating a garden is a serious matter, if you want to do it well and achieve profitable results. It requires the intervention of a special man, for the plan and arrangements to be made, and the indications to be given for execution. This man, almost always overloaded with work, needs the time to study the necessary arrangements in all their details, and also, for the workers, the indispensable time to carry out their work in good conditions; if haste is harmful in almost all the circumstances of our lives, I could say that it is the worst of all things in the creation of a garden. DIEEEGE GO ASSOLEMENT, FERTILIZER DISTRIBUTION, LAYERS. Crop rotation! It's the key to fertility, the basis of production in the vegetable garden and in field crops. And who doubts, in this day and age, among the majority of gardeners, and even among some doctors of horticulture, that a well-understood rotation of the vegetable garden is the first element of success, a sure guarantee of both richness and economy in production? Rational crop rotation is the basis of the soil's fertility, and the key to the vegetable garden's wealth, because crop rotation contains: 1° RESPECT FOR THE LAW OF ALTERNATION. This law means that the same product, however abundantly smoked and well cultivated, rarely yields a maximum crop two years running in the same plot. Old practitioners, measuring their knowledge not by experiments, which they refuse even to indulge in, but by their dexterity in handling the spade and rake, will say from the outset, and without any circumlocution whatsoever: This is all nonsense, I sow peas and onions too, for four, five years, in the same plot, and I still get some! Some go so far as to ask us in all seriousness whether we invented peas and onions, and then dare to hold it against them, who sowed them before we were born! We hasten to decline the honor of inventing peas and onions, and even go so far as to confess, in all humility, that we still harvest them by sowing ten years in a row in the same soil; But on the strength of our studies, our observations and our experience, we affirm that from the second year onwards, there is a decrease in the product; in the third, degeneration, tardiness and a very noticeable decrease in the harvest, and I would even go so far as to assert that the longer successive sowings are in the same soil, the greater the degeneration. What's more, I'm deeply convinced that the bastardized vegetable varieties, barely fit to feed animals, found in almost all regions of France where vegetable growing has been entirely localized, have only reached this state of degeneration and tardiness as a result of successive sowings in the same beds. I would go even further and say to those who want to propagate routine varieties: Take your seeds of varieties that have returned almost 4 to the original type through promiscuity; sow them for five or six years, and every year the new seeds in new soil, and you will succeed, if not in recovering the primitive variety, at least in appreciably improving the one you have sown. Neither you nor I made this law, it emanates from God, your greater or lesser skill in handling the spade and rake will not destroy it, and routine is powerless to ward off its effects. So let's put all false pride aside and sow our vegetable garden for four years, so that the same species only returns to the same patch every four years. In this way, we'll not only be assured of maximum harvests, but also of keeping pure the precious varieties that will replace your woody vegetables. I'm not calling for the upheaval of everything that exists in order to create something new. Far from it, knowing how great the force of habit is, I will say here, as in my courses, to all those who hesitate: Before destroying, experiment; make comparative tests, they alone can enlighten you in a certain way. If you have a vegetable garden made up of four squares cultivated in the local fashion, continue your usual cultivation in three; try the one taught in this book in the fourth, and when you have seen the results and compared the expenses and produce of the two crops, adopt frankly and without second thoughts, the one that will have given you the best and most abundant produce, with the least expense, 2° THE ECONOMY OF FERTILIZERS THROUGH THEIR REASONED DISTRIBUTION. I posit that the same quantity of fertilizer, used with discernment over a given area, will produce six, when the same quantity, and even a greater quantity, distributed wrongly over the same area, will produce barely one, with the same amount of labor. Let's suppose a vegetable garden of twenty-four ares, divided into four equal parts, spending annually twenty cars of manure. If this vegetable garden is not subject to crop rotation, these twenty cars of manure will be equally distributed over the twenty-four ares. In this case, the four squares will receive insufficient manure, and especially if it has been buried before it has entered a state of decomposition, as is done almost everywhere, under the pretext of warming the soil, here is what will happen almost infallibly: Artichokes, potatoes, cabbages, leeks, etc., etc., lacking sufficient nourishment, will give stunted, hard, stringy, stunted, in a word inedible products, which will take twice as long to complete their pitiful vegetation, as these same prime products would have required, with sufficient nourishment. Roots, strawberries, lettuces, etc. etc., will sprout leaves and go to seed on their fresh manure, before they have produced a passable root, a succulent fruit or a presentable apple. Poorly applied fresh manure, sufficient to destroy the first crop, will be almost totally absorbed by it, and the following crop will be nil. Peas, beans, etc., etc., on a quarter of fresh manure, will produce gigantic stalks and no grains. The owner and gardener will watch them grow every day, hoping for fruit when they stop growing. Finally, when they've smothered everything, the fruit appears so scarce that it's decided to uproot them to save the other crops, and the gardener says as he loads up a car with fodder: the land is worthless for vegetables, nothing succeeds! The soil is excellent, most of the time, the crop alone is bad. The twenty-four-acre vegetable garden, smoked in this way, has yielded nothing but pitiful produce: not a single melon, not the slightest early crop. If we sow this same vegetable garden with an equal quantity of manure for four years, we'll obtain maximum crops of open ground everywhere, and a good supply of melons and early vegetables, a huge advantage of crop rotation; 3° THE POSSIBILITY OF OBTAINING AN ABUNDANT PRODUCTION OF EARLY VEGETABLES AS AN ADDITIONAL CROP, NOT ONLY WITHOUT ANY INCREASE IN FERTILIZER EXPENDITURE, BUT ALSO BY IMPROVING FERTILIZERS AND MAKING THEM MORE SUITABLE FOR BURIAL! Fertilizers can only be assimilated in a liquid or gaseous state; in other words, plants can only absorb the nutrients they contain once they have decomposed. Once it has been proven that decomposed fertilizers have an immediate and more energetic effect on plants than fresh manure, it is to our advantage to decompose our fertilizers before burying them. The most economical and profitable way to decompose fertilizers is in layers. These layers will give us all the primeurs we want, at no cost other than the initial purchase of a few frames and bells. The savings in manpower that would have been needed to handle the manures will more than cover the cost of maintaining the frames and bells, and the annual harvest will only be burdened by the initial purchase of the equipment. In the past, the installation of a bed was considered difficult, expensive and even ruinous. It's a mistake we're beginning to come round to, and let's face it, when the use of diapers is popularized, and their usefulness is generally recognized, open-ground crops will double their output, and the smallest garden, while doubling its production, will yield melons and early fruit without any expense whatsoever; there won't be a village, a hamlet, where the parish priest or the schoolteacher can't harvest in quantity if they want to take the trouble. We are assured of the praiseworthy zeal of the country parish priests. We have seen them at work in several départements, and we know how enthusiastically they have welcomed our arboriculture lessons, and how devotedly they continue to teach them to their parishioners, in their own interest, and in that of public wealth. They eagerly await the modern Potager to complete their work, all charity and devotion. I would like to take this opportunity to express my gratitude to the clergy, especially those of the Oise department, for the special care they have shown me, and for their noble zeal in bringing light to the most backward regions. Thank you for having so helpfully assisted me in my work; thank you to the clergy, thank you for the country, thank you again to the Brothers of the Christian Doctrine for their praiseworthy eagerness, and their tireless zeal to teach the true principles of cultivation and arboriculture to children. Their noble efforts will be crowned with success, and soon their dedication will bear fruit in bringing light, ease and progress to small farmers. Thanks to the initiative of several prefects, and their particular benevolence for agricultural interests, primary school teachers are rendering important services by teaching cultivation and horticulture to the children of rural populations deprived of the courses given in the towns. This useful and praiseworthy teaching is already bearing fruit in certain areas, especially in the department of Oise, where primary school teachers, encouraged and stimulated by higher authority, are competing in zeal not only for teaching, but also for horticultural practice. The teachers of the Oise department, authorized by the Prefect to give their pupils time off to attend arboriculture lessons, have already achieved brilliant results in arboriculture, and are also waiting for the Potager Moderne to teach and practice vegetable growing, which is so useful and profitable for rural populations who are unaware of it, and can derive definite benefits from it. Assured of the intelligent and devoted support of the clergy, schoolteachers and a good number of landowners, I close my ears to the cries of routine and actively pursue a project which, I am convinced, will render important services by creating new wealth in regions deprived of horticultural education. Layers are, in fact, the factory of assimilable manure and fertilizer par excellence, and it is in the grower's interest to establish as many of them as possible, in order to increase both the crops grown in the open ground and the products from frames and bells, which are always very expensive, and cost nothing when layers are included in the vegetable garden rotation.
