It's easy to build up a large number of layers with a great saving in fertilizers, frames and bells, by using horse manure mixed with leaves for warm layers; cow manure mixed with leaves, mosses, etc., for warm layers, and herbaceous matter mixed with a little manure for dull layers, and by using economical shelters for certain products instead of frames and bells. In this way, we can considerably increase the mass of fertilizers and potting soils, so useful for certain vegetable plants and for flower breeding, and obtain everything we could possibly want with the greatest economy, while at the same time improving fertilizers. I shall devote a special chapter to the manufacture of hot, warm and muted layers, and to the shelters designed to cover them. I shall also devote a chapter to the rotation of each of the gardens I deal with: those of the owner, the tenant, the farmer, the presbytery, the communities and boarding houses, the teachers, the railroads and the camps, each of which requires a special cultivation and rotation. I'm only dealing here with the four-year vegetable garden rotation in principle, and reserving the right to modify it for each of the gardens I've mentioned. In the kitchen garden, we grow vegetables that require very copious manuring to give good results; these are artichokes, potatoes, cabbages, leeks, etc., etc.; others that require a large quantity of humus in the soil, and do not do well on fresh manure; these are roots, strawberries, lettuces, etc., etc.; still others that require a large quantity of humus in the soil, and do not do well on fresh manure; these are roots, strawberries, lettuces, etc., etc.; still others that require a large quantity of humus in the soil, and do not do well on fresh manure, etc.; still others produce only stems on fresh manure, and require a certain quantity of potash instead of fertilizer in order to bear fruit, such as peas, beans, etc., etc. We have seen the results of these various crops on a quarter, and even a third, of fresh manure, results that are unfortunately all too frequent in the many gardens we visit during our excursions in the départements; let's now look at those obtained in the vegetable garden subjected to four-year crop rotation. Let's assume a vegetable garden of twenty-four ares, divided into four equal parts, and with twenty cars of manure to spend annually. If we don't let anything go to waste, and if we carefully make our composts, we'll make twenty cars with five of manure. In the first year, we'll spread the twenty cars of compost on square n°1, to which we'll mix the most active manure from the demolition of the layers, and we'll bury the whole thing in this square, destined to receive all the vegetables requiring very abundant nourishment, then we'll reserve the most decomposed compost and manure from the demolition of the layers, to mulch and top-dress the vegetables in square n°2. The previous year, square n° 2 received the same amount of manure as square n° 1, and produced a maximum harvest of hearty vegetables. The composts and bedding manure are fully decomposed, and despite the abundant production of the previous year, the soil is saturated with humus, thanks to the maximum manuring given. This same year, we'll be growing roots, strawberries, tomatoes, lettuces, etc., in plot no. 2, without fertilizer, as these plants dread fresh fertilizer; a topdressing and mulches applied with our layer residues, which have been set aside, will be sufficient to obtain a maximum harvest in this plot. Two years ago, square no. 3 was fertilized in the same way as square no. 1, and the previous year with the same topdressing and mulch as square no. 2. This unfertilized, but abundantly ashed square will be devoted to the cultivation of vegetables with dried fruits, such as peas, beans, etc., which will still give a maximum harvest, in soil depleted of fertilizer, but provided with ashes, the only condition in which they can fructify abundantly. Finally, square No. L, which was fertilized three years ago, top-dressed and mulched two years ago, and ashed the previous year, will be destined to receive : 1 The beds we will build there with our reserve of fifteen cars of horse manure, with added leaves, for the warm and hot beds, and herbaceous matter for the dull beds; 2° Without any manuring, all the flower and vegetable seed crops, which will give the best results in a soil exhausted of fertilizer and still well ashed; 3° An early pea crop, without fertilizer and with partial ashing, and even a green bean crop if required; 4° Flower and vegetable nurseries with topdressing and even maximum fertilization, ending the rotation and starting it again the following year with full fertilization. So, after harvesting peas and green beans, it's easy to use the compost or manure from the first layers to establish the vegetable nurseries so necessary in the kitchen garden. Four-year crop rotation offers the following advantages over other crops: 1° To produce maximum harvests on each sole, the land being in the state of cultivation and fertilization that is most favorable to the plants it supports; 2° To spend no more on fertilizers or labor than crops with much lower yields; 3° To enable the grower to produce an abundant crop of early vegetables, with almost no additional expense; 5° To regularize the fertility of all parts of the garden, through regular fertilization and cultivation; 6° To further increase fertility, by introducing into the soil, at regular times, a stimulant (ash) that powerfully activates the action of fertilizers. Having set out these principles, let's look at the most suitable rotation for each of the gardens I'm working on, according to the fertilizer resources available, the means of producing the greatest quantity, and the vegetable varieties specific to each garden. 19 FERTILIZERS, CROP ROTATION, VARIETIES OF VEGETABLES TO BE GROWN. I have purposely excluded from this book the kitchen garden of a château that always has a skilled gardener. By owner's vegetable garden, I mean that of a house with a fortune of ten to twenty-five thousand francs, large enough to provide vegetables for the countryside during the summer, and for the town throughout the winter. FERTILIZER SERVICE. First and foremost, we need to organize the fertilizer service and production. Let's assume that the house has two horses, two cows, a pig and a farmyard, and that with the fertilizers from these animals we have to meet Siuseg G the needs of a park adorned with flower baskets, the fruit garden and the vegetable garden. This is possible if we take the trouble to make our fertilizers properly, and don't let anything go to waste. The first thing to do is to organize the fertilizer factory. We'll choose a northern location, sheltered by tall trees, where the sun doesn't easily penetrate; we'll set up two platforms, about 30 centimetres above ground level, and both inclined towards the center (A pl. 3, fig. 4), so that all the liquid parts fall into a pit that we'll establish there. LIQUID FERTILIZERS. The liquid fertilizer pit will cut the platforms in half along their entire length (B pl. 3, fig. A); it should be wide enough and deep enough to hold twenty to thirty bariques of liquid. This pit must be perfectly watertight; the best way to do this is to build it in bricks macerated with cement, and to line the inside with a good cement plaster, which is the most solid and perhaps the most economical. The liquid fertilizer factory should be all the more active as it is intended not only to supply the needs of vegetable and fruit gardens, but also to contribute to the manufacture of a large quantity of excellent compost. We'll put in this pit all the liquid manure from the stables and cowsheds, the urine from the house, the water from 8 dishes, soap, washing powder, in short, all the liquids we can get our hands on. Nothing could be easier than to collect the liquid manure and all the household wastewater in a receptacle of some kind, and carry it to the pit every day, or to install very economical underground pipes, with cement-welded drainpipes, which will carry the liquid manure from the stable and barn, and the household wastewater from the dwelling house, to the liquid fertilizer pit. In addition to the liquids brought into the pit, we'll add all the manure from the pigeon houses and hen houses, which must be carefully cleaned every week. This is an infallible way of ensuring that we don't lose the pigeons, keeping the poultry healthy and ridding them of the insects that devour them. Latrines with a movable barrel will be set up for servants and workers; all faecal matter from them will also be disposed of in the liquid fertilizer pit. If it is possible to set up mobile barrels, as is done in many places, for the masters, this should be done as a matter of urgency, and the product added to the liquid fertilizer. A few kilograms of iron sulfate thrown into the pit immediately neutralizes the odor. If it were not possible to collect the faeces, it would be necessary to buy

In all cases, liquid fertilizers should be disinfected, firstly in the interests of health, secondly to remove workers' reluctance to use them, and thirdly because iron sulfate, while disinfecting, also increases the value of the fertilizer. With twenty-five or thirty barrels of liquid fertilizer composed in this way, together with manure from cows, pigs, rabbits and the farmyard, it will be easy to make a very large quantity of excellent compost that will ensure the fertility of the vegetable garden. MANURE. Horse manure can be stored in reserve for making diapers. Simply place it in a dry heap, sheltered from rain, and store it for as long as you like. Horse manure has the property of keeping indefinitely when dry, and of fermenting and developing its full caloric action as soon as it is wetted. The mass of layers can be increased by mixing horse manure, which has been set aside for this purpose, with leaves from the park and, if necessary, those collected in the woods. Some cow manure, mixed with leaves, can also be used in the spring for warm, muted layers. Now that we've secured our layer service, let's get back to making fertilizer. With the pit filled, as we've just indicated, with liquid fertilizer made from liquid manure, urine and all household water, plus columbine and faecal matter collected in the house, and in their absence of poudrette, we need to think about increasing the mass of fertilizer, with composts of herbaceous matter, which are most often left to waste. Here's how it works: COMPOST. Start by making a heap of all the detritus from the vegetable garden; herbs from weeding and hoeing: stalks from vegetables, asparagus, artichokes, peas, beans, cabbage cores, lettuce, etc., etc.; in a word, all the vegetable matter you can get your hands on; all is good, provided it is used fresh and never left to dry out. To avoid weed production, it's a good idea to use only plants before they flower, or even in flower, but never in seed. This would have the disadvantage of filling composts with seeds that would soon germinate and, if not suffocate crops, at least cause enormous weeding labor. Any defoliated grass should be set aside for burning, and the ash added to the compost. Depending on your resources and the size of the property, you can considerably increase the compost mass by adding broom, gorse, heather, reeds, leaves, marsh and ditch grasses, seaweed, kelp, hedge and lawn clippings, etc., etc., etc... If the property contains a certain quantity of brambles and thistles, they should be cut back as a matter of urgency to prevent them from multiplying, and burning them produces excellent ashes that add to the quality of the fertilizer. 8 Sawdust and shavings left to rot in the woods for most of the year will provide valuable ashes, which will also bring their share of fertility to the vegetable garden, especially in regions where the soil is devoid of limestone, such as Brittany and Sologne. You can't make too much of ashes in these regions, where they're more valuable than fertilizers. Once the plant matter has been brought together, a first bed, thirty to forty centimetres thick, is formed on one of the platforms; this bed is covered with manure, then watered copiously, every two or three days, with the liquid fertilizer contained in the pit. This is done with a scoop when the pile is not too high, and with a liquid manure pump when it is higher. A few days later, the heap is covered with a new bed of herbaceous matter, which is then topped up with another bed of manure. Every day, all kitchen scraps are thrown onto the compost heap: vegetable peelings, eggshells, scraps, feathers and blood from poultry and game; soot from chimneys, house and yard sweepings, etc., etc.; ashes from the compost heap are also thrown on, (Wood ashes from fireplaces will be set aside and kept for pea and bean seedlings, etc.), etc.) Continue to build up the compost heap, alternating with a bed of herbaceous matter, manure and kitchen waste, until it reaches a height of two meters, taking care to water it every two days with the liquid fertilizer contained in the pit (C pl. 3, fig. 4). If this is done as I've indicated, fermentation starts immediately, and the plant matter rapidly decomposes. When the heap has reached a height of two meters, dismantle it by cutting it into vertical slices (D pl. 3, fig. 4), mix all the decomposed herbs with the manure, and form a heap next to the first (E pl. 3, fig. 3; water five or six more times with the liquid fertilizer, copiously enough for the liquid, filtering through, to animate all the herbaceous parts, then break up this compost heap a second time, mix all the parts well together again, and put it in reserve on the other side of the platform (F pl. 3, fig. 4). In this state, it's one of the best fertilizers, and perhaps one of the most powerful you can use. With a good supply of liquid fertilizer, it's easy to make masses of compost, and considerably increase the fertility of the vegetable garden, with no other expense than a little effort and activity to collect, gather and mix together all the fertilizing substances that are most often left to waste on the public highway, to the detriment of everyone's health. The manure factory should be set up in one of the reserve yards I mentioned when creating the kitchen garden, within easy reach of the gardener. The first platform is dedicated to manufacturing, the second to storage, i.e. to the manufactured fertilizers. So, as soon as the first compost heap is dismantled and rebuilt next to it, to receive a few more sprinklings of liquid fertilizer, a new one must be built in the place it occupied; as soon as the first has been reworked a second time and transported to the reserve on the other platform, the second, dismantled in its turn, takes its place, and a third is built. Pond silt, like aquatic grasses, is excellent in composts, but it is urgent to add a little quicklime before mixing it into composts. I insist on this lime mixture, because I've often seen these vases and herbs used fresh, and when they are, very poor results are obtained. Most often, they are left to air-macerate for a year, and are used with no other disadvantage than that of having piles of sludge spread out in a park, and this is one that can easily be avoided with the addition of quicklime, by leaving the vases to drain for only eight or fifteen days, and mixing them with the composts. When the manufactured composts remain in reserve for a long time, on the second platform, it is a good idea to water them, from time to time, with the liquid fertilizer from the pit, especially with the bottom, which is always thicker. I cannot overemphasize to owners and gardeners the importance of making composts; you need to have made fertilizers yourself, and had as many made as I did for others, to get a fair idea of the immense resources to be found in a host of objects that are allowed to go to waste, almost everywhere, to the detriment of cultivation. The manufacture and economy of fertilizers is an important question, not only from the point of view of the fraction of cultivation which is the subject of this book, but from the point of view of general production. Large-scale cultivation, which possesses a high level of capital and knows the value of fertilizers, not only lets nothing go to waste, but also buys artificial fertilizers for large sums, and makes considerable profits. Small-scale farmers, with little capital and no knowledge of the value of fertilizers, let their manure run through village streets, and sow their fields with weeds and crop failure, instead of increasing the mass of fertilizing substances. Small-scale farmers support themselves through hard work, and if I may put it this way, excessive hard work partly compensates for the lack of fertilizers. But if we could prove to him that, by wasting manure and a host of other fertilizing substances, he is wasting whole loaves of bread, we would quickly improve his position and significantly increase overall production. Many mayor-cultivators have issued by-laws to prevent the discharge of liquid manure onto the public highway; we congratulate them on this, as it is bread that they are giving to the peasant without him even suspecting it. If this measure, which is as wise as it is healthy, were taken everywhere, the mass of fertilizers would be significantly increased, and general production would rise, in proportion to the quantity of fertilizing materials introduced into the soil. The Emperor's powerful protection, and the very special care with which he surrounded cultivation, drove agricultural progress forward by leaps and bounds. Several prefects, faithful interpreters of the thought of the Head of State, have encouraged agricultural education; their efforts are tending to spread it in the countryside, and we are happy to note that primary school teachers, encouraged in this way, are competing with zeal to spread the first notions of agriculture and horticulture. I appeal here to those who have heard me so often; let them prove, in their lessons, to the children of the countryside, that it is as reprehensible to lose fertilizer as a piece of bread; they will have accomplished a great work, and contributed, more powerfully than they perhaps suppose, to the increase in public wealth. FOUR-YEAR CROP ROTATION. The most advantageous rotation for the owner's vegetable garden is the four-year rotation; but as it is urgent for the owner to be abundantly supplied with everything, it will be useful to reserve, outside the rotation, the space necessary to create an asparagus plan and an artichoke square. These two crops should each occupy a space equal to one of the four squares sown every four years. 0 Will that be a lot of asparagus and artichokes? It's true, but to have enough, you often have to grow too much, to prepare for bad years. VEGETABLE SALES. Asparagus and artichokes are vegetables par excellence, first for the home, then for sale; they can be transported, and always have a well-established value on all markets. If there are too many in good years, nothing is easier than to send them to market, and even to Paris, if the local market price is not sufficiently lucrative. In the past, it was difficult for the owner to sell excess produce, as commercial bad faith was an insurmountable obstacle. The owner was offered 25 centimes of what was worth three or four francs. In such cases, it was preferable to give the surplus to the animals after everyone had had their fill. Today, thanks to the fruit and vegetable factors set up by the City of Paris, the owner can still sell excess produce at the going market price, for a commission of 5 p. O/0, without having to deal with buyers. To sell whatever you want, all you have to do is ask M. Langlois, fruit and early produce letter carrier, rue des Halles-Centrales, Paris, for a few rubber-stamped addresses. You put your produce in a basket, stick the gummed address on it, put it on the railroad without franking, and the next day you receive your sales account and your money. Everything is sold by auction; and the bond paid by the factors, as much as strict inspections, vouch for their good management. The asparagus plant can last from 25 to 30 years, if well cared for. Artichoke patches, on the other hand, will only remain in place for four years to produce good products. Its size being equal to that of the crop rotation squares, and the artichokes having been fertilized four times in four years, we'll move them to crop rotation square no. 1, and place the crops intended for square no. 1 in the old artichoke square, without changing our crop rotation. Four years later, we'll put our artichokes back in the next square, and have them successively occupy all the squares in the rotation. In this way, the artichokes will only return to the same square every twenty years, so to speak, in new land for this crop, where they will always produce superb and abundant produce. When the asparagus is exhausted, we'll also have to create a new plan in another square, and subject the one that used to grow it to crop rotation. Crop rotation, I can't repeat enough, is the key to the richness of the vegetable garden, and it's only through it, with its manuring, its cendrages and its regular harvests, that we can be sure of producing abundantly, without more labor and without more fertilizers. But to achieve these results, crop rotation must be applied to the letter, not just about. Many gardeners, unaware of its importance, said to us when we introduced it into their gardens: "I do pretty much that, only I give less manure to square n° 1, and I give a little to all the others, that's better! They'll be able to cultivate in this way for many years, without having to bring their land to a state of cultivation. That is, to saturate it thoroughly and regularly enough with humus, to bring it to the highest degree of potency and fertility, in all parts of the garden. Almost everywhere, the same products, especially those that require a lot of fertilizer, come back every year on the same beds, especially in the flowerbeds, for which the gardener has a particular predilection, because he claims they are well greased. And every year he loads these beds with the same exhausting crops, and gives them half a manure, and since most of the time he doesn't make composts or layers, he misses the fertilizers and the rest of the garden goes without. The gardener then says: I've greased my beds well, I don't need manure to grow potatoes, beans, etc., etc., and he gets the results everyone knows about. We had great difficulty in introducing a regular crop rotation in certain vegetable gardens, where the owners had asked us to guide their gardeners. This regularity of cultivation offended received habits, disturbed the way things were done; changing the layers seemed monstrous. The owners wanted it, we persevered, and I'm happy to say today, after seven years of application, that the gardeners most opposed in principle to rotation, those on whom we imposed it, are now its strongest supporters. They say that no garden can compare with theirs, and that cultivation there takes care of itself. This is true up to a point; they complete the second rotation, their garden is in what we call a cultivated state in all its parts, and fertility, far from diminishing, can only increase with each rotation. If the owner's house is of a certain size, if he lives in the country in summer, in the city in winter, and if he wants to be supplied all year round by his vegetable garden, he should apply, to the letter, the four-year rotation as I have indicated, and establish six squares of equal size in the vegetable garden: four will be dedicated to the rotation, the other two: one to asparagus, the other to artichokes, and will return to the rotation when these two crops have been moved to occupy all the squares in succession. Artichokes will be moved every four years, asparagus every fifteen or twenty. As it takes three years before asparagus can be harvested, as soon as production begins to decline, half of an asparagus square should be created first, and half of the existing square destroyed; in other words, only what is needed to supply the house should be kept, and sales eliminated, so as not to hinder the other crops and give them the same amount of land. As soon as half the newly planted square is in a position to yield, the other half is planted, and the old square is completely destroyed and returned to the crop rotation. We create squares of asparagus that are a little too large to be able to replant half of them in another square, so as never to be deprived of this precious vegetable, to have enough with half a square, and to find a fairly high indemnity in the sale of the other half, when the whole square is in yield. By adopting this crop rotation to the letter, and with the quantity of layers that I have indicated, the landowner will always be abundantly supplied with all kinds of vegetables and early crops, throughout the year, and if the crop rotation is strictly applied, far from decreasing, the produce will only increase with each rotation, i.e. with each four-year period when the crop rotation starts again. Some landowners, out of a misguided sense of economy, and perhaps also under the influence of the prejudice that layers are ruinous, will want to apply crop rotation without layers. It will be a ruinous economy for them, and I'm going to prove it to them. Let's take a look at the expense of layers. If the park is a little large, and has a certain number of baskets, the flowers will first have to be cared for, and this is the job of the head gardener, who carries out this task in the same way as he does the kitchen garden; in addition, the paths have to be raked once a week, the borders mowed, and the heavy work in the kitchen garden carried out, which is the job of the gardener's assistant. A gardener's helper is indispensable for a property of any size, and costs an average of six hundred francs a year. If the owner wants to make a false economy, dispense with this gardener's helper and replace him with day laborers, on the pretext that he only lives in the country from May to November, this is what will happen. Day laborers are rare in the countryside, and during the summer they cost the farm 3 fr. a day; but as soon as they have to go and work for a rich bourgeois, they will demand 3 fr. 50 c. and even 4 fr. a day. A day laborer at 3.50 c. a day from May 1 to October 31 will cost the owner 649 fr. For this sum, he'll be giving his gardener a poor helper, incapable of doing any gardening work at all; he'll be more of a driving force than a helper, and even then, the gardener won't have the driving force he needs when he needs it most, because he's hired by the day. For example, during the scorching heat of July and August, when the vegetable garden needs to be watered twice a day, the gardener will be alone in the morning to do this work, which is materially impossible; he will have nothing to ask of his helper, who will show him the clock marking five o'clock. That's when the day begins, and the gardener has been watering for three hours. In the evening, the helper leaves at seven o'clock - that's the time, there's nothing to say about that - and the gardener waters until nine and sometimes until ten. This helper costs 49 fr. more than a boy who works hard, because he's learning a trade, and is always next to the gardener who gets him up at the same time as him, and only sends him to bed when he goes himself. The owner, who is the gardener's enemy, will say to me: "But in winter, what will I do with this boy whom I also pay? To which I reply: "Sir, your boy costs you 19 francs less than a day laborer who hasn't done half his work. I must admit that if you don't have diapers, frames and bells, it will happen quite often, when the winter planting and ploughing are finished, that your gardener's boy won't get much done, and your gardener will have to rest completely from his summer fatigue. You like activity, Monsieur, and you're right; give your two men frames and bells, let them do diapers, they'll send you to town, all winter long, salads, peas, green beans, melons, tomatoes, etc., etc., etc. You'll have the satisfaction of having early crops that cost you only the purchase of the glass, and their cultivation will keep your gardeners busy, who, while giving you great satisfaction, will also improve the manure destined for landfill by making the layers. It has been proven that manure used to make layers, and which has produced a harvest of early crops free of charge for labor and purchase, is preferable for landfill. So far, we've made a profit of 8. Let's see what glass costs. One-meter iron frames cost 8 francs. Single glass is 3.50 c. a meter, and double glass 7 francs. You won't take single glass, because you'd break windows all the time; double glass is as solid as stone, so you'll choose that one, it's the cheapest. Give your gardener 25 iron frame panels, glazed in double glass, at 15 fr. 375 " 150 button bells at 1 fr. . . . . 150 " Total... ... . 525 "This equipment is sufficient to obtain all the early fruit and melons you want, and will last for twenty years; so you have to spend 26 fr. 25 c. each year on your melons and early fruit. Sell fifteen melons for 2 francs, they're available everywhere at that price, and you'll have melons and early fruit for nothing, and what's more, you'll have improved your manure at a profit. To finish convincing the enemies of layers, should I tell them the account I give daily to the owners who come to visit my garden, and say to me on seeing my melons and vegetables: what a production! it's superb! but how expensive it must be! to which I reply: every year, in January, I buy two cars of manure to build up a warm layer on which I sow my melons and some 3 spring vegetables. In February, I buy about three cars of inn manure, the best of all, with which I build up a second warm layer, which I use to transplant my melons in the nursery, and in April, I buy another three cars of the same manure, which, mixed with leaves or moss, is used to make warm layers, on which I place, under cloche, the melons of the last season. Total, eight cars of good inn manure at 5 fr. .. .. . A0 fr. Plus, about three meters of manure, picked up by old men and children, at 5 fr. . . . . . . . . . .. . . . . .  55fr. With the three meters of horse manure, and the household water, I make about ten cars of excellent compost, mixing it with the detritus from the vegetable garden, the fruit garden and the kitchen. My composts, together with the manure from the demolition of the diapers, are more than enough to smoke my vegetable garden to the max. Every year, I mulch the fruit garden with my excess manure; in addition, the layers give me considerable quantities of potting soil. My total expenditure on fertilizer is 55 francs a year. I have about twelve ares of vegetable garden, the land is in perfect condition for cultivation, and these twelve ares, cultivated when we have the time, produce the following annually: 1° Two hundred first-quality melons; 19 2° All the vegetables I could possibly want for my household, consisting of three masters and two servants; 3° The complete supply for my gardener and his family, consisting of six people; 4° The surplus of certain harvests, so abundant that it is impossible to eat them, such as peas, artichokes, beans, etc., these are sold for around 80 francs a year. I must say, as a tribute to the truth, that my vegetable garden is cultivated in the worst possible conditions. During the winter, I use it to temporarily bury trees destined for expeditions and the creation of fruit gardens. I often have to keep a supply of trees until March, and even April. It's only towards the end of this month, and when my pupils are not too busy with work outside, that I put my vegetable garden fully into cultivation; and in these deplorable conditions, twelve ares that I fertilize with an expense of 55 francs produce for me annually: 1° 200 melons; 2° Vegetables for eleven people; 3° A silver product of 80 fr. I haven't taken labor into account. Let's assume that my garden spends one man's day a week, it doesn't, but let's count 52 days at 4 francs to account for all costs to the maximum, i.e. 208 fr. 8 Now let's draw up a strict account: Rent for 12 ares of land at 10 francs. 120 fr.
