Id. in June. 4 fr. 80 fr. 100 " 80 " Id. in July, 2 fr.50c. 50 " Id. in August.. 2 fr. 80 " Total... ... . 390 "Operating like this, my supply of vegetables for eleven people, including one hundred melons, would cost me 3 francs. I've drawn up this account here, which is shown to my many visitors perhaps a hundred times a year, because the produce of my garden, like my expenses, is a matter of public record. If this same garden were not overcrowded with trees for half the year, and my students were able to carry out all the work on time, it would produce half as much. If, in spite of everything I've just said, some owners persisted in considering layers as an object of great expense, and wanted to try to assolate their vegetable garden without layers, they would have to adapt the three-year assolement. This would be more costly and less productive, but admittedly far preferable to the cultivation practised there. The three-year crop rotation would still bring them significant savings on what they do, a marked increase in produce, and a great improvement in the soil. This rotation will serve as a transition between the old and the new crops, and will be a stepping stone to the four-year rotation with layers, the only one possible, to give a large production at the best market. In this case, the vegetable garden would have to be divided into three or six equal parts; all manures would have to be mixed with composts, three-quarters of which would be buried on the first sole, forming one-third of the vegetable garden, and one-quarter of the most consumed fertilizers would have to be reserved for topdressing on the second sole; the third would only receive energetic ashing. The rotation is established as follows: maximum fertilization on square or squares n° 1, if the garden is divided into three or six parts; topdressing on square or squares n° 2, and ashing on square or squares n° 3. The following year, the part ashed the previous year is smoked to the maximum; the part smoked the previous year is terreauteed, and the part terreauteed the previous year is ashed, and so on, without interruption, from year to year. But we mustn't forget that without layers, we're deprived of potting soil, the first element of success in vegetable growing, and that handling all manures to make them suitable for burial is as labor-intensive as making and cultivating layers. The same expense will be incurred, and the harvest of early vegetables and the essential potting soil in the vegetable garden will be lost. Everything we've said so far about crop rotation applies to crop rotation gardens. Let's now look at the most economical ways of introducing three- and four-year rotations, in new gardens as well as in old ones, which you may wish to subject to them. The first step in subjecting a new vegetable garden to a four-year rotation is to organize the manure and compost service a year in advance, to set up a liquid fertilizer pit, and to stockpile the necessary ashes. 1° All horse manure will be stockpiled for bedding. It's best to pile it up in a conical wheel, and only put it in the wheel when it's dry. 2° A reserve of leaves, equal in volume to that of the horse manure. These leaves should be collected and piled up very dry, to avoid fermentation; they will be used partly to make warm layers, mixed with horse manure, and partly to make soft layers mixed with cow manure. 3° At the same time, build the liquid fertilizer pit, and immediately fill it with liquid fertilizer, made as I indicated above for making compost. If liquid manure and faeces are lacking, the pit can be filled with water, and horse dung, collected from the streets and roads, and poudrette can be added. 4° Make as much compost as possible, and store it on the second platform as soon as it is made. 5° Store all ashes from fireplaces, and all ashes available from factories, lime kilns and even railway stations. If the soil is clayey, it's a matter of urgency to mix a quantity of lime or marl into the composts, to a greater or lesser extent, depending on its tenacity. The best practice is to first melt the lime with five or six times its volume of soil, amalgamate it well and then mix it with the composts. However, if the composts contain tough plants that are difficult to disintegrate, it would be advantageous to throw quicklime on top, and then mix the whole thing very carefully, dismantling the pile. Marl can be mixed as is with composts. I forgot to mention rapeseed straw when making composts. It's the richest in nitrogen, containing more than all the others, but it has the disadvantage of being difficult to break down. Rape straw is a precious substance, and has the added advantage of being very inexpensive. If you plan ahead at harvest time, you can get large quantities for very little money. It should be cut on a log, with a billhook, to a length of about 30 centimetres, mixed with herbs, and wetted a little; throw a few quicklime stones on top and cover the whole with manure. Once we've assembled all the fertilizing materials I've just listed, we'll still need potting soil to cover our layers. Potting soil is indispensable for seedlings, and especially for covering the beds, and it's the rarest thing in countries where horticulture is not advanced. There are even regions where we don't know what it is. Let's say we're completely lacking in it to start our new garden, we'll have to replace it with a mixture for the first year, because in subsequent years, the layers will provide us with it at our discretion. First of all, let's explain what potting soil is, for the benefit of those in distant lands. Quite simply, it's manure that has been used to make layers, and which has been left to decompose until it's powdered like snuff. It's pure humus, and anything is possible with potting soil. We mix it with half good soil to cover layers, and use it pure to cover seedlings. To replace the potting soil, we'll collect a certain quantity of horse dung from the streets and roads, which we'll leave to ferment in a heap. Next, we'll choose the best soil in the garden, put it through a sieve to extract all the stones, and mix it in halves with the horse dung; but it's urgent that the dung be ground to powder before mixing. Break it up well with the rake, and if necessary, crush it with your hands if it's too hard. This mixture is not as good as potting soil, nothing can replace it, but in its place, it can usefully cover the layers. Now that we're armed with all we need in the way of fertilizers, let's proceed, in the spring, to the cultivation of our new garden, newly broken in and devoid of fertilizers. First, we'll choose the oldest, most decomposed composts, to be buried in plot no. 2, destined for roots, lettuces, strawberries and so on. Depending on the richness of the plant, we spread from four to eight centimetres of compost on the soil, and bury it, taking care to reserve the most decomposed part to cover the seedlings. Two treatments a fortnight apart, and three or four successive waterings with liquid fertilizer will bring this result. We then build our warm layers on square n° A, with some of the horse manure in reserve; a few days later, the warm layers with the rest of the horse manure mixed with leaves, and later still, the deaf layers, with cow manure and the rest of our leaves. All these layers will be covered in the first year with the mixture I indicated earlier. (I explain how to establish these various layers in the chapter specially devoted to them). The layers constructed, and square no. 2 ter~M F=R: ----= i.:i ....... H54A LESt. 6́ reauté, we'll take all we have of manufactured composts, and spread it on square no. 1; we'll also bring in all we have left of available manure; we'll also spread it on top of the composts, so as to cover the soil with a thickness of fertilizer of about twenty centimeters, and we'll bury everything in the same square no. 1, destined for cabbages, artichokes, etc., in the ground. Plot n° 3, for peas, beans, etc., will not be fertilized, but will receive a good ashing. In addition, some of the best ashes from wood-burning fireplaces should be set aside to cover certain seedlings, as described below. As soon as the manure has been buried, it's time to make new manure, both for the season's needs, and for next year's reserve. All fertilizing substances must be constantly and relentlessly collected, and immediately put into decomposition. The fertilizer factory must always be in operation; when the service is well established, it's easy to leave nothing to waste, and to produce large quantities of excellent manure without too much work, and almost without expense. The organization of the first year is more difficult than that of the following years, and even if you put in more effort, you won't get as good results. The land is new, not yet ready for cultivation, and will not be until the fifth year, when the rotation will have been completed, and each of the squares will have received in turn maximum manuring, good topdressing or mulches, ashing and layering. It's hardly until the second rotation, when the maximum fertilization is applied for the second time, that the vegetable garden has acquired its full fertility, to be further enhanced in subsequent years. This is when gardeners who were unaware of the advantages of regular rotation, and who have tried it, somewhat in spite of themselves - which they agree with the best faith in the world - say that: the crop grows by itself, so abundant and fast does it grow. The harvest in the first year of the crop rotation, while lower than in subsequent years, is far superior to previous ones. It should also be said that the gardener was learning the ropes that first year, and that with the best will in the world, his services were not organized in the same way as in subsequent years. By the second year, when two squares have been smoked to the maximum, when the layers have yielded a certain quantity of compost, and also when the gardener is more up to speed, the appearance of the crops changes so much, and he achieves such rapid results that he redoubles his zeal, and by the third year achieves very fine production. When the beds are demolished at the end of the season, the old topsoil that covered them is first removed and set aside; it will be used to top-dress square no. 2 the following year. We then sort out the manure that has been used the least, and mix it with the compost, either by building piles on the reserve platform, or on plot no. 1, at the time of burial. Then, the most consumed parts are set aside in piles in the shade, to be converted into new potting soil, intended to cover the layers the following year, and to be applied to crops that require a slightly energetic fertilizer. You may be a little short of potting soil the first time you demolish the layers, but in subsequent years you'll have larger quantities, once the services are well organized. Once the first year's work is done, all that's left to do is to follow the steps laid out. When the vegetable garden has already been created, and you want to subject it to crop rotation, nothing is easier. Allow me to remind the reader of two of the most important recommendations: the first is to clear the garden of the sad fruit trees that line the squares, invading them with their shade and roots; the second is to break up and level the squares if they have not already been done. These two operations are indispensable, and are the first keys to success. If the landowner is too attached to his old trees, he should shave and level just one square, which he will then divide into four, so that he can practice the four-year crop rotation I have just described. It's a comparative trial that will be more profitable than he thinks, and it's my principle to provoke comparative trials, well convinced that in the third year, if not the second, the whole cluttered garden will be destroyed to make way for a modern vegetable and fruit garden. In the event that the square intended for the comparative trial has been smoked the previous year, it will be necessary to smoke square no. 1, top-dress square no. 2, ash square no. 3, and build up the layers on square no. 4. If the owner wants to cut down everything, and subject his entire vegetable garden to a four-year rotation, and finds it impossible to clear everything at once, he can carry out this operation in four years. Here's how to proceed: 1° Choose the leanest square No. 1 in the crop rotation, the one that will have remained the longest without manure, and have it completely ploughed over, smoking it no more than one month after ploughing. This square will be terraced in the second year, ashed in the third, and will receive the layers in the fourth to become square n° 1 again in the fifth year, with maximum manuring. 2° Choose the best-smoked plot from the previous year for plot no. 2, plow it very deeply, and add topdressing and mulch. This square will be ashed the following year, will receive the layers the third year, and will be broken up as soon as the crops have been removed. 3° Take the best-smoked square after No. 2 as No. 3, and ash it abundantly. This square will receive the layers the following year, and will be broken up as soon as it is cleared, to become square n° 1 in turn, with maximum manuring. 4° Establish the beds on one of the least 48 well-smoked squares. This square should be cleared as soon as it is free, to become square n° 1 the following year, with maximum manuring. In this way, the clearing can be done in four years, and the expense, as minimal as it is, divided over four. What's more, you have the resource to use up all your fertilizers. For all crop rotations without exception, you should always start by organizing the fertilizer factory, if not one year, then at least a few months in advance. If the owner has no fertilizers at his disposal, he can buy some, but it is urgent to handle them two or three times, and to water them with liquid fertilizer, to decompose them, before burying them; and in this case again, to immediately set up the liquid fertilizer pit, and the platforms to make the composts. If the garden to be planted under the four-year rotation had an asparagus bed and an artichoke patch in good condition, but insufficient for the house's consumption, the garden would have to be divided into six equal parts, and these two crops enlarged. If they were in poor condition, we'd have to keep them temporarily; plant two new divisions: one of asparagus and the other of artichokes, and destroy the old crops to bring them back into the rotation as soon as the new ones produced. The three-year rotation without layers will be introduced by the same procedures as the four-year rotation, but with the difference that the garden will be divided into three, if there is no reserve of asparagus and artichokes, and into four if there is. One quarter will be devoted to these two crops. VARIETIES OF VEGETABLES TO GROW. Now that the fertilizer factory is well organized, our beds built, and our land smoked according to the requirements of the crop rotation, let's get busy looking for the varieties of vegetables to introduce into the vegetable garden. I said earlier that most of those grown in regions where vegetable cultivation had been entirely localized were pitiful. I humbly apologize to gardeners who have not left their homeland, but this is all too true. Sometimes, landowners introduced a few good varieties and shared the seeds with their friends. Each gardener constantly harvested seeds from his garden, only to sow them the following year, and often the lack of suitable cultivation, combined with this abuse of the soil, led to the complete degeneration of the varieties. Then, the owner, more enlightened than the gardener, says: the new varieties are better, but they have no fixity, and the gardener repeats his eternal refrain: dam, sir, the land is worthless! If the owner had taken a little care of his garden, and not abandoned it entirely to the carelessness of his gardener, he would have preserved his pure varieties for an indefinite time, by first giving them the right kind and quantity of fertilizer, and then exchanging his seeds with one of his friends, in order to avoid the degeneration that infallibly occurs when seeds that have been harvested there are sown in the same soil for three consecutive years. A sure-fire way of preserving pure varieties when harvesting seeds is to share the first seeds with someone who has a garden whose soil is not quite the same as ours, and to exchange seeds every year. The varieties will remain pure as soon as the seeds are no longer sown in the soil that produced them. Let's also say that the choice of seeds is of great importance, and that the best varieties produce impossible seeds in the hands of gardeners who, most of the time, know neither how to grow them nor how to harvest them; let's also say that it's difficult to buy good seeds, and to always be sure of getting the variety you asked for from the merchant; this is usually due to commercial rapacity, sometimes to the disorder that exists among certain merchants, and to their lack of knowledge of cultivation. You should never buy seeds from a house that does not have a well-established reputation for honesty and order, or from houses that sell all kinds of things, and seeds over the counter. Seeds should only be bought from a seed merchant, not from wheat, oat, bran, etc. merchants, who sell vegetable and flower seeds as an accessory, but from a true seed merchant, selling nothing but the seeds needed for all branches of horticulture. I beg the reader's pardon for these details, which may seem superfluous, but I have bought, and sent to buy, so many seeds in my life...., that I want to avoid schools for those who will do me the honor of following my advice. So, send a small village gardener or a laborer to fetch seeds from a good house, if you don't demand a bill, he'll go to a one-eyed grocer who also sells seeds, and he'll go because the grocer is good-natured. He has excellent procedures, indeed, he's full of good intentions, offering your man a glass of beet brandy, but most of the time, his seeds, squeezed into the drawer intended for the serins' millet or the parrots' chenevis, are mixed up, and sometimes too old to germinate. The owner must always buy his seeds himself, or have them brought in directly. There are several firms in Paris that offer every guarantee. The first of these is MM. Vilmorin Andrieux et Cie, 30, quai de la Mégisserie. I mention this one because I've been in contact with it for over twenty-five years, and the many people I send there have never had anything but praise for their dealings with this company. Maison Vilmorin is unique; it has no depot in the provinces, and is only responsible for what leaves its premises. People who rarely go to Paris can write and have their seeds sent by post. Having said that, let's get back to the varieties to be grown in the owner's garden. What does the homeowner need? Good, beautiful vegetables, especially good ones, and he needs them in every season! So, as far as possible, we'll combine beauty with quality in the homeowner's garden, but we'll always sacrifice volume for flavour. I assume that the owner is a disciple, more or less ardent, of Brillat-Savarin; I believe I'm right, and act accordingly. If a few, by exception, profess the cult of bulk, and despise taste, let them take the trouble to choose their varieties in the farm's vegetable garden, they'll find some very large ones, making a lot of profit! In principle, we should stick to varieties that vegetate quickly, the early varieties, which are preferable for all crops. They occupy the soil for only a short time, make it possible to multiply harvests, and are generally of better quality. Each square of the vegetable garden should produce an average of three harvests a year. Later on, I'll show you how to obtain them easily, with the help of square organization, counter-planting and crop succession. I've only indicated the varieties to be introduced into the homeowner's garden, and I've listed them in alphabetical order to make it easier for the reader to find them. 18 VEGETABLE VARIETIES TO GROW IN THE HOMEOWNER'S GARDEN. ROSE GARLIC. Earlier and slightly weaker than the common variety. ARROCHE BLONDE. The most suitable variety for softening the acidity of sorrel. ARTICHAUT GROS VERT DE LAON. The best of all, the largest and most tender of all known varieties, as well as the most fertile, and almost as early as the VIOlET, good only for peppers. ULM ASPERGE. One of the most beautiful varieties of purple asparagus, very colorful and with the merit of being the earliest. Ulm asparagus should be grown in the homeowner's garden, to the exclusion of all other varieties; its earliness, combined with its beauty and quality, give it top ranking. When the harvest is too abundant for home consumption, Ulm asparagus will be the first to appear on the market, where it will fetch a higher price than other varieties. LONG PURPLE EGGPLANT. The best. BEET PETITE ROUGE DE CASTELNAUDARY. Remarkable for its quality, this is the only variety that can be introduced into the vegetable garden. NASTURTIUM PETITE. Gives enough flowers and seeds, and is preferable to the large variety that harms neighboring crops. CARDOON. Despite its thorns, this is still the best variety, and should be preferred to the others. 48 CARROT. There are several varieties, of which only two will be allowed in the homeowner's garden: The early red Dutch carrot, the best of all, and with the immense advantage of being able to be sown successfully all year round. With a little care, the short red Dutch carrot produces new carrots all year round, and I can't say it often enough: it's superior in quality to all the others. It is preferable for the first sowings in the ground, and successive sowings are perfectly successful right through to September. Here's what I usually do with this variety, which I grow alone and to the exclusion of all others: I sow two beds only at the end of February or first days of March. These two beds provide me with excellent carrots, from the end of April to the end of May; we only thin until then. By mid-June, the crop is ready for removal, so we clear the soil of it and store it in the vegetable greenhouse. I successively sow one bed at a time, in May, June, July and even into September, to supply the needs of the house; and the last seedling, that of September, is pulled up before the first frosts, and kept for winter provision. This way, you'll never have any hard, limp old carrots to send to the kitchen. BELL CARROT. A sub-variety of the previous one, but much shorter; this variety is never grown in the ground, and is used exclusively for early harvests, under chassis. CELERI. There is only one variety to be grown as a salad, the full white celery; it is a little less early than the short hative celery, but preferable in that the latter is not always full. The next most valuable variety is : ERFURT CELERIAC. The best and earliest of all celeriac, this excellent vegetable is not widely grown in France. FRESH CHERRY, for supplies. It has the same fragrance as common chervil, is just as easy to grow and has the added advantage of being very pretty. In my opinion, it's a sad vegetable; many horticulturists have become passionate about this plant; the majority of gardeners who have experimented with it claim that it's a bad vegetable; I share their opinion, until further experimentation. MUSHROOMS. In the interests of public health, we only allow cultivated mushrooms. I'll come back to this in the special crops section, which forms the third part of this book. CHICORICE. Let's start with wild chicory, which is excellent for hens and rabbits, and can be used to make a winter salad, commonly known as capuchin beard. The only variety to grow is the improved variegated wild chicory, an excellent variety with a tendency to peel, producing almost as many leaves for poultry as wild chicory, and having the advantage of giving, when grown in the cellar, a very pretty red variegated salad. For cooking, and for use in salads, we grow only two varieties: FINE ITALIAN CHICORICE, excellent and very fine, with almost no ribs, for all seasonal seedlings, and: The CHICORÉE DE MEAUX, for the last sowings only, those starting in June; and finally, as a number, the escarole, of which we'll grow a single variety in the owner's kitchen garden. The SCAROLE FRISÉE, not much better than the other varieties, but prettier. CHOU. The landowner who has neither harvesters nor carters to feed, must be very sparing with cabbages, and scrupulously choose those that are suitable for his table; above all, he must see to it that his kitchen garden is not invaded by those immense cabbages, with their gigantic apples, broad, tough ribs, and pungent, musky taste, which are the honors of the gardener's pot in general, and his happiness in particular. The only cabbages that the homeowner should admit to his kitchen garden, as worthy of a place on his table, are: The YORCK KALE, with its small, elongated head, very early and of excellent quality. This variety will supply first and second saigon cabbages. ULM KALE, belonging to the curly savoy variety, the smallest but the best; it is the cabbage par excellence for the proprietor, and is also the earliest. The Ulm cabbage is especially valuable for partridges with cabbage; it has no ribs, its head is hardly larger than that of a lettuce, but it is extremely delicate. It can be grown from early spring until the frosts, and harvested from June to January, stored in the vegetable greenhouse. It is, I can't say enough, the best of all cabbages, almost the only one that should be grown for gourmets. However, as a mansion is made up of a certain number of staff, we shall introduce into the owner's vegetable garden: MILAN, SHORT OR Dwarf, stocky, very dark green, quick to pick, and of remarkable quality; it keeps longer than Ulm cabbage, is worthy by its quality of appearing on the masters' table, and can be used profitably for that of the servants. BRUSSELS KALE, excellent and very valuable, providing excellent produce throughout the winter. Brussels sprouts are very hardy; they even thrive in snow. RED cabbage, used as a medicine, in salads, candied or stuffed; the best variety is the blackd̂tre D'UTRECK cabbage. Caou vear, is highly esteemed in western France for making soups. Green chom soup is very refreshing and even purgative. For anyone not born in the western departments, kale is purely an object of fantasy. The preferred variety is Poitou branchu cabbage. CHOU RAVE BLANC HATIF; the best variety of kohlrabi. CAULIFLOWER. We'll be adopting just two varieties for the homeowner's garden: ERFURT HATIF CHOU-FLEUR, the earliest of all, and the most suitable for early cultivation under frame and cloche, and : LE CHOU-FLEUR DEMI-DUR DE PARIS, a variety of the Lenormand cauliflower that has caused such a stir in recent years. The Lenormand cauliflower, which owed its fame to advertising, is soon as completely forgotten as the almanacs and small newspapers that praised it so highly. What remains from all this fla fla, and countless scissor blows, is the semi-hard Paris cauliflower, an excellent variety for all outdoor crops. I won't mention Broccoli, Pe-tsai (Chinese cabbage) and crambe maritime, whose reputation is declining as a result of the publicity they receive. We'll wait for further experimentation before talking about them. CIBOULE. The preferred variety is the perennial ciboule, also known as ciboule de Saint-Jacques. CIVETTE-APPÉTIT, very useful for sauces. CUCUMBER. If you want to harvest a lot of cucumbers, you need to grow three varieties: BLANC HATIF, for early harvest under a frame; 8 GROS BLANC DE BONNEUIL, for cultivation on a deaf bed and under a cloche, and : Le PETIT VERT, used exclusively to supply gherkins. CRESSON DE FONTAINE (see special crops). CRESS ALÉNOIS. Excellent supply for salads: sow curly watercress. ECHALOTTE (see special crops). BLOND SPINACH WITH SORREL LEAVES. This is the variety that takes the longest to bolt, which is the main advantage of spinach. We prefer this variety. ESTRAGON (see special crops). MARSH BEANS. It's useful to grow two varieties: RED BEAN, under chassis, and : FÈVE A LONGUES COSSES, very early and very fertile for the open ground. STRAWBERRIES. If you want to harvest excellent strawberries without interruption, from the end of May until the frosts, you need to grow the GAILLON STRAWBERRY, an excellent variety with remarkable fertility and the advantage of not producing runners. This precious variety could, in a pinch, replace all the others; it must form the base of strawberry plantations, and moreover it gives without interruption, throughout the winter, when you want to give it the honors of the frame or greenhouse. There are a great many varieties of large strawberries, some of which are highly recommended. I'll mention the following varieties, mainly for the sake of force: KEEN' S SEEDLING, an excellent variety, very beautiful and very productive. PRINCESSE ROYALE, very beautiful and very fertile. DUC DE MALAKOFF, large and excellent fruit. DU CHILI, the largest of all known varieties. Fruits reach the volume of a small hen's egg, and require a lot of chastening. It should only be grown in the open ground at noon. I quote a few varieties of large strawberries as being the most recommendable. There are countless other varieties that I will not mention here, as my aim in this book is not to collect varieties, but to provide assured harvests. I urge lovers of new varieties to ask for MM. Vilmorin and Andrieux's catalog, or to consult Le Bon Jardinier, where they can choose those that seem most deserving. GIRAUMON. The best variety is Girgumén Turban (see Special crops). HARICOTS. Several varieties need to be grown for dry and green beans, and especially for green beans, for most of the year. The best-known bean for eating as dry beans is the Soissons; it is of excellent quality, but does not give satisfactory results everywhere. There is a much preferable variety whose products are always of superior quality: HARICOT SABRE A RAME. This is the best of all beans for eating as dry beans; it has almost no skin, and always cooks well; its only disadvantage is that it rises very high and requires large oars. The oared saber is also very fertile, and its long pods contain a large quantity of beans. When it comes to eating green beans, there is no variety that can replace the HARIcOT FLAGEOLET, which is all the more valuable for its remarkable quality, high fertility and success at all times of the year. But you need to get hold of real flageolets, and be careful not to sow the ignoble beans to which many countries give this name. For red bean lovers: RED FLAGEOLET is the best and perhaps the most productive variety. For green beans, the following varieties are the most recommended: NAIN DE HOLLANDE, the earliest of all, and the best for growing under chassis, and even in the open, as an early green bean, in a warm, sheltered spot. HARICOT GRIS DE BAGNOLET is very popular in the Paris area for green beans. They remain firm and retain their regular8 shape after cooking; they are more sought-after for this reason than for their quality. HARICOT FLAGEOLET is the best of all and the most tender for making green beans. The pod is wide, curved and rather poorly shaped, a disadvantage that is compounded by the fact that they fall apart when overcooked, but these disadvantages are redeemed by the possibility of making excellent green beans in any season. Flageolet can be sown as late as September. This concludes my list of bean varieties: there are many others that are highly recommendable, but these are enough to obtain, without a doubt, an abundant production of green beans and beans all year round, and in the modern vegetable garden, as in fruit growing, I am sober with varieties. Experience has shown me that collectors often lack the necessities with the most extensive crops, and the shortage invariably comes from the fact that such and such varieties, on which one was counting, were lacking. I might even add that the richest in collections are the poorest in produce. True to my program, I confine myself to varieties that have been tried and tested, and that give certain results. Enthusiasts are free to try whatever they like. MILK. This is the salad par excellence: you need them in all seasons. To achieve this, we'll classify them into three categories: spring, summer and winter lettuces. SPRING LETTUCES. GOTTE LETTUCE. Quite small, sprouts quickly and rises steadily. Excellent for cultivation under frames and cloches, and for the first outdoor harvest. DAUPHINE LETTUCE. Beautiful, good lettuce, green, blond with a hint of red, but for spring only. SUMMER LETTUCE. VERSAILLES LETTUCE. Excellent variety, green, blond and hard to climb. BATAVIA LETTUCE. The largest of all, very good, golden green, a little red on top. PALATINE LETTUCE. Excellent, very rustic, green, almost red on top; best for late sowing. BLACK-SEEDED BLOOD LETTUCE. Charming and delicious lettuce, very strongly variegated with red on the inside. The heart is yellow and pink, the top dark brown. One of the best and prettiest lettuces, it can't be grown enough in the owner's garden. WINTER LETTUCES. PETITE CRÊPE OR PETITE NOIRE. Small and low yielding, but very valuable under frame and cloche. This is the only variety that can complete its vegetation without needing to be given air. It is the winter lettuce par excellence, LAITUE DE LA PASSION. Small and low yielding, but very versatile; its main merit is its earliness. MORINE LETTUCE. Slightly better than the previous variety, and better apple-holding. ROMAINES. Romaine is not as good as lettuce. We'll be content to grow varieties that cap naturally, i.e. that pump very well without being tied. This saves time, and in fact, these varieties are more than worth the others: MARAÎMER GREEN ROSEMARKS, suitable for early cultivation, very large and very good. MARAÎCHER ROMAINES BLONDES, also very pretty and very good. GREY MARAICHER ROMAINES, larger than the above. We make an exception for Romaines who don't wear their hair up, and want to be tied up, in favor of: RED WINTER ROMAIN. A very rustic variety, which tolerates frost fairly well. A precious resource in spring. LENTILLE, the blond, is the preferable variety (see Special Crops). MACHE. The Italian variety has broader, blonder leaves. It's the only variety to grow in the home garden. MELONS. Nothing is better and more desirable than a good melon, especially in the country, when you're far from Paris, which provides excellent ones at a moment's notice. Yet nothing is more difficult to obtain as soon as you move away from the capital. Most of the time, melons lack the necessary care; but often, too, the owner has a lot to do with the gardener's lack of success, by making him grow so many different varieties of melon. When he hears of a variety that is said to be good, he immediately starts growing it among the others. All these varieties fertilize each other, producing impossible mullets, melons that are shapeless, tasteless and inedible. This is sometimes blamed on the soil; I've heard owners tell me in all seriousness: "My gardener doesn't make melons any more, the land isn't worth anything to them. Melons are never grown in the ground; they are grown in artificial soil, so they can be grown with equal success in a paved courtyard, or even on a rock. For my own part, I've been growing only one variety of melon for over fifteen years, and I've never harvested a mediocre melon. Owners who grow twenty varieties at once will think themselves ruined if I advise them to grow only one, and yet this is the only sure way of harvesting good melons. I'll allow three varieties of melon in the owner's garden, three varieties for three different seasons, but with the express condition of growing them in such a way that they never flower together, to keep them pure. Not only melons, but all gourds fertilize each other, even at great distances. It is therefore essential that all the species grown in the garden: melons, cucumbers, pumpkins, giraumons, etc., never flower at the same time, if you don't want to risk losing everything. We exclude from the owner's garden all embroidered melons for the benefit of cantaloupes, and even watermelons, which only do well in the olive climate, and even then are far from being worth a very ordinary cantaloupe; we grow them all over France: The PETIT-PRESCOT, for early melon, and under chassis, as one of the earliest, among the best early varieties. The PRESCOT A FOND VERT, for the whole season, as being in my opinion the best of all melons. The ribs are very scabby, the flesh red; these melons are always very full and very smoky. They are also remarkably fertile and very rustic. This is the only variety I grow, to the exclusion of all others. I may have improved it through cultivation, but I never harvest a second-rate melon. I know a lot of people are going to object, and say that white bottoms are better! I don't think so, although the white bottom 16 cantaloupe is grown exclusively in many parts of the world. It's bigger than the fond vert, but it's almost never full; its pale, almost white flesh lacks flavor; I've rarely eaten a good fond blanc melon, and I've tasted hundreds of them, precisely because I was told they were the best. I even went so far as to grow them for three consecutive years, with all possible care, and the result of this trial was to make me abandon this variety altogether as being initially not very fertile, more delicate than the fond vert, indeed giving very-large melons, but always hollow, bland and far from being worth the fonds verts. Cantaloupe fond blanc is the only variety grown in a large part of the Loiret region. The result is that most owners harvest melons one year out of three, and their melons often have more in common with pumpkins than with melons. This doesn't stop owners and gardeners alike repeating in chorus that the white bottom is the best of all melons. I've persuaded a few gardeners to sow the seeds I gave them; they've obtained melons every year, and good ones at that; this lasted as long as they were willing to grow a single variety, but from the day they mixed white bottoms with them, scarcity and poor quality returned. The MELON D'ARKHANGEL is an excellent variety, very rustic, precious for the last season. The fruit is average, but excellent, and the variety is so hardy that in a pinch it could be grown outdoors. Owners who want to stick to these three varieties, and even to the last two, or to one of them, if they don't grow first-season melons, will always be assured of excellent melons every year, but on the express condition that they ruthlessly uproot the marvellous trial varieties; even one plant is enough to lose everything else. Green-fleshed melons can only be grown in olive climates. The Cavaillon variety is the best. NAVET DE FRENEUSE, excellent quality, remaining whole after cooking. It is one of the most prized for stews. NAVET DES VERTUS, very early white, precious for the first season, and excellent quality, but a tender turnip. NAVET JAUNE D'ECOSSE, semi-tender, fairly good quality, very rustic and frost-resistant. FINNISH YELLOW TURNIP, semi-tender, of remarkable quality, with a very long shelf-life. I highly recommend growing this precious turnip in the owner's garden. WHITE ONION HATIF. An excellent variety, and very valuable for providing the first onions, when sown in August and transplanted to a warm, sheltered spot. The white onion, sown very early in the spring, provides the best onions for preserving; it is milder than the others, and much preferable for this use. 9 OIGNON BLOND DES VERTUS. The best of all varieties for spring sowing. FLAT YELLOW ONION, also very good for the same use. PALE RED ONION, to be sown in small quantities for spring transplanting. BELLEVILLE sorrel, broad-leafed, less acidic than common sorrel. BLISTER-LEAF SORREL. A very fine variety, multiplying by bursts of plants, with very large leaves, very fine, and especially very early in spring. PANAIS ROND. This is the best variety to grow in the homeowner's garden. VIOLET POTATO. The most sought-after and best variety. VERY CURLY DWARF PARSLEY. A variety to be cultivated in preference to any other; remarkable for the beauty of its leaves and its slowness to bolt. SPANISH SWEET PEPPER; the best to grow. PIMPRENELLE, to be grown in borders, rather for animals than for cooking purposes. POITOU YELLOW PEAR, very large and excellent. VERY CLEAN PEA WITH FRAME. Very early and very small variety; valuable for cultivation under chassis only. MICHAUX PEAS FROM HOLLAND. Such an early variety that, sown in spring, it yields at the same time as peas sown at St Catherine's Day and sometimes earlier. Dutch Michaux peas are remarkably fertile and of excellent quality. I can't recommend it highly enough for cultivation in a homeowner's 18 vegetable garden; it combines all the qualities of earliness, goodness and fertility. In recent years, the Prince Albert pea has been highly praised, especially for its earliness. This pea is much less fertile than Michaux de Hollande; its quality leaves much to be desired, and in three comparative trials, I found it no earlier than Michaux de Hollande. I disagree with several people whose names have great authority in vegetable growing, I know, but I back up my opinion with three comparative trials, in which I could not find a day of earliness in favor of Prince Albert. Moreover, there is such a difference in quality and product that I would still prefer the Michaux from Holland, assuming it ripens eight days later. ALLEY PEA. An excellent variety, as remarkable for its quality as for its fertility and earliness. POIS MICHAUX or PETIT-POIS DE PARIS. Very fertile and excellent. This variety should form the basis of autumn and spring sowings. POIS DE CLAMART, quite productive, very good quality, and very valuable as a late pea; it can be sown until August, and always provides until the frosts. I completely banish snow peas and chickpeas from the owner's kitchen garden, as unworthy of being served on a master's table; they belong in the farm's kitchen garden. POTATOES. Only varieties of recognized merit for the table should be grown in the owner's kitchen garden: KIDNEY HATIVE, also called marjolin, and even marjoleine, elongated yellow, good and very early, excellent for chassis and for the first sowings in the open ground. SHAW, round yellow, good quality and very early. CHATAIGNE SAINVILLE, long yellow, superior quality to all other varieties. ROUGE LONGUE DE HOLLANDE, good quality and fairly fertile. VITELOTTE ROUGE, long, indispensable for cooking, in that it never crushes in stews. GREEN SPANISH PUMPKIN. Small but excellent green fruit, the only one allowed in the owner's vegetable garden. CHINA WINTER RADISH. The best variety to grow with: YELLOW HATIF RADISH, also excellent, and very good for autumn sowing. RAVE PETITE HATIVE; the only one to introduce into the owner's vegetable garden. RAIPONCE, to be sown in small quantities as a winter salad. RHUBARBUS. A British import, deserving of pride of place in the homeowner's garden. Makes excellent pies and compotes. The best variety to grow is Nepaul rhubarb, which also makes good jams and excellent syrups. SALSIFIS. A vegetable of very mediocre quality, to be grown in very small quantities. Spanish scorzonera is preferred to salsify. SARRIETTE. Aromatic plant to be grown as a seasoning. THYME, to be grown as a border plant. RED TOMATO WITH CRISP LEAVES. The best, earliest and most fertile. We can also grow: The BIG YELLOW HATIVE TOMATO, as a fancy; it is not as good as the previous one. TRUFFLE. The means of reproduction are as yet unknown. But we can tell them that the area around Etampes (Seine-et-Oise) is literally stuffed with excellent truffles, perhaps preferable to those of Périgord for their finesse and perfume. This is the opinion of some edible merchants, and it's ours too. Etampes truffles didn't find buyers in principle; they were first sent to Périgord, from where they returned to Paris with a patent of superiority. Today, Etampes truffles, which are better appreciated, are taken to Paris. They are sold every year for considerable sums. So let's hear it for amateurs. I have indicated the most suitable vegetable varieties for the homeowner's kitchen garden, all those that give certain results and combine the triple advantages of quality, fertility and earliness. There are many others, also excellent, but perhaps not combining the same advantages as completely. All the varieties I have indicated are available in very pure form from MM. Vilmorin et Andrieux, 30, quai de la Mégisserie. Those wishing to increase the number of varieties need only request a catalog from Vilmorin et Andrieux, or consult Le Bon Jardinier; they will find all the necessary information in this excellent book, written by the leading lights of horticultural science. See Special Crops for the care to be given to each species, and the place it should occupy in the ascolement. I exclude from this chapter the garden of the tenant with a certain fortune, and taking a lease on a house and garden, for a period of time varying between six and twelve years. This tenant will have to apply to the letter the cultivation indicated in the previous chapter, for the owner's garden. Some people, objecting that the soil does not belong to them, will shy away from the organization, and especially from the expense of fertilizer, necessary for the rotation of their vegetable garden, to avoid giving up, free of charge to the owner, the benefit of the fertilized soil. This is a false calculation. It's true that the tenant who improves the land leaves the landowner with a surplus value, but by the time he gives it up to the landowner, he has withdrawn almost all the benefits. If, on the contrary, he does nothing to improve, for fear of giving a profit to the owner, he places his garden in the condition of a cluttered garden, and is himself the first dupe of his parsimony! It's true that he leaves nothing for the landlord, but for the duration of his lease, he has spent without reaping anything, and if he would take the trouble to count the expenses incurred, he would quickly recognize that he is losing enormously by not wanting to leave anything behind. Whenever a tenant only has a nine-year lease, he will benefit from sowing the vegetable garden, and even planting a fruit garden. The vegetable garden will give him a definite profit on the first rotation; this profit will increase considerably on the second, and will cover him well beyond his expenses, while abandoning to the landowner a piece of land brought to a state of cultivation. The fruit garden will produce from the second year onwards; by the fourth, it will have repaid in fruit the capital spent on creating it; after full repayment, the tenant will still have five years to harvest, including three of maximum production. The tenant therefore benefits from planting under these conditions. There's no doubt that the landowner gains a great deal, but the lessee has a real advantage in doing so. The cultivation I'm going to describe in this chapter applies to the tenant with a limited income, who rents a house with a garden, not to make a literal profit from it, but to find a free supply of vegetables and fruit as compensation. This is easy: not only will he harvest all that is necessary for his household, but he will also be able to find in the surplus of his harvests, sold at the market, an indemnity sufficient to cover all his advances, and even part of his rent, if he operates with discernment, and above all without parsimony. There are many small annuitants, whose income is very limited; most of them have a taste for gardening, and find in the produce of a small garden a source of well-being. It is for them that I am writing this chapter; they will obtain more than they expect, if they follow my instructions to the letter; but let them not forget that the earth is a generous borrower, giving much when it has been lent, but refusing any produce to anyone who does not begin by making the necessary advances. The tenant's first concern will be to gather and manufacture the necessary fertilizers in advance. The only animals the tenant will own are a few rabbits. I don't even suppose he'll have any chickens; I assume he knows how to count, and half a dozen Cocottes locked up in an aviary, and fed on purchased grain, will lay eggs for him, at a cost of at least 50 centimes. Locked-up poultry is a luxury item, and very expensive; if you want the proceeds to cover your expenses, you need to have pedigree poultry, whose eggs sell for at least two francs, and you also need to be sure, in advance, of the placement of the eggs. If the tenant, placed in the condítions indicated, has Cocottes, he will put them in his marmnite, and will buy every aunóc, with half the expense of the grain, a car of horse manure, from an inn, it is the best, and that which carries the most profit to mix it. Let's suppose that our little annuitant, living with his wife and a maid, has a garden of ten ares: he'll keep two of them in bloom; cultivate the fruit trees in espalier, against the walls, and completely raze eight ares, to make a vegetable garden, subject to the four-year crop rotation. He'll be able to make enough layers, with his car of inn manure, by adding leaves. Let's look at the most economical ways of making enough compost, with no other resources than a few rabbits. Like the landowner, the tenant will first have to install a platform for making fertilizer; it will be smaller, but set up in the same way. An old broken-down barrel, two if necessary, will serve as a reservoir for liquid fertilizer. If it's possible to have mobile latrines, with a barrel that can be removed at will, this will be an excellent thing, a powerful means of obtaining, at no cost, a very energetic fertilizer for animalizing vegetable matter. If fecal matter is in short supply, there are old women in every town, and even in every village, who are too weak to go out during the day, and who are always happy to earn a few pennies. In the absence of old women, we take on kids, and deal with them, from one franc fifty to two francs, to collect one meter of horse dung. With two meters of dung, you can make four cars of excellent compost. The same people can collect leaves in autumn for next to nothing, and cut broom and gorse in the woods in summer. For ten francs, you can have mountains of all these things, and convert them into excellent composts. All household water is carefully collected in barrels to increase the liquid fertilizer. Then proceed to make composts, as I've described for the owner's garden, page 76 and following, adding every day all the wastes from the vegetable garden, kitchen and house. With the fertilizers made, and the garden divided into four equal parts, all that remains to be done is to organize the four-year crop rotation. The first act should be to build up the layers with the carriage of inn manure, mixed with the reserve of leaves. Don't be afraid of these layers, it's nothing to do; they'll bring you profit, and will double your full-land crops. The small rentier doesn't have the resources of the landowner, and will sometimes run out of frames, and even bells. But that's no reason not to make layers and do without melons; he'll make layers and have excellent melons, a little later than if he had frames and bells, but he'll have them! Glass, which is quite expensive, will be replaced by calico; nothing could be easier. All that's needed is a little imagination, not too clumsy hands, and a few pennies to work this miracle. Here's how it's done: to make an economical frame, first make a box with four planks of white wood and eight nails. White wood is preferable to all others for sash boxes; the more porous it is, the better, and you must be careful not to paint or tar it. Paint clogs the wood's pores, preventing the stoves from working; tar has the same effect, and also kills plants (pl. 3, fig. 5). Once the box is made, a frame of the same size is made to cover it. Four wooden rods, two centimetres thick and five or six centimetres wide, are required; a mortise is cut halfway through each corner; eight nails complete the operation (pl. 3, figs. 6 and 7). A calico hoop, or even a piece of old curtain, or anything else you like, is stretched over the frame, as long as it's white. Nail the fabric firmly to all four sides of the frame, taking care to stretch it tightly, and you've got yourself a trunk, and a frame that costs just a few pennies, and is likely to last for many years. Nothing could be easier than to raise excellent molons under this frame, only instead of sowing in January, as under glass frames, we'll sow from March 15 to 39, and we'll have the luxury of laying two doormats every evening instead of one, and even three, one on top of the other, if it's a bit frosty. 8 Now we need bells; it will be just as easy to make as many as we want, and it will be even cheaper. If we want to make them very solid, we need to get twelve small wooden rods, 40 centimeters long and one centimeter thick; nail them together to make a 40-centimeter cube (pl. 4, fig. 1), and nail on five pieces of fabric of any kind; four for each side, and the fifth for the top (pl. 4, fig. 2.) Should you find the fabric and rods too expensive, you can achieve the same result by making a wicker cage covered with oiled paper (pl. 4, fig. 3 and h). I give all these means, because I want to prove that to want is to be able. Anyone who doubts success, and is reluctant to spend the money on one or two frames and a few bells, should give it a try with calico or oiled paper the first year; their melon crop, taken to market, will put them in a position to mount themselves well in glass, without taking a penny out of their pocket. I insist, because a four-year crop rotation is only possible with layers, and layers, far from costing money, bring in a lot, while doubling the yield of field crops. Only warm, muted diapers can be made with economic shelters; they cannot be used for warm diapers. These layers will be set up in vegetable garden square A, as I said for the owner's garden, and the rest of this square will be used for flower breeding, seed and vegetable harvesting, and on a maximum smoked part, for vegetable nurseries, intended to repopulate the beds, as and when they are cleared of their crops. The horse manure and leaves will be used to make the beds on square No. 4. About a third of the compost, the most decomposed part, will be ploughed into square n° 2, taking care to reserve enough of it, and of the most consumed, to cover the seedlings. Plot no. 3 will be ashed, and two-thirds of the compost will be buried in plot no. 1. In the first year, we cover the layers with the mixture of horse manure and soil that I indicated in the previous chapter, page 96, to replace the compost. Finally, in the second year, we'll distribute the product of the demolition of the layers, the composts, etc., and we'll continue with the crop rotation as I've indicated for the owner's garden, page 99. If, in spite of everything I've just said and indicated to make cheap layers, the tenant persists in giving them up, he will have to sow his garden for three years, without layers. This crop will be far superior in yield to the one he has grown to date, but the lack of diapers will cause him to lose more than half his harvest. The three-year rotation will have to be introduced, and continued as I indicated in the previous chapter, p. 93. The tenant's garden, as I see it, must produce a high income, and give certain harvests. To achieve this goal, a strict choice of varieties must be made, and in the grower's own interest, he must obtain these varieties, very pure and from a good source, to be assured of success. VEGETABLE VARIETIES TO BE INTRODUCED INTO THE TENANT'S GARDEN. HATIF GARLIC, or ROSE GARLIC; better and earlier than the common variety. ARTICHAUT VERT DE LAON; the best and most fertile. ASPERGE VIOIETTE D'ULM; the earliest and most beautiful. A precious resource for the market, when there are too many. BIG RED ORDINARY BEET, Variety, very beautiful, very rustic, and asgez big; good in salads, and profitable for animals. CARDON DE TOURS. The best of all, with a long shelf life, HOLLAND SHORT HATIVE RED CARROT. The best of all, as well as the hardiest and hardiest. Sow from February to September for good carrots all year round. HATIT SHORT CELERY: very good, easy to grow. CURLED CHERRY: sow a few at a time, every two weeks. FINE ITALIAN CHICORY. Excellent, very beautiful and very good for outdoor cultivation. CHICORÉE DE MEAUX, for cultivation under frames and cloches. CHICORÉE SAUVAGE AMÉLIORÉE PANACHÉE, for making capuchin beard in winter, in the cellar, and for feeding to animals in summer. HATIVE ROUND SCAROLE, very good and easy to grow. COEUR DE BOEUF cabbage, excellent for spring. CHOU CONIQUE DE POMÉRANIE, excellent, for summer. CHOU DE MILAN DES VERTUS, for winter. CHOU DE BRUXELLES, for late winter and spring. CHOU-FLEUR DEMI-DUR DE PARIS, superb and excellent. HATIF WHITE CUCUMBER, for eating cooked, and : LITTLE GREEN CUCUMBER, for making pickles and cucumber salads. SHEEP CUCUMBER, to be grown in borders, to season salads. ECHALOTTE DE JERSEY, earlier than the common variety. FLANDERS SPINACH, very broad-leafed, very good and very productive. FÈVE A LONGUES COSSES, very early, excellent and very productive. FRAISIER DE GAILLON, delicious fruit, very productive. This variety can replace all others. Produces excellent strawberries from May to December. HARICOT FLAGEOLET BLANC, the most productive and the best, in green and in fresh grains, and very good in dry grains. Flageolet can be sown from the second half of April to September. FLAGEOLET ROUGE, the best and most productive red bean. SOISSONS NAIN, for eating as dried beans. As the locatairé garden is quite small, we have banned pole beans, which would cause shade damage to neighboring plants: LAITUE DAUPHINE, for spring. LAITUE DE VERSAILLÉS, for summer. LAITUÉ PALATINE, for late summer. LAITUE SANGUINE, black-seeded, for all summer. LAITUE MORINE, for winter: LITTLE CREEP, for winter cultivation only, under frames and cloches. ROMAINE VERTÉ MARAÎCHERE, very large, excellent, and peeling without being tied. ROMAINE ROUGE D'HIVER, very rustic, good, and withstands frost well. Requires tying for good flavour. ROND MACHE, very good; sow in squares to harvest in winter. MELON PRESCOT A FOND VERT: the best variety, very-rustic, very-fertile, and advantageous for the market. NAVET DE FRENEUSE, hard. NAVET BLANC DES VERTUS, tender. NAVET JAUNE DE FINLANDE, semi-tender. OIGNON BLANC HATIF, for sowing in August, and transplanting to give the first onions in spring 6; it is also sown in spring to have small onions to preserve with gherkins. OIGNON BLOND DES VERTUS, to be sown in spring to provide the year's supply. OSEILLE DE BELLEVILLE, fairly sweet, with broad leaves, sow in borders. PANAIS ROND, in small quantities. PARSLEY, to be sown in borders. PIMPRENELLE, to be sown in borders, for rabbits. POITOU YELLOW PEAR, very nice and very good variety. POIS MICHAUX, for sowing at St Catherine's Day. POIS MICHAUX DE HOLLANDE, for sowing in spring, in two batches, one month apart, the best of all. CLAMART PEAS, for sowing in summer. EARLY KIDNEY POTATOES, for the first season. POMME-DE-TERRE SÉGONZAC, excellent and very fertile. GREEN SPANISH STARCH, small, the best of all, and keeps well. RADIUS ROSE HATIF, very good and very fast-growing. RAIPONCE, for sowing in squares, as a winter salad. SAISIFIS. Preferably grow SCORSONERE from SPAIN, which is earlier. SARIET, in small quantities, for seasoning. TAYM, to be grown as a border plant. RED TOMATO WITH CRISP LEAVES. The best, the most fertile and the earliest. With these varieties, you can be sure of abundant harvests of excellent vegetables, if you really buy the varieties I indicate. See Special Crops for the cultivation of each species, and the place it should occupy in the crop rotation. Distsed CO I have made a distinction here between the farmer's vegetable garden and that of the farm, because according to the regions where this book will reach, there are farmers of all classes. I cannot confuse together, and indicate the same culture, for the farmers of Brie, Laonnais, Picardy and the North, often richer than their owners, and those of various regions where the farmer is at the head of a farm bringing in from 600 francs to 2,000 francs to the owner, and where the farmer, a hard worker, gives the example of manual labor to his servants, and lives with them. The needs of these different farmers are different; their vegetable crops cannot be the same. The big farmer who lives comfortably, heads a large staff and has extensive gardens around his farm needs a vegetable garden similar to that of the landowner. If, as is the case most of the time, the farmer is in charge of the cowshed, the dairy, the farmyard and the vegetable garden, she will benefit from a four-year rotation with layers. The vegetable garden, thus treated, will supply the needs of the farmer's table, and the surplus, taken to market, or sent to the Paris market, if the farm is near a railway line, will pay with wear and tear all expenses. As long as there are no large quantities of flowers or greenhouses to maintain, there is no need for a gardener to run a four-year rotation vegetable garden. I'd even go so far as to say that an intelligent farmer, with this book in her hand, will get better results with a man who knows nothing about horticulture, and a few women for the odd jobs, than with a head gardener. The day labourer, or all-purpose servant, such as there are on every farm, ignorant of horticulture, will carry out to the letter a concise order given by the mistress of the house. In this case, success will be assured, because the application will be exact. The gardener in charge, almost always as ignorant as the handyman, but believing himself to be the world's foremost horticulturist, will perorate on receiving the order, modify it in execution to suit his ego, and often the result will be negative. So we'll set up two vegetable gardens on the rich farmer's property: one subject to a four-year crop rotation, cultivated in much the same way as the owner's. This garden, adjoining the owner's house, will be planted in the same way as the other. This vegetable garden, adjoining the farmhouse, will be managed exclusively by the mistress of the house; its produce will supply the needs of the master's table, and any surplus will be sold. The second vegetable garden, established on the plain in the best parts of the farm, will be part of the crop rotation, with a huge profit for the farm. This kitchen garden will be managed by the farmer himself, and cultivated by his servants. The produce, consisting of large vegetables of hardy, fertile varieties, will be used to feed the farm staff. May the farmers, of whom we do not have the honor of being known, allow us to give a few words of advice to their ladies, and to enter the heart of their crops. We are almost authorized to do so by their colleagues, who have honored us with their friendship, at whose homes we have created fruit gardens, and who have all said to us: We lack vegetables for ourselves and our people; we have tried some orange blossom gardeners; they have given us masses of bouquets, but not a vegetable for us, nor a cabbage for our servants. We paid these gardeners crazy prices; the product was nil, we cut them out, because it was

a dry expense. Give us the means to have enough vegetables for ourselves and our servants, you'll be doing us a great service. To which I replied: wait until I have a few weeks to spare, and I'll make you a practical book, with the help of which Madame will be able to manage her vegetable garden, with the first man who comes along, provided he has good will and a little intelligence, and a little time taken, from far and wide, from the farm maids. With this same book, it will be easy for you, if your gardens are not big enough, to grow vegetables on the plain, and to harvest, not only without cost, but also by improving your land, all the vegetables necessary for your servants and your harvesters. You've got the modern vegetable garden in hand, so all you have to do is get to work. We're not going to have fun making compost. There's too much work to be done on a farm, and servants' time is too precious to spend on making a few truckloads of manure that wouldn't pay for the labor of a farmer with a large herd of cattle. If we have masses of manure in the yard, we'll choose fresh or consumed, depending on our needs: a cart driver will bring it in a dumper, and we'll have saved a lot of time. The same cart driver will take the garden's detritus and throw it into the yard, where it won't go to waste. The chickens will eat some of it, and the rest will rot in the manure. You have a medium-sized garden, sufficient to provide vegetables for your numerous staff, but more than enough for your provision. If you receive guests, you need to be well supplied with all kinds of vegetables. And if you live in a country where horticulture is on the increase, you'll be well advised to grow good varieties, because the exotic produce will be sold on the market at a high price, and people will come to your house to buy it, without you having to go out of your way. This garden, whose produce is destined solely to supply your table and the market, will be entirely under Madame's management. You, sir, will grow vegetables on the plain for your servants. Here's how you should proceed, to obtain certain results, in the masters' garden. Start, madame, by carefully examining your espaliers; if they produce little or nothing, don't hesitate to replant. Please consult Arboriculture fruitière; this book will tell you all you need to know about how to plant your walls properly, how to obtain fruit very quickly, and even how to care for your trees in such a way as to obtain a crop that's equal every year, to within a tenth. Then, you'll ruthlessly raze all the clutter that clutters the vegetable garden squares, starting with old cattails and funnel-shaped cups, which give you a few bad fruits by chance; prevent, by their shade, your vegetables from growing, and are only good for exhausting the soil, to the detriment of other crops. If there are any tall trees, cut them down, unless they are very valuable, in which case you will have to give up growing vegetables for a diameter of about 10 meters around them. Finish clearing your garden of anything that clutters or shades the squares. Divide the kitchen garden into four or six equal parts, if you want to grow asparagus and artichokes, as described for the owner's kitchen garden, and have the whole or part of it cleared, as you wish, as I mentioned earlier. We have a basin in the center of the kitchen garden; with a Perrault pump which will serve your husband for his liquid manure, and which you will find in case of fire; two twelve-year-old children will water the four or five squares (asparagus needs no water) in less than two hours. The Perrault pump costs 130 fr. with the air tank; it's sturdy, portable and easy for a child to operate. I'll talk more about this in the chapter on watering. February 15 is here; the vegetable garden, divided into four or five equal parts, is broken up; the basin is set up, the first layers are put up, the soil is fertilized, ploughed and sown. You have, madame, an intelligent and devoted man; you told him that he would obtain abundant harvests by following your advice, and he believes you. What's more, his self-esteem is at stake, and he's ready to be put to the test. You can count on him to carry out your orders to the letter, and the mere prospect of doing better than the gardener working for such-and-such a man will be enough to keep him working hard for a long time. Have him choose fresh horse manure to build up the first warm layer, on the leanest square which will take the n° h of the crop rotation. You'll sow the first melons and all the early plants on this layer, covered with sieves. Three weeks later, sow two warm layers to transplant melons and other plants; later, sow the warm layers, and finally the muted layers, in the same square no. 4 (see the chapter on layers, for how to make them, and the special crops for the vegetables to be sown in them, for sowing and transplanting times). Towards the end of February or in the first days of March, you'll have a selection of well-consumed manure chosen from the yard, to cover the least-smoked square, 15 to 20 centimetres thick; this square will be No. 1 in the crop rotation, and will be used for vegetables requiring abundant nourishment. The previous year's best-smoked square will receive no manure, and will be assigned to roots and crops that fear fresh manure. Seedlings will be covered with completely decomposed manure. This square will be No. 2 in the crop rotation. Finally, the last square, a little smoked the previous year, will be energetically ashed. It will be number 3 in the rotation, and will be used for dried fruit vegetables. If two squares are set aside for asparagus and artichokes, they will be planted as described for special crops, and will be included in the crop rotation, as previously mentioned. The following year, the rotation will continue, and the product of the demolition of the layers will be used as I have already said. In subsequent years, the vegetable garden will require only the manure needed to make the beds; a little well-consumed manure for plot no. 1, if the beds don't provide enough, and the necessary ash for plot no. 3. If you were afraid of layers - and this would be a great mistake, Madame, especially with the quantities of fertilizer you have at your disposal - you would have to adopt the three-year crop rotation, and carry it out in every respect as I said in the crop rotation chapter. An owner who is short of horse manure, and fears that he won't always be able to find suitable manure to buy, may hesitate to layer; this is a mistake, but as it has no reason to be on a farm, it becomes a fault; nowhere can you layer with more chance of success than on a large farm, where you only have to choose your manures. Let's now look for good-quality varieties for the farmer's vegetable garden, which can find, if necessary, an advantageous placement on the market, and are likely to be consumed by the farm's servants, if the vegetable garden is very large. I have only indicated the varieties here. See Special Crops for information on how to care for them and the place they should occupy in crop rotation. FARM VEGETABLE GARDEN. I have just dealt with the rich farmer's vegetable garden, the one intended to supply his own table, and if necessary that of the farm. More often than not, the farm garden is insufficient to provide for the consumption of his numerous staff, so the farmer must devote his garden to his own consumption, and grow the vegetables necessary for his servants on the plain. Vegetable seedlings and nurseries, intended for transplanting on the plain, will be planted in the farmer's kitchen garden, where they will occupy part of plot no. h, if the kitchen garden is subject to four-year crop rotation, or part of plot no. 3, if it is subject to three-year crop rotation. In all cases, a four-year rotation is preferable, especially if you're creating a vegetable garden on a plain. The potting soils provided by the layers make vegetable growing easier and less expensive. Vegetables are almost always in short supply on farms, even though they are essential for hygiene. Nothing is easier than to grow them on the plains, as much as is necessary, because this crop, far from being expensive for the farmer, will help to improve his land and increase his harvest. Only vegetables intended for domestic use should be grown on the plains: hardy, productive varieties. The best plots of land will be chosen for this purpose, and subjected to a six- or ten-year rotation, depending on the crop and the needs of the farm. Vegetable gardens on the plain can be cultivated in two ways: by plough or by hand. If cultivated by plough, the six-year crop rotation is adopted. If cultivated by hand, the ten-year rotation can be adopted. In both cases, all vegetable crops will be grown in rows, so that hoeing can be carried out with a horse hoe, and to avoid a lot of labor if done by hand. SIX-YEAR CROP ROTATION. The vegetable garden on the plain, which should provide inexpensive vegetables and be part of the farm's crop rotation, will be cultivated using agricultural implements. The plough, weeder, roller, harrow and horse hoe will perform three-quarters of the cultivation. Hand hoeing will only be used for tightly packed seedlings such as carrots, onions, etc., etc., etc. This vegetable garden, which should never be watered, will be created in substantial soil, without being too clayey, and with depth above all. In addition, the soil should be deeply ripped to reduce exposure to drought. This can be done with a powerful plough, followed by a digger. First, the ripper plough is ploughed lengthwise, followed by a digger, and then a second ripper is ploughed crosswise. In this way, the soil is broken up and excavated very deeply. The digger then breaks up the clods, and the soil is in the best possible condition. In the first year, we grow cabbages, artichokes, broad beans, potatoes, etc., etc., on a very plentiful supply of fertilizer (9). The second year, without manure: carrots, onions, salsify, turnips, etc., etc. The third year, without manure, but on good ash: peas, beans, lentils, etc. The fourth year, without manure, oats with clover. The fifth year produces excellent clover, and finally, in the sixth year, wheat completes the rotation. This rotation will produce three vegetable harvests, two cereals and one clover, in the best conditions: six harvests maximum; and when this land, cultivated in this way for six years, returns to the farm's rotation, it will be with an enormous increase in value, due to the breaking up, the most copious manuring, and perfect loosening. The farmer will benefit greatly from cultivating all his best pieces in this way, with savings on the one hand, and abundance on the other. TEN-YEAR CROP ROTATION. This rotation will be highly advantageous for farmers who do not have the necessary tools for vegetable cultivation, and for small farmers who have only low-powered plows and not enough garden space to grow the vegetables they need. For this vegetable garden, as for the previous one, we'll choose a piece of good, fairly substantial, not too compact, deep soil. It should be broken up with two spade irons. This is done by the job, at so much per acre. The soil will be very heavily smoked, and vegetables will be grown for the first three years, as in the previous crop rotation. In the fourth year, unfertilized oats and alfalfa will be sown. Under these conditions, the oats will produce an abundant harvest, and the alfalfa, which prefers deep cultivation to abundant fertilization, will produce remarkably rich cuts for five years, especially if care is taken to plaster it and water it from time to time with liquid fertilizer. The small farmer, who almost always lacks the necessary vegetables, will have an abundance of them; the surplus can be sold advantageously at the market, and the superb alfalfa he obtains on his spade-clearing, after the vegetable harvest, will be a real asset for him, in a small farm where, in a hurry to make money, cereal cultivation is often abused, to the detriment of animals which lack fodder. The small farmer will have a great advantage in introducing this method of cultivation to the best parts of his farm. If necessary, the vegetable garden can provide him with a certain quantity of weeded plants, grown with the vegetables, to maintain the animals. His alfalfa plants will enable him to increase the number of animals; and the quantity of fertilizers increasing in proportion, all crops will gain in richness. 9 If the small farmer has a garden insufficient for vegetable production, he will devote it to growing fruit trees and delicate plants, and to raising vegetables that will be transplanted to the plain; With the help of this rotation, the small farm, which lacks vegetables for its workers and fodder and roots for its animals, will find considerable well-being for the former, and a notable increase for the latter; and this improvement will also earn it a high added value on all other crops, as much from the increase in fertilizers as from the many good manures given to the soil. On-site sowing will be carried out with a wheelbarrow seeder on larger farms, and in rows, by hand, on smaller farms. We don't have to look for the finesse of produce in the lowland vegetable garden. Above all, we need very rustic varieties that yield a lot and require little care. Vegetable gardens on the plains will most often be cultivated by women on small farms, so we need to look for vegetable varieties that produce a lot with little care, and whose surplus can find an advantageous placement on the market.
