DieESS P COC The presbytery vegetable garden will often vary in cultivation, depending on its size, the importance of the parish and the parish priest's taste for horticultural work. If the parish is large, and the parish priest is an amateur, or better still, if, as in almost all the towns where I've taught in the Oise département, he wants to pass on his horticultural knowledge to his parishioners, he will benefit from buying the horse manure needed to make hot beds, and from subjecting his garden to a four-year rotation, growing the same varieties of vegetables as the owner, for his own personal consumption. In the event of the presbytery's garden extending over a certain area, the walls will be replanted to provide a supply of fruit. The espaliers and a double cordon around the edge of the espalier bed will provide enough. If necessary, the squares can be surrounded by a two-row uni-lateral cordon of pear, apple, cherry, plum trees, etc., but these are the only trees that should be tolerated in the kitchen garden. If there aren't enough walls to supply the house, part of the garden should be used to create a small fruit garden, as described in Arboriculture fruitière. We could also devote a plot of land to growing flowers and a few shrubs, and use the rest as a vegetable garden. If the vegetable garden is large enough, we can grow, in addition to the varieties of vegetables designated for the owner's garden, those I have indicated for the tenant's, farmer's, farm's, etc., etc., gardens, by way of experimentation. When parish priests are willing to devote their leisure time to horticulture, they render important services in towns, and in the countryside, by their wise and enlightened advice to gardeners and farmers. We owe a great deal to the zeal of ecclesiastics for the notable progress that has been made in arboriculture in recent years. They are called upon to render services no less great, by cultivating and introducing into their parishes good varieties of vegetables. Farmers, and even many small-town gardeners, don't know the names of the vegetable varieties they grow; often the variety grown in the country has no name, or is so degenerate that it would be very difficult, not to say impossible, to give it one. And yet, these bad varieties continue to be cultivated, to the great detriment of the grower, simply because no other is known. The day good varieties are introduced, especially early varieties, everyone will be quick to ask for seedlings. It doesn't take four years to extirpate a country's bad vegetables and replace them with good, beautiful varieties, thereby doubling vegetable harvests, which are always insufficient in the countryside. But to achieve this goal, you have to start by growing your own produce, and show the results to your heart's content. Horticulture is like high culture; as soon as something unknown in the country appears, everyone starts laughing, but soon the laughter ceases in the face of the results, and everyone follows the example set. If we were to shy away from the expense of horse manure to make layers, we'd have to adopt the three-year rotation, without layers, even though it's less advantageous. A few melons sent to market will quickly repay the expense of fertilizer, and produce will be much more abundant with the combined resources of layers and composts. In this case, whether the presbytery garden is subject to a four-year rotation with diapers, or a three-year rotation without diapers, it will become a small fruit and vegetable school, from which everyone can draw useful information. But most of the time, the presbytery gardens are large, and the parish offers few resources. In this case, the parish priest, if he is an amateur horticulturist, will still be able to render great services, while finding real well-being in the cultivation of his garden. However small the resources, the first thing to do is to arrange the fertilizers. This is difficult, you may say, with nothing! However, if we look hard enough, we'll still find a way to make enough fertilizer to subject a small garden to a three-year rotation. A wagonload of horse manure, mixed with leaves, would make it possible to make several layers; a warm layer first for the first melons and early seedlings, and then muted layers with leaves mixed with a little manure. This would make it possible to introduce four-year crop rotation, with the immense advantage of layer potting soils. If manure were very scarce, we would have to grow only the Arckangel melon, the hardiest of all, in fine season, almost without bedding and under very poor shelter. I insist on the adoption of a four-year rotation, because layers are the basis of the vegetable garden's fertility, and however modest a parish priest's position may be, he can always buy a wagonload of horse manure, but in this case, fresh inn manure is preferable. Finally, there are very few parishes where there isn't a kind soul who will be quick to offer one, or even two, manure wagons to his parish priest, especially if he is doing horticultural services for the country. Who wouldn't give a carload of manure in exchange for five or six melons? It's a feasible deal, and a win-win situation for both parties. A farmer will gladly give manure in exchange for a plant of a good variety of vegetable. It's an exchange of good practices, and we're glad to see that there's no shortage of them in our beautiful country of France. Every effort should be made to introduce a four-year crop rotation. If the curé is poor in manure, he will restrict the extent of his layers, and increase the mass of composts. The result will be much the same for field crops. It's easy to make a large quantity of compost, by adding garden and house debris to leaves and grasses from ditches, moors or woods, and livening them up with horse dung collected from streets and roads. It's a small job that can be done by the poor, the infirm, the elderly and children, at very low cost. Compost can be made everywhere, and without expense, from grasses, debris and horse dung. As I said earlier, all household water should be used to water the composts. If square n° L, destined for diapers, is sparsely stocked, due to the lack of fresh manure, a large part of the square will be devoted to the cultivation of seeds. The day the parish priest grows good varieties of vegetables, not only his entire parish, but even neighboring communes will ask for seeds: the parish priest's seeds will always be preferred, because if they are well grown and harvested with care, they 10 ASSOLEMENT. will be of the highest quality. The parish priest who cultivates in the interest of his parishioners' well-being will not sell his seeds, but if his financial means are very limited, he can ask for a little manure in exchange. Sowing and raising plants is difficult without potting soil; the more layers there are, the easier and more abundant the raising of seedlings and the harvesting of seeds will be. The devotion of a large number of priests to horticultural interests is well known to me, but for all their good will, many lack the necessary means to apply the science they have acquired; too modest, they content themselves with giving advice which, to be sure, has great influence, but this influence would be doubled if they had the means to do and demonstrate what they advise. In the general interest, I would like the gardens of country parish priests and schoolteachers to be specimens of good cultivation, small schools in which to raise plants of the right varieties, and where to collect seeds in sufficient quantity to propagate them rapidly. I put forward this idea because I know in advance that it will be adopted, if it has not already been adopted by a large number of clergymen and teachers. I will say later what I hope for, and what I have obtained from teachers with the support of prefects devoted to agricultural interests; I return to country parish priests who often have more time than teachers, and who by state as much as by character, are entirely devoted to the interests of their parishioners. Country presbyteries usually have fairly extensive gardens. If the parish priest is a horticulturist, he will render immense services by cultivating the right varieties of fruit and vegetables, and by giving grafts, seedlings and seeds to anyone who asks for them. This is possible, even assuming that the parish priest can afford to spend nothing. What does the parish priest need to create a small fruit garden, and subject his kitchen garden to a four-year rotation? A hundred or so trees of typical varieties, which he can multiply ad infinitum and without any expense whatsoever, by creating a small nursery in his garden; a certain quantity of horse or cow manure, leaves, herbs, two frame panels and a few bells. In almost every village, there is, if not a castle, at least one, if not several, owners' houses, and at least one farmer. What lord or lady of the manor, or even landowner, will not think of offering their parish priest, especially if he is providing horticultural services to his country, not only the manure he needs for his beds, but also the frames and bells he needs to raise the seedlings he will need? Which farmer will not be too happy, at the cost of one or two cartloads of manure, to find, every year, the vegetable plant, especially cabbage, necessary for his harvesters and his numerous staff? All of them will undoubtedly benefit from providing the manure needed to make up the beds of the presbytery, and from finding the seeds and seedlings that their multiplied occupations do not allow them to make. It goes without saying that the parish priest, however poor he may be, will find help and assistance from his parishioners, for the manure he lacks, and even for the shelters intended to cover the beds. He will return these advances a hundredfold to everyone, not to mention the well-being he will bring to the people who are always deprived of the necessary vegetables, and who, with good varieties and more careful cultivation, will not only have harvests more than sufficient for their personal consumption, but also a surplus, with a certain value, to take to market. The poor farmer, with a harvest of vegetables insufficient for his family, will be well off the day this harvest is sufficient for his needs, and rich when this same crop gives him a surplus of only two hundred francs. Two hundred francs in a peasant household is more than ten thousand francs in a city dweller's household. You need to have lived with small-scale farming, to know the amount of work it expends, and the economy with which it lives, to know what a small sum given by an additional crop is worth. Two hundred francs is a fortune; it represents not a certain amount of well-being - the small farmer doesn't give himself, nor his children either - but the possibility of buying a host of indispensable items to improve and increase cultivation. Small farmers are wealthy around Paris and the big cities; they are wealthy because they know how to farm, and possess the capital needed to give the land all it requires; this fortune, or rather this affluence, which is often the envy of city parasites, is acquired at the price of the hardest and most arduous work; it is not uncommon to see small farmers providing themselves, their wives and their children, eighteen to twenty hours of work a day. One member of the family leaves at eleven o'clock in the evening, with a cart, for the market; he spends part of the night unloading and stowing his load at the market, and as soon as he's unloaded, he heads back to his house, and works until evening, just as if he'd spent the night in bed. And the fry who spend their existence in front of wine merchants' counters, discussing the organization of work, covet the honey so painstakingly gathered by these laborious bees! The small farmer in the countryside is generally not very well off, although he takes as much trouble as those living near Paris and the big cities; his position is poor because he doesn't know how to cultivate, and often doesn't know the varieties that would give him twice the yield in half the time. He often lacks the necessary fertilizers and tools, and is denied them by the inadequacy of his harvests. It's this little farmer that I'd like to help with good advice on cultivation, good seeds and good seedlings, and it's on his behalf that I'd like to ask the helpful intervention of the country parish priest, who has great authority over him. The small farmer will be the first worker of public wealth the day good farming principles reach him. He will be the first worker of public wealth, because the day he knows how to cultivate, and has the plant and seeds he needs, the field that yielded two will produce twenty. When this problem is solved, and nothing could be easier, the small farmer will not only have an abundant harvest for himself and his family, but the surplus of this harvest will also give him enough money to increase his livestock, double the amount of fertilizer he uses and make a great profit from meat, butter and milk; and this prosperous result will also double the harvest of the fields, which, thanks to the livestock and a better-understood cultivation, will receive the indispensable manure and manners. The small farmer, placed in these conditions of improvement, while making his fortune, will find on the soil a product equal to the land value. And, while becoming rich, he will enrich his region; and each region, developed in this way, will soon enrich France in its turn. Need I remind you that the whole of northern Europe buys from France whatever fruit and vegetables appear on the market, and that produce is rarer than foreign buyers, who bring us their gold in exchange for the products that our privileged soil provides in abundance, when we take the trouble to cultivate it. Farmers don't cultivate, or cultivate badly, because they haven't been taught to cultivate, and they don't know what they need to cultivate to produce a high yield. But as soon as the light reaches him, we must see how religiously he listens to the few lessons we can give him, and how intelligently he puts them to good use. As proof of what I'm saying, I should mention the few free public lessons I give every year in the village of Hétoménil. Hétoménil is a humble village in the department of Oise, but its mayor is Mr. Vasselle, a wealthy landowner and farmer. Mr. Vasselle, in this age of dissipation and Ruoltz luxury, has set a fine example to the idle rich, by devoting his existence and his great fortune to agricultural interests. The mayor of Hétoménil, who had enough wealth to lead the most luxurious and idle life, preferred to work, improve the soil and breeds of farm animals, and enrich his region. Mr. Vasselle had a splendid château built, straddling a superb landscaped garden and his large farmyard. He lives in this château like a true patriarch; gifted with an immense benevolence, simple and modest like all men of real merit, he spends his life amassing all the agricultural treasures that can contribute to increasing the richness of cultivation, and distributes them with a generosity almost without example today. Mr. Vasselle's château is God's house," say peasants from miles around. It's true, they give a lot because they produce a lot, because the mayor of Hétoménil thought that God had not created man to remain idle, that fortune, far from being a patent for laziness, was a powerful means of doing good, supporting work, enriching his country and his fellow citizens. If one day, dear reader, you would like to see a man loved and esteemed by all, an existence filled with hard work, a beautiful fortune nobly spent, go and visit the vast and beautiful estate of Hétoménil. You'll find the best and most cordial hospitality. Ask the peasants about Mr. Vasselle, and you'll have a fair idea of the popularity a good man can acquire without seeking it, and by the sole influence of his personal worth. Arboriculture was very strong in the Oise region. Mr. Vasselle had put Fruit Growing in the hands of schoolteachers, and a good number of amateurs. Later, he asked me to come and give a lesson in Hétoménil, to complete the study of the book. A large number of parish priests from neighboring cantons attended the lesson. They all took up arboriculture with such enthusiasm that, in less than three years, all the fruit trees were completely transformed. The willow heads disappeared to make way for fruiting branches created under the best conditions. Garlands of fruit have replaced hideous deformities which, in time, would have led to the death of the trees. Thanks to a few lessons that I'm happy to give every year, and to the zeal of the parish priests who repeat these lessons to their parishioners, the abundance of fruit has given way to famine in a large number of communes around Hétoménil. These same clergymen are eagerly awaiting the Potager Moderne, to carry out vegetable cultivation and teach it to their parishioners. Without Mr. Vasselle, gifted to the highest degree with the spirit of progress, and who has since honored me with his benevolent friendship, the village of Hétoménil would never have heard a teacher of arboriculture, nor would the parish priests of the surrounding communes have been able to render the services they so zealously render to their flocks, by repeating lessons that are of the greatest use to them. Comices agricoles provide important services to field crops, which can be easily moved around. Horticultural societies make noble efforts to spread progress, but their action hardly extends beyond the towns: the countryside remains deprived of advice and lessons; and I cannot repeat too often, it is the countryside that has the greatest need of them, because it can make large-scale applications, with great economy, and. from there, significantly increase general production, the day it knows how to go about good cultivation. The countryside has neither the time nor the means to move to follow the progress of the cities; it remains in its village, with only its parish priest and its teacher as resources, who can do a lot when they want to, as has been proved in the Oise department, thanks to the initiative of Mr. Vasselle, and the benevolent intervention of Mr. Léon Chevreau, Prefect of the Oise. I cited the example of Hétoménil, where brilliant results were achieved in a short space of time, thanks to the intervention of the clergy, because I am convinced that the clergy of Oise, so devoted to agricultural interests, will find imitators in several départements. The day when country parish priests want to convert their presbytery garden into a small school of arboriculture and market gardening, they will be doing a great service, and will contribute more than they can at present to increasing general production. They will easily be able to adopt the four-year rotation, even with very limited resources, with the help of the means I have indicated. Finally, if it is not possible for them to obtain the fertilizers and shelters needed for the layers, they should apply the three-year rotation, without layers, and introduce it as previously mentioned. The varieties of vegetables indicated for the owner's garden should be grown in first-order presbyteries; those indicated for the tenant's garden in second-order presbyteries; and finally, those for the farmer's garden and farm in more modest cures. 18 Independently of his personal consumption, the amateur parish priest, especially if he has a large garden, can, in the general interest, experiment with varieties, raise seedlings and grow seeds. The following varieties may be cultivated on a trial basis, in order to introduce those that are most successful in the different climates where they are tested. ARTICHAUT GROS VERT DE LAON, for all northern France and the northern vine-growing region. ARTICHAUT ROUGE, for the southern vine-growing and olive-growing regions. See special crops for information on the care to be given to each species, and the place it should occupy in the vegetable garden rotation. SIEUEEG E (OO0 6 AND BOARDING HOUSES. Communities, hospitals and boarding houses have very similar needs. These establishments, especially hospitals and communities, which are able to manufacture large quantities of fertilizer, and most of the time have free labor, would benefit considerably from paying a very capable head gardener. The gardener, with the help of some of the residents, a few elderly people and even women, could easily put the gardens into full production, provide the establishment with everything it needs, and still find a fairly high income from the sale of surplus produce. There are hospitals which, with great resources of fertilizer, extensive walls, vast grounds, and a large workforce free of charge, lack the necessary fruits and vegetables. 41 They buy when they should be selling. This is due to poor crop management and an absolute lack of knowledge of arboriculture and market gardening. More often than not, these crops are managed by unqualified gardeners; fertilizer is expended, labor is expended, and produce is almost nil. We see here, as everywhere, poorly grown crops, badly applied fertilizers, fruit and vegetable varieties that are as infertile as they are late and bad. If we could change all this, and introduce rational cultivation in these gardens, we would obtain an abundant product in food and money, where cultivation has only been an object of expense. I visit all the hospitals in the towns where I teach, and most of their gardens have the disadvantages I have mentioned. This vice is not due to a lack of zeal on the part of the administrators; on the contrary, everywhere they actively seek, and with the greatest devotion, the means to increase the hospital's income. This is due solely to the lack of knowledge of cultivation on the part of administrators, bursars and even gardeners. When a member of the board of trustees is knowledgeable about cultivation, the situation changes dramatically; he or she takes on the special task of directing cultivation, and produce is soon multiplied tenfold. When a hospital or community has a large tract of land enclosed by walls, the first care should be to replant progressively, year by year, all the walls, which will give a high income in fruit, when they are covered with trees bearing first choice and first quality fruit. This is easily achieved at almost no cost by creating a small nursery. Once the house provision has been taken, the surplus will be sold on the town market, or in Paris, by mail order at the market hall, if the fruit does not sell well in the country. There's no shortage of hospital inmates to pack the fruit and make baskets, if need be. (See Arboriculture fruitière for planting methods, tree training, pruning and varieties to plant). Walls alone, well planted, with good varieties of fruit, will provide, if the trees are well cared for, not only the provision of the house, but also a cash product; all the land will be free for cultivation, and if this cultivation is well done, it will also give, independently of the provision of the establishment, a cash surplus, which will cover well beyond the cultivation costs, always low, in a hospital or in a community. Sometimes these establishments have very large plots of land and animals. One or two cows, pigs, poultry and so on. In this case, you need to organize the crops in such a way as to provide for the establishment; feed the animals copiously, and find abundant produce to take to market. The hospital is the home of the poor, and nothing should be neglected to increase its wealth, even if 3 in a small proportion. The benefits of good cultivation may seem small at first, but they increase every year, and become considerable after a while. Hospitals and communities possess a wealth of fertilizer like no other; this mass of fertilizer, which costs nothing, carefully collected and well used, would yield luxuriant harvests, if there were a serious cultivation direction in these homes. Let's suppose a hospital with a hundred boarders, half of whom can do some work. Let's assume that this hospital has three or four cows, a dozen pigs, a sizeable barnyard and ten hectares of land. As always, before starting anything, we'll organize the fertilizer factory. All animal manure, without exception, will be put aside to build up the layers. All the leaves will also be stored to mix with the manures in the manufacture of the layers. A very large liquid fertilizer pit will be set up in the center of two vast platforms. The liquid manure from the cowshed, the urine from the house, all the water from the infirmary and the kitchen, the soap water, the detergents, etc., etc., will be carried to this pit every day, or, simpler and more economical, underground pipes will be laid. Immense compost heaps will be set up on the platforms, with all the herbaceous matter collected in the garden, the detritus from the pharmacy and infirmary, and all the herbaceous matter that can be obtained inexpensively from outside: reeds, marsh grasses, heather, broom, gorse, etc., depending on local resources. These herbaceous substances will be mixed with the plant's own faecal matter. They are easy to transport, by installing mobile pits in the buildings, and in several places in the garden for the workers. A few kilograms of ferric sulfate will neutralize the odor and ensure sanitation. By operating in this way, a hospital or community can fertilize its land better than even the richest farmer can. With such a wealth of fertilizers, anything can be achieved. With the fertilizers made, we will divide our ten hectares as follows: Two hectares will be allocated to the vegetable garden, which will be subject to a four-year rotation with layers, and in which we will grow the varieties of vegetables indicated for the farmer's vegetable garden, and in small quantities, those from the owner's garden for the table of the heads of the house only. The crop rotation will be followed as I indicated above, but with the difference that, with a fairly large number of layers, we will grow fewer early vegetables than in the owner's kitchen garden. These beds will be devoted, for the most part, to seedlings, and to raising the seedlings that will be transplanted permanently into the other crops. 6 Nappies are just as essential in hospital and community gardens as they are in the homeowner's garden. They will be of little use for luxury crops, but they will make it possible to considerably advance all plein-terre produce, with the elite seedlings they will provide. Some of this produce will go to market, with the advantage of earliness, which doubles its value. So, the beds will give us a silver product, independently of the profit from their potting soils, which will further increase the plein-terre harvests by a large proportion, and facilitate the cultivation of medicinal plants, so useful and profitable, in establishments of this kind. The two-hectare kitchen garden will always remain as a kitchen garden; it will be completely cleared, as I have indicated in Chapter IV. If the soil is substantial, a little clayey, we'll create an asparagus plan, of one hectare only, for the needs of the house. We'll plant the Ulm violet asparagus, which is the most beautiful and the earliest. It's a good idea to grow this variety, even if you're only planting one hectare, because this area is more than sufficient for consumption, and the first bunches of asparagus, sold at a high price, will pay for the entire cultivation and beyond. If the soil is light and deep, it will be profitable to grow two hectares of asparagus. The proceeds will pay for the entire crop. The remaining seven hectares will be devoted to a mixed crop of vegetables, most of which will be sold; roots for staff and livestock consumption, and fodder and oats for the animals. The land for this mixed crop will be ploughed, like the farm's vegetable garden, or dug with two spades. The work can be done in one go, or in six or seven years, depending on whether we adopt the six- or seven-year rotation. If the soil is substantial and of good quality, it will be beneficial to adopt the seven-year rotation: First year, after breaking up the soil, with a plow or spade, and with copious manuring: large green Laon artichokes. These artichokes will remain in place for three years, during which they will produce beautiful and abundant products, if we take care to give them good manuring in the second and third years. Most of the artichokes will be sold, and we have our supply in the two hectares of vegetable garden. In the fourth year, the artichokes will be uprooted, and we'll grow in their place, without manuring, as needed, beets for the animals, or potatoes, and even a few roots, such as carrots, turnips and so on. In the fifth year, depending on the local market, onions can be grown without manuring, giving a maximum yield with a simple topdressing. But for this to happen, the onions must sell well in the country, and fetch a high price. In the sixth year, without manure, we sow 9 oats with clover, which gives a remarkable product in straw and grain. And in the seventh year, we harvest excellent clover, especially if we take the trouble to plaster it. In the event that onions have no market in the country, in the fifth year we would grow oats with clover. The result would be In the fifth year, a remarkable oat crop; In the sixth year, a superb clover crop that would be turned over; And finally, in the seventh year, without manuring, and with energetic ashing, you'd get an abundant crop of early peas, followed by a good crop of flageolet beans. There is often great profit in producing a large quantity of peas. It's an expensive vegetable when harvested early, and still fetches a high price when sold to canners. Dutch michaux should be grown for the market, and sickle peas for preserves. The most advantageous onion to grow for speculation is the blond vertus. If the soil were a little too siliceous, and artichoke cultivation would not be advantageous, we would create two hectares of asparagus instead of one, and subject the remaining six hectares to a six-year crop rotation, applied as follows: First year, after clearing with a plough or two spades, with very abundant manuring, leafy vegetables such as cabbages, artichokes, leeks, celery, etc., would be grown. Second year, without manuring, with topdressing: carrots, turnips, salsify, potatoes, etc.Third year, depending on local resources: onions for sale, without manure, and on top of topsoil, or on ashes: early peas and then flageolet beans; or, if green beans are valuable: early beans followed by late peas; Fourth year, without manure, oats with clover; Fifth year, flat clover; Sixth year, without manure and on ashes: early peas and harvest of flageolet beans. These two rotations are applicable to the gardens of hospitals, communities, seminaries, boarding houses, and all establishments with a large enough staff to provide a large quantity of fertilizer at no cost. This fertilizer, most often lost, is a source of wealth for general production, and of particular prosperity for the establishment that produces it. The day the managers of such establishments deign to turn their attention to cultivation, they will produce what they want with such resources. They will sell instead of buying, and will therefore have a very clear profit, while helping to increase the general production which, when abundant, brings health and prosperity to all. The distribution of crops, and the two crop rotations I have just described, will give the following silver results, in establishments where they are applied and well managed. 18 SEVEN-YEAR ROTATION ON TEN HECTARES OF LAND. 1° A two-hectare vegetable garden, providing enough food for one hundred people ... ... ... ... . 2° One hectare of asparagus, of which only the first 100 bunches will be sold at 1 fr. 50 c. ... . e . . . . 3° One hectare of artichokes bears 15,000 plants, let's say three thousand are missing, leaving 12,000 plants, giving an average of one large artichoke and two medium-sized ones, all at a minimum price of 25 centimes per plant. 150 12,000 plants at 25 cent... . . . . 3,000 4° One hectare of beets, potatoes and roots, providing abundant food for cows, pigs, poultry, and even an addition of vegetables for the house. . 5° One hectare of oats, providing straw and grain for livestock... 6° One hectare of excellent clover, providing fodder for the animals... 7° Depending on markets and local resources, either an onion crop followed by a green bean crop, or an early pea crop followed by a late pea crop... . 3,150 1 " M 21 n n n Onions, however cheaply sold, yield 2,500 fr. per hectare. The staff and animals are amply supplied with everything; I have not counted the product of the livestock in milk and meat, although this product is still given by our crops which, independently of all these advantages, still bring us, after providing people and animals with everything they need, an income of five thousand five hundred francs in money. It's true that I haven't counted the costs, but neither have I counted the sale of surplus fruit. Consult Arboriculture fruitière, dear reader, and you'll see that the sale of fruit will equal, if not exceed, that of vegetables. Your fertilizers cost you nothing. Labor costs almost nothing. You have to pay a foreman; 10 let's say we give him three thousand francs, which is a lot. The fruit will pay for it, and beyond that, what will you have spent to obtain abundance in everything, and a product of five thousand francs from ten hectares? A little intelligence and knowledge of cultivation, nothing more. You'll object, and I expect you will, that I'm establishing my account on the assumption that I'll have excellent soil to cultivate; now let's see what we'll get from inferior soil, from lighter soil. We have the same fertilizer and labor resources. Artichokes would give sad results in light soil; we'll only grow them in the kitchen garden, for home consumption. We'll replace this crop with asparagus, which loves sand and only fears humidity. Our ten hectares will be divided as follows: Two hectares of vegetable garden, subject to a four-year rotation, which will provide for home consumption. Two hectares of Ulm violet asparagus; and six hectares of mixed vegetable, root, forage and oat crops, in the following rotation: First year, after clearing with a plow or two spades, and with maximum manuring, cabbage and leeks, etc., etc.; Second year, with no plowing, and with maximum manuring, etc.; Second year, without manure and on topsoil, harvest of roots for home and animal consumption; Third year, without manure, harvest of onions 8 on topsoil, or early peas, and runner beans, on ashes; Fourth year, without manure, oats with clover; Fifth year, without manure, plastered clover; Sixth year, without manure and on ashes, harvest of early peas and runner beans. The soil is less suitable for large-scale cultivation, and the less beautiful harvests will deprive us of two head of large livestock, but this same slightly light soil allows us to force the cultivation of vegetables, the most productive of all. Let's estimate the products of this new crop rotation: It gives us annually; 1° On two hectares of vegetable garden, subject to four years of asolement, all the vegetables necessary for the consumption of the house... ... ... ... ... ... ... ... . . 2° Two hectares of early asparagus. One hectare yields a minimum of 4,000 "n bunches of asparagus at 1 fr . . . . . h.000 " I reserve a whole hectare for the house. 3° One hectare of cabbages and leeks yields at least... ... ... . 1,500 " 4° One hectare of roots for the consumption of the house, people and animals. 5° One hectare of onions, beans and peas for household consumption. 6° One hectare of oats for livestock. . . "To be carried forward. 5,500 " To be carried forward... . 5,500 7° One hectare of trèfte for cattle. 8° One hectare of early peas followed by green beans estimated, given the poverty of the soil. 1,000 " Total sales... ... . 6,500 n We have a higher silver product, but two head of cattle less. This difference, which seems abnormal, can be explained by the introduction of asparagus cultivation, the most productive of all, which will give the best results in poor soil. I cannot repeat too often that there is no such thing as bad soil, only bad growers. France, endowed with the richest and most fertile soil, is divided into strong soils and light soils. Each has its own value, and each can yield a high income when properly cultivated. The first step is to study the soil; the second is to understand the needs of the plants you wish to cultivate, and the third is to know how to modify your crops according to the soil and climate; and then you can repeat that proverb, which acquires greater truth as science replaces routine: as much as man is worth, so much is the land worth. There are many large establishments with a large staff but no animals. They don't have enough fresh manure 19 to build layers, but they do have all the elements they need to make masses of compost, with which they can extract rich products from their land. If they want to produce a lot cheaply, they should adopt a four-year rotation with layers, and in this case, buy the manure needed to make the layers every year. The composts will be used to fertilize the squares under the crop rotation system. However, if the establishment were poor, or did not wish to incur the expense of fertilizers necessary for the creation of a good vegetable garden, and had only one or two hectares of land at its disposal, it could still obtain a high yield by adopting the three-year crop rotation, as indicated above, while creating asparagus plants or artichoke squares, depending on the value of the soil. The product of these crops would be sold in large part, and the squares that produced them would be included in the crop rotation, as I also said. If a house with a large staff had one or two horses assigned to the service of the house, and owned only three hectares of land, and wished to obtain a high yield from it without making layers, it would be easy to harvest the vegetables needed for the house, and the food for the horses, by adopting the six-year mixed crop rotation. In this case, it would be necessary to dispense with the pri8 meurs, mix manure with compost and proceed as follows: 1° Reserve the necessary space to create asparagus and artichoke plantations; 2° Divide into six equal parts all that remains of available land, and plant: First year, with ploughing or spading, and very copious manuring, all the vegetables requiring very abundant food: cabbages, leeks, etc., etc., etc.. Second year, without manuring and on top soil, roots: carrots, turnips, etc. Third year, without manuring and on good ashing, dried fruit vegetables. Fourth year: no manure: oats with clover. Fifth year: plastered clover. Sixth year: wheat. The straw would be used for the horses, and the grain would be sold to reimburse part of the costs. In this way, abundant produce could be obtained at almost no cost. Vegetables sown and transplanted in rows can be cultivated with the plough and horse hoe, when labour is at a premium, and with the help of these tools the land would be brought to a remarkably fertile state in a few years, and this in all parts of the farm. Before concluding what relates to the development of hospital, community or boarding house land, let us say that the crops I have mentioned 8 are only possible on soil that has been cleared of trees, and that above all, a special site must be devoted to the fruit garden, if the walls are not sufficient, and even to the orchard, if one is desired, but never leave trees on the land intended for cultivation. Disuises p CO I said earlier that the small farmer would be the first worker of public wealth the day he knew how to cultivate small crops, and I added that horticultural education, so necessary in the countryside, remained confined to the towns, even when serious teaching existed there. These are undeniable truths; everyone knows them, and unfortunately we have not yet been able to remedy this state of affairs, which we urgently need to put an end to in the interests of rural dwellers and general production. The peasant who owns a few acres of land, and cultivates it with his family, will be the first worker of the public fortune, the day he knows how to cultivate, because he will get from his land an income equal to the land value. The peasant is at least as hard-working as the city-dweller, but he knows no more about his own strength than he does about the wealth of his soil. He knows that certain products are bought at a high price in this or that country, but he doesn't know how to cultivate these products, and attributes the wealth of certain countries solely to the quality of the soil, when, more often than not, he possesses soil of superior quality that he doesn't know how to exploit. England and the whole of northern Europe bring us their cash in exchange for our fruit and vegetables, and even then they only find enough to buy on our markets to meet their needs. The fruit and vegetables taken from abroad are produced in the towns, at a high price, and the selling price still gives a large profit to the grower. The countryside can produce in quantity, almost of equal quality, not only at no cost, but also with a huge advantage for its other crops, for the production of wheat, wine and meat. But the farmer who has the soil and strong arms doesn't know how, and can't go and learn in the cities - it would take too much time and cost too much! He can only learn in his village, on Sundays, or during the long winter evenings. The peasant is the bee of our beautiful country of France, just as the city-dweller is its hornet. He loves the Emperor because his name is the emblem of inner peace; he venerates him because he protects agriculture, and in times of need, he would gladly leave his plough to pick up a gun, if social order were threatened. The peasant gives all his sweat to the land, all his blood to his country, without hope or thought of reward; the feeling of duty, of what is true and just, is the only reason he acts. Who were the most valiant soldiers of order during the continual riots of Louis-Philippe's reign? The peasants of the Paris suburbs! The phalanxes of volunteers who, in 1848, came from all over France to fight anarchy were made up of farmers and peasants. Finally, in our most recent elections, it's worth remembering that all those involved with the land voted as one for the candidates of the Empire. Forgive me, dear reader, for this lengthy digression, but I believe I am doing justice in praising the brave people who produce ceaselessly, and rigorously fulfill their duties as heads of families and citizens, without asking for anything or aspiring to anything, and I make it my duty to ask them for a little horticultural instruction, for which they are so grateful when by chance it reaches them. I am convinced that once the teaching of fruit and vegetable growing has penetrated every commune in France, soil production will have doubled. For France, this will mean a considerable increase in the products it is deprived of, products which bring great well-being, and a notable increase in cash through purchases from abroad. This goal can only be achieved by serious teaching, and numerous examples of good crops and advantageous varieties to cultivate. It's impossible for us to give courses in small localities; their budgets don't allow it, and despite all the dedication we put into it, neither our health nor our purse could suffice to accomplish such a work. I have already mentioned the eagerness of the clergy in the Oise department to repeat my lessons, and to propagate them among their parishioners; a word now about what has been done by primary school teachers. When Mr. Vasselle first called me to Hétoménil to give a lesson in arboriculture, he approached Mr. L. Chevreau, Prefect of the Oise department, with the aim of obtaining permission for primary school teachers to give their pupils the day off to attend my lesson. Mr. L. Chevreau is a horticulture enthusiast, so he granted permission not only for the cantons of Crévecœur, Grandviller and Marseille-le-Petit, neighbors of Hétoménil, but also for the neighboring cantons of Beauvais and Noyon, where lessons were also given. Thanks to this measure, the most brilliant results were obtained in less than three years. The teachers rivaled each other in zeal for study and application, and today they teach their pupils a pruning method whose fruitful results they demonstrate to all. In the meantime, the Prefect of Oise has done me the honor of entrusting me with the horticultural teaching at the Beauvais Normal Agricultural Institute, with a view to turning the future primary school teachers of his department into good horticultural teachers. Allow me to express my deep gratitude to Mr. L. Chevreau for all the kindness he has shown me and for the kind words he addressed to me at the end of my lesson in Hétoménil. The thanks of M. le Préfet de l'Oise, and those of the parish priests of Lihus, Hétoménil and N. D. du Hamel, are the dearest rewards I have received. The schoolteachers' gardens in part of the Oise department offer remarkable specimens of fruit and vegetable growing. These two crops are taught to children who will benefit from them, but this is not yet enough. The results obtained in the teachers' gardens have struck the peasants, who are all the more eager to learn as they have seen what can be achieved with reasoned cultivation. Wouldn't it be possible to set up a basic course in arboriculture and market gardening for men in villages with the most capable teachers? This course would be given by the teacher in the evening, in his classroom, and the practical lessons would be given on Sundays in his garden. This teaching, backed up by practical lessons, and confirmed by the results obtained, would be very widely followed, and would be of immense service. I know that teachers have a lot to do, but I also know their zeal when it comes to being useful to their fellow citizens and their country. These courses could easily be set up with the help of a small subsidy from the département or the commune, in all the places whose teachers have taken our courses. As I said before, farmers have everything they need, but they don't produce because they don't know how to use it. If we teach him in his village, he'll apply it immediately, and every week he'll have a certain amount of produce to take to market. The number of buyers will increase in proportion to production, and the wealth of large-scale cultivation will increase in proportion to that of small-scale cultivation, which provides well-tended soil, fodder and fertilizer. To achieve this humanitarian goal, the teacher's garden must be a real school, where he can show everyone the results obtained by good cultivation, and the superiority of good varieties destined to dethrone bad ones. If the garden is enclosed by walls, the walls should be planted with good varieties of table fruit, leaving in front of them a bed 1 meter 50 centimeters wide, so that a double row of uni-lateral cords can be placed at the edge. These walls will be planted in oblique cordons, or in large shapes according to their height. But, above all, the teacher should, as far as possible, do and teach things that are easy, productive and within everyone's reach. If the garden is enclosed by hedges, plant all the way around. Lay out a 2-metre-wide flowerbed, where you can place vertical or oblique counter-trees, or alternating Gressent palmettes, depending on the layout and size of the planting (see Arboriculture fruitière for planting instructions).) The entire middle of the garden, cleared of trees and overgrowth, would be devoted to growing vegetables, and subjected to the four-year crop rotation, or in the absence of sufficient fertilizer, to the three-year rotation, as described on page 60 ff, after levelling and breaking up the soil, as described in chapter IV. Four-year cropping, with layers, should be preferred in all cases, especially for teachers' gardens, which should serve as a model, and in which a certain quantity of vegetable seedlings will be raised, and seeds grown in large quantities. The seedlings and seeds will more than compensate for the expense of the manure needed to make the diapers. I also insist on the cultivation of melons, which always produce a high yield, because nobody knows how to make them in the country, and we prefer to do without them than to bring them from Paris when we're far away. The day good ones are brought to the country markets - and nothing could be easier - they'll be snatched up immediately. The three-year crop rotation, without layers, should only be introduced when it is impossible to obtain fresh manure. Teachers of horticulture can easily obtain, at almost no cost, the manure they need to make their diapers, and the materials they need to make their composts. First and foremost, he will need to organize his fertilizer factory, a double platform with a liquid fertilizer tank in the center, as described on page 73. The older pupils in the class will be excellent helpers for most of the work in the garden; not only will they assist their teacher, but the application of theory will be of the greatest use to them in their horticultural training. Most of the time, the village mayor is a farmer; the schoolteacher is almost always the town hall secretary, and the good relations that develop between the mayor and the town hall secretary are enough to ensure the latter's supply of fresh manure for the diapers. If the mayor isn't a farmer, there will always be one in the country, and he'll gladly trade seedlings or even melons for a cartload of manure. At worst, nothing is expensive in a village, and the schoolteacher would still benefit from buying one or two carts of fresh horse manure, and mixing it with leaves to make diapers. In that case, he'd give up hot diapers, and make only lukewarm, dull ones. There's no substitute for manure and potting soils from layers, and I can't stress enough that harvesting layers pays two or three times the value of manure while improving it. The schoolteacher's compost and liquid fertilizer factory should operate with all the more activity as his garden should be a model garden, and as he has at hand the first elements of fertility. Depending on the country's res12 sources, it will be easy for him to obtain a certain quantity of herbaceous matter that he can animalize with the fertilizers of his class. All he needs to do is set up a mobile pit for the children, or send them to the compost heap, to obtain a quantity of excellent solid and liquid fertilizer that can easily be disinfected with a few kilograms of iron sulfate, which will further increase the energy of the composts. Above all, the teacher must teach children and farmers how to make fertilizer. Nothing should be wasted at home, and he should set the example: fecal matter, manure, pellets, kitchen scraps, house, classroom and yard sweepings, herbaceous matter, soot, ashes, urine, dishwater, soap water, lye, etc., etc., all should be brought together and used to make fertilizer. We need to visit villages to find out how many fertilizing substances are lost to agriculture, while at the same time harming health, and this in countries where fertilizers are totally lacking. Once the example has been set, it will soon be followed, but it is urgent to show it, as much in the interest of general production as in that of public health. The four-year rotation with layers, or the three-year rotation without layers, will be introduced into the schoolteacher's garden using the same procedures as for the homeowner's garden; he will be able to choose whether to break up the soil in one go, or in three or four years, and even to subject only part of his garden to rotation, if he is unable to plant the whole GARDEN in one year, or if he wishes to retain a few related crops. (See chapter VI.) Schoolteachers' gardens vary in size, but however small they may be, they will benefit greatly from regular rotation. This is the basis of production, and the only way to bring the land quickly to a state of cultivation in all parts of the garden, with both four-year and three-year rotations. Teachers who love horticulture, and there are many of them, will easily find, by following my indications, the means to use their garden, and to profit from it for themselves, for their pupils, and for the inhabitants of their country. It would be difficult to specify which varieties of vegetables should be grown in teachers' gardens. The choice depends on the size of the garden, the position of the teacher, the state of progress in their country, and the resources and future it offers for horticulture. All the varieties of vegetables indicated for the gardens of the owner, tenant, farmer, farm, presbytery, etc. are excellent; the teacher will have to choose among them, according to the extent of his garden, and the state of horticulture in his country, both for his personal consumption, and to found his little vegetable school. Obviously, if his garden is very large, he should refrain from growing pole beans, large pumpkins, monstrous cabbages etc. etc., in short all vegetables that take up a lot of space and cast shade on neighboring crops. In this case, the varieties indicated for the tenant's garden will be excellent. If the teacher's garden is large, if he teaches horticulture to his pupils : whether he is giving a course to the villagers or even just giving them advice, and if the country is very arid, he will have to convert his garden into a fruit and vegetable school, introducing on a trial basis the varieties indicated for the owner's garden if he has gardeners from middle-class houses to guide; those of the farmer's garden and the farm, for the supply of the farmer, the small farmer, and for the market, and finally, as an experiment, those indicated for the presbytery garden, which, like the teacher's garden, is destined to become a garden of public utility, by its good cultivation, as much as by the plants and seeds it must provide in regions where good varieties are unknown. I have written this chapter for teachers in advanced departments who teach and practice horticulture. There are many departments where this science is unknown, and where the teacher is content to grow bad potatoes and beans in his garden. It is often easier to introduce progress in a country where everything is unknown, than in one where half-knowledge and prejudices are real obstacles to logical teaching. In this case, the teacher will benefit greatly from cultivating his own garden. He will have to start with the three-year rotation without layers, and grow the varieties of vegetables indicated for the farm's kitchen garden. His cultivation, as well as his vegetables, will attract the attention of private individuals and small-scale growers sooner than he expects, who will soon be asking him for advice, seedlings and seeds. The first step will be taken that day, and the country will be on the road to progress, thanks to the example set by the teacher. Once the three-year rotation, without layers, and the farm's vegetable varieties are accepted, the teacher will be able to adopt the four-year rotation, with layers, in his garden; his work will be followed with interest, and he will soon have many imitators and fervent followers. What does a schoolteacher risk by trying this? Nothing, less than nothing, since the worst he can do is increase his personal well-being! And, if he succeeds completely, which he undoubtedly will, he will have the immense satisfaction of having done his country a great service, by introducing a new industry, an undeniable source of well-being, as well as an element of prosperity. Time and work devoted to the general interest are never wasted; public opinion, a severe and impartial judge, always rewards us with consideration and general esteem, and makes us quickly forget the bruises produced by the stupid struggles and senseless hatreds of blind people who try to momentarily hinder the progress of good people. I have said what I was fortunate enough to obtain in the department of Oise, through M. Vasselle, with the support of M. le préfet de l'Oise, the sympathies of an enlightened clergy, the devotion of intelligent teachers, zealous landowners and amateurs. because in less than six years, routine will be killed off by science in this department, whose prefect, as enlightened as he is devoted to the interests of small-scale farming, has taken steps to ensure that fruit and vegetable growing is taught in every commune by primary school teachers, and I hope to meet in other countries imitators of Mr. Vasselle. Vasselle; prefects who, like M. L. Chevreau, are faithful interpreters of the Emperor's thinking and his concern for agricultural interests, and who will support their impetus; priests, teachers, landowners and amateurs who, while indulging in a pleasant occupation, will serve the great cause of general production. At almost every station on our railroads, there are plots of land not used by the companies, which they temporarily give up to their employees to create gardens. These gardens, which are more or less well cultivated, offer both the employees, who are sometimes poorly paid, a healthy distraction, and a small profit from the vegetable harvest. So why shouldn't these small gardens also produce everything they're capable of, with the help of reasoned cultivation? This is made all the easier by the fact that they are cultivated by the growers themselves, who are eager to get as much out of them as possible, and that we don't have to fear the foolish vanity of certain gardeners, who are often more ignorant, but who reject any improvement because it hurts their self-esteem. Let's put ourselves for a moment in the shoes of a modest railway employee, paid just enough to eat and clothe himself and his wife, and bring up his children, if they are not too numerous, and to whom the Company gives the enjoyment of eight or ten ares of land. This small, well-cultivated garden will give him, and more, not only the supply of vegetables he needs for the whole year, but also the means to support one or two goats and about twenty rabbits. The milk from the goats, the meat from the rabbits, and all the vegetables are a small fortune in a household that has to count by the penny, and it's very easy to acquire all this, and even a small sum of money, by selling the surplus of certain products, devoting to good cultivation the hours left free by the service. The small station garden will be subject to a regular crop rotation; levelled and broken up like the previous ones, to give an assured product. A three-year rotation, without layers, is preferable for the employee who has only wasted hours to spend on his garden, and at his disposal, the fertilizers he can collect and make without any expense. However, if an employee had a garden of some size, at the gateway to a town that offered some resources, and his wife wanted to look after the garden, he could make quite a lot of money from it, by adopting a four-year rotation, with layers, and devoting his layers exclusively to growing melons. He would benefit greatly, I repeat, even if he had to buy a certain amount of manure. The resources of a railroad employee are very limited, I know, but with a little activity, he will easily remedy the insufficiency of his fortune. Let's suppose that a garden such as the one I've just described is to be subjected to a three-year rotation, without layers. The first thing to do would be to gather and make the necessary fertilizers; nothing is easier than making compost at a railway station, or even at a station where horses are always present, and where there is often a lot of straw, hay, etc., etc., etc., etc. All this will be carefully picked up by the women or children, and thrown as it comes onto the heap, in the process of being made, mixed with garden debris and grasses that can easily be cut in the ditches and along the embankments. A certain quantity of locomotive ashes, mixed with these composts, will increase their energy; household water and urine that can easily be collected will provide the liquid fertilizer needed to make composts. In large railway stations, and in all those of low speed, where horses are constantly brought in and animals are taken on board, it will be easy to collect a very large quantity of excellent manure; enough to set aside the best, to make layers by mixing it with leaves, if the employee wants to grow melons and get a silver product from his garden. At the gateway to the big cities, and with a lot of fertilizer, it would be profitable to introduce a four-year crop rotation, with warm and muted layers only, dedicating these layers to the cultivation of melons. In this case, the varieties of vegetables designated for the tenant's garden should be grown, and any surplus can be sold on the market. Crop rotation will be introduced and practiced in the same way as in the owner's garden. For the gardens of small localities, stations, and even for those of gatekeepers, the three-year rotation will be adopted, without layers, and the varieties of vegetables designated for the farm's kitchen garden will be grown. See Part Two and Part Three, General and Special Crops, for instructions on how to care for the garden and each type of vegetable. Digtige /C The introduction of horticultural work into the army can bring the most fruitful results, for the army itself, and for the countries where cultivation will be carried out by our soldiers. I am devoting a chapter to the vegetable garden in the camps, because I count among my most assiduous listeners generals, senior officers and officers of all classes who, I know in advance, will be doubly happy to guide their soldiers in work which, while creating a salutary distraction and real well-being for them, is destined to bring prosperity to regions where horticulture is unknown. The Châlons camp was established on land that had never been considered for cultivation. The presence of an army corps brought enormous resources of fertilizer, and our soldiers immediately created gardens in this land condemned to infertility. These gardens were created under the direction of officers with a love of horticulture, and as soon as the idea was conceived, thousands of arms were offered. If a local man had been offered all the fertilizer in the camp, on condition that he create a one-acre vegetable garden, he would have replied: "That's impossible; this land has never produced anything! The word impossible has been removed from the French army's dictionary. The army is the heart of France! If you doubt it, observe it! If a fire breaks out, you'll see the last conscript rush into the fire like the heroes of Crimea and Italy, exposing his life twenty times over to snatch the last shred of the peasant's household from the flames, and doing so with a heart and spirit that can only be found in France! If you thank this man, or rather this soldier, for his devotion, he looks at you all astonished and seems to say: has this gentleman lost his mind, to compliment me: it's quite natural, what I've done! This conscript is essentially French, he represents the chivalric type of our country; and, without suspecting it, he is quite tall compared to some of today's rich, who measure each of their steps by the profit it will bring them. For them, homeland and honor are empty words: only one has the power to make their ears perk up: money! In our worst days, when certain parasites in the big cities were saying to all the workers: "You alone are sovereign!

saved our crops that were about to rot in the fields for lack of manpower? The army! The army again, happy to put down the musket for a moment and pick up the scythe! If, as an experienced farmer and deep thinker, you dream of a trial of cultivation designed to enrich your country, you will meet with ardent opposition from ignorance, and coalition from routine. You will often see a beautiful discovery, a generous and profitable thought for the country, untried for lack of arms and the goodwill of the first interested parties to make use of your discovery! Don't be discouraged, and call on the army, which will give you as many brave arms as you want, and with its ardor and zeal will execute, in a few hours, what you wouldn't have obtained from well-informed workers, in a few weeks. If you want to see a happy man, put a soldier to work! He'll do whatever you want without ever risking an observation; he'll obey passively and act with zeal and drive whatever you ask of him! Anything is possible with such helpers. The soldier works like four and sings like six, so happy is he to work. Treat him well, and you'll make a friend out of him, a devoted person you can count on any time! The day the army wants to do some serious farming, it will fertilize the globe! The trial at the Châlons camp is a success, and if the same trial were tried 13 in Algeria, it would have the most brilliant results, not only for the army itself, but also in proving to timid French people that to want is to be able. All it would take would be a few examples of good cultivation by the army to attract to Algeria, not applicants for concessions, but purchasers of land who would come and settle there with their families and workers, and very quickly wrest from the Maltese the monopoly of vegetable cultivation so lucrative in these regions, where everything is at hand: fertile soil in the first place, fertilizers thrown into the sea more often than not, where everything comes together to enrich numerous French families, and where all that's lacking are workers, farmers and horticulturists. The entire valley stretching from the maneuvering field to the Hama nursery is cultivated by Maltese, who in December and January load ships with peas, green beans and artichokes to supply the markets of Paris. If, under another reign, when cultivation was considered madness, the army had seized these rich and vast lands and begun rational cultivation, they would have been sold at the weight of gold to the French, who would have enriched their country. Africa will give France more wealth, through the fertility of its soil, the day it counts workers among its inhabitants, than all the gold mines in California could provide. Some settlers say the trapistes are lucky. The Staoueli establishment is formidable; its wealth is unequalled; its success is due not to chance, but to the four first elements of successful cultivation that the Trapist order brings together, namely: capital, manpower and discipline. I visited the province of Algiers, and travelled through much of France. Everywhere, I've found elements of wealth, even in the poorest regions, elements ignored because the resources of cultivation are unknown. Teach culture to the least fortunate countries, and they will become rich! I consider my teaching a priesthood, because my experience of teaching has shown me that everywhere, and in all classes of society, one meets a good number of people devoted to the interests of their country, and who consider it their duty to repeat our lessons, and to show everyone the example of good culture. I was fortunate enough to find fervent followers among landowners, the clergy and primary school teachers, who, as a result of our lessons, are rendering valuable services in several départements. Today, I appeal to the army, which will also contribute to the great work of horticultural instruction, by setting an example in France and in our colonies. It's enough to do and show results, to immediately find a host of imitators. I'm certain that the army will do the job, firstly because it is commanded by distinguished men who are totally devoted to their country, and secondly because it is made up of generous hearts and valiant arms. Let's imagine that, as at the Châlons camp, an army corps spends a few summer months on chalky soil, and tries to fertilize part of it with its fertilizers. I'm convinced that the creation of vegetable gardens, or rather large-scale vegetable cultivation, would be one of the most useful and profitable ways of making chalk soils suitable for large-scale farming. What is needed in soils of this nature to obtain good harvests of oats, rye and even wheat? Fertilizer in large quantities, nothing more! That, at least, is the opinion of the most experienced farmers in Champagne. It's all about saturating the soil with humus to make it fertile. To achieve this goal, we have at our disposal an army corps, and a considerable quantity of horses, which will provide us with human fertilizer and horse manure at our discretion. In addition, we have as many men as we need to create vast vegetable crops which, by preparing the land to produce cereals and fodder, and by increasing its value tenfold, will provide a quantity of excellent vegetables, which will vary the very uniform ordinary of the soldiers, and will copiously supply the officers' table. This result can easily be achieved by the army, at no other expense than the fertilizers it produces, and the arms of a few willing men, with judicious use of fertilizers and well-calculated crop rotation. Let's start with fertilizer management, depending on whether we adopt a five- or six-year crop rotation. The six-year rotation, with layers, will produce, on plot no. 1, a crop of leafy vegetables such as cabbages, artichokes, etc., etc.; on plot no. 2, a crop of roots, lettuces, etc., etc.; on plot no. 3, a crop of leafy vegetables such as cabbages, artichokes, etc., etc.; on plot no. 4, a crop of lettuces, lettuces, etc., etc., etc, etc.; on plot no. 3, a crop of dried fruit vegetables, peas, beans, etc.; on plot no. 4, bearing the layers, a crop of melons, the plan and the necessary seeds; on plot no. 5, a crop of oats, and on plot no. 6, a crop of clover. The five-year rotation, without layers, will yield three vegetable crops like the previous one, one oat and one clover. The four-year rotation, with layers, will be much preferable, in that the layers provide a considerable quantity of topsoil, the first of all riches in a soil that requires a high addition of humus. A crop rotation in which a twelfth of the layers are used will seem impossible to those with no experience of this type of cultivation. These layers, of course, will not be covered with glass frames, but with canvas frames and economical cloches, under which excellent third-season melons are easily grown. Just give our soldiers this idea, and the layers will soon be assembled and the shelters quickly made, and the officers won't complain about the prodigious quantity of melons that will be harvested in the camp. It's worth repeating here that diapers, far from costing money, are profitable, and that the manure used to make them is better for burial than that which is not decomposed. What's more, the layers provide a considerable quantity of potting soil, which is essential for successful sowing and transplanting, and whose action will be most energetic in chalky soils. Let's assume that we want to fertilize six hectares of chalky soil, which has not yet produced anything, with the resources of a camp and the arms of soldiers, and that we want to subject these six hectares to a six-year crop rotation to speed things up. First of all, we'll organize our fertilizer department to fertilize six hectares at a time, so as to establish our crop rotation in one go, which is possible with large quantities of fertilizer. First, we'll stockpile about fifteen hundred meters of horse manure to build up the layers; if we can get hold of a certain quantity of leaves, a thousand meters will suffice. Then, on an inclined platform equipped with a liquid fertilizer pit (pl. 3, fig. A), we'll make a large quantity of compost with all the debris from the gardens and camps: herbaceous matter, vegetable scraps, savarts, ditch grasses, etc., etc., mixed with faecal matter, all watered with the liquid manure from the stables and the urine. Ashes and soot from the camp will be mixed with composts, cow, pig and poultry manures, and kitchen scraps. 8 Every effort will be made to increase the production of composts and liquid fertilizers, which will be disinfected with iron sulfate as they are produced. Our six hectares are divided into six equal parts of one hectare each, called squares n° 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6. Our reserve of horse manure will be used to build the warm layers on half of square n. h. This half of the square will produce melons, early vegetables, seedlings and the planting required for the four hectares of vegetables. The other half of plot no. 4 will produce a crop of pumpkins for human consumption, and if necessary to feed a few cows. These pumpkins, raised on a bed, will be planted when frosts are no longer to be feared, in the middle of a hole filled with a wheelbarrow of manure, covered with 20 centimetres of compost. The following year, pumpkin manure only will be spread over the whole of square n° h, which will also be ashed and watered with liquid fertilizer, and in the second year an early pea crop will be grown, followed by a bean crop. The first year, square n° 2 will receive five hundred cubic meters of the most consumed composts; carrots, onions, turnips and, in the last season, some green beans will be grown. The following year, square 2 will be sown with oats and clover. Plot no. 3 will receive two hundred cubic meters of high-consumption compost and ashes; in the first year, early peas will be grown, followed by a crop of beans. The following year, plot no. 3 will receive 1,000 cubic meters of the most active composts; all vegetables requiring abundant nourishment, such as cabbages, artichokes, leeks, potatoes, salads, etc., etc., will be grown here. The first year, plot no. 1 will receive 1,000 cubic meters of compost, the least consumed, to grow artichokes, cabbages, leeks, potatoes, etc., etc., etc. The following year, square No. 1 will be ploughed up with some of the layer debris, and will produce carrots, turnips, onions, etc., etc... The two squares Nos. 5 and 6 will each receive one hundred cubic meters of compost, and will both be sown with oats; clover will be sown with the oats in square No. 6 only. The following year, layers will be established on plot no. 5, and plot no. 6 will yield good clover. Crop rotation summary: SQUARE No. 1. 1st year. On a thousand meters of compost: cabbages, artichokes, potatoes, leeks, etc., etc. 2nd year. On topsoil: carrots, turnips, onions, lettuce, etc. 3rd year. Without manure: oats with clover. he year. Without manure: clover. 5th year. Layers; early vegetables, seedlings and pumpkins. 6th year. On pumpkin manure, liquid fertilizer and ash; early pea harvest, followed by a bean harvest. No manure: clover. 6th year. Layers: early vegetables, seedlings and pumpkins. SQUARE No. A. 1st year. Layers: early crops, seedlings, pumpkins. 2nd year. With pumpkin manuring and ashing: early peas, followed by beans. 5th year. On a thousand meters of compost: artichokes, cabbages, leeks, etc. 6th year. On topsoil: carrots, turnips, onions, etc. I chose this rotation for the camp vegetable garden to achieve two goals of great importance to me. 1° To use the fertilizers of the camps and the work of the soldiers to provide well-being for the army, from the staff, who will consume the melons and early vegetables, to the last soldier, who will be able to augment his very limited diet with excellent vegetables of all kinds. This well-being will be further enhanced by the presence of a few cows, which will easily live in the camp with the fodder, pumpkins and vegetable scraps, and provide milk and butter every day. This rotation also makes it easy to raise and fatten a number of poultry, while providing the State with a sole of excellent oats every year. 2° To fertilize and develop abandoned land, which has never been used for general production, an obvious way of increasing public wealth and that of the regions where the army will set an example. Far be it from me to pretend that my assolelement is the best of all; it will be modified, and I would probably modify it myself if I were on the spot, from the dual point of view of the army's needs and the added value of the land. I'm setting a milestone today, nothing more, and I'm setting it by appealing to our beautiful and valiant army, because I know that noble minds, happy to second the Emperor's thought, will gather around, and seek with me the solution to the problem I'm posing: material well-being for the army; progress in regions deprived of education, and an increase in public wealth. What expense will a trial entail? Zero! The camp supplies a considerable quantity of fertilizer, some of which is wasted. Compost production alone would be more than enough to bring a good number of hectares into cultivation. What's needed to achieve this goal? The army offers you thousands of hardy men just waiting to take action? Not only does the army offer its arms to the cause of public fortune, but it also contains among its officers elite intelligence which will be happy to contribute to a work of general utility. Nothing is more contagious than example. Tomorrow, let the army produce beautiful crops in lands considered infertile; immediately the peasants who were the first to anathematize these lands will imitate our soldiers, and will seek to obtain the same products there; but for this to happen, the peasant must have seen! The day he has seen, nothing costs him, he does it anyway, and the example set in the most deprived regions is soon followed by fruitful results. Let's not forget that in France, where our soil is so rich, production is not in line with consumption, and that it will hardly be in line with the demands of foreigners who come to take everything away from our most distant markets! If tomorrow, following the example of the army, the lousiest Champagne region produces vegetables in abundance, speculation, always on the lookout, will immediately move to where the production is and quickly exhaust its markets. We have a hundred examples of this, a hundred times we have seen the poorest countries become the richest, the day they produced to satisfy foreign demands. These trials carried out in the camps will cost the State little or nothing. The army provides everything, fertilizer and manpower. It will derive real well-being for itself from its work, and moreover it will give great value to land that had none. I've chosen a six-year rotation, with layers, because after six years of the cultivation I've indicated, the army will derive an unquestionable profit, and the land can easily be given over to cultivation, which will compete for it once it has seen the produce. I admit that the crops only pay for fertilizers and labor, which, far from being a burden, will provide a salutary distraction for our soldiers. I count for nothing the well-being brought to the officers and soldiers by these crops, and at the end of six years the undeniable added value of the land, which has become fertile from the barrenness it was. This added value can be estimated at a minimum of five hundred francs per hectare.
