If the farmer is reluctant to buy after six years, let the army continue with a second rotation of six more years, at the end of which time, the soil will be saturated with fertilizer and fertilized for many years to come; it will be enough to maintain it to obtain the most abundant products, and its added value will be quoted according to its fertility. I've started with a six-year rotation, with layers, with a mixed rotation of garden and field crops, because I'm looking forward to developing land in the worst areas. My aim is to have them fertilized by the army, which has the fertilizers and the manpower to make them suitable for field cultivation as soon as possible, and to enrich the country through the production of the soil. If I were only consulting the army's interest in vegetable production, I would adopt a four-year rotation for the special cultivation of vegetables, and would encourage our soldiers to always cultivate the same land which, after two rotations, at the end of eight years, would be remarkably fertile. The army would save on fertilizer and manpower, but the State would be deprived of land sales that could yield considerable profits. The four-year crop rotation, with layers, would bring significant savings in fertilizer, because from the second year onwards, the product of the demolition of the layers would be sufficient, with very little compost, to fertilize plot no. 1, to top-dress plot no. 2, and the liquid fertilizer would sufficiently fertilize plot no. 3. Cultivation would be easier, more economical and more productive in the second rotation; but the army would not have to show the unbelievers superb oats, magnificent clovers, and if necessary rich sainfoins, and good alfalfa on soils long condemned. The State, for its part, would be deprived of these harvests and of the ability to sell, at a high profit, land fertilized by six years of cultivation. It seems to me that it would be more profitable for the State to sell land that has been brought into a state of cultivation after six years, and to fertilize other land that will be sold in turn, six years later, with fertilizers and manpower that cost the State nothing. If the complication of the layers seemed too great for a large-scale trial, we could adopt a five-year rotation, without layers. The land would not be brought into cultivation as quickly, but the results would still be highly profitable, and the added value of the soil would be considerable after five years. In this case, you would have to divide the five soles into five equal parts, mix the horse manure with herbaceous matter and debris of all kinds, turn the whole thing two or three times to decompose the manure before burying it, and thus proceed to cultivate five hectares. SQUARE N° 1. - First year: one thousand cubic meters of manure for artichokes, cabbages, leeks, lettuces, potatoes, etc. Second year: simple topdressing for carrots, onions, turnips, lettuces, green beans, etc., etc., etc. Third year: harvest of early peas, followed by beans on ashes and watering with liquid fertilizer. Fourth year, without fertilizer: oats with clover. Fifth year: clover. SQUARE No. 2 would receive, in the first year, a manuring of five hundred meters of highly consumed fertilizer, for carrots, turnips, onions, etc., etc..., the second year, watering with liquid fertilizer and ashing for early peas followed by beans; the third year, without manuring, oats with clover; the fourth, clover; and the fifth, one thousand meters of manure for artichokes, cabbages, leeks, potatoes, lettuces, etc. LE CARRÉ N° 3 will receive, in the first year, two hundred meters of highly consumed fertilizer for early peas followed by beans; in the second year, without manure, oats with clover; in the third, plastered clover; in the fourth, one thousand meters of manure for artichokes, cabbages, etc.; in the fifth, carrots, turnips, onions, etc., on top of topsoil. LE CARRÉ N° h will receive, the first year, one hundred meters of fertilizer, for oats; the second, one thousand meters of manure for artichokes, cabbages, etc.; the third, on topsoil, carrots, turnips, onions, etc.; the fourth, on ashes and liquid fertilizer, early peas, followed by beans, and the fifth, without manure, fodder. SQUARE N° 5 will receive, in the first year, one hundred meters of manure for oats and clover; in the second, clover plaster; in the third, one thousand meters of manure, for artichokes, cabbage, etc.; in the fourth, on soil, carrots, turnips, onions, etc.; in the fifth, on ashes and watered with liquid fertilizer, early peas, followed by beans. If, finally, we wanted to grow only vegetables, without layers, we would have to adopt the three-year crop rotation as described in the chapter on crop rotation. The deprivation of layers leads to the deprivation of topsoil, the greatest resource for vegetable crops, and for rapidly saturating humus in land that is deprived of it. I have just put forward some new ideas that my teaching and farming experience authorize me to publish, in the interests of the army, of the backward regions it is called upon to visit, and of general production, the mother of public prosperity. I am delivering my first ideas to the army, in the hope that they will try them out. No one has forgotten the eminent services rendered by M. marshal, duc d'Isly, has rendered to agriculture; as we know, there are marshals of France, eager to continue the work begun by the illustrious marshal Bugeaud; if this book falls into their hands, and if it can determine them to try a few experiments, the author will have received his dearest reward by contributing, for his modest share, to bringing a little well-being to our valiant soldiers, a little ease to our hard-working peasants, an obolus to the public treasury! Before concluding on the subject of camp vegetable gardens, let us say that the choice of vegetable varieties to be cultivated is of great importance, as the early and esteemed varieties are virtually unknown in countries foreign to the science of horticulture. For the six-year crop rotation and for the five-year rotation, use the varieties indicated for the tenant's, farmer's and farm gardens, choosing the most profitable and hardy. Large-scale cultivation of Argenteuil asparagus at the Châlons camp would produce brilliant results, and would be a real asset to introduce into the country. See part three, special crops, asparagus. See general crops and special crops, first, second and third parts, for the choice of vegetable varieties, the care to be given to each and the place they should occupy in the crop rotation. SayS C CHAPTER IeS OF INSTRUMENTS AND TOOLS TO BE USED. In the interest of homeowners, I thought I should indicate and draw the instruments and tools that are essential for vegetable gardening. In the Paris area, where the rantings of manufacturers who only know about fields what they have seen in books abound, the amateur is exposed to buying impossible tools every day on the merchant's assertion. In certain regions, on the other hand, where horticulture is still in its primitive state, tools are so crudely made that it is almost impossible to use them to cultivate the soil properly. Digiized by The essential tools are : The spade, the first and most useful of all; but the spade capable of good ploughing, wide enough and long enough to move earth, and not a short, thick iron plate, incapable of penetrating even slightly consistent soil, or a child's spade as big as a fire shovel, with which one ploughs to a depth of 20 centimetres, and still, we see in countries where such tools are used, men resting every ten minutes from the fatigue they could have given themselves. The spade (pl. 4, fig. 5) must be well soaked, 32 centimetres long, 23 centimetres wide at the top and 19 centimetres at the bottom. It should be a little hollow in the middle, to penetrate the soil more easily (a, pl. 4, fig. 6), and be fitted with a solid socket (b, same figure), to accommodate a good fresne handle, turned, and ending in a ball on which to rest the right hand (a, pl. 4, fig. 5.) Never buy ready-made spades from an ironmonger; they cost barely a franc and are worth nothing. They should be ordered from a good taillandier; there are some in every country, and they can make excellent tools with the indications and design I've given. In addition, spades, like all tools that fit into an iron socket, should be hot-hammered, to avoid the inconvenience of seeing your men spend half their day lining the handles of their spades with rags or bits of leather, and perforating them with nails, which doesn't prevent the blade from leaving the handle every half-hour. All sharpeners know how to hot-hammer, and so must the craftsman: The point of the handle is trimmed so that it doesn't go all the way into the socket; it is wetted so that it doesn't burn, then the socket is heated almost to red-brown; the handle is inserted into it and forced in by striking two or three blows on a stone with the head, then the whole thing is immediately immersed in cold water to tighten the iron, which expanded when it was hot. A spade handled in this way needs no nail, and can never be dismantled. The length of the handle, including the socket, should be between 95 cm and 1.4 cm, depending on the height of the worker. THE FLAT-TEETEN FORK (pl. A, fig. 7), is very expeditious for ploughing in heavy soil, and indispensable for all ploughing in clumps or near trees. This excellent tool, too little used, does not tire men in heavy soils, never cuts roots in beds where there are fruit trees, and is very useful in the vegetable garden for pulling up roots. THE PIOCHE (pl. 4, fig. 8), indispensable for ripping, is an essential tool with: THE WOODEN SHOVEL (pl. A, fig. 9), iron-tipped at the end (a, same fig.), for scooping up soil in rips, stones and garbage in the garden. THE MUMMING FORK (pl. A, fig. 10), indispensable for loading wheelbarrows and carriages, and raising layers. THE CROCHET (pl. A, fig. 11), very expedient for unloading manure wagons and demolishing layers. THE BINETTE (pl. A, fig. 12), for hoeing between rows. THE SHOOTING RATISSOIRE (pl. A, fig. 13), very convenient for cleaning alleys. PUSHING TOOL (pl. 4, fig. 14), very expedient for hoeing beds. THE HOOK SERFOUETTE (pl. h, fig. 16), a valuable tool for small-scale hoeing and harrowing. THE POINTE SERFOUETTE (pl. A, fig. 17), very convenient for tracing radii, sowing peas and edging. THE SARCLOIR (pl. 4, fig. 18), very expedient for purging beds and flowerbeds of weeds. THE CROSS FORK (pl. 5, fig. 1), excellent for pulverizing clods after ploughing. THE DEGROSSING RAKE (pl. 5, fig. 2), for harrowing beds and raking paths. THE FINE RAKE (pl. 5, fig. 3), for raking after sowing and covering seeds. THE PLANTOIR (pl. 5, fig. 4), a pointed piece of wood bent at the handle for transplanting. LE DÉPLANTOIR (pl. 5, fig. 5), for removing plants with the root ball. THE ROPE, a rope 70 to 80 meters long, about the size of a little finger, fixed at each end to a stake. BROUETTE for carrying manure and other heavy items. WATERERS. Despite all the models that have been invented and produced over the years, the best of all are the antique market gardeners' watering cans with their wide necks, large heads and very fine holes (pl. 5, fig. 6). These tools are essential for good cultivation in the vegetable garden, and can be made inexpensively by a good gardener. There are others, useful for the greenhouse, but I'll refrain from using them, as I don't want to go beyond vegetable growing. Digitized be The following can be used to make layers: 1° Horse, donkey and mule manure; these give the strongest heat, but it doesn't last very long. These manures should be used fresh, i.e. before they have fermented. When horse manure has to be stored to make layers, it should be left to dry out a little before being piled, and care should be taken to place several poles across it from distance to distance, so as to aerate the center and prevent it from fermenting. Horse, donkey and mule manure are particularly suitable for making warm layers. The best manure is that of inns, which receive many whole horses, and save on bedding; 2° Cow manure, whose fermentation is less turbulent than that of horse manure, but lasts longer. Cow manure is excellent for making warm layers, mixed with a little horse manure, and for making soft layers, mixed with leaves and herbaceous matter. 3° Rabbit and pig manure, mixed with grasses for muted diapers only. 4° Wool waste, which ferments very quickly, gives very strong heat, and can become a valuable resource, mixed with leaves, in the absence of fresh manure. 5° Leaves collected from parks and woods. They should be used before fermentation; they should be picked up as soon as they have fallen, left to dry and stored in a sheltered place. The leaves ferment gently and retain their heat for a very long time. They make the best warm layers when mixed with horse manure, in that they retain the heat developed by the manure for a long time. Leaves are also very valuable for warm layers, where they can be used in the proportion of two-thirds with one-third horse manure. 6° Moss can also be used to make layers; it is less active than leaves, but its heat lasts quite a long time, and excellent warm and muted layers can be made with moss and manure; this is very economical in the vicinity of woods. 7° Freshly cut grass clippings and grasses, mixed with a little manure, can also be used to make 14 muted layers on which to grow excellent melons. Finally, with a little intelligence and activity, nothing is easier than making a fairly large number of layers with great economy, and obtaining very satisfactory results. If leaves, moss and even grasses, which cost nothing in the vicinity of the woods, are used in very large proportions in the manufacture of diapers, you'll have to make do with warm, muted diapers, which will produce products just as good as warm diapers, but a month or six weeks later. Of course, this is better than doing without early fruits and melons, which are always a pleasure, especially when they cost nothing. Layers with a high proportion of leaves, moss and grasses provide excellent manure and good quality potting soil, ten times the value of labor for field crops. Whatever the resources of a house in fertilizers, it is always advantageous to make a large quantity of layers. There's almost always a shortage of early vegetables and melons in country homes; we're forced to bring them in from Paris, which is expensive, especially when the cost of shipping and baskets is added on. The garden, I can't repeat enough, must provide all this with its beds, and provide it all the more since beds are the primary cause of fertility in open-ground crops, whose yield is doubled with the manures and potting soils of the beds. If the house is rich in horse and cow manure, and there is the possibility of obtaining a few cars of leaves, the owner has the right to demand all the primeurs possible; the large quantity of layers will provide him with a mass of decomposed manure and topsoil that will also double the product of open-ground crops. If, on the other hand, the house offers few fertilizer resources, you need to redouble your efforts to gather together everything you can economically obtain to make the layers: leaves, moss, scraps of wool from factories, etc., which you can mix with the fertilizer you need, These will produce melons and small early vegetables six weeks later than hot layers, but they will produce them at discretion, and will provide the manure and potting soil scraps that are essential for putting open-ground crops on a sound footing. On the contrary, it's when manure resources are limited that you need to increase the number of beds, by all possible means, instead of giving up, letting everything that can be collected go to waste, and spending the winter by the fire or poaching in the park and woods, as too many gardeners do, on the pretext that they lack manure. These same gardeners bury the little manure they have in the spring, condemning their gardens to infertility through lack of soil and decomposed fertilizers, whereas the park and woods can provide them with excellent ones in superabundant quantities. WARM LAYERS. These are raised from January to March for seedlings, and to raise the first melons. However, if the plants to be raised in these layers are destined to remain there for some time, it is urgent to mix a third of leaves with the fresh horse manure, in order to maintain a more sustained heat. Leaves can be added in the proportion of half to the warm layers when horse manure is in short supply, and excellent results can still be obtained by activating the layers with good stoves. When assembling warm and hot layers, it is urgent to place several parallel lines, to give the stoves more action and to be able to heat them at will with less manure. Here's how to set up hot layers: After calculating how much land you can occupy with the manure you have available, divide the land into parallel planks, 1 m. 20c. wide, to lay one-meter-wide frames, and separate these planks by 50-centimeter paths (pl. 5, fig. 7), then drive stakes at points (a, same figure) at each corner, then place a chalk line on these stakes to set up each layer with great regularity. The horse manure used to make these layers was stored dry in a millstone. The leaves that were collected were also kept dry to prevent them from fermenting. Before carting the manure and leaves to the place where the layers are to be made, the manure must be mixed with the leaves, and the whole must be moistened, i.e. as the manure pile is broken up, mix together the long and the short; that which is consumed with the most straw, divide the dung patches, and mix them with the longest straw; amalgamate the necessary quantity of leaves with this manure, taking care to remove any stones or foreign bodies that may be present, and then water the whole thing in small parts with the head of the watering can, so that everything is evenly moistened. I insist on these details because gardeners, unaccustomed to making layers, neglect this little care, and spend, to make bad layers, twice as much manure as it would take to obtain excellent ones, by going to a little more trouble. When the manure has been mixed with the leaves, and sufficiently watered so that all parts are wet, it is carried to the place where the layers are to be set up, and unloaded in heaps at points (b, same figure), on the site to be occupied by the layers, and not in the alleys, which you spoil while losing a certain quantity of precious fertilizer. Next, manure is taken from each pile in small fonrchées and laid flat on the first plank, taking care to press each fourchée with the back of the fork, so as to d̀ form a very-even bed of manure and above all without cavities. We start at point c and work backwards to the other end at point d. We thus place a bed of manure about 20 cent. thick along the entire length of the layer, then we tread it well with our feet, or rather with our hooves, the only shoe suitable for treading a bed of manure so as to form a homogeneous paste. Pour in a few more watering cans as you tread, and the manure will settle better, ensuring that all parts are sufficiently moistened. However, although it is urgent to wet the manure well to obtain even and sustained fermentation, you must be careful not to drown it, as manure that is too wet rots without giving off heat. When the first bed is well trodden and sufficiently wet, a second is built up in the same way, trodden and wet, then a third and fourth are built up in the same way, taking care to trod and wet each bed, until the layer is about 80 centimetres high. Great care must be taken in assembling the layer. If the manure has been well placed in small bunches and well pressed down with the back of the fork, it will be as even as a driveway, and its sides as straight as a wall. A layer made in this way gives off a lot of heat and retains it for a long time. If, on the other hand, the manure has been thrown carelessly and in large quantities onto the layer, it will be full of cavities, all bumpy, the sides will not be solid; such a layer will give a transient and disordered heat in some places, and will never heat up in others. Plants will both burn and freeze. I insist on these details, which may seem puerile to those who see things too superficially, because experience has shown me that they are the first elements of success, and I know from experience the tendency of many gardeners to neglect the making of layers. They usually have their helpers do it, pouring in the manure by wheelbarrows, by hottées at a time, and shaking it with the fork as if they were making bedding for a horse. This way of operating is deplorable, and cannot produce serious results. When the layer has been raised to the required height, we line it all around with a bit of long manure, folded in half, to hold down the compost (a, pl. 6, fig. 1), cover it with about ten centimetres of compost; we place the boxes and fill them with twelve to fifteen centimetres of compost, mixed with half good soil, then we place the frames which we immediately cover with straw mats to facilitate fermentation. Eight or ten days later, when the layer has begun to catch fire, it's safe to sow or transplant. When the layers start to lose their heat, or the temperature drops significantly, we apply rechauds to them, an operation which consists in filling all the paths separating them with fresh manure, which we wet to make it develop all its heat. More or less manure is added, depending on the temperature and the degree of heat in the layers. During frosts, the paths are filled up to the level of the frames. When the stoves don't need very much heat, you can use horse manure mixed with cow dung and leaves, and after a while give these stoves a new action by handling them and adding a little fresh manure from the stable. This reshuffling, with the addition of a little fresh manure and a few watering cans, is enough to give the layers a new heat that lasts quite a long time. If fresh manure is in short supply and wool waste is available, mix it in the proportion of a quarter with leaves, and you'll obtain very active stoves, while improving the wool waste, which is more suitable for burial after initial fermentation. For the construction of the layers, as for the application of the stoves, a certain intelligence is required on the part of the grower, because here, as in all operations, a fair appreciation on the part of the operator is needed to achieve good results. Let's not forget that gardening is one of the sciences that requires the most intelligence, and is perhaps the most hostile to routine. In the provinces, gardeners are often full of goodwill and intelligence, but they don't know how. It's urgent that the owner guide their first steps in a way that's completely new to them. It won't be long before they get the hang of it, but it's necessary, I repeat, to guide them the first year; the second they'll go their own way. The most important thing is to get them to achieve a result that is unknown to them; the first success animates them with the sacred fire, and the conversion is done more quickly than the owner would have expected, when he has given the benefit of his knowledge to a good and devoted servant who only wants to stay with his master and serve him faithfully. If you run out of manure, you can still make excellent warm layers with the manure used for the hot layers. When they have yielded a harvest, they are demolished, and with the least decomposed manure mixed with new leaves and a little fresh horse manure, excellent warm layers can still be made. HEATED BEDS are made in the same way and with the same care as warm beds, but with the difference that they are made in March, using less active manure, more leaves and moss where leaves are lacking. You can build very good warm layers with cow, pig and rabbit manure, mixed with half leaves or a quarter of leaves and moss, but the whole must be perfectly amalgamated to give good results. A third of horse manure, a third of leaves and a third of moss, well mixed, gives a very good layer on which to grow the best melons, starting only at the end of March or in the first days of April, to harvest them in July. A fifth of wool waste, mixed with two-fifths of leaves and two-fifths of moss, produces much the same result. I give all these means, tried and tested by me for many years, because I want to encourage owners to make layers. The day when a large number of layers made of whatever material have been established, all the gardens that have received them, rich in fertilizers and potting soils, will double their harvest in the open ground. The warm layers will be set up like the hot layers, in beds 1 m, 20 cent. wide, separated by 50 cent. aisles, in which stoves can be placed, In this case, these layers will only be edged at the ridge, like the hot layers. However, if the warm layers are erected late enough to dispense with stoves, in April for example, they should be edged all around from base to top with manure folded in half, to give them greater solidity (pl. 6, fig 2). Then cover them with potting soil mixed with half good, well-purified soil, to a thickness of 20 to 25 cent. All layers, without exception, should be oriented east to west, and the potting mix covering them should slope slightly to the south, to give the sun's rays a better grip. This rule must be observed in all cases, whether the bed is covered with frames or cloches, or even with low-cost shelters. THE SOURDE LAYER is the ultimate expression, the supreme means of artificial cultivation. Those who prefer to heat all winter, waste ten cars of good horse manure, and lose twenty cars of leaves without obtaining a satisfactory product in their garden, will cry impossibility. Let them shout, let us demonstrate the usefulness of deaf layers, and let us act to first obtain products of which we are deprived, and prove to idlers that with good will and a little courage you can get what you want. The muted layers go up at the end of March or in the first days of April, when the frosts are no longer more than accidental. They are laid in parallel trenches, 1 m. 20 cent. wide, 40 cent. deep, and separated by 50 cent. aisles. The soil from the trenches is kept in reserve; some of it will be used to cover the layers, after being mixed with potting soil, or if there is no potting soil, with horse dung, as I mentioned on pages 96 and 97. It doesn't matter which fermentable matter you use, as long as it gives off a little heat when mixed with manure of any kind, and produces potting soil, the vegetable garden's first and indispensable asset. So, with a third of horse or cow manure, mixed with pig and rabbit dung, and two thirds of leaves, mosses, tender stems of broom, gorse, reed heather, or even two thirds of grass clippings or grasses cut in ditches, on paths or pond edges, we'll still make good muted layers on which we can put melons in place, under cloches and even under economic shelters, in the first days of April. These layers will be a precious resource for delicate sowing and transplanting of frost-sensitive plants, tomatoes, cucumbers, etc., etc., and will provide us with assimilable manure and potting soil for open-ground crops. The mixture of manure and herbaceous matter to be used should be as complete as that of horse manure and leaves, for warm and hot layers. The whole mixture should be wetted and transported, mixed and moistened, to the edge of the first trench, at the bottom of which a first 20-centimeter-thick bed should be established with the same care as for the other layers; it should be compacted and wetted as I said above; then a second bed should be added, followed by a third that will be about 20 centimeters above ground level. Only the last bed should be edged, then covered with compost. If the owner is deprived of manure and can only establish muffled beds, he can maintain their warmth for quite a long time by applying a half-heater. In this case, the alleyways are emptied to the depth of the trench as the layers begin to cool, then filled with a mixture of leaves and fresh manure to the height of the potting soil. The fermentation that immediately takes place in this mixture of manure and wet leaves imparts a new warmth to the layers, a warmth that can be further prolonged by handling the stove from time to time, and adding a little fresh, watered manure. You can achieve complete success in the cultivation of pumpkins, cucumbers and even Arckangel melon by making a round hole, one metre in diameter and 40 centimetres deep, and filling it with one third any kind of manure mixed with two thirds leaves, moss or herbaceous matter, well trodden and sufficiently moistened, and covering the whole with 25 centimetres of good soil, mixed with half compost or horse dung. Nothing is easier than making melons in the last hamlet. Soon, I hope, the parish priests and schoolteachers will set an example which, if followed by the peasants, as I have no doubt they will, will not only bring them a certain well-being, but also a notable increase in their open-ground crops, through the addition of fertilizers and soil from the most modest layers. My aim is to produce fertilizer in every possible way! By making 15 fertilizer from layers, no matter how bad they are, mountains of wasted material will first give a highly-remunerative product to the intelligent grower, and will then bring a very-notable share of fertility to large-scale cultivation, the first and only true and indisputable wealth of our beautiful country of France. Chassis boxes should be made of white wood, as described on page 133. The best and most economical frames are those made of iron, which never need repair, never warp, close tightly and cost little more than wooden frames. When double-glazed, they're a piece of furniture you can't see the end of. These heavy, high, pointed cloches, made of waste glass and with no grip, should be banned from the vegetable garden (pl. 6, fig. 3). The inferior quality of the glass makes them darker for plants, and as their shape offers no grip, precious time is wasted removing and replacing them, breaking many of them in the process. The cloche à bouton (pl. 6, fig. 4) should be preferred. It's easier to handle, saves time and breaks less; what's more, the higher-quality glass gives more light to the plants. Finally, if you don't have glass (frames and bells), and can't make hot layers, you can stick to warm, muted layers covered with economic frames and bells as I said on pages 133 and 134, and two months later, obtain the same products as on hot layers covered with glass frames and bells. The glass in the frames and cloches must always be very clear. Light is essential for plants, so give them as much as possible, and avoid daubing them with white, which is very convenient for lazy people, but very harmful to plants. When the sun is too hot, give it some air, and shade with canvas or a bit of straw. For all crops grown under frames and cloches, you should also provide air as often as possible, to fortify the plants. By providing a large amount of air and light, you'll always obtain hardy, well-built and highly fertile plants. With a little intelligence and a lot of hard work, nothing is impossible. The example I've set will be quickly followed. The field is open, and I confidently await the trials and soon the results! LAYING OUT THE BEDS. The aim of cultivation is to: 1° to expose as much of the soil as possible to the influence of atmospheric agents, so that it can disintegrate, and at the same time become impregnated with the gases contained in the atmosphere; 2° to loosen the soil to a certain depth, so that the roots can spread out freely; 3° to make the soil permeable to water and air, so as to keep it fresh, without excess moisture, and to allow oxygen gas to decompose the fertilizers it contains; 4° to destroy weeds, i.e. all plants foreign to the crop, which choke good plants, and absorb the fertilizers intended for them to no avail. The means employed to achieve these various aims in the vegetable garden are: ploughing, harrowing with a hooked fork, and hoeing. Ploughing is a very energetic means of loosening soil, but it needs to be done deeply enough, and in good time, to give all the desired results. Ploughing should be increased in clay soils, which are naturally compact and moist. In such soils, it is urgent to do a rough ploughing in autumn, to expose the clods to the influence of frosts, which pulverize them completely; then, in spring, when the soil is healthy, a second, deeper ploughing is done, making the soil very loose. Compacted soils should not be ploughed when they are very wet. In this case, each spade stroke forms a brick, and you risk making the soil infertile for several years. Strong soils should only be worked when they are healthy, i.e. neither too dry nor too wet. Spring ploughing is sufficient in soils of average consistency and in siliceous soils. But this ploughing must be done in good conditions, and at a sufficient depth, like the second ploughing, the spring ploughing, done in strong soils. Many professional gardeners will shrug their shoulders when they hear us giving them a lesson in ploughing; they know their trade and have done nothing else all their lives, they say. True, but we wouldn't want the work of these practical heroes in our nurseries. Ploughing is generally regarded as a mechanical operation, usually left to day labourers who have no idea what good ploughing is, or when it is done by the gardener, he is too eager to do it properly. Day laborers, especially hard-working peasants, plow the vegetable garden as they would the plains; they remove huge spades of earth and simply turn them over, which produces a large, hard clod and makes the soil impermeable to air and water. This is the worst of all ploughing; twenty energetic harrows are powerless to loosen the soil, when such work is done in strong earth. Gardeners break up the clods, but they use spades so small and so short that the depth of their plowing rarely exceeds twenty centimeters. The surface is even and well loosened, but the ploughing is so shallow that the slightest heat immediately dries out the soil. Plants grow well at first, but as soon as they acquire a certain strength, the roots can no longer find loose soil to spread out on, and vegetation stops for no apparent reason. Ploughing should be done as deeply as possible; the spade should penetrate the soil vertically, to its full height, to give a ploughing depth of at least 30 centimetres. But to do this, you need to use real spades, as I've described them, and not imitation fire shovels. You need to take little soil at a time, about 15 centimetres thick, turn it over and pulverize it well. In this way, you can move just as quickly as you would to remove masses of earth that form huge clods; the worker has less trouble, and ploughs in a way that contributes almost as much as fertilizer to activating vegetation, instead of damaging the earth with twice the effort. A word about fertilizer burial, inherent to ploughing. When ploughing to bury manure, the manure is usually placed at intervals at the bottom of the gauge. This operation has several serious drawbacks. The first is that it only very partially fertilizes the bed or square, where the manure forms small serpents under the soil, as shown in fig. 5, pl. 6. All the space occupied by the white parts receives no fertilizer, and the plants placed on these spaces benefit nothing from the often very abundant manuring. The second disadvantage is no less serious: the manure is buried too deep for the roots to reach. It's only in the second year, when the soil is ploughed and turned over again, that the manure, completely decomposed, is mixed with the soil occupied by the roots. Then it's a "terreautage", nothing more. This is the way to spend huge amounts of fertilizer without achieving the slightest success. We must never forget that plants can't fetch their food like animals, and that this food must be within reach of the roots to encourage their development. When smoking, start by spreading manure evenly over the soil before ploughing, so as to put an equal quantity everywhere, and reserve enough to mix with the soil set aside to plug the gauge. Before starting ploughing, open a fairly wide gauge, as indicated by line A, pl. 6, fig. 6, dig vertically B, same fig.; set the gauge at an angle C, and, after throwing a shovelful of earth over the whole length of the gauge, place a little manure at an angle D, same figure, at a depth of 10 centimetres. In this way, it is impossible for a plant, placed vertically in the ground, not to receive its share of manure, as shown by the straight lines F. I said to place the manure at a depth of 10 centimetres, and here's why: plants, as we know, absorb fertilizers mainly in a liquid state. In part A of figure 7, what will happen to the liquid parts of the fertilizers buried in serpentine form at the bottom of the gauge, at a depth of 30 to 35 centimetres B, same figure? They will be washed away by rainwater or watering in C, where the cabbage roots will never reach, and they will saturate the subsoil with excellent fertilizers without the cabbage D absorbing a single drop. What's more, if it's planted between two mounds of manure, it will only have access to soil that's completely devoid of fertilizer, and even if by chance it's placed immediately above the mound of manure, a few rootlets will scarcely reach it. The manure is wasted, however copious it may be, and the harvest will be virtually nil. Let's move on to part B of the same figure, ploughed and smoked as I've indicated. The cabbage root F, was pricked out in the smoked part, and had it been, even between the intervals E, it is impossible that the root would not have been in full manure immediately. What's more, watering and rainwater completely saturate the E gaps with liquid fertilizer and carry it all the way to the G line, far beyond the root tips. So the whole layer of soil, from H to I, is completely saturated with fertilizer; each rootlet has a large share of it, and cabbage F, abundantly nourished from the day it is planted to the day it is fully grown, will be twice as big as cabbage D, which dying of starvation will take twice as long as its neighbor to form a stunted, hard and bad apple. In addition, cabbage F will still reach double the proportions of cabbage D, with half the expenditure on fertilizers. Whatever little it has, it will benefit from it, while its neighbor will suffer Tantalus' torment. May the reader forgive me for such a long explanation of such a simple thing, but this book is destined to fall into the hands of gardeners, and I have seen so much ploughing done, and so much manure wasted, that my experience obliges me to go into complete detail, so as to be understood by all those who wish to apply and reason. Burying manure is like using sand in the strong soils of the Loire Valley. Farmers use it with good reason, but instead of mixing the sand with the soil or with fertilizer, which would be even better, to divide compact soil and make it permeable, they place the sand on top of the soil. Quite apart from its inefficiency, its white color repels the sun's rays, and a soil that is already naturally cold cannot warm up. The result is an enormous delay in harvesting, whereas a remarkably early harvest could have been obtained by mixing sand with fertilizer and burying it instead of spreading it on the soil. The best things applied incorrectly often produce the opposite results to those expected, and the soil is then blamed. Immediately after ploughing, it is always useful, in compact soils, and even in those of average consistency, to give a good harrowing, with the hooked fork, to break up the clods that have escaped the spade; then give a blow with the roughing rake to remove the stones, and get on with erecting the boards. I said earlier that watering was the most expensive part of vegetable gardening. Later on, I'll explain how to save more than two-thirds of the labor, but however cheap water may be, it shouldn't be wasted any more than manure, because it's often scarce and you always have to keep some at your disposal. We organize our beds in such a way that all the water expended benefits the plants. If the ploughed, harrowed and coarsely raked bed is to be planted with seedlings, we'll use the back of the rake to create a rim of h to 5 centimetres all around, to trap water from watering and rainfall in the bed, and prevent it from running off into the paths (pl. 7, fig. 1). If the bed is to be used for transplanting, make a border all around it, but not as high as 2 to 3 centimetres, and use the spiked trowel to trace as many radii 3 to 4 centimetres deep as there will be rows of plants (A pl. 7, fig. 2). This will force water to infiltrate the rays and thus the roots of the plants, and will greatly increase the effect of watering with the same quantity of water. Seedlings require a certain amount of care if they are to give good results; they are of great importance in the vegetable garden, as the season's harvest depends on their success. The gardener must therefore give them his utmost care, and neglect nothing to ensure their prosperity. The best plant is the one you make yourself; you must never rely on your neighbor to provide you with the plant you need; if the neighbor gives you a plant, it's only after you've taken the best for yourself. When the gardener gives to a friend, it's even worse; his self-esteem is at stake, he's delighted to see his friend fail, and he never fails to give him his worst. There are two ways of sowing: broadcasting and rows. Broadcast sowing can be adopted without inconvenience in the vegetable garden, for onions, carrots, turnips, etc., etc., but never for cabbages, lettuces and all plants that need to be transplanted. In such cases, sowing in rows should be adopted exclusively. Germination cannot take place without water, air and heat. Water softens the seed and makes it swell; air acts chemically on the seed's contents, making it suitable for use as primary food; heat accelerates germination. The soil to be sown must therefore have a certain amount of moisture, easily obtained by deep ploughing and watering; it must be very permeable to air, which is obtained by dividing the soil well, using ploughing, harrowing with a hooked fork, and raking, which facilitates the removal of stones and all foreign bodies. In addition, the soil must be abundantly supplied with assimilable, i.e. fully decomposed, fertilizer to provide the young plants with ample nourishment to help them grow rapidly. Potting soil fulfils this condition completely, which is why it is indispensable in the vegetable garden to ensure the success of seedlings and transplanting of delicate plants. In addition, the temperature must be high enough for germination to take place quickly. Insufficient heat is easily remedied by sowing in layers, under trays or cloches, or in the open ground against walls exposed to the south, under shade. Young plants are very tender, and dread too much sun. In summer, seedlings are planted in the shade. When the bed to be broadcast in spring has been deeply ploughed, harrowed with a hooked fork and raked with a roughing rake, it is raked again with a fine rake to remove stones, clods and any foreign matter; then seed is sown, spreading the seed evenly over the whole bed, and covered with a centimetre of loam from the demolition of the layers. This covering contains a high level of humidity, is highly permeable to air, and provides the young plants with abundant nourishment as soon as germination is complete. Many authors recommend lightly beating the soil with the back of a spade or a board, to help the seed adhere to the soil. Some practitioners even go so far as to trample the soil completely with their hooves before sowing. I consider this practice to be more harmful than useful. Threshing, however light it may be, always tends to make the soil impermeable. I did away with it completely several years ago; my seedlings are very beautiful and have never failed. I've carried out several comparative trials, and always the unthreshed seedlings were more vigorous and better yielding than those that had been threshed. With the exception of radishes and perhaps onions, I consider soil threshing to be completely useless, and even then I could limit its use to radishes only, as for onions, its usefulness is very questionable. A single watering is enough to make the seed adhere to the soil, and I prefer it to threshing. It is very rare for a spring sowing, when the drying north-easterly winds prevail almost continuously, to go without watering for the first few weeks. If the weather is wet, the first rain and even the watering can can replace the bat with advantage. Carrots, onions, turnips, etc. are also sown in rows 15 to 18 centimetres apart. I haven't experimented enough with this type of row sowing to recommend it, but for planting out, i.e. sowing plants intended for transplanting, row sowing offers undeniable advantages. Radii are traced, the distance of which I'll indicate for each species in the special crops, one or two centimetres deep, depending on the size of the seeds. The plant, evenly spaced, is always more vigorous than in broadcast sowing, where the center, over-hasty, withers and suffers greatly from transplanting. In broadcast sowing, only the plants at the edges are well constituted, whereas everything is good in row sowing; and all things being equal, the plant is always hardier and earlier. When the seedling is ready for transplanting, the beds are prepared as described in the previous chapter, and transplanting begins. Before describing this new operation, a word on the deplantatlon of the plant is necessary, as most often it is not deplanted, but pulled out with force, an operation which breaks all the roots, makes recovery more difficult, and greatly delays the harvest. When deplanting seedlings, be careful not to pull them out on the pretext that the soil is not hard and that they won't hold, but plunge the deplanter deep into the soil, and remove the seedlings in large clods, which are then carefully divided, without breaking the roots, and taking care to keep any bits of soil or compost that remain attached. In addition, you should always remove very few plants at a time, transplant them immediately, and never leave them to wither in large quantities in baskets, as is often the case. The only tool that can be used for transplanting is the planter. Many authors have pointed out its shortcomings in long articles, but as they have given us nothing to replace it, and we have found nothing ourselves, we will try to make the best use of it. Care must be taken to make the hole deep enough for the plant's root to enter completely without curling, which is detrimental to the plant's growth and vegetation. Gardeners know this problem, and to avoid it they cut or rather tear the root with their fingers. This very energetic method is too contrary to the laws of vegetation for us to use it. It is simpler and more beneficial for the seedling to make a deeper hole, and fill it with loose soil that is lightly packed down to the bottom with the tip of the planter. A single, essential watering immediately after transplanting is all that's needed to attach the roots to the soil. Under no circumstances should you crush the roots with the planting tool to attach the plant to the soil. Transplanting should always be done in the evening, at sunset or when the weather is dark or rainy, and never in broad daylight when the sun is shining. A plant that wilts will take a long time to recover; ten hours in the dark after transplanting is enough to ensure its recovery. By keeping the soil cool at all times, mulch makes a powerful contribution to the plant's recovery and rapid vegetation. Its use is very limited in gardens due to a lack of layers and composts. Salads, strawberries and tomatoes gain in size, quality and earliness when carefully mulched. The mulching operation consists in covering the soil, before or after transplanting, with a layer of manure from the demolition of layers, or composts, about h centimetres thick. This manure blanket, which is constantly irrigated and rained on, provides a considerable quantity of liquid fertilizer, which saturates the entire layer of soil occupied by the roots, preventing the fertilizer from evaporating and the soil from drying out. With a certain quantity of layers, we have mulch and potting soil at our discretion for all crops. Watering is one of the most important operations in the vegetable garden, and undoubtedly the most expensive. Owners usually take great care in creating a vegetable garden, but rarely think of watering it. The most careful think they've taken care of everything by digging a very deep well, painstakingly providing, at great expense, barely a tenth of the water needed in the middle of a huge garden. They say: The well is good; the gardener will draw water and water, without realizing how much water is needed, how easy it is to draw, or how long it takes to water by hand. The result is the same in all gardens lacking water or the equipment to bring it in and distribute it. As soon as May arrives, the gardener and his assistants are busy pulling on the well rope and carrying the watering cans; that's all they do; it's impossible for them to do anything else, and even then, with such vicious means of action, and an almost complete lack of water, they can't obtain satisfactory produce; they devote all their time, from May to September, to preventing the plants from dying, nothing more. Let's just say that during this period, all crops are neglected, because everything that exists requires water, on pain of death. The landowner must be convinced of this before creating a vegetable garden: water is as necessary as manure, and even more indispensable, for mountains of manure buried in parched soil will have no effect on vegetation, if we cannot introduce into the soil the quantity of water necessary to decompose fertilizers, and dissolve them, so as to make them assimilable, and repair the losses of evaporation through the leaves. The higher the temperature, the greater the evaporation, and the greater the soil's need for watering; if it ceases to be sufficiently moist, nutrition is poor and vegetation languishes. In principle, it's better not to water at all than to do it sparingly. A little water from time to time excites plants without advancing them; copious watering in well-smoked soil determines vegetation that is as prompt as it is vigorous. Water is the most pressing need of plants, especially vegetables, which grow very quickly. 8 Water, even in its pure state, is part of the plant's organization; it gives flexibility to its organs by penetrating them; it maintains the upward movement of the sap by the continuous evaporation of the leaves; finally, it dissolves the nutritive substances contained in the soil, and introduces them into the plants. Without water in the soil, no vegetation is possible; with insufficient water, vegetation languishes. The quantity of water to be distributed in the vegetable garden must be in proportion to the quantity of fertilizer to be applied, if we want to obtain certain results and abundant harvests from very small areas. The first thing to do before setting up a vegetable garden is to make sure you have enough water. (See page 2h.) The best water to use for watering is rainwater, because of its purity and the quantity of air it contains; its dissolving properties enable it to be easily loaded with fertilizing principles. Whenever it is possible to collect rainwater, it will be a precious resource for watering, and should preferably be used to water seedlings and young plants. Although not as good as rainwater, spring and river water are preferable to well water, in that they are less mineral-laden and more aerated. Well water can be used for watering, but it must be drawn off in advance, in order to warm it up, and above all to aerate it, by leaving it exposed to the air and the sun for at least one day. Water intended for watering is all the better for containing a greater quantity of air. When water is laden with putrefactive matter, and no longer contains oxygen, it becomes harmful to vegetation. Pond water, especially when it is crusted, should never be used pure for watering; to do so would be to risk the perishing of plants; it can be used as liquid fertilizer, but spread with three to four times its volume of clean, aerated water. Before turning to watering, a word about hydraulic machines is in order. Until now, the only machines used to raise water have been steam-powered machines, which are expensive, subject to repair and require a skilled operator to operate safely. It was the most perfect of all, and some people tried it out in preference to wind-powered machines, given that when the greatest need for water is felt, there is no wind; the machine remains at rest, while the plants roast on the ground, to operate with the greatest energy in spring and autumn, when the Creator often waters our gardens with too much prodigality. A young mechanic from Blois, M. Samain, has just invented a hydraulic machine of rare perfection; I've seen it in operation, and I can say, without fear of contradiction, that M. Samain's admirable machine will make us abandon all steam and sail engines for raising water. I can do no better here than to cede my place to a friend of mine, Mr. Drion. Drion, a distinguished horticultural enthusiast, and to reproduce part of an article he published in the Journal de Beaugency: "Agriculture is no longer dying of impotence : the country is being reforested, pestilential marshes are being cleaned up, drainage encouraged by protective laws is restoring food fertility to over-wet lands, canals criss-cross the country and give life everywhere, watercourses irrigate meadows everywhere, springs are used for the benefit of the masses, the poor have their share of the benefits like the rich, and all this without the one who owns an abundant river or a powerful spring being able to dispose of it as he pleases. Steam, mechanics and hydrology are creating marvels, giving everyone the water they need: propeller mills, the Mahoudeau system, windmills, the Chauvin system, perfected turbines, feed or drainage pumps, the Letestu system, Leclerc pumps, the atmospheric system, and a hundred other systems compete for the distribution and use of water. Follow regional competitions, horticultural shows, agricultural fairs and all the festivities devoted to the production of the soil, and you'll notice a large number of the most interesting hydraulic systems. But alas! it has to be said that, however perfect each hydraulic invention may seem, it is no less fertile in terms of results than it is in terms of expense and misfortune of all kinds; in the absence of improvement either in the mechanism itself, or in the multiplicity of friction points, the imperfection or near-impossibility of regular lubrication leads to disorganization, breakage and downtime, and worn or broken parts have to be quickly replaced at great expense, and the repairman is not always around; maintenance becomes ruinous, and so ruinous that in some cases, water costs the same as wine; soon, the owner is discouraged and gives up using the system that had smiled on him: when will we come up with a system that's both inexpensive and truly perfected? " "Well, we dare to say, without fear of contradiction, that we have this perfected hydraulic system. It was Mr. Samain, a young mechanic from Blois, inventor of the best known wine press, who was given the task of creating a hydraulic elevating machine, destined for the greatest success, and of creating a marvel which does him infinite honor. "We have seen it, and everyone can see it as we have, and it has been working admirably for over a year at Tavers, without steam, without auxiliary equipment, without a mill of any kind, without the help of any arms, and better still, without any maintenance of any kind: it takes water from a spring at the foot of a hill, transports it nearly six hundred meters away, and supplies, at a rate of eleven liters per minute, three reservoirs placed fifteen meters above sea level. Had it been 50 or 60 meters higher, it would have been easy, with no increase in expense, but 15 meters being the height of the vegetable garden where Mr. Meynard de Franc wanted the water from his spring to be brought, the clever mechanic had to stop there. One of the precious merits of this machine is that it raises the water to an unlimited height, without the height of the water level in any way hindering the regular operation of the device. With this machine, given a sufficient source - God knows how many springs are lost in France - we can create rivers, raise water to the highest apartments, have powerful jets of water in gardens, rocks with cascades without a break in continuity, irrigate meadows, supply mills, factories, towns and the countryside, and there will be no need, as in the use of the water wheel, for the indispensable assistance of three pumps to obtain regular operation; There will never be any irregularity unless it arises from the irregularity of the source exploited. Meynard de Franc's, an equal volume of water at all times, Mr. Samain's hydraulic machine will raise it constantly with perfect regularity and without any wastage. What machine other than Mr. Samain's has ever produced similar results? And if we consider that, once purchased, the Samain machine does not stop working until the height of the waterfall is reduced to Om 20 c., we can consider it to be the near realization of perpetual motion. Its motor is water, and it takes it from the upper reach by itself; it takes one hundred liters of it in a flat sheet-metal box; when the box is full, its own weight lowers it vertically, exerting pressure on a piston which pushes it back into the ascending pipe; At the bottom of its stroke, the box, which has been emptied by the opening of four valves placed underneath and protruding from it, has become light again; a counterweight removes it again, it is reloaded and descends again to empty itself, resuming its to-and-fro motion with chronometric regularity, with an effect that is always certain; we repeat, nothing has yet come close to this perfection; unforeseen expenses are no more to be feared than the hopeless failures of the systems employed to date; let us add that the device is simple, not very apparent, and is so graceful in form and appearance that far from blurring the symmetry of a well-kept garden, it will always be its most attractive ornament. " "The first Samain machine was created at Guignes, in the commune of Tavers (Loiret), on the property of Mr. Meynard de Franc, a member of the Court of Cassation. Let's take a brief look at how and why it was invented. Mr. Meynard de Franc wanted to move his vegetable garden to the top of the hill overlooking his country house; a vegetable garden needs plenty of water, and Mr. Meynard's spring was 600 meters away and 15 meters down. 16 WATERING. low. He called on Mr. Samain to help him fulfill his wishes. After a careful examination of the situation, Samain asked for time to think things over. Two months later, the mechanic had fulfilled the owner's wishes, and brought him under his arm a model to the tenth, a little jewel of a machine which was tested at the salon, and which worked so well, that Mr. Meynard de Franc, seduced, said to Mr. Samain: "Hurry up and build it! Samain: "Hasten to patent this ingenious invention, which should make your fortune and render immense services; but at the same time as you patent it, get to work on making a large-scale model for me; it's my source that gave you the idea, and it's my source that should be honored with the first realization of this hydraulic masterpiece. "We have these details from M. Meynard de Franc, and we couldn't resist paying a tribute of praise and homage to M. Samain, who is unknown to us, and who will forgive us, we hope, the preceding revelations. "The machine was built over a year ago; since then, it has been working without a moment's hesitation. People come from far and wide to see it, and any owner who visits it, with a view to using the spring he owns, goes from Guignes to Mr. Samain, and says: "And I need one too!" After a trip to Tavers, M. Dejean, the director of Le Cirque in Paris, was quick to obtain from M. Samain the second copy of this elevating device, which he had placed on his property in Seine-et-Marne, where it works well and is the admiration of the country. "We could not conclude better than by giving our readers the letter written by Mr. Meynard de Franc, to Mr. Samain. "Monsieur Samain, Paris, March 9, 1864. "I congratulate myself every day on my confidence in you concerning the hydraulic machine, the idea of which you have so happily realized at the Clos de Guignes. "The regularity of its operation, combined with the simplicity of the mechanism, leaves nothing to be desired; for almost a year since it was built, it has caused me no expense, required no supervision, and the three basins it feeds are kept full at this distance of 550 meters, at this height of fifteen, without anyone having to put a hand on them. "It is with the greatest pleasure that I will welcome those who would like to see your ingenious invention for themselves; for you know that my desire to succeed, for myself, was greatly increased by that of a success that would be profitable to you; and I believe I am not mistaken in promising you a complete success. "I renew to you the cordial assurance of my best sentiments, MEYNARD DE FRANC. "To close, let us say that the cost price of the Samain machine is not high, but that it varies according to the distance of the water elevation transport." J. DRION, Membre titulaire de la Société impériale et centrale d'horticulture. 8 I don't have the honor of knowing Mr. Samain in particular, but I have seen the results produced by his machine, and after examining it carefully, I believe I'm doing my readers a real service by telling them about the Samain machine, which I'm sure will be a legitimate success. Once the water has been brought to the vegetable garden's basin(s), it must be distributed to the plants. Watering cans are the simplest method, but also the most expensive, and the least likely to aerate the water. Watering with watering cans is ruinous. If the vegetable garden is a bit large, and you want to harvest something, you need at least two men to water it all day, from June to September, for three months. Two men at 3 francs a day, for three months, costs the sum of 540 francs, and often you need four, not counting the man, or men, who pump the water. If there's a manege, a horse is needed for three months. Watering under these conditions is so costly that many owners who know how to count have given up, preferring to let everything perish during drought years. Faced with the impossibility of watering, I set out to find a means of watering that was as copious as it was economical, and after much research and no less fruitless trials, I settled on the Perreaux pump, which fulfilled my purpose perfectly. 28 This portable pump can be placed at any pond; it is extremely simple and sturdy, it throws water at a distance of around 20 meters, and can be operated by a woman or a child. The Perreaux pump throws enough water to thoroughly wet a vegetable garden in two or three hours. By fitting a swallow's tail to the end of the lance, we obtain a small, very fine rainfall, invaluable for watering, in that the water, divided and thrown to a great height, is charged with oxygen, the first condition for making it dissolving, and giving well or spring water a value almost equal to that which falls from the sky. What's more, watering is much more abundant and inexpensive with an instrument as energetic and easy to operate as watering cans. To distribute twice as much water, all you need is a woman, a child, or a soldier if arms are scarce, who pumps for two or three hours, while the gardener, one of his assistants or a clever servant holds the lance. The very low price of the pump is more than reimbursed the first summer, by the savings in labor, and the garden is watered to perfection, copiously, and with excellent, well-aerated water. Let's also mention that the Perreaux pump can be of great help in the event of fire; and that, for a sum of barely one hundred and fifty francs that this excellent pump costs, there is no owner who would not benefit from having it in his countryside. This pump is suction and treading; it dispenses with the need to draw water; it fetches it easily from a pond, spring or river, to a depth of five meters, and requires barely the strength of a man. It's the best, cheapest and easiest to operate of all the pumps I've examined and tried. For further information, contact M. PERREAUX, 16, rue Monsieur-le-Prince, A PARIS. Whenever the size of the garden allows it, it is always a good idea to water with a pump, using the swallowtail at the end of the nozzle. First of all, you'll save an immense amount of labor, but secondly, watering will be much more profitable for the plants, because : 1° It is much more abundant than with the watering can; 2° The water that is thrown down in a very fine rain at a certain height is loaded with oxygen, and acquires the dissolving properties that it lacked; 3° The water that falls does not beat the earth, never runs to the surface; everything penetrates without loss; h° As the soil is not beaten, it remains permeable to air, and requires half as much hoeing as watering with a sprinkler; 5° The pump makes it easy to sprinkle fruit trees, and even those in beds, during hot spells. When the leaves are scorched by the sun, or covered with dust, a simple sprinkling on the leaves in the evening, after sunset, gives the trees a fresh, spring-like appearance. 18 When the garden is not large enough to make use of a pump, or when the owner does not wish to purchase one, the watering can with its wide head and very fine holes (pl. 5, fig. 6) should be used, to divide the water as much as possible. Watering should always be done in the evening, as it is more profitable, evaporation is less than during the day, and the water penetrates deeper into the soil. Watering in the evening is not an absolute rule; it is undoubtedly more profitable than watering during the day, but it does not follow that you should refrain from watering because you can't do it in the evening. In cultivation and gardening especially, it's not always possible to do things in the best conditions; in that case, you have to do them as well as possible. If there's no time in the evening, water in the morning, and if you can't water everything in the morning, water all day long. Watering by hand takes a long time; you have to wet the soil well and make the water penetrate deeply. I insist on this, because in general, gardeners who don't know much about cultivation would happily water an entire garden with six jugs of water. Almost all vegetable garden plants, with the exception of lettuces, cabbages and cauliflowers, should be watered from the head, not the spout. Spout watering is the worst of all, and should only be used for pom9 med vegetables, those whose leaves fear water. In all other cases, you need to water from the head, i.e. wet the whole bed. Here's why: When plants are watered by the spout, i.e. at the base only, the roots are moistened, but we forget that all the unwatered parts are dry, and as their surface area is much larger than that of the parts wetted by partial watering, they absorb all the local moisture by capillary action in less than an hour, and the plant watered at the base is almost as dry two hours after partial watering as it was before. To water a bed profitably, proceed as follows: Give a little water the first time, enough to moisten the soil, but not too much at a time, so that it doesn't sink to the surface; let this first watering penetrate well, and a quarter of an hour later, give a second one, which penetrates equally well; if the water sinks to the surface, stop, because as soon as the water sinks, it beats the soil and makes it impermeable to water and air. Half an hour later, a third watering is given, more abundant than the previous two, and this time penetrating very deeply into soil already soaked to a certain depth. If the water has been poured in too quickly, and the soil is beaten up, hoe it in with a hook a few hours 8 after watering, and before giving another, which would have no effect on a flat strip with a smooth surface beaten up like a driveway. The water would run off without penetrating the soil, and a certain amount of work, time and money would have been spent watering the paths, while the plants would perish for lack of moisture, to dissolve the fertilizers and speed up the ascent of the sap. I humbly beg my readers' forgiveness for this long dissertation on how to water, which will seem at least useless to some, but teaching experience makes it my duty to produce it. My listeners fall into three categories: the first is made up of intelligent men who are not afraid to put their hand to the work; for these, success is assured. The second is made up of intelligent men who claim to love progress, but don't want to do anything themselves. They come to our classes and applaud our teaching, whose logic seduces them; they applaud in good faith; they bring a more or less intelligent gardener with them, and tell him: apply this. The result is often bad, because the master has not supervised the execution, and often contrary to what we have taught them.

prescribed. Things remain as they are, and the owner has nothing to say to a labourer disguised as a gardener who tells him in a solemn tone: you see sir, the system is worthless. The third group is made up of purely practical men, which in the French language translates as: knowing nothing and willing to learn nothing. These men have often been sent to our courses, somewhat against their will! They say to each other, during all the theoretical lessons: J'avons pas besoin d'écouter ce monsieur qu'a un habit et des gants, mais nous rirons quand y viendra toucher à ste terre! There'll be time enough to look at him and not listen: I'll laugh! These good people base their opinion on a false idea. It's that it's harder to drive a plough, handle a spade, pickaxe or rake than to get a bachelor's degree. That's their belief, and they're mistaken in good faith. Don't believe them to be enemies of progress, they'll embrace it; but when they've seen it! let these restricted intelligences, enemies of study, see results first, and they'll immediately set to work to imitate. The uneducated man's distrust of gentlemen who cultivate can be explained to some extent by the failures of fools who wanted to do everything themselves, to change and upset everything, before they had acquired serious knowledge. The landowner has intelligence; he must work, not at digging and watering, but at acquiring solid knowledge, so as to direct his servants. He who acts in this way cooperates more than he thinks in the prosperity of his country. Do something better than what exists, and you'll soon find imitators, but do it first, and don't tell a man, who is an enemy of study and theory, to apply what he barely understands, and I'd even say, what he doesn't want to understand. This is so true, in fact, that the practitioners I'm talking about are enemies to us for the duration of the theoretical lessons; they come to the first practical lesson to laugh, and with the certainty of making fun of the gentleman who dares to touch the ground! But as soon as they see the gentleman, tool in hand, demonstrating that with half the trouble, they can do a better job, they become serious, listen, watch conscientiously, and come back for the second practical lesson, which they listen to religiously; by the third, they regret not having followed the theoretical lessons with success. If we give a course the following year, the most recalcitrant, in principle, are our most fervent followers, and sometimes our best pupils. Growing crops requires two things: knowledge and manpower. If the owner acquires the knowledge and applies it with the manpower at his disposal, before long France's wealth will have doubled. That's the question. Sine quà, no! Weeding is an operation as important as it is neglected in the vegetable garden; it consists of removing weeds from seedlings, by hand when? the seedlings are very young and tightly packed, and with the weeder when they are stronger. Weeding with the weeder is equivalent to hoeing. In principle, there should never be any weeds or foreign plants in seedlings intended for transplanting, nor in beds sown by broadcasting. In other words, we absolutely forbid this deplorable custom of sowing lettuces in onion and carrot beds. The lettuces grow up in the middle of the main sowing and smother it, producing only deplorable seedlings; they considerably delay the harvest of onions and carrots, only to produce a few mediocre and late salads. As soon as the seedlings are well established, the first weeding is done by hand, i.e. all weeds are pulled out one by one, and care must be taken not to let them become stronger than the seeded plants, under the pretext that the grass is too small or the weather too dry. The weed is never too small to be destroyed; it grows excessively fast when left in the middle of seedlings; this luxuriant vegetation is to the detriment of seeded plants, whose fertilizers it has absorbed, and it soon chokes them with its broad leaves and long roots. There's another serious drawback to waiting this late to weed: part of the root breaks off, and the grass grows back; when the root is pulled out completely, the surrounding plants are almost uprooted and suffer greatly. Drought is an inadmissible excuse for preventing weeding. Just moisten the soil with a few watering cans, and the weeds will pull right out. The first weeding should be carried out as soon as the weeds appear, watering beforehand if the soil is too dry, and afterwards to reattach to the soil the roots that have been shaken. It's a good idea to thin out areas where there are too many plants, by doing the first weeding. As soon as the weeds grow back, weed again, but this time with the weeder, a very convenient and expedient instrument (pl. 4, fig. 18). Weeds can be destroyed and thinned out with the weeder, and hoeing, done very superficially with this instrument, loosens the soil, makes it permeable, and reshoots the plants. 17 It's rare to have to do a third weeding when the first two have been well done, and above all on time. Weeding is inexpensive, and is usually carried out by women; it brings the harvest forward considerably, and increases its quality and quantity enormously. For seedlings in rows and those intended for transplanting, weeding between the rows is done with a weeder; only weeds mixed with the plant are pulled out by hand. Hoeing is done with blade and toothed instruments, with hoes for plants far enough apart, and with hoes of different sizes for transplanting in nurseries, and for plants close together. Hoeing is a very important operation, making a powerful contribution to the success of all crops. It is generally regarded as an operation designed solely to destroy weeds, but this is a profound error. Hoeing makes the soil permeable to air, and keeps it moist. As a result, it has an energetic effect on vegetation, facilitating the decomposition of fertilizers and accelerating the ascent of sap. This is its main purpose, and hoeing is just as effective on vegetation as ploughing. Everything in the vegetable garden that is not mulched must be hoeed very frequently, especially after watering, which beats the soil and forms a hard, dry crust on the surface. 2 This surface crust is highly inaccessible to vegetation, especially in slightly compacted soils. On the one hand, it prevents air from penetrating, and therefore prevents fertilizers from decomposing; on the other, as this hard, dry crust stops evaporation, there is no moisture left on the surface. As soon as the surface crust is broken, air penetrates the soil, and evaporation is re-established, the moisture at the bottom rises to the surface by capillary action, bringing to the roots the freshness they were deprived of. It is impossible to determine the number of hoeings, or when they should be carried out. This depends on the nature of the soil, its consistency and temperature. Clearly, clay soil needs to be hoeed more often than light soil, as it crusts more quickly and is so cohesive that it cracks during drought. Hoeing should be more frequent in all soils: when the temperature is very high, good hoeing is more effective than poor watering, especially if it is badly applied. A small amount of water thrown on suddenly beats the soil without wetting it, and only increases the thickness of the surface crust. Hoeing is essential after watering, because no matter how well administered, it always results in the formation of a crust on the surface of the soil, which must be broken if watering is to be successful. Hoeing with the hoe (pl. 4, fig. 12) is practiced in beds and squares of vegetables planted at great distances, and which receive little water. The hoe is a very expedient instrument, but it is imperfect: it does not penetrate deeply enough and does not loosen the soil sufficiently. The use of the hoe in the vegetable garden should be restricted to potatoes; for all other crops we prefer to use the flail (pl. 4, fig. 16). The serfouette is the most perfect instrument for hoeing in the vegetable garden; the blade is used to cut weeds between two plots of land, then the hook is used to harrow the soil energetically, completely loosening it. The same hook is used to move the soil deeply around the roots, without damaging or even shaking them. You urgently need two hook hoes, a large one for vegetables planted a long way away, and a small one for seedlings in rows, transplants, and all very close plants, requiring all the more hoeing as they are watered more frequently. With the hook, you can hoe a bed in an instant, and it's urgent to do it often, whenever a crust forms on the surface of the soil when it's not mulched. I can't sum up this chapter any better than by saying that hoeing is the scale of production in the vegetable garden, and that without it, fertilizing and watering lose more than half their effectiveness. 9 CROP SUCCESSION AND COUNTER-PLANTING. Crop succession and counter-planting are part of, and divisions of, the crop rotations I have already discussed; they are the art of organizing vegetable seedlings, transplants and nurseries, so as to harvest from each species over a large part of the year. Harvesting little of each kind of vegetable, and never running out, is the goal that can easily be achieved by introducing into the various crop rotations I've mentioned, a succession of vegetables and the counter-plantings I'm about to indicate!
