Let's start by stating the principle that, from March to November, there should never be an empty bed in the vegetable garden, and that each of these beds should yield, in a single season, at least three harvests on average, if the garden's crops are well managed. To achieve this result, each vegetable species must be constantly sown, and then raised in nurseries, on beds and in the open ground, to immediately fill the gaps left by the harvests. As soon as the harvest is removed, the bed is turned over and immediately planted with the produce from the vegetable nurseries, which are always abundant enough to replace what has just been consumed, and to provide for accidents. You must therefore sow often, and then raise in nurseries of each species, so as to always have good seedlings to put in place, as soon as there is a gap, and replace them in the nursery with younger seedlings. In spring, the beds supply raised seedlings to line the beds, as soon as the temperature allows them to be planted in the ground; they also supply the second and third seedlings, to be planted in the nursery in the ground, and finally, in summer, the ground supports the vegetable seedlings and nursery. What I have said about seedlings in chapter 4, dispenses me from giving further explanations on how to sow, it remains for me to deal with the vegetable nursery, the succession of harvests and counter-planting on layers, and in the open ground. Let's start with layers, which provide us with the first plants for full soil. Vegetable nurseries are planted on warm or hot beds, depending on the plants' need for warmth. As soon as the seedlings are a little strong, we transplant them a few centimetres apart, between the plants in place; then we remove the nursery as soon as it is in the way of the permanent plants, to put it in place in its turn. 3 As I said before, we do warm layers, lukewarm layers and muted layers. All the layers are never done together; we build them up as needed, or when we have manure available. So, I admit we have six hot layers to do. We'll start with two of them, on which we'll sow the first seedlings so that we can raise them in the nursery or put them in place on warm, muted layers, or even in the ground. A fortnight or three weeks later, we'll set up two other warm layers, on which we'll transplant the plants that need the most heat from among those sown first, and sow them again. Finally, a few days later, we'll set up the last two hot layers, and at the same time, a few warm layers to receive : Plants requiring less heat will be placed on the first warm layers and on the warm layers, and between these plants, nursery stock will be transplanted, or vegetables will be counterplanted and harvested on the spot. Later, the number of warm layers will be increased, and the muted layers will be built up to receive, as well as the full soil, raised seedlings. A word about counter-planting is necessary before going any further. It consists in planting other plants, which provide little shade and occupy the soil for only a short time, between plants that have been planted and must be harvested within a certain time. It's a way of doubling harvests without harming production, when the counter-planting is well done and well calculated. This operation is a great help for beds, especially where space must be saved, and where it gives the most brilliant results when skilfully carried out. Counter-planting is also very useful in open-ground cultivation, considerably increasing the yield without additional expense, but it must never be forgotten that the counter-planted vegetable must never harm the permanently planted one, and must be harvested when the latter begins to grow. For example, lettuces can be counter-planted between cauliflowers; a small nursery of cabbages or tomatoes between melons that have just been planted; sorrel, parsley or chervil between cabbages; cauliflower between melons just about to be harvested; white onion between clumps of peas; sorrel between green beans; cauliflower in early carrots, while harvesting, etc., etc. In the open ground, we counterplant: white onions in strawberry plants; lettuces, garlic, leeks between cabbages, etc., etc. I'll describe the counter-planting techniques that can be used for the special crops that make up the third part of this book. Let's get back to layers, seedlings, nurseries, crop successions and the counter-planting to be carried out there. As not all our followers will have at their disposal warm, lukewarm and deaf layers, I will give a few examples for each of these three series of layers, so that they can guide as surely those who possess all three species of layers, as those who will have at their disposal only lukewarm and even deaf layers. WARM LAYERS. 1st example. Around December 15, we sow melons, tomatoes, winter lettuce and a few radishes under a sheet of chassis. The melons are first removed and placed on a new warm layer; the tomatoes are transplanted between the melons; the radishes and part of the lettuce are harvested; between these two harvests, early potatoes are counter-planted, between which a few sorrel plants can still be counter-planted when the radishes and lettuce are removed; then, once the potatoes have been harvested, we plant cucumbers or late melons, between which we insert a few nursery plants for counter-planting, along with cauliflowers towards the end of the melon harvest. 2nd example. Early carrots are sown around the same time, to be harvested in the first days of April; while the carrots are being harvested, early tomatoes are counter-planted with pickling cucumbers, which are then counter-planted with cauliflowers. 3rd example. In January, you plant beans to be eaten green, between which you counterplant a few sorrel plants, or sow a few radishes; once the green beans have been harvested, you plant cauliflowers, counterplanted with cucumbers. Any empty spaces can be filled with a pot of Gaillon strawberries or large strawberries, which are then buried in the potting soil. WARM LAYERS. 1st example. Towards the end of January we plant early peas, between which we sow a few seedlings to be replanted, in a nursery, under another frame; as soon as the peas grow, we counter-plant them with early cauliflowers or tomatoes, or we pull them out to plant melons counter-planted with cauliflowers. 24 C early carrots, counterplanted with cauliflower, counterplanted in turn with cucumber. 3rd example. Around February, we plant early potatoes, between which we sow melons, tomatoes, etc., etc., which we remove to place in a nursery under another frame, as soon as the potatoes grow. After the potatoes, we plant melons, counter-planted with cauliflowers, towards the end of the melon harvest. Pots of strawberries can also be placed in the empty spaces of the warm layers, and some of the 8 plants raised on the warm layers can be transplanted to these layers, just as the warm layers will in turn receive the transplants born on the warm layers. MUTED LAYERS. You can grow more or less the same crops on these layers as on the warm layers, but with the difference that you have to start two months later, and while giving up early crops, you can still obtain very-early produce on these layers, if you compare them with those grown in the open ground. See part 3, Special crops, for more information. As you can see, the layers, even the muted layers when you don't have any others, provide the first seedlings to be planted in the open ground. Our vegetable garden, as you will recall, is subject to a four-year rotation with layers. Plot no. 1, smoked to the max, is for leafy vegetables. Plot no. 2, smoked to the maximum the previous year, and ploughed in the second year, is for roots and vegetables requiring decomposed manure. Square n° 3, smoked to the maximum in the first year, earth-mixed in the second and vigorously ashed in the third, is intended for vegetables with dried fruits, which require little fertilizer and a certain quantity of potash to bear fruit abundantly. Square No. h, having received maximum fertilization in the first year, topdressing in the second and ashing in the third, will be occupied in part by layers, seed-producing plants, a few dried-fruit vegetables and, if necessary, vegetable and flower nurseries, which will be fertilized with compost mixed with the product of the demolition of the first layers. Now that the season has arrived, it's time to sow and plant each of these squares. Let's start with n° 1, smoked to the max. EXAMPLES OF SUCCESSIVE CROPS IN VARIOUS BEDS OF PLOT NO. 1. 1° In early March, bed-sown leeks are transplanted; around mid-June, after the leeks have been harvested, they are ploughed, then celery is planted, which is counter-planted with lettuce. 2° In February, Yorck cabbage is planted, with garlic and shallots; after the cabbage harvest, cauliflower is planted with lettuce. 3° Very light radishes are sown in a plantation of Yorck cabbages; after harvesting the cabbages, cardoons are counter-planted with lettuce. 4° White onions are transplanted in February; after this harvest, savoy cabbages are planted with lettuces. 5° In February, cauliflowers are planted, between which spinach is sown; after the cabbage harvest, leeks are planted, into which a few lamb's lettuces can be sown. 18 EXAMPLES FOR PLOT NO. 2. 1° In February, Gaillon strawberry plants are transplanted, into which white onions are counter-planted. 2° Early carrots are sown in February; after the carrot harvest, lettuces are planted, counter-planted with chicory; after these last two harvests, spinach or lamb's lettuce are sown. 3° In March, we plant green romaine or red lettuce, between which we sow a few radishes; after the lettuce harvest, we plant tomatoes, counter-planted with summer lettuce. 4° In February, onions are sown; in July, lettuces are planted, into which late carrots or turnips are sown. 5° In March, early turnips are sown; after the turnips are harvested, late carrots are planted, followed by a sowing of lamb's lettuce. EXAMPLES FOR PLOT NO. 3. 1° In March, sow early peas with onions, carrots, turnips, etc., etc., for seed. After the peas have been harvested, beans are sown to be eaten green. 2° In April, early beans are sown to be eaten green, counterplanted with vegetables for seed; after the green beans have been harvested, late peas are sown. 3° In March, swamp beans are planted, between which late peas are sown, followed by a final season of green beans. 98 4° In March, lentils are counter-planted with vegetables for seed, followed by a harvest of late peas or beans. SQUARE N° A. This square, destined to become square n° 1 the following year, is intended for beds of all species. If the kitchen garden is very large, you can grow a few peas or beans watered with liquid fertilizer, or forage and a few seeds for poultry. If the kitchen garden is of ordinary size, the entire area not occupied by beds will be devoted to open-ground vegetable nurseries. A word about their organization is essential. Transplanting into a nursery is one of the most profitable operations for most vegetables, in that as soon as they are developed, they are planted at a small but equal distance, in very fertile, heavily fertilized soil, which, because of its small size, can receive all the watering it needs, three or four times a day if necessary. Without a nursery, there's no such thing as a good plant. I know that many gardeners will smile when they read these lines and say: I've never nursed vegetables and I have superb seedlings. I'd be quick to convince these disbelievers by showing them nursery-raised seedlings of the same age, which are much stronger and will produce better and earlier produce than the seedlings that have been set. 8 In most broadcast sowings, the seedling is very close together and therefore etiolated. This plant has spindly roots and poorly formed leaves, with a thin, elongated crown. As soon as such a plant is exposed to the sun, it first loses most of its leaves, its weak roots partly perish, and it always takes some time for it to recover and grow well. Add to this the difficulty, or rather the impossibility, of properly watering weak seedlings placed at great distances, and you'll recognize that we've spent six times as much time and trouble getting bad seedlings to grow again, as it would have taken to create two nurseries. Our seedlings are planted in rows, and our seedlings are never etiolated, with short, thick collars and excellent roots. We unfold it and replant it in the nursery, with all its roots. Here's how to set up the nursery. A plank is ploughed deeply and thoroughly smoked with fertilizer, and a little potting soil is added and mixed with the soil, after ploughing, by harrowing with a hooked fork. Rake out the stones, make ridges around the bed to retain water for watering, and then place stakes; two at each edge of the bed, 10 centimetres from the edge, the middle ones 20 centimetres apart. When all the stakes have been placed, the rope is laid all along the rows at once, and all the gullies into which the seedlings will be planted are dug out with a pointed trowel. Remove the string, and transplant the plants with all the care indicated in the transplanting chapter, 20 centimetres apart and staggered. Water immediately after transplanting, but wet the bed thoroughly with the watering can. When you water transplants at the base, you make a hole with the neck of the watering can, which exposes the root to the air, and the plant takes longer to recover when it does. What's more, watering with the spout only partially wets the soil, and a few moments after watering, the moisture is absorbed by the dry parts. Vegetables transplanted in the nursery do not wilt most of the time, they grow quickly and after a few weeks provide seedlings that no longer fear anything because of their vigor, and which can be planted with certainty of success. Vegetable nurseries also offer the immense advantage of providing double the yield; if the soil is not available for planting, the plant will strengthen in the nursery (cabbages start to fade); only when the plants in the nursery are very strong should they be removed, and replanted in clods using the trowel. In this chapter, I've given a few examples of successive cultivation and counter-planting, for beds and for the open ground; the combinations can be modified ad infinitum according to the importance of the crop, the quantity of species grown, and the rotation introduced into the kitchen garden; in the third part, devoted to special crops, I indicate the cultivation method specific to each species, and the place it should occupy in the kitchen garden rotation and in counter-planting. DiSueed CO AIL. The cultivation of garlic is of little importance; it doesn't deserve a special place in the vegetable garden, so we'll be content to counter-plant it in squares n° 1 and n° 2, with cabbages or strawberries. Garlic needs strong soil, with plenty of smoke, but it fears excess humidity, so it should never be counter-planted in crops that are watered too frequently. It is propagated by cloves, i.e. by separating the cloves and planting them separately. To make sure you never run out of garlic, plant it twice a year: in February and March, for harvesting in July or August, and in October, for harvesting in spring. Garlic does very well in borders; in fact, this is one of the best ways to grow it. Diguized D Cloves are planted 15 centimetres apart, and when the stem is fully developed, a knot is tied to stop the ascent of the sap, and concentrate all its action on the root. When garlic is harvested, as soon as the stem has withered, it should be left in the sun for two or three days before being brought in. ANGELIC. A triennial plant whose stems are used for candying, and whose seeds are used for various liqueurs. Angelica likes moist, very subsoil; it grows vigorously beside streams and fountains. Sow in March and water assiduously until the plant is strong enough to be transplanted. Plant out the following spring, spacing the plants one metre apart. The stems are harvested in the second year, the seed in the third. If you want to grow angelica in beds, plant it in a bed in plot no. 1, staggered 80 centimetres apart in all directions, and water very copiously. ARROCHE. To be grown from seed, in borders in plot no. 1, or in beds in plot no. 2; sow often if you want to have it all year round. Arrowroot can be planted between cabbages, artichokes or cardoons. 18 ARTICHAUTS. This is one of the best vegetables, to be harvested all year round, with a little care, a lot of water, and a well-understood culture. I have recommended the cultivation of a single variety: the green aros artichoke from Laon, as very fertile, and unquestionably the best of all; it is always tender, even when peppered, when it has acquired more than half its development. Artichokes like damp, clayey soil. It should be fertilized copiously in all cases, but the dose of manure and water should be increased as it is planted in lighter soils. There are two ways to grow artichokes: in permanent squares, where they produce good yields for three or four years, and as annuals. The choice between the two depends on the size of the garden, the quantity of artichokes needed, the yield, and the gardener's activity. If the owner has a very large vegetable garden, planted in substantial soil, with plenty of manpower, water and manure, and if artichokes sell well in his locality, it would be to his advantage to create an artichoke patch outside the crop rotation, as I said on page 102. If, on the other hand, the soil is light, and the owner only wants to harvest what he needs for his own consumption, he should adopt an annual crop. We will now describe these 9 two crops. You can experiment with both, and choose the one best suited to the soil and the grower's needs. GROWING ARTICHOKES INDOORS. When creating an artichoke patch intended to remain in place for three or four years, the first thing to do is to plough it in, or rather to break it up with two good spades, i.e. to a depth of around 60 centimetres, even when the vegetable garden has been broken up. A very copious manuring of fresh manure is applied to the soil, and if wool waste or similar is available, it is mixed in with the manure. Stakes are then placed as follows: 60 cm from the edges, and all others 1 m 20 cm apart. Place the chalk line on these stakes, mark the lines with a pointed trowel, and remove the chalk line. Before planting, measure one of the lines to leave a distance of 60 centimetres between each end of the bed and the first artichoke plant, and 1 metre 20 centimetres between the plants. A small stick is driven into each spot where a foot is needed, taking care to place the small sticks in staggered rows. In this way, the artichoke vines are placed 1 meter 20 centimeters apart, in all directions. Once this is done, we proceed with the planting. First, holes of about 30 centimetres are made in the marked places, using a spade or a large trowel. At the bottom of the holes, place a good handful of well-consumed fertilizer, which you mix well with the soil at the bottom, using the trowel; place the eyecup in the middle of the hole, at a depth of 10 to 12 centimetres, Then add a little more soil, followed by manure spread across the entire width of the hole, and cover with soil, taking care to form a basin around the base to force water from watering to seep down to the base of the plant. Artichokes planted as indicated grow vigorously and bear fruit four or five months after planting (pl. 7, fig. 3). Water copiously, immediately after planting, and continue to give a watering can of water to each plant every day, until it sprouts again, and even when there are four or five sprouting leaves. At that point, we only water every two or three days, but we give a watering can to each plant, so as to wet the soil thoroughly. It would be better not to water, and to hoe often rather than half-heartedly. We hoe from time to time to keep the soil in a constant state of permeability. You can counter-plant cabbages, cauliflowers, lettuces, white onions, and sow radishes in artichokes, without harming them in any way, and I'd even go so far as to say that they do very well with these counter-planted vegetables, often and copiously watered, as the entire surface of the soil is kept in a state of humidity that is very beneficial to the artichoke. The counter-planted vegetables must all be removed when the artichoke covers the ground, and shows its first apples. The soil then belongs entirely to the artichoke, and you need to make it produce quickly and a lot. As soon as the first apples appear, hoe vigorously and water every day, even twice a day if the weather is very dry. The beauty, quality and number of artichokes depend on the amount of water you give them. One large and two medium-sized fruits can be harvested from each artichoke stalk. There is often a greater quantity, but in a plant destined to produce for several years, it is advisable to remove them to avoid tiring the plant, and to avoid harming the following year's harvest. As soon as the fruits have been removed, the stem should be cut off as deeply as possible under the ground, and care should be taken not to leave it on the plant or to cut it in half, as it rots on the spot and considerably impairs the development of the eyelets, and consequently the next harvest; sometimes it even leads to the loss of the plant, which rots on the spot. Artichokes are very prone to frost, especially as they are planted in damp, clay soils to produce good quality produce. Preserving the plants over the winter is more laborious than cultivating them all year round. This is the other side of the coin in artichoke cultivation, and it's such a surface area that we've had to look for and experiment with another crop to avoid incessant and very expensive labor. It's true, artichokes are grown like most other crops. We know the dangers to which they are exposed, and we rely on Providence to preserve them. When winters are not harsh, they can often be kept for several years without noticeable damage, but if a harsh season occurs, with prolonged frosts, almost all the plants are destroyed, and there are scarcely enough plants left to repopulate everything that has been frozen; then people cry famine, storm after the harshness of the season, and artichokes are in short supply just about everywhere. Annual cultivation, which is easy in the kitchen garden and possible on the plains, gives us the means to avoid these disasters. We don't want to leave anything to chance, we need an even harvest every year, so let's first look at ways of preserving permanent plantations, then move on to annual cultivation. When you have an artichoke plantation of a certain size, it's easy to preserve it from frost with a little vigilance. Keep an eye on the weather; as long as it remains soft, mild and damp, you can let the artichokes grow, but take care to hoe them from time to time to destroy weeds. This is very important before ridging, because if you ridge in soil overloaded with weeds, these weeds, cut 9 and buried green, ferment and rot, and can, in damp weather, pass on their decomposition to artichokes, which are very prone to rot. As soon as the weather turns dry, and frost is likely, first cut off the largest leaves and butter the stems, i.e., bring the soil back to the base with the hoe, as shown in figure 4, plate 7. I said cut off the largest leaves, i.e. those around the base, and not shave off all the leaves, as is wrongly done in some countries. To deprive the artichoke of all its leaves is to delay the first harvest by more than a month, and reduce it by half. Ridging preserves the crown and the rudiments of the eyelets, and even encourages their development, but is insufficient to protect the plant from the effects of an intense, continuous frost. Ridging is the first preventive operation. It's done as soon as the weather threatens, and as soon as it's done, you need to check the barometer and get ready for the other operations. Prepare decomposed manure or compost, and if frost seems imminent, surround the mounds with it, placing a small wheelbarrowful at each foot (pl. 7, fig. 5). This manure will be buried in the spring; it is all transported; it is a job well done, and it will ensure the preservation of the artichokes, however harsh the winter may be. As soon as the manure is placed, it is urgent, in the event of a severe frost, to place a certain quantity of dry leaves, dried grass or straw between the mounds, to cover all the leaves as soon as the frost becomes severe. Artichokes that have been mounded, smoked and covered with leaves or litter can brave the harshest winters with impunity, but you mustn't forget that artichokes rot very easily, and that every time the thaw comes, you have to uncover them completely, otherwise they will rot under their shelter; if it freezes at night and thaws during the day, and the weather remains dry, you have to cover them in the evening and uncover them in the morning. If you don't uncover as soon as it thaws, you expose yourself to rot. If the thaw is serious, i.e. accompanied by rain, not only should you uncover completely, but you should also loosen the collar a little, so as to expose it to the air. When the weather's dry, you put the shoes back on, and when it's frosty, you cover. It's a never-ending job, requiring constant monitoring throughout the winter. Most of the time, artichokes are like fruit trees, which are sheltered every day. You put in a lot of hard work at the beginning, but by the end of the season you've cooled off, you've sheltered so often that you no longer believe in the danger, and the day you stop, a frost takes everything away. Towards April, when frosts are no longer to be feared, the bedding is removed; the manure covering the mounds is spread evenly over the entire square, the mounds are demolished, and a deep plough is given over to the manure. A fortnight later, around April 15, when the plants are starting to grow well, we remove all the useless shoots from the collar and plant the new ones. But when you want to replant, it's better to wait a fortnight, as the eyelets are better formed, with small roots at the base, and then you choose an elite plant, whose precocity and fertility are assured. Here's how to proceed with carnationing: the plant is completely dethatched all around, and the two largest stems are chosen to keep (only two per plant). If more are left, the harvest is significantly reduced in quantity and quality. All horticultural treatises recommend bursting the eyelets; this has always seemed barbaric to me. The tear produces a wound that always creates a bad scar, and it seemed to me that the harvest should suffer from the numerous tears made to the mother plant. For several years, I carried out comparative trials; in the same square, I eyeballed by splinters, and cut, instead of splintering, over one or two rows. Years of experience have shown me this to be absolutely true. The plants whose eyelets had been cut off were much more vigorous, gave more fruit, more beautiful fruit, and ripened three weeks earlier than those of the splintered plants. As this happened over a period of eight or ten years, I abandoned splinter eyeletting, and I now use and recommend eyeletting with a serpette, to the exclusion of the previous method. This explains the recommendation made in several horticultural treatises: "cut off the torn parts of the eyelets with a serpette, and leave them in the cellar for four or five days before planting, to heal the wound and prevent rotting". I'm far from denying the effectiveness of this method against rot, but I do affirm that a carnation that has withered for four or five days in the cellar, has experienced a fatigue that considerably delays its development when planted; not only does this give rise to less vigorous vegetation, but it also bears fruit later, and gives smaller and less abundant fruit than one that has been detached with the serpette, and replanted immediately. When eyeleting with a scythe, all the weak eyelets are sacrificed and the best ones, those with a thick collar and a good root ball, are carefully removed. It's easy to keep a few small rootlets by cutting them off the mother plant (A, pl. 7, fig. 6); these rootlets contribute powerfully to the vegetation and fruiting of the planted subject, since we are planting a perfect subject, composed of a stem and a root, instead of an imperfect subject, a cutting, composed solely of a stem and deprived of roots. As soon as the eyelet is detached from the mother plant, cut off the large leaves to prevent evaporation (b, pl. 7, fig., 6) and plant it immediately. As soon as the eyelet has been removed, the mother plant is planted again. Water copiously as soon as the apples appear. The same care is applied during the winter, with carnationing every spring and so on for the four years of the artichoke plant's life. I'm not talking about manuring, because it's understood that every year the mounds are covered with manure buried in the spring. If, instead of manure, litter or leaves were used to cover the mounds, manure would have to be applied every year and buried by spring ploughing. ANNUAL ARTICHOKE CULTIVATION. This type of cultivation is far preferable to permanent cultivation: firstly, it avoids all the trouble of wintering, which requires constant supervision; secondly, it produces earlier and later crops with less effort. With annual cultivation, it's easy to grow artichokes almost all year round. In this case, artichokes are grown in the same way as other vegetables. Instead of leaving the artichokes in the ground, buttering them, covering them and uncovering them constantly throughout the winter, at the risk of seeing them freeze or rot, we pull them up at the end of the year, a little before the frosts. From the strongest carnations, those with the largest necks, we select the quantity needed to produce early artichokes, and plant the rest in the vegetable greenhouse. The choice carnations are planted in slightly large pots, and placed in a cold tarpaulin, where, protected by glass, they continue to vegetate slowly; when the season becomes hard, when it freezes, a stove is placed around the tarpaulin, and covered with straw mats. The artichokes, preserved in this way over the winter, are planted out in April, when frosts are no longer to be feared, in plot no. 1 of the vegetable garden. The slow vegetation, contained by the tarpaulin, has favored fruiting, and the apples show up very soon after planting, and sometimes before. Artichokes stored this way over winter produce at least six weeks earlier than those left in the ground. Artichokes can be stored in pots, in an orangery if there is no tarpaulin, and if you have enough frames, you can force them to produce fruit towards the end of winter, by putting a layer on the bottom of the tarpaulin. Artichokes kept in pots over winter are intended solely for early production, and it is best to grow them very quickly, in order to speed up harvesting and clear the soil. We counterplant them with cauliflower; we water very copiously, and as soon as the harvest is done, we pull up all the artichoke stalks, and throw them in the manure to increase the mass of the compost; we also make, in the place they occupied, a harvest of winter cabbages, counterplanted with lettuces, garlic, etc., and we plant them in the compost. Autumn artichokes are planted in the vegetable greenhouse or cellar, using artichoke vines that have been planted, or rather buried, in slightly greasy sand or mixed with soil. Towards the end of March or in the first few days of April, the best eyelets are carefully removed and planted as I indicated above, in vegetable plot no. 1. Only one carnation should be planted per plant, never two, as is too often the case, because when artichokes receive the necessary watering, they form clumps that are too large, which is detrimental to production. All artichokes grown annually should be planted 90 centimetres apart, staggered. Carnations taken from the vegetable greenhouse and planted in spring will bear fruit in August, September and October if they are sufficiently watered; they will begin to produce when artichokes kept in pots have stopped yielding. Often there are several small artichokes left when the frosts approach; we pull up the whole plants, and replant them in a cold tarpaulin, in the vegetable greenhouse or even in the cellar, where they finish ripening, and still give excellent fruit when they come in, and sometimes in the middle of winter. If you want to grow artichokes in winter, and have enough frames to shelter them, nothing could be easier. To do this, plant a special patch of artichokes in plot no. 1 of the vegetable garden; water to ensure a good start, then stop watering for a while to delay the harvest; in a word, manage your artichokes so that they don't show their apples until October; pull up the whole plants and place them under a tarpaulin, either in the vegetable greenhouse or in the cellar, where they will finish growing over winter. The second artichoke planting, made with the eyelets from the vegetable greenhouse, is pulled out as frost approaches; it provides the plants for potting, and the eyelets for the second and third plantings if necessary. Nothing is easier than this type of cultivation, and although it may appear complicated, it is less laborious than growing artichokes indoors, while producing earlier and later fruit almost without interruption throughout the year. What's more, you can be sure of never running out of one of the best and most precious products in the vegetable garden. ASPARAGUS. Asparagus is perhaps the most precious vegetable, and the one that was the most poorly cultivated. Asparagus is always eagerly awaited, it's the first vegetable, it's healthy, excellent, and often recommended for the sick and delicate stomachs. As a result, consumption of this precious vegetable is colossal; Germany, Holland and Belgium cultivate it successfully. Purple asparagus from Holland, the Marchiennes and Ulm varieties, were the most perfect we had, 9 before asparagus cultivation became essentially French, and a French variety, the improved Argenteuil asparagus, dethroned all known varieties. I have previously indicated the Ulm asparagus, and especially recommended it as the earliest and best. Here's how: I began writing this book in 1863, it was due to appear in September, and at that time, I had not grown Argenteuil asparagus, I knew it imperfectly. Delays in the appearance of my book, which only saw the light of day in July 1864, ten months later than the time indicated, enabled me to examine carefully not only the improved Argenteuil asparagus, but also the cultivation of this magnificent variety. After mature and conscientious examination, I adopt, to the exclusion of all other varieties, the improved Argenteuil asparagus, as the largest, perhaps the most fertile, almost as early as the Ulm asparagus, and unquestionably the best. Argenteuil has not only endowed us with a variety of asparagus that surpasses all others, in volume and quality, but also with a cultivation method that is as simple as it is fruitful in results, and which victoriously dethrones all the timid trials of the past. When properly cultivated, the improved Argenteuil asparagus measures 8 to 10 centimetres in circumference, is 35 centimetres long and is so tender that it can be eaten at least halfway through a length of 18 centimetres. It's a French conquest, a novelty without rival. This asparagus is neither green nor purple, but variegated. Every year, the Société impériale et centrale d'horticulture, whose rulings are the law, awards first prize to M. Lhérault-Salbœuf, Argenteuil's most distinguished asparagus grower. M. l'Empereur appointed this intelligent and hard-working grower as his table supplier. I said earlier that progress in arboriculture and vegetable cultivation would contribute mightily to the increase of public wealth; that when these two sciences were spread throughout France, and popularized by primary school teachers in every village, the fortune of France would be doubled. The asparagus crops of Argenteuil are a striking example of what I've been saying. I'll step aside to let Mr. J. Drion, a distinguished amateur horticulturist, whose friend I have the honor of being, and who is making the most praiseworthy and disinterested efforts to popularize the cultivation of improved asparagus in the Orléanais and Sologne regions: "But no sooner had our railways extended their networks, than every spring the capital's market halls were clogged with asparagus, quite mediocre, arriving by the wagonload from all the départements and even from abroad. The Parisian, a tireless practitioner of the science of Berchoux and Brillat-Savarin, set about analyzing the specific qualities of each species, with his distinctive sure-footedness of palate. He compared them, as a fine connoisseur that he is; then, all things considered: the shape of some, the ease of cooking of the little green ones, sometimes bitter; the pleasing appearance of the Hollandaise, sometimes lacking in taste; the appropriation of the sauce appropriate to each kind; he decreed, and this decree has the force of law on all the tables of Europe : that first prize went to Ghent asparagus, second to Vendôme asparagus, third to Châteauroux asparagus, fourth to Besançon asparagus. Asparagus from Ulm, Marchiennes and Holland, with their appetizing shape and color, but weak taste, received a mere second place. The asparagus from Orleans, green, bitter, thin, grown in vineyards, and therefore poorly cultivated, was placed last. The asparagus from the outskirts of Paris remained: Epinay, Saint-Ouen, Pantin, Sartrouville, Montesson and Argenteuil, judged equal in merit, were not ranked; their merit, however great, was recognized as perfectible. The farmers of these communes were told: "You have exceptional soil and the privilege of the best fertilizers; as you can produce bigger and better, we're only giving you an honorable mention, we're only awarding you an encouragement prize. You can become the kings of asparagus; work hard, and we'll let you know! "Argenteuil took the decision seriously, and got to work. Several of its growers, led by M. Lhérault-Salbœuf, have devoted themselves with intelligence and perseverance to the improvement of asparagus cultivation; they have carried out five or six methods of cultivation at the same time, and kept a daily record of the results; crop rotation, sowing, fertilization, choice of location, preparation of the land, ploughing of all kinds, they have neglected nothing; Six years later, the Argenteuil growers had truly become the kings of asparagus; they had created the third breed that led them to fortune. " Mr. Lhérault-Salbœuf wasn't rich then; today he has a very respectable fortune, one that many notaries would be content with, and he owes it to Argenteuil asparagus. Not only has Argenteuil's monstrous asparagus made Mr. Lhérault's fortune, it has also made that of all the surrounding communes. Faced with such conclusive results, imitators have been numerous; improved asparagus crops now occupy more than five leagues of land. England and Russia have set up trading posts wherever improved asparagus is grown. The grower never comes to the market; he doesn't even bother to sell his produce. Russian and English agents wait for him when he returns from the fields, climb into his car and, most of the time, make the price themselves, so afraid are they of seeing these magnificent products, unique in the world, slip through their fingers. 16 I have cited these facts as an example to follow, and to make the masses appreciate what importing a lucrative crop into a country is worth. Improved asparagus has enriched all those who have cultivated it, and the number is great; now let's count the added value of the soil, occupied by these crops, and we'll see that the soil is worth ten times what it was fifteen years ago. Not only are the farmers rich, but they have also enriched the landowners. France finds millions every year, paid for by Russia and England. May the reader forgive me these lengthy details, but as I am dedicated to the teaching of horticulture, my efforts are aimed at enlightening backward populations, and getting them to adopt crops that would make their country's fortune. I have cited these facts, such as the honorable mention given to the ancient asparagus of Argenteuil, preferable to that of Ulm and Marchiennes, to prove that when stimulated, a little hard work can produce immense results, for oneself, for one's fellow citizens, and for one's country. May the example of Argenteuil be followed by many horticultural societies in not lavishing their medals too lavishly; the same results will be obtained wherever these societies are content to encourage first efforts and reward serious results handsomely. The cultivation of improved asparagus is a source of wealth for all countries willing to undertake it. This crop is as new eight leagues from Paris as the asparagus variety, better known to the 49- ASPERGES. The English and Russians know more about it than the French, for these gentlemen barely leave a few bunches for the Paris market, and even then, they are not the most beautiful. I shall therefore deal with this crop from two points of view: that of the owner's garden, and that of speculation. Let's begin by stating the principle that we must absolutely abandon the old method of growing asparagus in deep pits, which are very expensive to build up, and in which mounds of manure were buried, all of which wasted. We also absolutely prohibit the annual replanting of soil, which has to be covered with racks and prepared with great expense, a method of cultivation that is as ruinous for the owner as it is contrary to the vegetation of the asparagus. In a word, it's time to make a clean break with the past, to frankly abandon our erring and groping ways and boldly embrace the path of progress. Owners who follow the cultivation methods we've outlined to the letter, without changing a thing, will be sure to harvest an abundance of monstrous asparagus within the timeframe we've indicated. This is addressed to all landowners, even those who have dug pits for twenty years, buried stones, fascines and half their manure in them, if not all, without ever having succeeded in harvesting a passable asparagus crop. I'd like to respond in advance to an objection that will be raised many times: My soil is not that of Argenteuil! To which I reply: the first improved asparagus I saw were planted by Mr. J. Drion on his Beaugençy estate. They were planted in the worst conditions; their volume and quality equal, if not exceed, those of asparagus harvested on the Argenteuil plain. I found the same results in the poor soils of Sologne, and in twenty other places, and it was after having observed these equal results in all soils, with the same variety of asparagus, and with the same cultivation, that I went to Argenteuil to study asparagus cultivation in depth, before writing the lines that follow. I did more, delaying the appearance of this book by over a month, to see asparagus crops in full production. That said, I'll leave the field open for those who don't want to convert, and I'll start with the sowing: ASPARAGUS SOWING. Towards the end of March, a bed is ploughed deeply, and abundant manure of half-decomposed fertilizer is applied. The bed is then set up with edges all around to retain water for watering, then stakes are placed at each end, 25 or 30 centimetres apart; a chalk line is placed and furrows four centimetres deep are traced with a pointed trowel. Once this has been done, remove the string; put the potting soil in a basket and sow it about a centimetre thick at the bottom of the furrows. The seeds are then sown three centimetres apart in the furrows, covered with about a centimetre of potting soil, and finally covered with pure horse dung, well crushed with the fingers. When the weather is dry, water often until the plants emerge. Watering over the horse dung dissolves all the soluble parts, saturating the entire layer of soil to be occupied by the roots with assimilable fertilizer. Any weeds are pulled out by hand, if they are present before the plant has emerged. As soon as the plant has emerged, we lightly hoe it with the weeder, and hand-pull any weeds growing between the plants. Water from time to time, if the weather is dry; when the plant has reached a height of about ten centimetres, hoe more vigorously, and cover the soil with fresh dung, well crushed with the hands, into which we mix a few handfuls of plaster or ash if plaster is not available, but plaster is better. The following spring, this plant is good to put in place, especially if you've given it all the care I've indicated, and even if some of this care had been lacking, it would still be good to plant; it would be a little less strong, but better for sure than two- and three-year-old plants, as we have the bad habit of using them. Asparagus claws are very fragile; they don't suffer from replanting in the first year, when the roots are underdeveloped, but in the second year, a large part of them are broken off, causing a noticeable delay in vegetation. If you want to force asparagus under a frame, leave a claw every 50 centimetres, plough, taking care not to damage them, and cover with half-decomposed manure. These claws can be pulled out the following year and replanted under a frame. Asparagus can be sown in plot no. 1, or better still, in plot no. 4, where a bed can be prepared for the purpose. PLANTING IN THE VEGETABLE GARDEN. Asparagus requires a soft, chalky soil, lighter than strong, and above all free of humidity; it likes chalky soil, and needs a considerable amount of humus. With enough fertilizer and the addition of some form of limestone, asparagus can be grown in any soil, provided it is healthy. There isn't a vegetable garden, however clayey, where you can't grow an asparagus plant. In this case, you need to amend the soil, and whatever amendment you give it, it will always be less expensive than planting in deep pits, draining the bottom, and spending mountains of manure in pure waste. If the soil is very compact, very moist, and retains stagnant water in the lower layers, the asparagus plant will have to be drained, but this is an exceptional case that does not occur 8 once in a hundred times. When the soil is strong and clayey, an amendment is sufficient; depending on the compactness of the soil, sand mixed with crushed plaster, old lime mortar, lime or, failing all that, ashes from wood, lime kilns, factories and even locomotives are spread evenly over the entire surface, if you're near a railway line and have nothing else to hand. I'm a great believer in locomotive ashes, because you can get them in large quantities for next to nothing, and experience has shown me that they work very energetically in clay and siliceous soils, which are essentially lacking in limestone. When the soil to be planted is very compact, it is useful to give it a rough ploughing before the frosts; we leave it like this throughout the winter, and in spring, we spread the amendment, which we amalgamate well with the soil, taking care to extract the stones and break up the clods, by carrying out a ploughing with two spade-irons; a few days later, we also spread abundant manure, which we bury by another ploughing. These three ploughings are only necessary on very strong, very clayey soils. In medium-consistency soils, and in soils containing sufficient limestone, manure is spread evenly over the soil, and ploughed in with a single ploughing operation using two spade-irons, once the soil is healthy. In siliceous soils where asparagus gives very good results, a single ploughing is also necessary in spring, to bury the fertilizer, which should be supplemented with limestone or ashes. Once the land to be planted has been levelled, ploughed, smoked and cleared of stones, stakes are placed at a distance of 1 meter 20 centimeters, the distance at which the lines of asparagus will be planted, oriented from east to west. Instead of making beds containing two and sometimes three rows of asparagus, planted 40 to 60 centimetres apart, we'll make a single row every 120 centimetres, and plant the asparagus stalks at the same distance along the whole length of the row, taking care to plant the following rows staggered, so as to have a distance of 1 metre 20 centimetres, in all directions, between the stalks. This planting method will seem exaggerated to those accustomed to planting asparagus at 40 centimetres. One metre twenty centimetres is the desired and tested distance for Argenteuil asparagus, which is six or eight times larger than green asparagus, and four times more productive. If you plant closer, you risk losing a lot in terms of volume and quantity of asparagus. When the stakes are placed 1 meter 20 centimeters apart, a stake is driven in on each side, 20 centimeters from the middle one, giving a 40-centimeter-wide line when the string is laid. Small trenches, 1h centimetres deep and 40 centimetres wide, are opened to plant the claws, with the extracted soil thrown back to the right and left (pl. 8, fig. 1). Once the trenches have been opened, a little half-consumed manure is placed at the bottom (A, pl. 8, fig. 2), and two or three handfuls of the same fertilizer are set aside, every 1 meter 20 centimeters, where each asparagus claw is to be planted. Once this is done, proceed with planting. Use your hands to form a small mound of earth in the middle of the trench, in the place where the claw is to be planted (B, same fig.). Place it on top of the mound, carefully spreading the roots all around (C, same fig.); cover the roots with 2 or 3 centimetres of soil, taking care to ensure that it adheres well to the root tips (D, same fig.); then, using your hand, distribute your supply of fertilizer all around the perimeter of the soil-covered roots (E, same fig.). and finish the operation by covering everything with soil as shown in line F. The neck of the claw should not be covered by more than ten centimetres of soil (G, same fig.). As soon as planting is complete, a small stick (H, same fig.) is placed next to the planted claw, to identify its position and avoid injuring it by hoeing. When the weather is dry, it's a good idea to mulch the entire surface of the pits. A few weeks later, the asparagus starts to grow; all it needs throughout the summer is a few hoeings to destroy weeds, and two or three waterings with liquid fertilizer if the season is very dry. Towards the end of October, cut the stems in half, so as to easily recognize the place of the claw; dig up the crown so as to leave only a 6-centimeter covering of soil (A, pl. 8, fig. 3); then spread manure all around the perimeter occupied by the roots, avoiding putting any on the crown (B, same figure), and leave the plants in this way throughout the winter. Asparagus fears neither cold nor frost, but it does obey the imperative need felt by the roots of all plants to be in contact with the air. The roots of asparagus that are too deeply buried are constantly coming up to breathe, as the Argenteuil growers say. In other words, the roots buried too deeply, and therefore removed from contact with the air, rot, and new ones form on top of the old ones, to the great detriment of the plant's vigor. So we say that asparagus goes up, give it air during winter and it will stop going up. This is a fact recognized by asparagus growers and confirmed by years of experience. This method of cultivation is diametrically opposed to everything that has been done up until now. The plant was asphyxiated every year; the Argenteuil growers give it air, without which no plant can exist. The old methods have never produced an asparagus comparable to those of Argenteuil. The Argenteuil culture produces only monstrous asparagus, twice as many at half the cost as the old methods. It's a fait accompli in all its brutality. I'll let the creators of Argenteul asparagus and its cultivation speak for themselves: "The asparagus claw comes up in winter when it's too overgrown; it uses its resting time to fortify its roots, it acquires new strength, but it needs air, and it's to get enough of it that it spends part of its strength coming up. Remove the thickness of the layer of soil that covers it, and it will breathe easily and will no longer move; don't let it try to move during the vegetation period, and take it for granted that it will only occupy itself with throwing its jets outwards, because then these jets are so many respiratory organs that it will obstinately multiply as you remove them. If, like us, this statement leaves you incredulous, do as we have done, observe, and your conviction will soon equal ours." When we have dethatched and smoked as I have just indicated, towards the end of October, we are careful not to cover the manure with soil, for fear of preventing the asparagus from breathing. It's only around March 15th that we cover the manure, which the winter rains have largely dissolved; the roots are saturated with fertilizer, so we bring the soil over each claw, marked by the stems (C, pl. 8, fig. 3), which have been intentionally kept to a length of 30 to hO centimetres, and a mound of earth 25 to 30 centimetres high is formed around them; this well loosened earth, exposed all winter to the action of the frosts, gives easy passage to the asparagus, which will soon sprout. The first year, we covered the collar of the claws with only 10 centimeters of soil; the second year, when we won't yet be harvesting asparagus, we dethatch, smoke at the end of October and rethatch in March with only 15 centimeters of soil. In the third year, when we start to cut the biggest asparagus plants, we'll use the same autumn methods, and cut them to a height of 30 centimetres, and so on every year for as long as the asparagus plant lasts. The way in which asparagus is harvested is of great importance in terms of volume and quantity. You have to abandon the ancient knife that was plunged deep into the earth, and with which you often cut two beautiful invisible asparagus plants, to harvest one visible one, as big as a pencil. It's urgent to cut asparagus as it emerges from the claw. Here's why: if you leave part of the asparagus stalk on the claw, as is always the case when you cut underground with old-fashioned knives, the remaining section lives for two weeks, during which time it absorbs sap from the roots, to the detriment of the newborn asparagus. As soon as the asparagus emerges from the ground, it is removed by scooping out the mound, and cut cleanly (A, pl. 8, fig. 4) at its birth with a small serpette, then the earth is lifted. In this way, you never cut two asparagus shoots at a time, and there are no sections left on the roots. What's more, the soil, which is stirred every day, acquires a softness that further encourages asparagus development. If you follow all the above instructions to the letter, you'll unerringly obtain a large quantity of monstrous asparagus in all soils, and this in the third year, by planting one-year-old claws, which you can buy to avoid the one-year delay caused by sowing. But there is an urgent need for improved asparagus from Argenteuil. PLANTING ON THE PLAINS. Growing asparagus on the plain is the same as in the kitchen garden; it differs only in the preparation of the soil, to which greater care is taken. Asparagus is only planted on the plains for speculative purposes; the first condition is to choose a suitable soil, which can be prepared without adding too costly amendments. To do this, choose a medium-consistency soil, or a greasy calcareous sand, free of stones. At a pinch, a good ploughing could be followed by scarifying, but for an operation of this importance, it's better to plough with a spade. This ploughing is followed by a good manuring, then the lines are drawn at 1 meter instead of 1 meter 20 c., and the claws are also planted at a distance of one meter. Planting is done in the same way as in the vegetable garden, with the difference that the pits are made with a hoe and only the bottom is fertilized. All mounding and ripping procedures are the same. To make the most of the land before the asparagus starts producing, you can counter-plant the intervals between the rows in the vegetable garden with early cabbages, tomatoes, beans and dwarf peas, a few very light seedlings of early carrots or turnips, but in the latter case you should never sow closer than 25 c. to the edge of the pits, and put nothing between the asparagus plants. Only plant between the rows. FORCED ASPARAGUS. In our asparagus seedling, we kept a claw every 50 centimeters to provide us with a plant for forcing. With this two-year-old plant, we will grow green asparagus in winter, known in Paris as asperges aux petits pois, and with a one-year-old plant, white asparagus: these are two different crops; we will describe them both. GREEN ASPARAGUS. Green asparagus can be obtained in fifteen or twenty days in a greenhouse, and can therefore be grown at will throughout the winter, provided there is a good supply of claws. We build up a layer 60 to 80 centimetres thick, capable of providing 20 to 25 degrees of warmth; we cover with a few centimetres of potting soil, place the boxes, and wait a few days. When the layer has caught its first fire, we cover it with claws 18 of asparagus, laid side by side, then, using our fingers, we pour potting soil between the roots, and cover them with about four centimetres of potting soil. Maintain humidity with light sprinklings; if the temperature is cold, apply heaters more or less actively as needed, but in such a way as to obtain a temperature of 20 degrees under the frames. Cover at night with one or more mats, depending on how cold it is. When the asparagus start to grow, we take advantage of the sun to give them a little air in the middle of the day, when it's not freezing. We cut the asparagus as we go along, and if cultivation is well managed, they will produce for about two months. Claws that have produced green asparagus are no longer good for anything, so pull them out after harvesting and throw them in the manure. WHITE ASPARAGUS. The planting of asparagus to be forced is the same as that of asparagus in squares, with one-year-old claws planted with all the care indicated above, but with the difference that instead of establishing lines 1 meter 20 centimeters apart, and plant the asparagus at 1 metre 20 centimetres apart, form beds 1 metre 30 centimetres wide, separated by aisles 80 centimetres apart, and plant two lines of claws in this bed 1 metre 30 centimetres wide; each line will be placed 42 centimetres from the edge, and the claws will be planted 60 centimetres apart on the lines. Treat asparagus as you would vegetable garden asparagus for the first two years; force the third year, but as you can't force asparagus every year without damaging its life, take care to plant twice the quantity needed, so as to force only half and let the other half rest for a year. Asparagus is not harvested from resting plants, but left to grow untouched, and their vigor is further enhanced by removing the seeds as soon as they have formed. We start forcing asparagus in December and January; thirty days is enough to obtain produce. The asparagus is mounded with potting soil, the boxes are placed, filled with fresh horse manure, and the frames put in place. Next, the aisles separating the beds are emptied to a depth of around 50 centimetres, and filled with warm manure, well trodden and watered, right up to the top of the trunk. The stoves are reshuffled from time to time, and fresh manure is added when necessary. Finally, they are governed so as to maintain a constant temperature of 15 to 20 degrees under the frames. As soon as the first asparagus appear, remove all the manure placed in the trunk, and double the covers with straw mats during the night, so as to maintain the same temperature under the frames until the end of the harvest. There's no need to ventilate white asparagus; all care is taken to maintain an even temperature under the frames. When the harvest is over, the stoves are removed, but the frames are left on the chests for a few days, to prevent the asparagus from immediately moving from the temperature of the heated frames to that of the atmosphere. Air is breathed in every day, covered only at night, and the boxes and frames are removed when frosts are no longer to be feared. If the asparagus cultivation method I have described at length frightens some owners who are too attached to old customs, let them keep their old plants, and try only a line of 10 or 12 feet grown as I have indicated. Once they've seen the results, they won't hesitate to create a plant using the Argenteuil method. AUBERGINE. Eggplants are not widely grown in northern and central France, and can only produce passable fruit on bedding. It is sown under a frame in February, then transplanted very close to the nursery, on a slightly cooler bed, and then transplanted again, at a greater distance, on an even cooler bed. These transplants are necessary to make the roots branch out and strengthen the plant. Towards the end of April, the plant is placed on a dull bed and covered with cloches. Eggplant requires frequent watering. To speed up fruit production, eggplant should be pruned in the same way as melons. The main stem is cut back to two or three eyes at most, to allow arms to develop. The flowers appear on the branches of the arms, so pinch them off on the seventh or eighth eye, to force the branches to develop. Independently of this care, we remove all new stems growing on the root collar. When the fruits are well formed, we pinch at two nodes above, and ruthlessly remove all new shoots, to concentrate the action of the sap on the fruits, so that they acquire remarkable volume, and ripen faster. 4 BETTERAVE. Table beet is grown in very small quantities in the kitchen garden. It doesn't deserve a special place, but can be counter-planted in garden plot no. 1, with cabbages or in an autumn artichoke patch. Castelnaudary beet is preferable for salads. Sow around March 15 on a worn layer, and replant when the roots are well formed. If you wanted to harvest beet for animals, nothing could be easier, and without any expense. The yellow Globe variety is preferable for this purpose; around March 15th, sow on a worn layer, like the previous one; using this method, you obtain excellent plants, which you counter-plant: 1° Between the rows of the asparagus patch; 2° Between the various cabbage, artichoke and cardoon crops in patch no. 1; 3° In patch no. 2, between strawberry plants, lettuces, etc., etc.; 4° Finally, in the rows of the asparagus patch, sow on a worn layer, like the previous one, and counter-plant when the roots are well formed, etc.; 4° Finally, in plot no. 3, counter-planted in green beans, where it will still produce quite satisfactory products for the animals. Beet does not deserve the honors of a special crop in the vegetable garden, but nevertheless in a house where there are cows and pigs, a clever gardener can give a certain quantity of beet counter-planted in several squares. It's a crop that yields a product without requiring a penny of expense. CAPUCIN. The small variety should be grown, as it has the advantage of being able to do without supports. A bed of nasturtiums is useful in the vegetable garden to decorate salads with flowers, and to harvest green seeds for preserving. Sow in the ground, around mid-March, in a bed in plot no. 3, in staggered clumps, 40 centimetres apart in all directions. A bed of nasturtiums produces flowers and seeds in sufficient quantity. CARDONS. An excellent vegetable for winter storage. Sow in pots, under a frame and on a warm bed in April; when the plant has acquired a certain strength, and no longer has anything to fear from grubs or curculio, place it in plot no. 1, one meter apart in a staggered row. Cardoon requires a lot of water and abundant feeding. It can be counter-planted in May in a bed of Yorck cabbages; if the cabbages are good to harvest at the same time as the cardoons can be put in place, we benefit from waiting a few days; when the early cabbages are removed, we plough, plant the cardoons, and counter-plant them with cauliflowers, which have time to give their harvest before the cardoons have invaded all the space intended for them. These two crops combine all the better as they both require a lot of water. Around September, the strongest cardoon stalks are bleached, but this should only be done as and when necessary, as stuffed cardoons soon rot, especially in wet weather. When the stalks are strong enough to be blanched, first tie them loosely with three or four straw ties; then wrap the whole stalk with long straw, securing it with three or four new ties. Twenty or twenty-five days at most after this operation, the cardoons are ready to eat. They are blanched as needed, and towards the end of the season, before the frosts, any plants that have not been blanched are pulled out and planted in the cellar or vegetable greenhouse, where they blanch naturally without being stuffed, and provide a vegetable that is a pleasure to find for part of the winter. Cardoon is very easy to grow; if necessary, sow it in the open ground in April, and it still gives good results. CARROT. This is an indispensable vegetable in the kitchen, and one that should never be lacking; with a little care, you can avoid those unfortunate carrots, hard as wood and with no flavor whatsoever, that are still so often found as soon as you leave Paris. I've said that the short red carrot from Holland provides new carrots all year round; it's the best, the most tender, the one that should be grown to the exclusion of all others, in the homeowner's garden. The first seedlings of this excellent variety are sown in March, and the last in September. Towards the end of February or in the first few days of March, a bed in plot no. 2 is ploughed deeply; a border is laid around the bed to retain water for watering, and the soil is vigorously harrowed with a hooked fork to break up any clods; the seed is then broadcast, and the seed is harrowed with a roughing rake, followed by a final stroke with a fine rake to remove stones and foreign bodies. Finish the operation by covering the whole bed with about two centimetres of manure from the demolition of the layers, almost in the form of compost. If the weather is dry and sunny, water every day until emergence, and even afterwards if the drought persists. A sown bed must never dry out, and a young seedling must be watered copiously until it has acquired a certain strength. The success of the harvest depends on how well the seedling is raised: if it has suffered in the beginning, the harvest will be poor. The layer of manure that covers the seedling protects it from drought; this is one of the purposes it must fulfil, but the main purpose is to be dissolved by watering and rainfall, and to provide the young roots with abundant nourishment; if moisture is lacking, the manure is not dissolved, and the main purpose is missed. It goes without saying that lettuce should never be sown in carrot beds, as this produces bad lettuce plants and considerably harms the carrot harvest. As soon as the carrots are well established, weed them as described in the chapter on weeding; thin out any areas that are too thick, and if necessary, transplant carefully into empty areas. If cultivation has been carried out correctly, the first carrots will be harvested at the end of April and the first days of May. As soon as the whole crop has reached a good size, the leaves are cut off and everything is pulled off with a flat-tine fork; the crop is put into the vegetable greenhouse, the bed is turned over, and turnips are sown on top. The carrots from the first field sowings always arrive at the end of the harvest of carrots grown under frames. You could even save yourself the trouble of sowing early carrots, as late sowings can replace them. Short red Dutch carrots are sown successively until September. Sow in June after an early turnip or salad crop, in July and August after an onion crop, and in September after salads or tomatoes. September sowings can dispense with frame cultivation, and here's how. As frost approaches, carrots are a third to half their size; the vegetable greenhouse is well stocked with May, June and July seedlings; September seedlings are kept in the ground throughout the winter, to provide for the last needs. This is done by covering the whole bed with straw during hard frosts, and in spring we have excellent new carrots, which we pull up and bury in sand, in the cellar or in the vegetable greenhouse, and this harvest only costs the trouble of preventing the carrots from freezing by covering them with straw. September sowings are often preferable to those grown in frames, as the carrots are almost as tender, but much tastier. The bell carrot is the only carrot that should be grown in a frame. Square no. h, which is used for beds, seeds, vegetable nurseries and flower breeding, can also be used for carrot seedlings, once the first flowers have been removed or after a vegetable nursery has been pulled out. This can be a valuable resource in a house with a large staff of servants and animals. The first 16 are sown with Achicourt carrots, the second with white carrots with green collars. If the gardener is intelligent and hard-working, if he takes good advantage of his crop rotation, if he counter-plant well, and if he doesn't lack water and manure, he will easily find the food for a head of large livestock, without any expense, and without harming the production of vegetables, in a slightly extended kitchen garden. When carrots are brought in for storage in the cellar or vegetable greenhouse, cut off all the leaves near the collar, lay them on a bed of sand; cover with sand, replace with a bed of carrots, and so on. Carrots laid down and buried in sand grow very little. Remove the leaves as soon as they appear. CELERY. There are two kinds of celery to grow in the vegetable garden: celery, whose leaves are eaten as salads, and celeriac, whose root is edible. (See varieties for the various vegetable gardens, owner's, tenant's, etc.). Celery requires a lot of fertilizer and a lot of water: it is planted in plot no. 1 of the vegetable garden. To grow celery for most of the season, sow in rows, in layers, from January to March; as soon as the plant is a little strong, transplant it into a nursery on a less warm layer, and finally, as soon as frosts are no longer a threat, counterplant it in Yorck cabbage, in 8 first-season cauliflowers, or in an artichoke patch. We continue sowing in the open ground from April onwards, and establish nurseries in the open ground, in plot no. 1, or in plot no. 4, in a bed smoked for the purpose! On beds, as in the open ground, we always sow in rows, in order to have a better-formed plant. Planting in a nursery, in a well-smoked and copiously watered bed, is the best way to ensure that the seedling comes into its own. Transplanting prevents the stem from developing too much, and encourages the formation of the plant. Transplanting is done in the nursery, in rows 25 to 30 centimetres apart, with the plants staggered 20 centimetres apart. The purpose of this operation is not only to encourage the development of the plant and make it grow, but also to preserve the celery for some time, without delaying the harvest in any way, if there is no free soil on which to plant it immediately. First-, second- and third-season celery is tied with two or three straw ties to blanch it. Celery planted in early cabbage is removed as needed. When you want to keep celery throughout the winter, or blanch it on the spot to preserve all its qualities, here's how it's done: This celery is counter-planted in early savoy cabbage, to be harvested in September; the far-sighted gardener should always have a few beds of Ulm cabbage, good for harvesting at the opening of the hunting season, to eat the old partridges. As soon as these cabbages have been removed, the celery planted in them is milled to a height of 25 to 30 centimetres, after tying it with two or three straw ties; it is then completely blanched by filling the spaces between the rows with straw; when it freezes, a straw blanket is added over the leaves, to protect them from the frost to which celery is very sensitive. The cover is made thicker or thinner, depending on the temperature, and is lifted every day to remove the necessary number of plants. Celery root, grown solely for its tubers, requires a different type of cultivation. It is sown in the open ground in April, transplanted in a nursery at the end of May or in the first days of June, then planted out in July in the vegetable garden's No. 1 plot, in 25 to 30-centimetre-deep pits; the pits are 60 centimetres apart, and the celery stalks are spaced 25 centimetres apart. The soil extracted from the pits is kept in reserve on the right and left sides, to be used later to refill the pits and butter the celery. Celery root requires a lot of water. When planted in pits, you fill them with water once or twice a day, depending on the temperature. As the plants develop, they are replanted with the soil from the pits, and towards the end of the season, the celery is ridged, bringing the soil from between the 20 CELERY. rows to the foot of the plants. This last mounding greatly encourages tuber development. Celery root is left in the ground until the first frosts, at which time it is dug up and buried in the cellar or vegetable greenhouse, to be consumed throughout the winter, when this little-known and little-cultivated vegetable is often a precious resource. CERFEUIL. A far-sighted gardener should never let chervil go to waste. Nothing could be easier: sow little and often, from March to September, in the open ground, and during part of the winter, in a greenhouse. In spring, sow in plot no. 1 until April, between cabbages, cauliflowers, artichokes etc., etc., a few at a time, and repeat every two weeks. From April to June, seedlings are sown in square no. 2; and from July to September in squares no. 3 and h. The latter seedlings set seed less easily; they can be sheltered from the first frosts with litter or straw mats. During the winter, chervil is sown between melons, tomatoes, etc., under a frame. MUSHROOMS. Mushroom cultivation is not essential in towns, where markets provide large quantities of mushrooms with every guarantee, 16 but it does become essential when you're far from the main population centers, since in such cases you often have to resort to mushrooms harvested from meadows, and this has often led to fatal accidents. Cultivated mushrooms are never poisonous. Nothing is easier than to bring them, with a little care, into a cellar or vegetable greenhouse. The success of this crop lies in the choice and preparation of the manure. The best of all manures for making mushrooms is donkey manure, but only manure from donkeys fed on hay, not green plants, in which case the manure is worthless; in second place comes mule manure, and in third horse manure. It goes without saying that all manures intended for use in mushroom production must come from dry-fed animals; that these manures must be used pure, without any mixture of foreign bodies: hay, plant detritus, etc. Horse manure is the most suitable for mushroom production. Horse manure, the most commonly used, requires special selection for this purpose. Manure from luxury horses is too strawy; farmyard manure is often mixed with other types of manure; the best is inn manure, where only whole horses are received, and where, as bedding is spared, the manure is free of long straw: the straw it contains, broken up and trampled on by the horses, disintegrates very easily. It takes about two to three truckloads of pure manure to build a mushroom bed. You start by placing the manure in beds, in the open air, taking care to mix it well as if making a layer, and to moisten it enough to develop fermentation. A fortnight later, the manure is reshuffled, treaded and moistened again if it is too dry, so that it begins to decompose completely. A third reworking is sometimes necessary, when certain parts are still tough. Manure is best used when it no longer gives off much heat, when it is in the process of decomposing into what gardeners call "fat butter"; in other words, when it has acquired a uniform brown color, when the straw breaks at the slightest touch, and when it is neither too dry nor too wet, which is easily recognized by pressing it into the hand, where it should retain its given shape, remaining moist and unctuous, but without letting any water escape. It takes some experience to recognize when manure is right to use, but I repeat, that's where success lies, and if it fails, it will be because the manure was mixed with grass or hay, or was used too dry or too wet, too cold or too hot. To enlighten those who lack practice, to be used successfully, manure must have thrown off its fire, while still retaining some heat, but a very gentle heat. When the manure is well prepared

either in a cellar, or in the vegetable greenhouse, 3 it doesn't matter, as long as the chosen spot is of equal temperature, and is deprived of air and light; if necessary, the openings are blocked to obtain these conditions. In the chosen spot, a humpback-shaped layer is then built up, about 80 centimetres wide at the base and 20 centimetres at the top. The manure is placed in beds as for the other layers, treaded as you go, and watered if necessary. Leave the bed for a few days; if the manure has been well prepared, and the bed well raised, it will give off 15 to 20 degrees of heat after seven or eight days. Now is the time to plant it, i.e. to introduce the mushroom spawn. If you don't have mushroom spawn at hand, you can buy some from a seed merchant. Holes are made five to six centimetres wide on both sides of the bed. Two rows are usually made: the first 30 centimetres from the ground, with the holes about 35 centimetres apart; the second 35 centimetres from the first, with the holes staggered at 35 centimetres. A piece of spawn is inserted into each of these holes to a depth of four or five centimetres, and immediately covered with the extracted manure. The spawn is packed down well with the hand, so that it adheres to the layer on all sides, and we wait another eight or ten days. At the end of this time, the white is well set, i.e. it has gained the layer, and is reproduced in all its extent. Then finish the operation by covering the entire layer with dry earth containing half plaster or lime mortar, all well amalgamated together. This covering, about three centimetres thick, is applied with the back of a shovel. If the layer is too dry, it should be lightly drenched from time to time with a very fine-headed watering can. Moisten, but do not wet; moisture is necessary for the development of the fungus, but water destroys it. It takes a little experience to apply the right amount of water, in the right proportions.
