Six weeks later, production begins, and is sustained for four or five months when the bed is well made and cared for. People who have not yet grown mushrooms should not be discouraged if they do not succeed the first time. Most of the time, their failure will come from nothing at all; for this type of cultivation, a little experience is needed, and this experience can only be acquired through practice. There are various ways of making mushroom spawn: I'll refrain from describing them, as mushroom cultivation is a very secondary activity in the kitchen garden. In addition, a certain amount of experience is needed to make good blanc de champignon, and the owner who grows only for his own consumption will benefit from buying it; he will have the double advantage of saving money, and the certainty of having good blanc. CHICORICE. Chicory deserves a large place in the vegetable garden; it makes a good salad all year round, and an excellent vegetable when cooked. We grow three types of chicory: Wild chicory, which is useful for animals in summer, and produces an etiolated salad called capuchin beard in winter; curly chicory, for eating in salads and for cooking; and lastly, escarole, for salads and also for cooking, either on its own or mixed with chicory. WILD CHICORY. I said earlier that an intelligent gardener should provide animals with inexpensive fodder in a large vegetable garden. Wild chicory, whose roots alone can be used to make capuchin beard at the start of winter, will give us a certain quantity of leaves throughout the summer, which are excellent for poultry, rabbits and even cows. We sow wild chicory in borders in squares n° 3 and n° h, around the month of March; we make several leaf cuts during the summer, and towards the end of October, we pull up the roots with a fork, without breaking them, which we lay down in the cellar, on a bed of soil that is neither too dry nor too wet, placing the collar outside, and the pivot of the roots in the center of the layer of soil; cover with a bed of soil ten to 15 centimetres thick; place another bed of roots, and so on up to a height of about one metre (pl. 9, fig. 1). If the soil is too dry, the plant is lightly hoed, and a few days later, the leaves begin to appear yellow and very tender. Harvest as they grow. Capuchin beard is far from being a sought-after salad, especially for the landowner who can obtain curly endive and lettuces all winter long under his frames, but it becomes a precious resource in the countryside where lettuce is in short supply all winter long, and the farmer will not be indifferent to harvesting leaves for the animals in summer, and a very healthy salad in winter, without any expense whatsoever, which will only cost him the trouble of lowering a few roots and three or four barrows of soil to the cellar. CURLY ENDIVE AND ESCAROLE. As their cultivation is exactly the same, I combine these two species in terms of cultivation care, noting however that scarole, being less tender than chicory, should be grown in smaller quantities, and always in the open ground, whereas curly endive should be grown in beds, under frames and cloches, so as to be available in all seasons. There can never be too much chicory in a vegetable garden; what isn't eaten as salad is cooked as a vegetable. The gardener who manages his crops well must have plenty of chicory at all times of the year. This is easy to do, with the right counter-planting and the care I'm about to describe. The first chicory seedlings are sown indoors, in December and January; fine Italian chicory 9,357 is the best variety for growing indoors and under cloches, and even in the open ground, except for the last open-ground seedlings, when Meaux chicory gives very good results. Chicory sown indoors has the disadvantage of rising quickly, which can easily be avoided by transplanting two or three times in the nursery before planting. As soon as the seedling has four leaves, it is removed and transplanted into a nursery at a distance of h or 5 centimetres, between other crops, under a cooler frame. A fortnight or three weeks later, the seedling is removed again and transplanted under a colder frame, and sometimes even a third transplant, always on a more temperate bed. These successive transplantings have the effect of momentarily suspending the plant's overactive vegetation, and preparing it for harvesting. When the plant is strong enough, it is placed under a frame on a colder bed. Chicory can be planted under a frame between harvests. Seedlings sown in February under a frame are also raised in a nursery under a frame, but placed on a warm layer under a bell. The frames are covered with straw mats, and the cloches with bedding, and air is supplied whenever the temperature permits. Sow under frames every two weeks, from December to March, to ensure a constant supply. Chicory requires a lot of water, and it's only by watering copiously that it becomes tender; when it has acquired a certain strength, it is tied with a straw tie to whiten the heart, and as soon as it is tied, it is watered only at the foot, with the neck of the watering can. If you wet the leaves after binding, the salad will rot. Chicory is sown in the ground from mid-April to mid-September. In a pinch, you could thin out the plant and wait until it's strong enough to be transplanted, but it's better to put it in a nursery, as it's less likely to bolt, especially during the summer when it's been transplanted. Chicory seed needs to be very lightly covered for good emergence. Sow in rows, and cover with a little top soil. In May, the good seedling can be counter-planted in plot no. 2, between early lettuces. Once the early carrots have been harvested, the bed can be turned over, mulched and planted in its entirety with chicory, staggered at 30 or 35 centimetres in all directions. The bed is edged to retain water from watering, and mulched before transplanting. During June and July, chicory beds can be established in plot no. 3, after a harvest of peas or green beans. Good mulching and frequent watering are enough to obtain very fine chicory, which almost never rises in plot no. 3 during the hottest weather. You can counter-plant chicory with other lettuces, or sow lamb's lettuce in the bed for the off-season. The last sowings, at the end of August and September, provide chicory for part of the winter. Those planted in September are good for binding in October, planted in rows 40 centimetres apart and staggered 35 centimetres apart. As soon as the chicories have been bound, they are lightly ridged to protect them from the cold, watering is stopped and, when it rains, they are covered with straw mats to prevent rotting. Excellent chicory can be kept for a long time using this simple procedure. If frost threatens, cover at night with bedding. Chicory, like all other lettuces, should only be tied when thoroughly dry, and in dry weather. August and September seedlings can be transplanted under cloche, with five chicory plants per cloche; they are left in the air as long as the temperature allows, covered as soon as it gets cold, and if necessary covered with straw, at night and even during the day, if frost is felt. In this way, chicory can be harvested for part of the winter. Another way of keeping chicory for a long time is to transplant it at the end of the season, side by side in a cold frame, i.e. without a layer, and simply in potting soil. Cover with straw mats during the night, and if the cold becomes intense, surround the box with manure. In this way, with little effort and no expense, you obtain chicory, the heart of which can be eaten as a salad, and the head of which is good for cooking. To save on winter supplies under frames, cloches and bedding, it's always wise to have a certain quantity of chicory bound and almost ready to harvest towards the end of the season. We pull out the best ones, and replant them side by side in the vegetable greenhouse or cellar, where they complete their growth. A good supply harvested in this way lasts for a long time, and spares a great deal of effort in the cultivation of cloches and frames. For this, as for everything else, the gardener needs to organize his crops well, direct them with intelligence, and look after them constantly. CABBAGE. The cultivation of cabbage is very important, not in the owner's vegetable garden, but in those of tenants, farmers, schoolteachers, communities, stations and camps, where cabbage is a precious resource. I'm going to divide cabbages into five distinct classes, and choose the most suitable varieties for each of the gardens I've mentioned. These five divisions are: 1° Smooth-leaf cabbages; 2° Curly-leaf savoy cabbages; 3° Evergreen cabbages; 4° Root cabbages; 5° Cauliflowers. The main varieties of cabbage are : Yorck, cœur de bœuf, cabus blanc, Saint-Denis, Alsace. Quintal, Vaugirard, red cabbages, etc., etc. 8 Sow cabbages in the following order: Yorck and cœur de bœuf, second half of August and first days of September. They are transplanted to the nursery in October, to be planted again in March and April. All other cabbage varieties can be sown from the second half of July to the second half of August, and in March and April for later cabbages. The best varieties of savoy cabbage are: Ulm cabbage, Milan short, Milan des Vertus, Brussels sprouts, etc., etc... Savoy cabbages are not as large as cabbages, but they are more delicate, have no large ribs, and do not smell musky. The smallest and earliest of all, the Ulm cabbage is especially recommended for its good quality, as much as for its earliness. Savoy cabbages are sown from March to May, and transplanted to the nursery in April and June. Savoy cabbages tolerate the first frosts very well, which soften them and make them taste better. Cabbages, on the other hand, fear the cold, and must be harvested before frosts, which inevitably destroy them. Green cabbages are much appreciated in western France; they are used to make purgative soups and are a precious resource for animals. The most productive variety is the Poitou cabbage. They are sown in March and April, and in July and August, to produce the first crops in autumn and spring. In the second 21 year, the harvest of leaves is considerable, and becomes a precious product for animals when you have a garden of a certain size. Root cabbages are cultivated more for animals than as edible plants; the main varieties are: kohlrabi, Turnep cabbage, Rutabaga, etc. Root cabbage is sown in June and July, between other crops, and provides a good winter supply of roots that are excellent for animals. This plant is highly frost-resistant, and is harvested as and when needed. Kohlrabi can be sown with advantage in plot no. 1, after two cabbage harvests; after early potatoes, cardoons, etc.; and as a catch crop, when the land has already amply paid its rent with one, two or three harvests. Cauliflower is widely cultivated in the kitchen garden: there are several varieties, of which the main ones are: tender or Salomon, hard and semi-hard, and finally, in my opinion, the most recommendable variety, semi-hard Paris cauliflower. Since cauliflower must be harvested in all seasons in a well-tended vegetable garden, it requires special cultivation, which we'll come back to after dealing with the other varieties. In general, cabbages like humidity; they need substantial, abundantly smoked soil, and copious watering. They should be planted in plot 1 of the vegetable garden. Cabbage seedlings planted in the open ground should be placed in the shade, in rows, in deeply ploughed soil, abundantly smoked with decomposed cow manure, which is the best for cabbages. Cabbage plants should also be watered copiously. The surface of the soil must never be dry: one or two waterings a day are necessary until emergence, and when the seedling emerges from the ground, it must be watered two or three times a day, if the weather is dry, until it is ready to go into the nursery. All these special seedlings can be sown in plot no. 4, in carefully prepared soil. Readers should not be alarmed by the precautions to be taken to raise good seedlings, as nothing is easier or takes up less space. From five to eight hundred first-rate cabbage plants can be grown on one square metre. In principle, all cabbages and cauliflowers should be transplanted in nurseries. These nurseries are created in plot no. 4, in deeply ploughed beds that have been smoked as much as possible. The beds are edged to retain water from watering; the rows are set at 25 or 30 centimetres, and the plants are transplanted in staggered rows 20 centimetres apart. The nursery offers the following advantages: 1° Replanting as soon as the seedling has four leaves tires the plant and prepares it to bolt, by halting vegetation a little; 2° Seedlings placed at equal distances vegetate equally, acquire the same vigor and bolt in the nursery, which cannot happen in seedlings where they are in too much of a hurry; 3° Seedlings that do not suffer in the nursery can be left there until the soil is available; It can be removed in clods with the de-planter, and this second de-planting also helps to increase the size of the head; 4° Cabbage nurseries occupying a very small space offer the advantage of easily providing all the necessary fertilizer, and the most copious watering, the only means to be used to infallibly obtain elite seedlings; 5° Nurseries also offer the advantage of multiplying harvests, by giving half-raised seedlings which immediately replace the harvest removed. The nursery is the key to production, when it is properly understood: not only are the products of the vegetable garden doubled, but plantings are also made only with elite seedlings. Spring cabbages, especially Yorck cabbages, often go to seed without budding. We pull them out and replant them a few hours later, in the same place, to tire them out and prevent them from going to seed. Sometimes, in hot, humid springs, almost all the early cabbages go to seed, and the harvest is lost. This inconvenience rarely occurs with cabbages grown in nurseries and planted out in spring. Yorck cabbages can be counter-planted in salads, 10 in second-season artichokes, in plot no. 1, and in plot no. 2, between strawberry plants. The other cabbages, sown in July and August, March and April, with the care I've just indicated, are also transplanted in the nursery and set out, counter-planted in square no. 1, between early cabbages, spring cauliflowers, early potatoes, or after a harvest of first-season artichokes. Savoy cabbages are preferable for spring sowing, as their quality is far superior to that of cabbages. Ulm cabbage can be sown on a bed, under a bell and under a frame, in February, transplanted in a nursery in March or April, in the open ground, and three months later you'll have a perfectly heady cabbage of superior quality. The other varieties of Savoy cabbage are successively sown in the open ground, and transplanted into the nursery as I have indicated, to replace the crops removed in plot no. 4. Cabbages are planted at different distances, depending on the proportions they need to reach. Yorck, oxheart and Ulm cabbages can be planted staggered at 50 centimetres in all directions; cabbages at 70 to 80 centimetres in all directions, depending on their size; quintals should be planted at 90 centimetres and even at one metre; Milans at a distance of 60 to 70 centimetres, depending on their size. General rule: avoid planting cabbages too close together, as they tend to grow too high. Brussels sprouts should be planted at a distance of around 70 centimetres, after having been raised in a nursery, like all other cabbages. If you want small, hard apples that don't burst, take care to remove the leaves in whose axils they are born, as soon as they have formed. When the leaves are left on, the apples burst, become very thick, and remain hollow; they are not presentable in this state. Brussels sprout seed harvested in France is worthless. You have to buy them in Belgium to be sure of getting good products. Let's not forget that cabbages in general need a lot of water, and that it's urgent to water them copiously, if you want to get good and beautiful cabbages quickly. As soon as the head forms, water it at the base, using the neck of the watering can, and avoid wetting the head. What I've said about kale and root cabbage doesn't need to be repeated here. Let us say, however, that if the vegetable garden is very large, and the house has animals, these two plants will provide a valuable resource for them during the winter, by transplanting the former and sowing the latter in square No. A, in all the beds that will have been devoted to vegetable nurseries and flower breeding. Root cabbage can also be sown very lightly, in July, in plot no. 1, between winter cabbages, cardoons, etc., etc. I said that cauliflowers should be harvested almost all year round in the vegetable garden. For this purpose, we sow them at different times: 1° On beds and under frames, during winter, for harvesting in spring and summer. 2° In the open ground, during the summer, for harvesting in autumn; 3° On a bed and under a bell, in autumn, for harvesting during part of the winter. In the second half of January and the first half of February, cauliflowers are sown on beds and under trays. As soon as the first two leaves have formed, transplant to a cooler bed at 6 centimetres, and transplant again, but a little further away, to a cooler bed between permanent plants. Towards the end of March or in the first few days of April, this plant is placed in the ground to produce cauliflowers towards the end of May or in the first fortnight of June. For the first few days, we shelter the plants at night with straw mats, placed on stakes driven around the bed, and joined together with a spur. Sow again in March, on a warm bed; transplant a first time in a nursery on a worn bed, then transplant again in April, in the open ground, to counter-plant in salads, late artichokes or early cabbages, to obtain cauliflowers in July and August. Towards the end of April and in the course of May, cauliflowers are sown in the open ground, in the shade; they are also transplanted into nurseries in the open ground, like head cabbages, but with the difference that transplanting takes place twice. The first nursery is planted as follows: rows 15 cm apart, staggered plants 8 cm apart. When the cabbages touch each other, they are transplanted a second time: rows 30 centimetres apart, plants staggered 20 centimetres apart. Replanting cauliflower is one of the first keys to success. When the plant has been well raised in the nursery, success is guaranteed. A lot of manure, a little bit of water and a huge amount of water is the best recipe for magnificent cauliflowers. Let's also say that cauliflowers don't like soils that are too clayey; a crumbly, slightly light soil suits them better than clay. If your garden is very clayey, you should always choose the loosest beds for planting; in this case, you'll get excellent results by mixing a little sand and ash with the fertilizer. In June, sow cauliflowers in the open ground; transplant to nurseries in the open ground, taking care to water two or three times a day, if the weather is hot and dry; then counter-plant into melons, on used beds, in August. With copious watering, magnificent cauliflowers can be obtained in September, October and even November. When the melon beds, and some of the beds in plot no. 1, are well stocked with late cauliflowers, you can keep them for a very long time, by doing the following when the frosts are approaching: pull out all those with ripe apples, cut off the core and the largest leaves, and hang them upside down in the vegetable greenhouse, where they will keep for a long time. When cauliflowers are stored in this way, they lose a certain amount of their vegetable water through evaporation. When you want to eat them, it's useful to cut out the core and soak them in water for a day, taking care not to wet the apple. Any plants whose apples have not fully developed are removed in clods and planted side by side in potting soil under a cold frame. Water carefully, without wetting the leaves or shoots, to ensure a good start. Cover at night with straw mats; provide air during the day, and you'll still get passable cauliflowers for part of the winter. When it freezes, we surround the trunk of the frame with a manure stove. The last cauliflower seedlings are sown in September, either on a used bed or in the ground. Transplant to a nursery, in the open ground, under a cloche, in a sheltered spot. Towards the end of October, a second transplant is made in a nursery on an old layer and under a frame. If the season is fine, we can risk a certain quantity of seedlings, under cloche, in the open ground, in a warm and sheltered place. Take care to cover the cloches well, and surround them with bedding when it's freezing, and give them air whenever the temperature permits. As frost approaches, warmers are placed around the boxes. This well-cared-for plant is good for setting out in November and December. It is counter-planted on layers and under chassis between chicory or seedlings that are to be removed shortly. Six cauliflowers are placed per frame panel, and again counter-planted with lettuce or nursery plants. Air is given as often as possible; then, as the cauliflowers grow, we raise the boxes, placing bricks or even straw plugs underneath, and taking care to plug all openings when it's cold. Cauliflowers treated in this way can be harvested from April to May. Growing cauliflower is not difficult, but it does require care. Nursery transplanting is the first step to success. Cauliflower requires a great deal of water, and I can confirm that most of the failures in this crop are due to sparing watering. You need to water a lot until the apples have formed; then you water again, but less abundantly, and only with the spout, so as not to wet either the apples or the leaves. Care should also be taken to cover the apples with fresh leaves; when left exposed to light, they turn green and are never tightly packed. Cauliflowers should only be planted in square No. 1 of the vegetable garden, and in layers, if you want to obtain beautiful produce. Several authors recommend growing tender, semi-hard and hard cauliflower. Tender cauliflowers don't hold their heads well, while hard cauliflowers are hopelessly slow to peel. The semi-hard variety does well everywhere, especially the demi-dur de Paris, whose cultivation I have no hesitation in recommending exclusively. CIBOULE. To be planted as a border plant in plot no. 2; propagated by sprouting cloves planted in spring and autumn. CIVETTE. To be planted in borders in plot no. 2; multiplies by root splinters and is planted in spring. CUCUMBER. Cucumbers can be grown in three ways: under a frame, under a cloche, or in the open air, depending on when you want to harvest them. Early cucumbers are sown under a frame towards the end of January, and transplanted to a nursery on another layer as soon as they can be transplanted. They are shaded in the sun with light-colored cloths, and covered at night with straw mats. About three weeks later, they are placed on warm layers, four feet per frame panel, and buried up to the cotyledons to give them more vigor. The con14 combre is sensitive to the cold, and needs to be well covered at night; as with all sashes, we double or triple the cover with straw mats, depending on the temperature, and provide air whenever the weather permits. When the main stem has four or five leaves, it is cut with a grafting tool or a serpette on the first two leaves to obtain two lateral branches, which are pinched off at three or four leaves. It's a good idea to mulch the entire bed when making the first cut. The new branches are pinched off at three to five eyes. Eight to ten fruits are kept per plant; the best are chosen, the surrounding ones are removed, and the fruit is pinched at two leaves above. Once you've chosen the ten cucumbers you want to keep, pinch off any branches that are growing too long, and any new shoots, to concentrate all the sap's action on the fruit. This method produces cucumbers in May and June. To grow cucumbers under a cloche, sow towards the end of March under a frame, then plant in April under a cloche. Air is provided whenever weather permits, and the cloches are carefully covered at night, if necessary with bedding. Air is essential for plants, so it's a good idea to gradually get them used to air and light, and to uncover them completely as soon as they can stand the sun. Prune in the same way as under glass, but lengthen the pruning by one or two eyes, depending on the plant's vigor. Bell cucumbers produce from June to August. To grow in the open air, make a hole 40 centimetres deep and 50 centimetres in diameter, fill it with manure, cover it with 20 centimetres of compost and sow it in May in the open air. Prune as indicated, and cucumbers are produced in August and September. Gherkin cucumbers are grown in the open ground, as I've just indicated, with only the pruning differing. The main stem is trimmed to three leaves to obtain three lateral branches, which are left to elongate at will; they are carefully laid out on mulch, so that they do not intertwine. Gherkins are removed every day, which should never be overlooked, as by the next day they are too large to be used. All cucumbers require copious watering. WATERCRESS. Watercress has been cultivated in tubs three-quarters filled with soil and covered with water. This requires infinite care, and despite all the effort, it is very difficult to prevent the water from rotting and imparting its foul odor to the cress. It is better to abstain than to obtain such a product. Watercress is easy to grow with a little running water. Dig a pit one meter fifty centimeters wide and forty centimeters9 deep, in a place where a stream or river water can pass through. The bottom of the pit is smoked with cow manure, and cress is ploughed and transplanted in rows 20 centimetres apart, with the feet staggered at 15 centimetres. It is useful to secure each plant with a small wooden hook to prevent water from uprooting it. After transplanting, let about 10 to 12 centimetres of water come into the pit, and adjust the gutters so that it runs slowly but continuously. A few weeks later, the cress starts to grow, and is harvested as needed. CRESS ALÉNOIS. Watercress is prized for its pungent flavor; it makes an excellent seasoning, but has the disadvantage of going to seed very quickly. It should be sown every two weeks or three weeks at the most, to ensure a continuous supply. It can be sown as a border plant in plot no. 4, so that it doesn't go to seed as quickly, and in slightly shaded areas. SHALLOT. Shallots multiply by shattering cloves, the smallest being the best and most fertile; they can be planted in borders or beds, 15 centimeters apart in plot no. 2; they can also be counter-planted in the same plot, in strawberry beds. Shallots are pulled off as soon as the leaf has wilted; they are left to dry for three or four days in the sun in an alley, and then put in a dry attic, where they can be stored until the next harvest. SPINACH. Although it is a vegetable of more than secondary importance, it is useful to grow a few beds of it in the vegetable garden, as a variation to bring in vegetables, and as a resource when the garden is poor. Spinach requires well-smoked soil, and fairly copious watering; the greatest disadvantage of this vegetable is that it goes to seed very quickly; you have to sow it every three weeks to always have some. The sorrel-leaf variety is the least prone to bolting, which is why I adopt it for all vegetable gardens. Spinach can be sown from March to September; as a secondary crop, it can be sown in clumps in plot no. 1, between cabbages, cauliflowers, artichokes, cardoons, etc.; and in plot no. 2, in beds, after a harvest of carrots or onions. Sow in rows 30 cm apart. July and August seedlings are less likely to bolt, especially in plot no. 2, and can be cut several times; if you want to have spinach in winter, you can sow it in autumn on a cold bed and under a frame. When the spinach has been affected by frost, soak it in cold water and leave it to cool slowly in a room. It will come back to life perfectly and can be eaten without any problems. Spinach loves water and requires frequent watering. ESTRAGON. This aromatic plant is much sought-after for seasoning, and is propagated by planting in August, 30 centimeters apart, in borders in plot no. 3. As frost approaches, we cover the stumps with a little decomposed manure to protect them from frost damage. If you want to have tarragon over the winter, nothing could be easier: in the autumn, you unfold a few plants and replant them under a frame, on warm or lukewarm beds, as required. BOG BEANS. Beans can be grown as an early crop under a frame, but they have one serious drawback, which is why they should not be grown at all: they are invaded by so many aphids that it is very difficult, not to say impossible, to get rid of them. If you want to harvest broad beans early, sow them in a greenhouse in January, and plant them in the ground in early March. A plank is laid out in plot no. 3, in which furrows are made 35 to 40 centimetres apart and around 15 centimetres18 metres deep. The beans are planted in staggered rows 25 centimetres apart. Cover at night with bedding when frost is feared. When the plant is well established and has grown a little, hoe it vigorously and fill in the furrows; the plants thus re-planted acquire greater vigor. You can sow a few very light radishes while hoeing; you water quite copiously, and as soon as the beans flower, you pinch the end of the stem to concentrate all the action of the sap on the fruit. If the beans are harvested in dresses, i.e. when they are a quarter of their size, cut them off at the base as soon as they have been harvested, cover them with a blanket of well-consumed manure and water copiously for five or six days, so that the sprays still produce a crop; but to obtain this result, the beans must be picked very early, all together, and at a quarter of their size; if they are left too long, the stalks no longer grow back. Beans are sown in the open ground from February to May, in plot no. 3, in clumps, staggered 30 centimetres apart in all directions. Two or three beans are placed in bunches; the bunches are dug with a serfouette or hoe, the seed is sown, the holes are half-filled, and when the beans are well established, they are completely covered by hoeing. Water often, and pinch off the main stem as soon as they flower. Second-season beans can be sown as a counter-plant between clumps of early green beans. STRAWBERRIES. Strawberries are an excellent, healthy fruit that can easily be harvested all year round with a little care. We'll divide strawberry plants into two species: the large ones, which bear fruit only once in spring, and the small four-season ones, which bear fruit all year round. These are two distinct crops in the vegetable garden. Let's start by saying that when you want to propagate any variety of strawberry plant, you must do so by sowing, not by dripping. Seedlings are robust and vigorous, and produce superb fruit, whereas plants grown from runners lack vigor, are not very fertile, and are always exposed to varying degrees of degeneration. Excellent strawberry seed is available from all conscientious seed merchants. If you want to harvest it yourself, choose the best strawberries and let them ripen completely on the ground. Crush them in water, wash them several times and extract the seed. Let it dry a little, mix it with half the soil and sow. Strawberry sowing presents a number of difficulties: for the seed to germinate properly, it must 2 be sown in light, constantly moist soil, and the seedbed must be exposed to plenty of heat, while remaining shaded. The essential combination of all these conditions has caused almost all inexperienced gardeners to fail when sowing strawberry plants. Good strawberry plants can only be obtained by sowing, so I had to find a simple and easy way to get the seeds to germinate, to popularize sowing and make it easy for everyone. Nothing could be simpler or easier, without heather soil, frames, layers or cloches. Here's how it's done: In June and July, during the hottest weather, we plough deeply a one or two-metre section of bed in square No. 4; when the soil has been well divided by the ploughing, the clods well broken up, and when it is completely loose, we put about ten centimetres of top soil on the spot to be sown, which we mix well with the soil by means of a good harrowing with a hook. Sow a very light strawberry seed, and mix it with half the soil; then throw in a few very distant radish seeds among the strawberry seed, and cover the whole with about a millimetre of potting soil, well crumbled with the fingers. Water two, three or even four times a day if necessary, using a very fine-headed watering can to avoid beating the soil. The most important thing is to keep the soil constantly moist. Four or five days later, the radishes emerge; their broad leaves soon cover the soil, and a few days later the strawberry plants emerge shaded by the radish leaves. As soon as the strawberry plants have two well-formed leaves, the radishes are thinned out a little, and gradually removed as the strawberry plants gain strength, and three weeks later the sown spot is covered with the finest strawberry plants. Never stop watering, at least once a day, until the seedlings are ready to go into the nursery. In plot no. h, prepare a bed which is firmly earthen as for sowing, and make a rim to retain water from watering. The strawberry plants are carefully unfolded to preserve all their roots, and transplanted in staggered rows at 10 cm intervals in all directions. Water copiously every day, lightly hoeing with a weeder, and six weeks or two months later you have an excellent strawberry plant, with abundant roots, an indispensable condition for obtaining good produce. Strawberry plants require soft, medium-consistency soil, smoked over from the previous year; they shy away from fresh fertilizers and require constant humidity to produce abundant, beautiful produce. It should be planted in plot no. 2, in well-mulched beds, and not in borders, as is often the case, where it gives almost negative results, because it is almost always bare and constantly deprived of water. I've mentioned several varieties of large strawberries in the various vegetable gardens of the owner, tenant, etc. Each person will choose the varieties that suit his or her garden. Everyone will choose the varieties that suit their garden. As a perpetual strawberry, I choose, to the exclusion of all others, the Gaillon variety, which is excellent, very fertile, bearing from May until the frosts, and having the precious advantage of not producing runners. Runners are the scourge of the strawberry plant; not only do they produce bad seedlings, but they also exhaust the mother plant, damaging the production, quality and volume of the fruit. They should be removed as and when they are produced in all well-tended strawberry crops. Large, single-bearing strawberries should be removed immediately after harvesting. They cannot occupy the land all year and produce so little. Here's how it's done: Towards the end of August or in the first days of September, when the hot weather has passed and the rains are beginning to fall, select beds in vegetable garden plot no. 1 that have already produced at least two harvests; High ledges are built up to retain rainwater and watering water; a thick mulch made with manure from the demolition of the beds is laid down, and the strawberry plants from the nurseries are transplanted, staggered at 40 centimetres in all directions, and the rows at the edges at 20 centimetres from the path. For perfect transplanting, it's best to remove the clumps of strawberry plants; place them carefully in a basket; spread the mulch at each place occupied by a strawberry plant; make a hole with the trowel, plant in a clump, adjust the mulch, and water copiously after planting. To make the most of the land, leeks can be planted between the strawberry plants. Water copiously whenever the weather is dry, and destroy runners as soon as they appear. The following year, this bed of strawberry plants will form part of plot no. 2; the vigorous, well-rooted strawberry plants will produce an abundant harvest; immediately afterwards, they will be uprooted, and late carrots sown in the beds they occupied. Some of the strawberry plants that have been pulled out will be kept, to line the following year's beds in August, and to be potted for over-wintering strawberries. Here's how it's done: we split the strawberry plants in two to select the youngest stems; they must have a thick collar, as they are the most fertile. The stems you wish to keep are cut off with a serpette, taking care to remove them with part of the root, and they are transplanted in the nursery in a bed in square n° A, 25 centimetres apart in all directions, with the care indicated above. In August or September, set out in square n° 1, as we have said, and reserve your supply of seedlings for forcing. If there is a warm greenhouse, the strawberry plants are planted in pots and placed under a cold frame or in the orangery as frost approaches, to be placed in the warm greenhouse in January. If you don't have a greenhouse, you can force them into a warm bed under a frame, by burying the pots in potting soil on the bed. Warm layers are sufficient to force strawberry plants; they only require a heat of about 15 degrees, which is maintained with the help of stoves. Give them air whenever the weather permits, cover them carefully at night with mats, and water them at a temperature of 15 degrees whenever necessary. It is urgent to remove runners under frames, as in open ground, as soon as they appear. Gaillon strawberry plants, which produce throughout the year, will remain in place for two years, after which they will be pulled out and new beds created. However well cared for a strawberry crop may be, the fruit degenerates and production diminishes in the third year. Second-year Gaillon strawberry plants are a valuable winter resource when you have a temperate greenhouse. The most vigorous plants are selected and potted in October; when they are well established, pots of strawberry plants are attached to the greenhouse walls with wire, and strawberries are harvested continuously throughout the winter, with no trouble other than watering the pots. If you don't have a greenhouse, you can remove the strawberry plugs and line two or three panels with frames, then heat as I've indicated for large strawberries. Gaillon strawberries are planted in plot no. 1, from August 25 to September 15. Soil preparation and planting are the same as for large strawberries, with the difference that the distance between plants is only 30 centimetres. Gaillon strawberry plants, which produce continuously, require more fertilizer than the others, and some special care to sustain their production for two consecutive years. In the spring following planting, a layer of potting soil about three centimetres thick is spread over the mulch placed in September at the time of planting, then a new mulch is applied after energetic hoeing with the tines of a serfouette. In the second year, at the same time, the strawberry bed is placed in plot no. 2; in spring, it is given a new top dressing and mulch. Before hoeing and topdressing, the strawberry plants should be cleaned at least once a year, i.e. all dry leaves and dead stems should be carefully removed. If strawberry plants are only cleaned once a year, it's a good idea to do so before replanting. Strawberry plants always have a tendency to heave, and when the crown is above ground, it becomes infertile if it is not resodded. Don't forget, either, that the strawberry plant requires a lot of water; if we want its production to be sustained, we need to water it constantly; we can water the flowers without any inconvenience; this doesn't prevent the fruit from knotting, and tenderizes the strawberries. This cultivation requires care, but is well rewarded by an abundant harvest. You need to have grown strawberries in good conditions to get an idea of their production. GIRAUMON. Giraumon is excellent and easy to grow in the home garden; its very sweet fruits make very good soups. Sow in warm soil under a greenhouse in March, and transplant to a cooler soil in the nursery as soon as the first two leaves appear. Around April 15, plant out on deaf soil at a distance of 1 m 50 cm, and shelter at night with clocles or straw mats, as long as frosts are to be feared. Water copiously throughout the plant's growth, and double the watering once the fruit has formed. Let the main stem grow to a length of 1 meter 20 centimeters, taking care to make the stems run straight and alternately to the right and left, to allow the lateral branches to spread freely (pl. 9. fig. 2). Pinch the main stem to a length of 1 meter 20 centimeters, to allow the lateral branches to develop, on which the meshes (female flowers) soon appear. When the fruit is the size of a large apple, select two or three of the finest per plant, and remove all the others. Never keep two fruits on the same branch, and as soon as the fruit is well tied, cut the branch carrying it two leaves above, to encourage its development22 by giving it more sap. Care is taken to ensure that the shoots do not grow too long, and they are pinched off when they become too long, in order to concentrate all the sap's action on the fruit. Giraumons are harvested around August and September, and can be stored until March on dry straw in a humidity-free cellar. BEANS. The cultivation of beans is of great importance in the vegetable garden; this crop must provide green beans for most of the year, fresh beans at different times of the year, and finally, a supply of dry beans for the winter. I have indicated the best varieties to grow for each of the vegetable gardens in the first part of this book (consult them to make your choice). Beans like soft, cool, not too compact soil; they hate new manures; ash makes them bear fruit abundantly. They are best planted in plot no. 3 in the vegetable garden. Beans are not difficult to grow, but horticultural literature has been so full of claims that they are easy to grow, that most of the time we think we don't need to give them the care they need, and we get pitiful results, even when we don't run out of beans. Beans are grown in three distinct divisions: green beans and 18-grain beans, early green beans, on beds and in frames; green beans, and 18-grain beans, in the open ground. Green beans can be grown quite easily, on a bed and under a frame, for most of the winter. The most important thing is to avoid the humidity that causes them to rot. This is avoided by providing air as often as possible, and by keeping the windows of the frames constantly clear, so as to let in bright light. The Dutch dwarf bean is the only bean that should be used for forcing. They are sown from December to March on a warm bed, and as soon as the first two leaves have formed, they are transplanted to a slightly cooler bed, between other plants, to be planted on a special bed three weeks later. Transplanting into a nursery is quite important: beans transplanted twice grow fewer leaves, and fruit more quickly and more abundantly than those sown in situ. A 50-centimeter-thick layer of soil, capable of providing 20 to 25 degrees of heat, is sufficient for planting beans. As the temperature drops, heaters are added and the number of mats increased to cover the beans at night. Four rows of beans are placed under the frames, 20 centimetres from the edges and 30 centimetres apart; they are traced out in rays, and the beans are staggered on the rows, at a distance of about 15 centimetres. Air is supplied whenever the temperature permits, and this is important to avoid humidity. To this end, we remove all very large leaves; we water lightly, very carefully, and as much as possible, during the day, and before giving air. The beans are harvested as they form, and towards the end of the harvest cauliflowers can be counter-planted in them. Green beans are obtained by the same process, and with the same variety, the Dutch dwarf. When you don't want to have a large early crop, it's easy to obtain very-early green beans without a great deal of care. Sow on a warm, worn-out bed, i.e. one that has been in use for a long time and has given off most of its heat, towards the end of February; transplant onto a warm bed, and then place on a deaf bed around April 10. You can also sow on warm soil in March, prick out on deaf soil and plant out in the first days of May. Finally, if you don't have a lot of bedding, or if you don't have a frame, you can still get fairly early green beans by sowing them in March in pots that you hang in the orangery, or even better, in a temperate greenhouse, transplanting them into other pots in April and planting them out in the open ground in May. Wherever you have a few frames, a greenhouse, an orangery, or even a greenhouse where light penetrates, it's easy, with a little effort and a lot of good will, to obtain excellent seedlings, which considerably advance the harvest, compared with seedlings planted in the ground. As beans are very sensitive to frost, they can hardly be sown in the open ground before the second half of April, or if sown earlier, they should be planted in beds and covered at night with straw mats. I've said that beans like fresh soil, but without excess moisture. Consequently, for all dwarf beans, we'll sow in the open ground as follows: In moist, clay soils, we'll line up furrows five to six centimetres deep and 30 centimetres apart, using a pointed trowel; at the bottom of these furrows, we'll sow a bean every ten centimetres or so, and cover with only two centimetres of soil; when the bean is buried too deeply, it rots and doesn't sprout. When the beans have four leaves, in dry weather and in the middle of the day, we give them a vigorous hoeing, uniting the soil and filling the furrows completely. In this way, the beans become more vigorous and productive. When ashing, an operation which greatly increases the harvest, the ash is spread along the furrows over a width of 20 centimetres, when the beans are sown and covered, and left on the ground until the first hoeing, when it is buried. Be careful not to scatter the ash before sowing, or to bring the seed into contact with it, as its causticity would prevent germination. I also recommend hoeing only in dry weather, and in the middle of the day, because hoeing when the bean leaves are wet with rain or dew causes rust, and the harvest is significantly reduced. I exclusively recommend row planting in wet soils, because it allows air to circulate between the rows, and the beans are less likely to rot. Experience, moreover, has amply demonstrated that sowing in rows produces twice as much as sowing in bunches in damp soils. In addition, the more clayey the soil, the greater the amount of ashing required. In light soils, on the other hand, beans are sown in bunches, five to six centimetres deep, and staggered at 30 centimetres in all directions. The clumps are made stronger or weaker, depending on the consistency of the soil. For Flageolet, which we'll be growing the most, five beans are placed in medium-consistency soil, and seven, eight or even ten in siliceous soil. The main aim is to cover the entire surface of the bed with leaves, to prevent the soil from drying out. Pockets are sown in the same way as rows; the seeds are covered with two centimeters of soil, then ashed and hoeed as for row sowing, when the beans have four leaves. A second, and sometimes a third hoeing is necessary for bush beans, especially if you water them, which is essential if the weather is dry, at sowing time, to ensure emergence, and when the beans are forming, to make them grow and harvest them tender. When you devote a few bean beds to green bean production, you must pick as the beans form, picking anyway, even if it means selling or giving away if you have too many, but never letting the beans form. This destroys the green bean crop, while producing a pitiful bean product. One of two things is true: you make planks to harvest green or beans, one or the other, but never both, otherwise the product will be reduced by more than two-thirds. Planks destined for green bean production must be constantly picked, the more you pick the more you harvest; those destined for bean production, on the other hand, must remain intact. The first pods formed are the most vigorous, and their production is assured; if they are removed, those that follow produce few, stunted, poorly nourished beans. Green beans are easily obtained by sowing flageolet beans, the only variety that does well in all seasons, from April to September. By sowing late, you never risk more than a few handfuls of beans, as the soil in which you sow them has more than paid for itself with previous harvests. A little trouble is well rewarded when the product succeeds, and we can add that it will always come to a good end, whenever the beans are protected from the first frosts, with frames, simply placed on the boards. Runner beans are sown in the open ground only, like bush beans: in rows in clay soils, and in bunches in siliceous soils, but with the difference that the bunches are made less strong. Four beans per bunch is enough for Soissons, and two or three at most for Sabres. As soon as four leaves have formed, hoe as for the previous varieties, and row. Sabre beans, which are very vigorous and fertile, require two-meter oars. LETTUCE. Lettuce likes soft soil, a little substantial but not too compact; it needs a fair amount of fertilizer to become beautiful and grow rapidly, but it fears fertilizers that are too fresh, which make it rise and prevent it from peeling. Lettuce, like most lettuces, belongs in vegetable plot 2, where it grows to enormous proportions without ever climbing. Lettuce loves water; it needs plenty of it to grow fast and stay tender. For this reason, when growing lettuce in the open ground, it is essential to mulch the beds well, to conserve soil moisture and provide abundant nourishment, by dissolving the nutrients contained in the mulch, 8 through watering, and introducing them into a layer of soil already saturated with humus, from the decomposition of the maximum amount of manure ploughed in the previous year. Growing lettuce is simple: the most important thing is to get a good plant, which is easy with row sowing; to remove it well, with all its roots, and above all to transplant it well. When the neck of the lettuce has been squeezed by the trowel, or by compact soil hardened by watering, it comes up poorly and gives only pitiful results. This crop will always be successful in the open ground, when the manuring is not too recent, and when the bed is well mulched, sufficiently watered, and the soil is kept very loose all around the crown. We will divide the cultivation of lettuces into three parts: the first covers spring lettuces, the second summer lettuces, and the third winter lettuces. See the various gardens of the owner, tenant, etc., etc., for the choice of varieties. Gotte lettuce is excellent for producing early spring lettuces, and especially for cultivation under cloches and under frames. It can be sown in rows, around the second half of October, on a worn layer, or broadcast, under a bell, in potting soil. When sowing under a bell or in the open ground, choose a warm, sheltered spot, plow fairly deeply, and put about 10 centimeters of potting soil on top of the soil. When the lettuces have two well-formed leaves, they are carefully unfolded to avoid breaking the roots, and transplanted into a nursery four or five centimetres apart, under a cold frame, or under a cloche, in the open ground, covered with compost, as for sowing. They are sheltered at night, when it's freezing, and given air during the day when the temperature permits, and as often as possible to strengthen the plant. Around mid-January, the lettuces are placed in beds, under frames or cloches. Make a layer 40 to 50 centimeters thick and 1.35 meters wide. Cover with about 15 centimetres of potting soil, and lay the boxes, or three rows of cloches, depending on what is available. Under the frame, draw five rows 27 to 28 centimeters apart, and plant the lettuces in staggered rows 20 centimeters apart. Give them air as often as possible, and cover them at night with straw mats. When it's very cold, we apply a stove to the bed. Under a bell, lettuces are planted in triangles, three to a bell. Air is provided, and they are carefully covered at night with bedding and straw mats, depending on the intensity of the cold. Lettuces planted in January can be harvested in March. They can be counter-planted on beds and under trays in various crops. Several varieties of spring lettuce can be sown in January and February on beds and under frames, transplanted in a nursery on a cooler bed, and planted out in the open ground around April 15. These lettuces can be counter-planted in all the crops in plot no. 2, or made into special beds. All varieties of summer lettuce can be sown in rows 15 to 20 centimetres apart in the ground, from April to July. However, a special seedbed should be sown in plot no. h, on a piece of board covered with potting soil which is mixed with the surface of the soil, and never sown in onion or carrot beds. When the plant is strong, it is removed with a trowel, leaving the roots as free as possible for planting. Seedlings are planted every three weeks or so, so you never run out of lettuce. Summer lettuces don't need to be transplanted in a nursery; they are removed from the seedbed to be planted out, but it's urgent to have elite seedlings, which can only be obtained with seedbeds, potting soil and copious, frequent watering. In plot no. 2, plant beds 1 m 20 cm wide, with a rim to hold water for watering; mulch the whole bed with manure from the demolition of the beds, or from the compost heap; then place four stakes at each end of the bed, 30 centimetres apart, and the two at the edge, 15 centimetres from the alley; place the chalk line on these stakes; and you have four rows on which to stagger the lettuces, 35 centimetres apart. You spread the mulch a little with your fingers to transplant the lettuce, and as soon as you have done this, you bring it closer to the collar, and hold it together with your hand. Lettuce can be counter-planted in a bed of romaine or chicory, and the lettuce in turn can be counter-planted with other lettuces. Growing winter lettuces requires care to ensure good results. The variety par excellence is the petite crêpe or petite noire, the only one that can complete its vegetation without being given air, and because of this particularity, this lettuce requires special cultivation, which we will describe below: The first season of petite noire lettuce is sown in September, under a bell, in the open ground covered with potting soil, or what is preferable, on an old, worn layer. As this plant doesn't need to breathe, we proceed as follows for sowing: we take a bell and press it firmly onto the potting soil, so that the edge of the bell enters the loose potting soil by one or two centimetres; in this way, we mark the location of two or three bells, depending on the quantity we want to sow. Broadcast the seed, not too thickly and evenly, in the place where the bells are to be placed; cover the seed with potting soil, and replace the bells in the marks made so that they enter the potting soil, and the air doesn't penetrate underneath. As soon as two leaves are well formed, but two leaves without counting the seminal leaves, the plant is removed with the greatest care to avoid breaking the roots, and placed under cloches, in the open ground, covered with potting soil, or on a cold bed, in a nursery at a distance of h or 5 centimetres. The cloches are hermetically sealed by pressing them into the potting soil, for transplanting in the nursery as for sowing. Water lightly, if necessary, and shade with straw, or better still with canvas when the sun is too persistent. Around mid-October, we plant permanently, on a deaf layer or on a warm, worn layer, under frames or cloches. When planting under a frame, take care to fill the boxes with potting soil, so as to leave a gap of only 15 cm between the potting soil and the frames. If a larger gap is left, the lettuces will bolt, pump badly or not at all. Remove the clods and place them in a staggered pattern under the frame, 20 centimetres apart in all directions. I'm insisting on transplanting in clods because this is the first element of success, and I'm aware of the tendency of gardeners to neglect small-scale care, which is the only way to obtain great results. If the temperature drops appreciably, we give the bed a warmer and shade it with the sun, without giving air, so that the lettuces can vegetate constantly, at an average temperature and in a calculated light environment. Cover at night with straw mats to prevent frost. In the event of a lack of chassis, crepe or little black lettuce can be grown under cloches, 23 and even under economic frames, on muted or cold layers. All you need to do is prevent frost; a simple stove provides the heat needed to bring the harvest to fruition. But I can't stress it enough: it's only with constant, intelligent care that success can be achieved. Not too much heat, avoiding frost, and keeping the plants in an average state of light and warmth, you're sure to succeed. When planting under cloches, place four lettuces per cloche, and three rows of cloches on a bed one meter 35 wide. The bed is covered at night with bedding and straw mats, and shaded during the day, without providing air. Heaters are applied as needed. Pancake or little black lettuce, sown in September and raised under cloches in a nursery, is good for planting from November to January, depending on its strength. Passion lettuce also makes an excellent winter salad. Sow it in late August or early September, in the open ground, covered with potting soil; transplant it in the nursery, in the open ground, in a warm, sheltered spot, where it can overwinter, taking care to cover it during cold nights with litter that is removed as soon as it stops freezing. You can plant them in the ground, in a warm, sheltered spot, taking care to cover them with straw mats during cold nights and days. This produces good lettuces around Holy Week, when winter is not very harsh. If it's possible to plant under a frame or under a cloche, on a warm layer, success is assured. If you don't have enough frame and cloche equipment to grow winter lettuces, you can replace them with lettuce for cutting. Nothing is easier than to obtain this lettuce without the slightest expense, and it is a delight in winter. We sow gotte lettuce or small crepe seed in the open, on a bed, and under a frame, between other crops, and we cut it to eat it as soon as it has four leaves. ROMANS. Romaine is grown in the same way as lettuce; it is sown under frames and cloches; it can be transplanted in nurseries under frames and cloches, and counter-planted in early crops. Romaine varieties grown as early vegetables all need air to thrive. Summer cultivation in the open ground is the same as for lettuces, in which they can be counter-planted without inconvenience. Winter red romaine can be sown in the garden, transplanted in the nursery or in the garden, and planted in autumn or spring, also in the garden, where it produces excellent spring salads. This variety, the hardiest of all, tolerates frosts well, although it is advisable to cover with bedding or straw mats during severe frosts. Romaine is less esteemed than lettuce, so we will only admit it as a very secondary crop, in the owner's garden where we will counter-plant it, as a catch crop in plot no. 2, with chicory, lettuce, tomatoes, etc., etc. (See various vegetable gardens, part 1, for varieties to grow. LENTILS. Lentils are generally little grown in the kitchen garden, and wrongly so, for they provide a fairly good dry vegetable for winter, and valuable fodder, the most nutritious of all, for animals. There are advantages to growing it in extensive vegetable gardens, especially those of tenants, farmers, communities and camps. Blond lentils are the preferred variety. It is sown in rows, 30 centimeters apart, around April, in plot no. 3 of the vegetable garden. One ashing after sowing, and two hoeing operations during the course of its vegetation, is all it takes to produce a good dry vegetable for winter, and excellent fodder for animals. When lentils begin to ripen, they can be counter-planted with green beans, and after this second harvest, a good crop of turnips can still be obtained. If you have cows, you can sow lentils in rows, only 15 cm apart, in plot no. 4, around April or May 8, and mow them for fodder in June. After that, there's still time to grow an excellent crop of buckwheat for poultry. MACHE. An excellent little lettuce, offering a valuable winter resource, especially in gardens where there are no layers, frames or cloches. Lamb's lettuce is the lettuce of the small farmer and of the peasant during the winter; it is easy to obtain during the entire period when lettuce is in short supply, by sowing it very lightly, broadcast, among the crops of squares n° 2 and n° 3, during the months of August and September, to harvest in December, January, and even February, when the winter is mild. The seed is broadcast and buried with a rake. From the end of September to October 15th, lamb's lettuce is sown in beds, which are covered with litter during frosts, so as to be able to harvest this excellent salad during the most rigorous cold weather. The bedding is then lifted and cut as needed. Lamb's lettuce can be sown in squares no. 2 and no. 3, in the latest harvests of chicory, lettuce, romaine and green beans, in tomatoes, etc., etc. MELON. Melon cultivation is possible everywhere, and by everyone, but the difficulties have been exaggerated to such an extent, and pruning methods have been invented and propagated that renfer14 mant combinations, that gardeners of mediocre intelligence have given up on them, and homeowners, weary of continual failure, no longer want to see them in their gardens. Nothing, however, is easier than obtaining a large quantity of melons, at low cost and in a very small space. We'll divide melon cultivation into three series: the first includes early-season melons grown in frames and on hot beds; the second, in-season melons grown on warm beds and under cloches; the third, late-season melons grown on deaf beds, under cloches or under economic shelter. I told the owner's vegetable garden that to be sure of obtaining excellent melons, it was necessary to stick to no more than three varieties (see pages 119 et seq.). 1° Le petit prescot, for the early season; 2° Le cantaloup à fond vert, for the late season; 3° Le melon d'Arkangel, for the late season. Let's start with early melons: sow on a warm bed, under a frame, in the first days of January. If you want a good plant, sow only one seed at a time, and never two in the same hole. Melons don't like to be replanted, and the slightest disorganization in their roots will lead to their death. This is why we recommend sowing it in pots, which is pointless unless the plant is destined to travel. The roots are often hindered in pots, as they 8 grow all around them, and the melon suffers when placed in potting soil; it is preferable to sow it directly on the bed, and remove it as a clod, using the trowel, to place it. Sow on the bed, making a small hole with your finger, placing a single seed per hole, and sowing in staggered rows, 20 cm apart in all directions. Cover carefully at night, and even during the day when it's cold, with one or more mats, depending on the temperature. It's vital to keep the windows of the frames very clear, and free from humidity, which melons fear greatly. When the melons are up, we keep the bed at a temperature of 25 to 30 degrees Celsius, which is easy to do by placing active stoves right up to the frames, handling them and adding a little fresh manure when they lose heat. Whenever the temperature permits, you can provide a little air by half-opening the frame on the side facing away from the wind. Air and light are indispensable to plants; that's why I've banned daubing windows and bells with white paint; it's true that this protects plants from sunburn, but all you get is a stunted, etiolated plant that can't stand replanting, and gives pitiful fruit when it does. It's urgent to get melons used to air and light from birth, if you want to obtain remarkable products. When the sun is too hot, shade them with canvas or a bit of straw, and let them breathe, but you need to shade as little as possible if you want to get the best melons. The gardener must always be on the lookout for the weather, looking after his trays, opening them when the sun is shining, and closing them as soon as the sun disappears or it gets cold. When the melons have four well-developed leaves, not counting the cotyledons, they are pruned for the first time. The main stem is cut off with a grafting tool or pruning knife on the first two leaves. I said cut, and cut with a very sharp instrument, not pinch with the fingers. The cut heals in a few days, whereas the pinch, which crushes the very tender, very watery stem, results, if not in death, at least in very prolonged suffering, highly detrimental to the volume and quality of the fruit. As soon as the amputation has been made, the wound is cauterized with a little wood ash. I have already mentioned the need to keep the windows of the frames very clear and free from humidity; this is especially important during the first pruning. If there is any water vapour inside the panes, they must be wiped dry, otherwise the melons could go bad. As soon as the melons have been pruned, they are placed in warm layers. These layers are given a height of around 80 centimetres; they are topped up with 20 to 25 centimetres of potting soil, mixed with half good soil, the boxes and frames are laid, and when the layer has caught fire, which takes about eight days, the melons are placed on these new layers. The two lateral stems then begin to develop; the clod melons are carefully removed with the trowel, and placed on the new layer, in two rows 40 centimetres from the edges of the box, with the stalks planted at around 70 centimetres apart. Once planted, water very lightly, not on the root collar, but on the edge of the rootball to weld it to the potting soil of the frame. When replanting melons, you should bury them right up to the cotyledons, to give them new roots and vigor. After watering, cover the trays with straw mats for two days to ensure recovery, then cover only at night, and during the day when it's freezing. Give them as much air and light as possible, whenever the temperature permits. Once the melons have become accustomed to air and light early on, it's a good idea to remove the frames completely when the sun is shining, from midday to 2.5 a.m., especially during flowering. Fruit tied in the open air is always more beautiful and tastier. As soon as the temperature eases a little, and the humidity of the atmosphere is no longer to be feared, and before the two arms that develop in the axils of the two leaves on which we have pruned are too long, we carefully mulch the whole inside of the frame, to keep the soil moist. Spread out the arms, one on the right, the other on the left, so that they don't become entangled, and let them extend freely until the eighth or ninth eye (A, pl. 9, fig. 4), to pinch them off on the sixth or seventh leaf. A few days later, branches develop in the leaf axils (B, same figure); on these new branches appear the meshes (female flowers); several fruits are left to knot, and when they have reached the volume of a walnut, one is chosen on each arm, two eyes are pruned above (C, same figure), and all the others are removed, by pruning the branches to two or three eyes (D, same figure). The purpose of this pruning is to concentrate all the sap's action on the fruit, and in just a few days it grows noticeably. This very simple pruning method is within everyone's reach, and experience has shown that it produces fruit that is much more beautiful, fuller and better nourished than that obtained by continuous mutilation. Once the fruit has been pruned and selected, all that remains to be done is to remove any yellowing leaves, and to cut back any shoots that grow too long and threaten to burst out of the frame. To obtain the most beautiful melons, choose those tied close to the main branch and on the first branches (C, same figure), but never keep those that sometimes tie at the collar (F, same figure), as they always come off badly. The volume and quality of the fruit can be further increased by applying highly active fertilizers near the root tips as soon as the fruit has been selected. Remove the mulch, carefully remove the roots without exposing them, and spread fresh horse manure, crumbled with the fingers, or pigeon or chicken droppings, mixed with potting soil, over the space occupied by the roots. Cover this manure with potting soil, and replace the mulch immediately. It's dangerous to over-water melons, especially at the base, as it often causes cankers at the crown. However, a certain amount of humidity must be maintained in the potting soil, especially once the fruit has formed. Water with the spout all around the stem, but never on the crown. When the weather is very dry, it's a good idea to slightly wet the leaves with a very fine-headed watering can, but to water the roots and leaves, it's urgent to use only water at the temperature of the frames. When the water is cold, the melons seize up, suffer greatly and rarely produce good fruit. When the melons have reached the volume of an apple, we cut small boards of about 20 centimetres square, and place one under each melon, so that its weight doesn't cause it to sink into the mulch, and so that it can ripen evenly all around. When you take the trouble to turn the melons a little on the board as they approach maturity, it's hard to find the side that was touching the layer. Early melons require assiduous care and close monitoring. The most important thing is to prevent them from getting cold, which is difficult for them to recover from; you must constantly observe the weather, and choose the right moments to give them air. Once you've got the hang of running frames, you'll rarely fail. Seasonal melons present far fewer difficulties; they are easy to grow for all gardeners, even the uninitiated. Sow in March on a warm bed, and if necessary on a lukewarm bed; raise the plant as for early melons, and prune before setting. Establish a warm layer about 80 centimetres wide; remove the rootball melons in the second half of April and set them in the middle of the layer, 60 centimetres apart. Water lightly, place a cloche on each plant, shade for two days to ensure recovery, then provide air whenever the temperature permits, and cover at night with bedding. The bells are cleaned every time they become dirty, to let the light in, and the steam that forms inside in the morning is carefully wiped away. A few days later, the whole layer is carefully strawed; the arms are evenly spread out, one in front, the other behind (pl. 9, fig. 5), to avoid confusion. During the day, as long as the sun isn't too hot, the bell is placed on three small racks, to provide both air and warmth. In the evening, the bell is laid flat on the mulch to protect it from the cool of the night, as long as the stem of the melon can fit underneath; when it exceeds the diameter of the bell, it is placed on the first notch of the racks, i.e. a centimetre above the ground, to avoid crushing the arms. When the sun is blazing, the bells are removed completely, as long as it's still shining, and replaced when it's gone. In-season melons are pruned in the same way as early melons, but as they are more vigorous, both arms are pruned on the seventh or eighth eye, to encourage fruiting. We prune two eyes above the fruit, place boards under the melons, and remove any shoots that are too vigorous, as I said earlier. The most important thing is to protect them from hail and heavy rains, which is something you don't have to worry about when growing under a frame. Hail wreaks frightening havoc on melons; ten minutes of hail are enough to destroy the most beautiful melon field. When the weather is stormy, you should always have a supply of mats next to the melon beds, to cover them as soon as the hail falls, and before if you can, as each grain of hail produces a black spot that almost always causes the fruit and leaf, as well as the stem, to decompose. Continuous rainfall is also to be feared, as excess humidity causes cankers to form on the crown, and destroys the plant in a short space of time. As soon as a canker appears, it should be converted into a wound; remove all the diseased part with a sharp instrument, and cauterize the wound with a little powdered plaster. When it rains for several days in a row, or even for a whole day, we keep the bell on the rack above the melon stalk. When you want to bring a melon forward, you put a bell on it at night, and during the day you hang it over the racks when the sun isn't too hot. Melons, while requiring a great deal of heat, are quite prone to sunburn. It's a good idea to keep them shaded by the leaves for as long as they grow, as this is the best way to get them looking good. Sunburn is still to be feared after a few days of stormy, dark weather, followed by great heat and scorching sun. In such cases, if the melons are too big to remain hidden under the leaves, the sunny side should be covered with a cabbage leaf. Melons are easy to grow on a sour layer, under cloche, and even under economic shelter. You can get melons as good this way as on warm and hot beds, under frames and cloches, but later. This is the back-season crop, for melons ripening in August and September. Two excellent varieties can be grown under these conditions: 1° The green cantaloupe, provided it is not sown later than April; 2° The Arkangel melon, the hardiest of all varieties, which can be sown as late as May. Late-season melons can be grown in two ways: sown in trays or under cloches, for transplanting on deaf soil, or sown in the field. Choose one of the two growing methods, depending on your resources and your wealth of manure and shelter. I said earlier that I hoped to see the cultivation of melons soon introduced in the humblest village, and successfully carried out by parish priests, schoolteachers and peasants alike. Nothing could be easier, if you only cultivate late-season melons. I have also said that the introduction of this crop would be most profitable, not only because of the pleasure or profit it would give by itself, but also because of the wealth of fertilizer it would inevitably create. The most seemingly insignificant cause sometimes produces the greatest effects. The day that the use of diapers, however bad they may be, becomes popular, a multitude of valuable substances for agriculture, lost almost everywhere, will be carefully collected. They will be collected because the farmer, necessarily thrifty, will find in their use a slight profit in the sale of a few melons. The detritus of the diapers will produce a host of vegetables that will also bring the farmer a small profit in money and a notable well-being in his household. The field cultivated with vegetables, smoked to the maximum with the diaper debris, and the herbaceous matter, gathered everywhere, will produce wheat that will make the farmer rich, and then clover and alfalfa that will pay ten times the rent of the land, in milk, butter and lard. Ten melons taken to market will make the farmer a small fortune. It's not the value of the ten melons that will greatly increase his nest-egg, but it's the prospect of a minimal sum that will have made him carefully collect all the fertilizing substances he had let go to waste until now, and the fertilizer factory established at first on a small scale, growing every day, will soon provide him with vegetables, bread, milk, butter, and meat, without his suspecting it. This farmer has been told a hundred times: The manure from your cows is lost in the streets of your village, to the great detriment of your fertilizers; the grasses from your ditches, the reeds from your pond, the broom and gorse from your woods, mixed with your manure, and the manure you let go to waste, would double the mass of your fertilizers, and considerably increase your harvest. The farmer remained deaf to this advice, because he was convinced that he had the best wheat and fodder in his region, and saw in the fertilizer factory only an expense of time and effort, with no profit. He didn't believe, and he doesn't believe in the value of fertilizer. Say to this man: Collect all these fertilizing substances, make a layer of them to raise melons, which you'll sell at a high price on the market, he won't believe you. But if he sees a dozen melons, worth two francs each, on a bed in the presbytery or schoolteacher's garden, he'll go to a hundred francs of trouble to try to do the same, and without suspecting it, he'll carry out the program designed to lead him to affluence; he'll increase public wealth, through the manufacture of fertilizers, and bring the soil to the highest degree of power and fertility. Let's get back to late-season melons. You can sow green cantaloupe in the second half of March, on a warm bed made with one third cow manure and two thirds leaves or moss, under cloches or canvas frames. The melons are then sown and raised as described for early and seasonal crops. They are planted on a muted bed towards the end of April or in the early days of May, when they have undergone their first pruning. At this time, a layer can be built up with a quarter of cow manure, mixed with three-quarters of herbaceous matter, and even with herbs alone, provided they are used fresh. In the absence of glass bells, you can use economy bells (see page 134), and still obtain excellent melons. 8 If you don't want to go to the trouble of making a bed, you can raise excellent melon plants by covering a pile of manure with fifteen to twenty centimetres of light soil, mixed with a third of horse dung, and sheltering the plant with cloches or canvas-covered frames. I give you all these simple means, because they are within everyone's reach, and it's always preferable to raise the plant and transplant it. Care should be taken to establish the mounded beds, intended to receive the melons permanently. Since melons are sensitive to humidity, it is important, especially when simplifying cultivation, to place them as much as possible on an elevation, never in a hollow, where they would inevitably wilt. Take into account the settling of the materials used to build up the layer, and raise it accordingly.
