If you don't want to go to the trouble of raising seedlings by the means I've just indicated, you can still obtain melons by sowing the Arkangel variety in situ, towards the end of April or in the first days of May. A layer of cow or horse manure and three quarters of any kind of grass is placed on top of 20 centimetres of good soil, mixed with a third of horse dung, and two seeds are sown together every 60 centimetres. Shelter with glass bells, or if these are not available, with economy bells. When the seedlings have risen, the strongest is kept and the weakest is uprooted or, better still, cut between two plots of land to avoid shaking the root of the one kept. The first pruning is applied when the first four leaves are well formed, but as this plant has been sown in place, and will not be re-shoed when replanted, it is carefully unshovelled before pruning, and the whole area occupied by the roots is covered with fresh horse dung, well crushed with the fingers, and the melons are re-shoed with the soil from the layer, up to the cotyledons, to give them more vigor. Pruning and care for late-season melons are exactly the same as for in-season melons. Shelters are less effective, but as the season is milder, you can achieve more or less the same result six weeks later, by taking the same precautions. Willingness is power! Anyone who seriously wants to try will succeed, and will obtain very good melons with the worst of all layers, and will also have a certain quantity of potting soil which will be a resource, the richness of which he does not suspect, for seedlings and open-ground crops. TURNIPS. Turnips require medium-consistency, slightly substantial soil and fully decomposed fertilizers. It belongs in plot no. 2 for seasonal seedlings, and in plot no. A for early seedlings. Seasonal turnips are usually sown in plot no. 2 from June 15 to the end of August, staggering the sowing period so as to harvest constantly. Early turnips are sown in plot no. 4, from March 15 to May 15. These seedlings often succeed in soil with little or no smoke, using slightly old seed and sowing only tender turnips. The turnip of virtues is the most successful, and still sometimes goes to seed when sown before June. Turnips are sown in beds, on the fly; as the seed is very small, it is a good idea to mix it with a third or half of the soil to avoid sowing too thickly. After sowing, cover the bed with a little compost, weed and thin out as soon as the turnips have emerged. All turnips sown in summer require frequent watering; when they lack water due to the heat, they go to seed without forming roots. (See various vegetable gardens, part 1 for choice of varieties.) Turnips can be sown in plot no. 2, after a harvest of early carrots, lettuces, etc., etc., and in plot no. h, before growing buckwheat. ONION. The onion likes a slightly substantial soil, rather light than strong, and above all fears fresh manure. As a result, we'll grow it in plot no. 2 of the vegetable garden, where it will give the best results. 08 Onions can be grown in two ways: by sowing in the field, or by transplanting. All seasonal varieties can be sown on the spot; transplanting is used only for white onions; however, it can be very useful when you want to obtain very large onions, which are often invaluable on farms, in communities, and so on. The early white onion is sown fairly lightly, in beds, around August 15, for transplanting in October, and in the first days of September, for transplanting in spring. This sowing can be done in plot no. 2, in a lettuce bed. Onions fear frost, so it's a good idea to cover them with bedding over winter. In October, the white onion is countersown in the strawberry beds just planted in plot no. 1, which in spring will become plot no. 2. When the white onion is transplanted in spring, it is counter-planted in plot no. 2, between the strawberry plants and the lettuces. White onions, sown in August and transplanted in October, are good for eating in May, when preserved onions are worthless. Early white onions are invaluable for pickling, like gherkins, but they are not so good for preserving.

strong, and very tender. For this purpose, it is sown in March, broadcast in a bed, and very thickly to harvest it small. When you need a fairly large quantity of very large onions, you sow the pale red onion in May, in a bed, and excessively thick, in square no. 2. Sowing is tight enough to produce onions barely the size of a hazelnut. These onions are ripe in September; we pull them out, and when they are dry, we store them in the attic to transplant them in February or March of the following year, in plot no. 2. Transplanting is done in rows 15 cm apart, with onions planted 10 cm apart. Monstrous onions are harvested around July. All in-season varieties - blond virtues, flat yellow, etc. - can be broadcast in February and March. Better in February, the harvest is made a little earlier; land cleared in July can still give two harvests before the end of the season. I've already said that threshing is not necessary for onions. Sow on ploughing that has been in place for three weeks or a month, and the soil will be sufficiently firm, and the crop the best it can be. Onion beds need to be watered several times, so before sowing, create a border around the beds to retain water from watering; then sow, and cover the seed with a centimetre of top soil. Water until emergence if the weather is dry, and weed as soon as weeds appear. Water from time to time to keep the soil fresh, until the onions have acquired a certain strength; weed a second time, and even a third time if necessary, and suspend watering, which, if too frequent, would prevent the onions from turning. When the onions are well turned and fully developed, cut off the stems as they emerge; don't cut them off, but bruise them and lay them down with the back of a rake to hasten the bulb's ripening; when you don't have many onions, you can twist the stems by hand, but the back of a rake is more expedient. As soon as the stems are completely wilted, the onions are pulled out. Always choose very dry weather for this operation. Leave them in an alley exposed to the sun for four or five days, then put them in a very dry attic, and when the cold approaches, cover them with straw to keep them from freezing. You can sow early carrots followed by lamb's lettuce, after the first onions have been harvested, or three harvests of lettuces, using counter-planting. ONION. When a large quantity of sorrel is needed, sow Bellevièlle sorrel in borders from March to June, in squares no. 3 and no. h. This is the most productive sorrel, but it is acidic, especially in summer. A sorrel border sown in spring yields several cuttings during the season. Successive sowings can be made up to July to ensure year-round availability. Blistered-leaf sorrel is much preferable for the home garden: it is less acidic. It can be propagated by splitting off plants and planting them in the borders of plot no. 3. Don't cut this sorrel like the previous one, but pick it leaf by leaf, if you don't want to greatly reduce the harvest. Sorrel is indispensable for cooking, and you need to have it all year round; nothing could be easier: just pull up a few clumps as winter approaches, and plant them in the corners of the frames, between other plants, where they give leaves all winter long. PANAIS. Parsnips can only ever be grown on a very small scale, but it is useful to have some in the kitchen garden. Like carrots, parsnips are sown in beds in plot no. 2, in February for harvesting in July or August, and in July for harvesting at the end of the season, according to need. Weed, thin and water as needed, as with other broadcast seedlings. Three crops of lettuce can be grown after summer parsnips, and winter parsnips can be sown after a crop of onions. Parsnips do not fear frost, and July seedlings can be kept in the ground throughout the winter without any inconvenience. PATATO. The potato is not as good as the potato, but as it is more difficult to grow in our climate, and as it is a vegetable belonging to the do8 maine of fantasy, it is a good idea to have a few plants in the vegetable garden. I attach too little importance to the potato to grow it as an early crop, and clutter up the frames with it, so we'll be content to grow it on a deaf bed, and harvest it around August. In April, potatoes are planted in warm soil. As soon as they have produced shoots eight to ten centimetres long, they are removed with a small piece of the tuber, and planted permanently, in the middle of a deaf layer, at a distance of 60 centimetres. Cover with cloches, and shade for a few days to ensure recovery. Ventilate whenever the temperature permits, and replace the bells at night, as long as frosts are to be feared; the potato is very sensitive to them. Watering is useful, but only moderately. Potatoes don't like excess humidity, so only wet them thoroughly during hot spells. When frost is no longer an issue, remove the cloches completely. Tubers can be harvested as they form, if desired. This is easily done by digging into the bed with your fingers; they are detached when ripe, and the root is immediately covered. The whole crop is harvested in September. Potatoes are quite difficult to preserve; they are very sensitive to cold, and rot when 24 9 bruised. It should be harvested with as much care as the most delicate fruits, and stored in a warm, dry place, buried in ash or heather soil, completely dry, bed by bed, and placed far enough apart so that they do not touch. PARSLEY. Parsley is a must in any well-kept garden. Sow it in the borders of squares no. 2 and no. 3 in spring and August, so that it is always in full bloom. Sow parsley in pots in August for winter supply. The pots are completely buried in the ground, and as frost approaches, they are moved into the orangery, or if there is no orangery, into a cold frame. With twenty pots of parsley, there's never a shortage during the winter. When the cold becomes severe, bury two pots in the compost of a warm layer, and replace them with two others when the parsley has grown enough to be picked. CHILI. Chillies ripen only imperfectly in the vineyards. Its main use is for preserving before ripening. It is sown on a warm bed under a frame in February or March, for transplanting to nurseries under a frame as soon as the plant has four or five leaves, and for setting out on a warm or 18-deaf bed in May. The Spanish sweet pepper is the variety that ripens best in France. It can be counter-planted on a melon bed, or between cucumbers. PIMPRENELLE. PIMPRENELLE is sometimes used in the kitchen, as a vegetable. It's a poor plant for the vegetable garden, but a precious fodder for animals. The burnet comes from everywhere, and succeeds everywhere. It is usually sown in the borders, in March, in squares n° 3 and n° h, for kitchen use, but when there are a certain number of animals in the house, and the vegetable garden is large, it is worth growing it as fodder. In this case, sow in February, in square no. h, in rows 30 centimetres apart. The soil can be turned over and a buckwheat plant sown, which will provide abundant seed for poultry, especially if it is vigorously ashy. If there are a certain number of animals, we surround the whole square n° 3 with borders of burnet, and we keep them for two years, to destroy them when the square in which we sowed becomes n° 1, then we resow the borders of square n° 3, for two years, and so on. LEEK. Leeks require substantial, slightly clayey soil; they like slightly decom8 posed fertilizers. We sow it at two times of the year to make two seasons, which is enough to ensure that we never run out. Leeks are sown in March and July, in plot no. 2. The March seedlings are transplanted when the plant has grown to the size of a feather pipe. The root tips and largest leaves are cut off and transplanted in beds, or counterplanted in other crops. To obtain very white leeks, transplant at a depth of 10 or 12 centimetres. When you need a large quantity of leeks, you transplant in beds in plot no. 2, after a harvest of lettuces or large strawberries: the bed is ploughed deeply, with edges to retain water from watering; stakes are placed 20 centimetres apart and 10 centimetres from the edges. Place the string on these stakes, and trace the lines with the spiked trowel. Stagger the leeks on these lines, 10 centimetres apart. Hoe often and water copiously. Leeks planted in rows in May can be harvested in summer and autumn. If you don't need to use a large quantity of leeks, you can counter-plant them in squares n° 1 and n° 2. In square n° 1, between cauliflowers, autumn artichokes etc., etc., etc. In square 2, between strawberry plants, lettuces etc., etc. July seedlings are planted in September, in beds, in plot no. 1, for harvesting in winter and spring, or are counter-planted in plot no. 1, in strawberry beds, between last-season cabbages etc., etc. PEAS. Peas are an excellent vegetable. It should be given a large place in the vegetable garden, and harvested as long as possible. Peas like light, medium-consistency soils; they do poorly in clay soils, but do best in new, fertilizer-free soils. Fresh fertilizers are harmful to it, and it grows huge stalks without producing grain. Peas, like all pulses, need a certain amount of potash to bear fruit abundantly, and give pitiful results when sown two years in a row in the same bed. It needs new soil, or at least soil that hasn't produced peas for two years. Many authors have claimed that the law of alternation should not be respected for vegetables, except for peas, which reduce their production by more than half when sown two years in a row in the same bed. The excuse given for violating the first of nature's laws was the amount of fertilizer buried in the garden. All vegetables seemed to agree with this error, but only the pea protested. It is indisputable that many vegetables 18 produce fairly satisfactory yields when grown for several years in succession on the same bed, but it is at least as indisputable that these vegetables produce almost double the yield when they are grown only every three or four years on the same bed. This, at least, is what our experience of vegetable rotation has shown us over the last ten years. A hardy species will tolerate a poor crop, but if given a good one, it will produce twice as much. It was experience gained through observation that led us to adopt a four-year rotation, with layers, and, thanks to this rotation, the right distribution of fertilizers, and respect for the law of alternation, we obtain double produce without further expense. You never know where to put peas in a garden that is not subject to rotation. In our kitchen garden, we'll grow them in plot no. 3, where they'll find soil that is no longer loaded with fertilizer, has been resting for three years from pea production, and is abundantly ashed, the last condition for obtaining large quantities of good-quality grain. We'll be growing just one variety under a frame: the very-dwarf pea under a frame; early cultivation differs essentially from that in the open ground, and we'll be giving it the honors of primacy. In November, we sow very young peas on a warm layer under a frame, in rows 15 centimeters apart, with the peas side by side in the furrow. Cover at night with straw mats, and provide air whenever possible. In the first fortnight of December, peas are unfolded and transplanted by two or three feet together, on a warm bed under a frame, in rows 15 to 18 centimetres apart, with the clumps staggered at 10 centimetres on the rows. The practice of transplanting is excellent for peas, especially for frame peas. Transplanted peas grow less stemmed and bear fruit more quickly than those sown in the field. As soon as transplanting is complete, spread a little wood ash between the rows. Cover with straw mats for two days, to help the plants take root. As soon as it is well established, we bury the ash by lightly hoeing with a weeder; we give as much air and light as the temperature allows, and we control the stoves so as to regularly obtain a heat of 15 to 18 degrees under the frames. When the peas have formed three or at most four flower stages, we pinch the main stem to stop it elongating and concentrate the action of the sap on the knotted fruit. We turn up the heat on the stoves a little, and a few days later we start picking peas. Chassis peas should only be grown when far from Paris, where in December, January and February, peas harvested in Algiers are sold at low prices. According to an ancient custom, peas are sown in sheltered beds on St Catherine's Day, November 25. These peas are quite early, it's true, but even greater earliness can be obtained, with less trouble, by sowing under a frame and transplanting in the open ground. For this purpose, we sow the very dense Dutch dwarf, in rows 15 centimeters apart, on a warm bed and under a frame, in January or February, to be planted out in the open ground in April. Choose a bed against a south-facing wall; plough well; place stakes 25 centimetres apart at each end of the bed; lay down the chalk line, and after tracing the lines with a pointed trowel, transplant the peas in staggered rows, 25 centimetres apart. We shelter them from the sun with straw mats for two days to ensure a good start, then spread ash between the lines and lightly hoe it in. Four or five stems are placed in each hole. Water very sparingly to avoid damaging fruiting, and cover at night with straw mats if frost is to be feared. When there are four stages of flowers, pinch off the main stem to stop vegetation and make the fruit grow, and harvest as you go along. Peas grown in this way require little more care than those sown at St Catherine's Day, and ripen a month earlier. I admit to growing in clumps only for early peas that will be transplanted. For all open-ground crops, without exception, we will adopt row sowing, which gives triple the results of clump sowing. The Michaud de Hollande pea produces its first pods almost at the same time as the Ste-Catherine peas, if sown early. This is the pea par excellence, in terms of quality, earliness and fertility: there can never be too many in the vegetable garden. The Michaud de Hollande pea, sown in February, bears fruit at the same time as the Ste-Catherine peas, when properly cultivated. It is sown in square no. 3, in rows at least one metre apart, but in a single row dug with a spiked trowel, at the bottom of which it is sown very thickly (pl. 10, fig. 1). I insist because people who don't usually sow peas in rows never want to sow them thick enough, and place the rows far enough apart. The distance between rows is one meter for Michaud de Hollande; one meter 10 cent. for Ruelle and Michaux peas, and one meter 20 cent. for Clamart. No less seed should be planted than is available (fig. 1, pl. 10). Furrows should be five to six centimetres deep, and oriented north to south. As soon as the seed is sown, it is covered, taking care to break up the clods; then a rough rake is used to remove the largest stones, and a layer of ash is spread over all the lines, about 30 centimetres wide, one centimetre thick on the line and five millimetres on the edges. The fine rake is used to attach the ash to the soil, without burying it, and to remove any foreign matter. When the peas have risen well, i.e. when they are about three centimetres high, we give them a vigorous hoeing, amalgamating the ash left on the surface with the soil. When the peas are twelve to fifteen centimetres high, hoe them a second time and row them in. It's very important to row on time, and, I repeat, the peas must not be more than 15 centimetres high, or the harvest will be significantly reduced. To row the peas, don't use twisted, branched branches, but straight, unbranched sticks. Two rows of oars are placed at each row of peas, and the oars are driven into the ground as follows: one row of rods at a distance of twenty-five centimetres, driven firmly into the ground, inclined to the same side (A, pl. 10, fig. 3). A second row of rods is then placed, driven in the opposite direction to the first (B, same figure), and crossed at points C, for added strength. The rods thus laid form a very solid trellis with regular lozanges. Two lines of trellis are constructed for each line of peas, one on each side, leaving a spacing of 25 to 28 centimetres between the two lines (pl. 10, fig. A). The line of peas is trapped between two trellises; the tendrils cling to the oars on either side, and when the peas are large, they form a hedge as straight and strong as a well-stocked and carefully mown thorn hedge (pl. 10, fig. 5). Paddles should be set at the following heights: 1 m for Michaud de Hollande; 1 m 20 cent. for Ruelle, Michaux and Clamart peas. All rods must be driven in so as to be at the same height, and to form a regular trellis. This method of rowing peas requires more wood, and also much more time than the one commonly used: this is undeniable, but wood and labor, even if they cost five times what they are worth, the owner will still benefit immensely from rowing as we indicate. You can row rows of peas with ten or twelve bundles of oars, and one or two days' work for the woman; the expense is almost nil, the garden will be much more regular and you'll get the following benefits: 1° A harvest at least twenty times greater than that of peas sown in bunches, or in rows that are too tightly packed and poorly rowed. This is easy to understand: our spaced rows, aerated and evenly lit on both sides, are remarkably fertile, whereas the pods can't form in the shade, in the middle of the clutter we see all too often in gardens; 2° An enormous time saving when picking the peas. All the pods hang outside on either side, so you don't have to look for them, just pick them. It's much quicker to pick a large basket 3 of peas on our rows than a handful in our seedbeds, and even picking this handful means breaking a large number of stems. The time saved in harvesting far exceeds that used in rowing; 3° Harvesting more than a fortnight earlier. This precocity is due to light, which is the most powerful agent of fruiting. Earliness is always very profitable if the crop is sold, and even if it is consumed, as it allows you to harvest one more crop in the year, while giving yourself the satisfaction of primacy. I recommend the pea cultivation I have indicated, to the exclusion of all others; both for the owner and for the speculator, since the yield is so much higher than that obtained by other cultivation methods. It's easy to convince yourself of this by making a comparative test. The landowner who sows in bunches, and devotes two ares to this crop, should continue it on one are, cultivating the other as I have indicated, and should take into account the time spent on each of the crops and their yields. This is the best way to cut short all objections, and reverse vicious habits in favor of a fruitful crop. When peas have formed five or six tiers of flowers, they are pinched off, i.e. the end of the main stem is removed. This operation is of great importance; it hastens the harvest and considerably favors the development of the fruits, by concentrating all the action of the sap on them. Nothing is lost by pinching; on the contrary, a few flowers are removed from the end of the stem, but these flowers, which would only have produced a few bad pods containing two or three seeds, would have delayed the ripening of the lower pods by more than a fortnight, and would have hindered their development. Once the peas have been pinched, all that's left to do is to hoe them from time to time, to destroy the grass growing between the rows, and to pick them as they ripen. It's urgent to sow peas often, every fortnight or three weeks, so as never to run out. Once you've picked four rows of peas, you're ready to pull them out. So you have to sow a few at a time, but very often, so you never run out. Michaud de Hollande, Ruelle and Michaux peas are sown from February to May, and Clamart from April to July. When harvesting peas for seed, be careful not to do so from a crop that has already been picked. The first pods provide the best seed, and by letting the last pods ripen, you only obtain waste seed that is prone to degeneration. What's more, you have to tie up one or two beds for a long time to harvest one liter of peas, and it would be better to buy it than to deprive yourself of a harvest. When you want to harvest seed, you make a special sowing in square n° A; you pinch early, and let everything dry without picking anything, it's the only way to get good seed. 25 POTATOES. Potatoes are extremely easy to grow, both as an early and seasonal crop. They are an indispensable vegetable, and you should never be short of them. It is therefore necessary to grow early varieties in trays and in the open ground, and then in the open ground a few good seasonal varieties chosen from the various kitchen gardens of the owner, tenant, etc., etc. The early Kidney or Marjolin potato is an excellent early variety, which benefits from being grown as an early crop, both indoors and outdoors. It is planted on a warm bed from December to February. Four rows are planted per frame panel, staggered 32-35 cm apart. In the early stages, we counter-plant with various nursery vegetables. Warmth of 15 to 20 degrees is maintained under the trays, with the help of stoves; air is given whenever the weather permits, the soil is covered at night with straw mats, and watered moderately to keep the layer moist. Potatoes are harvested as they come up, by digging at the base, and immediately covered with potting soil. If you have a temperate greenhouse at your disposal, plant a few potatoes in large pots and place them in the greenhouse. This is the easiest growing method, requiring only a few waterings. Harvesting is done in the same 8 way, as the tubers develop, by carefully digging at the base. You can still obtain very early potatoes with Kidney, by planting it in the open ground in plot no. 1, in the second half of February. To save time, germinate the potatoes under a frame, or, even simpler, in a warm place, in a greenhouse, or even in the gardener's bedroom. Four rows of holes, 25 to 30 centimetres deep, are made in each board, staggered 50 centimetres apart in all directions. The sprouted potatoes are placed at the bottom of the holes; they are covered with five to six centimetres of soil, and a small reserve of bedding is placed in the alleys to cover them in case of frost. When the potatoes are well established, we give them a vigorous hoeing, filling the holes completely, hoeing them from time to time, and harvesting them in the first days of June. We grow the seasonal varieties in squares in plot no. 1. We plant in March in holes staggered 70 centimetres apart in all directions. The plant is hoeed several times and lightly ridged to encourage tuber development. Potatoes can be counter-planted with garlic, leeks, etc., in the first period of their vegetation, and with winter cabbage in the last. 8 PUMPKIN. Pumpkins are sown on a warm bed in March, to be transplanted in a nursery between other plants in April. Alternatively, they could be sown on a dull bed and covered with cloches, or, if precocity is not a priority, they could be sown on a warm bed in April and transplanted in May; Whether the seedling is transplanted onto a warm bed and placed in a frame, or sown a month later, it cannot be placed under cover before May. They can still be sown in April, but with a cloche or at least a flowerpot for shelter. Pumpkins are sensitive to the cold, but once frosts are no longer a threat, they can be grown in the ground. Around May, round holes 50 centimetres deep and one metre in diameter are dug about two metres apart. These are filled with well-tilled and sufficiently watered manure, covered with 20 to 25 centimetres of good soil mixed with loam, and the pumpkins are planted in clods in the center of these small layers. When pumpkins are planted before May 15, it's a good idea to shelter them at night with a glass bell, an economy bell, or at least an upside-down flowerpot, to protect them from the white frosts that destroy them. When planted, they should be sheltered for at least two days to ensure a good start. 16 Pumpkins require a great deal of water; you must not spare watering if you want them to grow early and in good volume. The more you water it, the fatter and tastier it becomes; pumpkins don't mind two or even three thorough waterings when the season is dry. Pruning a pumpkin is very simple. The main stem is allowed to grow to a length of 1.5 meters for the large yellow pumpkin, and 1 meter for the Spanish pumpkin. Pinch to develop the branches on which the meshes (female flowers) appear. If you want monstrous pumpkins, leave only one fruit on the large yellow, or two if you don't want monsters. You can leave two or even three on the more delicate but much smaller Spanish pumpkin. Once the fruit is set, choose the best-formed and most advantageously placed to preserve them, and sacrifice all the others. The fruit-bearing branch is pruned two eyes above the pumpkin, so that it can benefit from the full action of the sap, and care is taken to ensure that no new branches develop that are too vigorous. If they do, remove them. Water copiously, but not excessively, until the fruit has formed, then increase watering as soon as it has, as the plant can never have too much water. There's a very simple way for everyone to increase fruit volume considerably, and that's to pickle the stem 50 centimetres above the fruit's attachment. Make a hole 25 centimetres wide, 50 centimetres long and 30 centimetres deep. Put about 15 centimetres of consumed manure in the bottom; cover with a centimetre of earth, on which you fix the pumpkin branch with two hooks, and cover completely with earth. A few days later, the buried part of the branch has sprouted roots, and this new root system gives the fruit all its sap, considerably increasing its volume. Marcotage makes it possible to keep one more fruit per plant, thanks to the root systems created. Pumpkins can be counter-planted with a few lettuces, if done in small quantities. But in vegetable gardens where an entire square is planted with pumpkins, they can be sown with green beans and harvested before the pumpkins have invaded the field. This harvest costs nothing, but should be removed as soon as it gets in the way of the pumpkins. RADISHES. We sow radishes all year round on beds, and in the ground, a little at a time and very often, so as never to run out. But as we only need a very small quantity of radishes at a time, we only sow them in counter-planting, on beds and in the ground, i.e. between other harvests. Layered radishes are sown in December, January and February, between lettuces, melons, cauliflowers, etc.; from March to August, in the garden, 9 in plot no. 1, between cabbages, artichokes, etc., etc. Radishes require a lot of water; they should be watered at least twice a day in summer to keep them tender and not too pungent. When sowing radishes in the ground, it's a good idea to beat the soil a little with the back of the spade; they are always rounder and better formed when the soil has been lightly beaten. RAVE. Cultivation is the same as for radishes. RAIPONCE. An excellent small salad, invaluable for those lacking layers and frames, in that it is harvested in the open ground during winter. Ruiponce is broadcast in square 2, in lettuce beds, as a counter-planting, and in special beds around June. The very fine seed should be mixed with twenty times its volume of soil, so as to sow fairly lightly. Cover the seed with a little top soil, and water assiduously until emergence. We start by harvesting the raiponces that have been sown in counter-planting, and keep those that have been sown in beds for harvesting at frost time. All you have to do is cover the beds with litter as soon as it freezes. Lift the cover, and harvest as needed. 3 Riponce is little cultivated, and wrongly so, as it is a precious resource for tenants, farmers, teachers and small landowners. RHUBARB. Rhubarb can be propagated by sowing it in seedbeds or in the ground, in rows, around March, or by splitting it off. Transplant to nurseries at a distance of 50 centimetres in all directions, whether the plant comes from seed or from cuttings. Hoe and water thoroughly throughout the summer. The following spring, place the plants in square No. 1, staggered one metre apart in all directions. Water often and thoroughly. Harvesting of the petioles begins in summer and continues the following year. SALSIFIS. Sow salsify and scorzonera in squares 2 and 3, in rows 25 to 30 centimeters apart, around March, to harvest the roots in autumn and winter. A little watering to ensure that the young plant emerges and grows well, and occasional hoeing to loosen the soil, is all that salsify needs. They can be left to overwinter in the ground without suffering, as they don't freeze. If you want to eat them over winter, it's a good idea to pull them up at frost time and store them in the cellar, or cover a few beds with bedding so you can pull them up when it freezes. 18 MI Aromatic plant essential for cooking. It is sown in spring, in borders, in plot no. 3 or plot no. h. We use it green throughout the summer, and pull it up in autumn to dry it and keep it all winter. Dried savory retains its aroma. THYM. To be grown in borders in squares 3 and 4, by splinters of plants, planted in spring. TOMATO. The tomato is one of the best condiments we have; its use has become more popular in recent years, thanks to advances in horticulture; its yield exceeds that of the potato. Nothing is easier than growing tomatoes in the open ground; early cultivation, under frames, is more difficult, but nevertheless accessible to gardeners of very average strength. To obtain early tomatoes, we sow in December and January, on a warm bed and under a frame, then transplant to a nursery on a slightly cooler bed, and even transplant to a nursery at a greater distance, on a third bed, before planting. These successive transplantings are intended to prevent the plant from growing too many leaves and to hasten fruiting; they are necessary, especially for tomatoes destined to ripen under chassis, as they always have a great tendency to grow too vigorously. Around February, tomatoes grown in nurseries under warm conditions are counter-planted with first-season green beans or other crops soon to be removed. Four tomato plants are planted under each panel. They are covered at night with straw mats, given air during the day whenever the temperature permits, and watered with care. The tomato plants are planted on two branches attached horizontally to small stakes or, more simply, to transverse wires. As soon as the two reserved branches are full of flowers, pinch off the ends and remove any new shoots that appear. As soon as they start to turn red, remove all the leaves covering them to speed up ripening. Tomatoes can be harvested in the first days of April. Tomatoes can also be harvested very early, by planting them at the edge of the in-season melon beds, on the north side; they provide shelter for the melons, keep insects away with their smell, and bear fruit well before tomatoes planted in the ground. Stakes one metre high are placed every two metres, and this one-metre height is divided into three. The stakes are linked together by three wires twisted around each stake, arranged as follows: the first 30 centimetres from the ground; the second 30 centimetres from the first, and the third 30 centimetres from the second (pl. 10, fig. 6). These wires are hand-tightened as much as possible, and a tomato plant is planted every 80 centimetres along the fence. As they grow, we tie the tomatoes to the wires, like a vine, taking care to leave a certain distance between the branches, and to remove any that might obstruct the light. As soon as a certain quantity of fruit has set, all the branches on the wires are severely pinched, without exception, and all new shoots are ruthlessly removed. We water very copiously when the fruits are well formed, and remove the leaves when they start to turn red. This palisade shelters the melons from the northern gales, and is literally covered with tomatoes by July. Tomatoes are only planted in the open air in May, when frosts are no longer to be feared, or if they are planted in April, they are sheltered at night with a bell until May. For open-ground cultivation, three wires are also laid on stakes, with the rows 1 metre apart, and the tomatoes are staggered at 80 centimetres, around May 15. Water copiously for recovery, suspending watering a little at flowering time and doubling it when the fruit has reached the size of a hazelnut. Pruning is the same as above, and must be carried out energetically if you want to harvest a lot, i.e. spend all the sap on the fruits by removing all the new shoots, and bring forward their ripening by exposing them to the action of the sun's rays, i.e. by cutting off all the leaves that shade them as soon as the fruits have reached their full size. A certain amount of green fruit always remains on field tomatoes as frost approaches. Pick them carefully, and place them on dry straw in a room where it won't freeze. Tomatoes are planted in square No. 2, and when a special bed is made, it is carefully mulched; tomatoes can be counter-planted with lettuces. You can also counter-plant salad beds already mulched with tomatoes. TRUFFLES. I won't go into the subject of truffle cultivation, since the means of reproduction are not yet known. What I have said about Etampes truffles has stirred up the whole Périgourdine community, and given rise to a host of articles to which I have not wished to reply, as I have other things to do than to write slogans in favor of the Périgourdins. The town of Etampes supplies excellent truffles, and harvests considerable quantities every year; moreover, Etampes produces truffes à la reine, a variety much appreciated by gourmets. These are the facts in all their brutality. Everyone can verify them by passing through Etampes, and this is my only answer to the Périgourdins: Périgord truffles have lost none of their value, because Etampes produces excellent ones. Why deny it? DISEESCQO There's no medal that doesn't have its reverse side; gardening has its own; they are numerous, that's undeniable; but we mustn't exaggerate them. I would be failing in my self-imposed mission if, after indicating all the means of action, and pointing out the successes that are easy to achieve, I were to pass over the setbacks in silence. But before we get discouraged and repeat in chorus with the unintelligent and mediocrely hard-working masses: we're out of luck! or the soil is worthless! let's look for the cause of most of these failures. Let's start with those of large-scale cultivation. Alas! how many stars we have seen spinning! Why? because most of the time, highly intelligent men with a certain amount of capital, but who knew nothing about cultivation and didn't want to take the trouble to study it, threw themselves like madmen into clearing land, and spent their fortune and their intelligence on it. These men forgot that cultivation is a science, that it is necessary not only to know, but also to know how to do it; they unfortunately ruined themselves, and the irrefutable setbacks and obvious failures that have befallen so many families. Does this mean that large-scale cultivation is bad speculation? Obviously not, since the operations begun by these agricultural madmen, and continued by farmers, have produced the most brilliant results, and created more fortunes than they have ruined! Back to the vegetable garden! I've said that it's not just a question of knowing how to do things, but also of knowing how to do them. Most of the time, we fail at the easiest things, because we don't know how to do them, and we know even less how to do them. There's a very simple remedy to this state of affairs, and that's to learn; it's easy with a little intelligence, a lot of goodwill, and putting all false pride aside. You can't blame a man for not knowing something he wasn't able to learn; you can only pity him for not having been able to learn, especially when he's intelligent. Having said that, let's look at the causes of the main setbacks in vegetable growing, and try to avoid them. We have to constantly fear, and constantly fight, three causes of ruin for the plants we cultivate: 1° Atmospheric influences; 2° Diseases; 3° Insects. Humidity, frost, wind and storms are all enemies to be dreaded, but by fighting them with perseverance and energy, we manage to ward off their disastrous effects. Humidity is particularly pernicious for crops grown in trays and cloches; in the absence of sunlight, it causes plants to rot. That's when you need to redouble your efforts: carefully wipe the inside of glass panes and cloches, give as much air as possible to evaporate it, and make sure that no clutter forms under shelters; in other words, that plants don't get tangled up together; that they lack neither air nor light. Frost destroys everything if you can't fight it and let it surprise you. As early as October, you should prepare all your blankets, mats, litter, etc., to be used at the first sign of frost: when wild ducks and geese pass in flocks, heading from north-east to south-west, it's a sure sign of frost a few days later, just as their passage from south-west to north-east is a sure sign of thaw; thick, continuous fog almost always precedes frost: When, at the onset of winter, the wind stays to the north-east, the weather clears, and the stars shine brightly, we can expect a hard, continuous frost. We must be ready to cover everything as soon as these conditions appear; we must uncover with the same eagerness, as soon as the thaw comes, but keeping the shelters within reach if there is a recurrence of cold. Violent winds also cause great damage. You have to anticipate them, tie everything down and secure everything to avoid their ravages. When the wind is very still and uncertain, and the sunset is very red, it's a sign of a storm. Thunderstorms also cause great damage, as they are often accompanied by hailstorms and sometimes hail. You need to prepare everything when you see it coming: close the frames, take down the bells, and keep straw mats ready to cover melons and seedlings in case of hail. Diseases affecting vegetable plants can sometimes wreak havoc. In principle, however, diseases only affect weak plants that lack food and are placed in poor conditions. We never see diseased plants in the Paris marshes, which are typical of vegetable cultivation. When vegetables are cultivated in a more rational way, provided with the right fertilizers, and the soil is given the necessary care, diseases will disappear, as they are most often only an effect of poor cultivation. The same cannot be said of insects, which sometimes suddenly invade our crops and wreak havoc. Spider mites cause serious damage to seedlings, particularly carrots. They pierce the stem, usually killing the young plants. They can be destroyed by spreading ash or quicklime in the pathways, and at the same time drenching the seedlings with a decoction of soot or a solution of black soap. Caterpillars of all families cause great havoc, especially in cabbage plantations. They can be destroyed fairly quickly by spraying the affected plants with a black soap solution. A mixture of water, oil and ammonia would achieve a more rapid result, but the oil clogs the leaf stomata and impedes vegetation, whereas the black soap stimulates it. Courtillères cause real havoc in beds and seedlings. They are very difficult to destroy, but some can be killed by pouring soap water into their holes, or by pouring a few drops of oil and water over them. Ants are rare in well-watered vegetable gardens, as they fear excess humidity. When an ant-hole is discovered, it should be dug out with a pickaxe and boiling water poured over it. Slugs are formidable enemies, eating everything. You can destroy some of them by spreading ash around the boards they visit. They can also be hunted early in the morning, when there are plenty of them, but when there are many, and the garden is walled, the easiest thing to do is to get two or three hedgehogs and let them roam free. In just a few days, they'll devour all the slugs, without ever touching the plants. The flea beetle is very damaging to cabbage seedlings, which it sometimes destroys. When infested, we sacrifice the seedling to destroy them, by covering it with fine, dry grass and setting fire to it. Aphids are easily destroyed with two or three syringes of black soap water. White grubs are the scourge of the vegetable garden, and unfortunately we have no effective means of destroying them, as it is impossible to reach them underground. Their ravages will only diminish appreciably when the beetles are properly controlled, as is the case in some départements. They mainly attack the roots of strawberry and salad plants; as soon as you see one of these plants wilting, you dig around it and kill the worms. When there is a large quantity of worms in a bed, it is worth sacrificing the harvest and ploughing it very deeply, as this destroys many of them. In any case, it's always a good idea to have a few tame ducks, which you can train to eat the worms when you're ploughing. Nothing is simpler than training ducks to do this. All you need is for them not to be wild, and to follow you when you need them, which is easy if you throw them a bit of bread. As soon as they see soil moving, they'll place themselves in front of the ploughmen, and not a worm will escape them. When ducks have been to the plough once or twice, you only have to show them a spade to take them a league away; they follow with the greatest alacrity, place themselves in front of the ploughmen and never leave the top of the gauge, so impatiently are they waiting for the shovelful of earth that will provide them with a worm. I've noticed that in vegetable gardens with regular crop rotation, square No. 3, the ashy one, is always less attacked by grubs. The causticised ash keeps them away, if it doesn't destroy them. Another excellent measure is to have your chickens weed your gardens. Leave them free in the vegetable garden for part of the winter, when there's nothing to eat, as they're almost as fond of greenery as they are of insects. Or, better still, have 2-metre-square racks made, and when there are a lot of insects, park them with the racks, like sheep, in the most infested part, after removing the crop and giving it a light ploughing. Ten hens devour more insects in an hour than a man can destroy in a year. Small chickens are also excellent caterpillar destroyers for part of the summer. From birth until they are six weeks or two months old, they don't scratch, or not enough to do any damage. So when a cabbage patch is lost to caterpillars, it's difficult to destroy them because you only have the resource of picking them up by hand. Take a hen with her chicks; place the hen under a chicken cage in the middle of the square, leaving an opening big enough for the little chickens to get in and out. The chicks will spend their whole day eating caterpillars, and will refuse any other food as long as they can find it. The cage is changed every day, and when it has gone all the way round the square, it is purged of caterpillars. SUMMARY OF WORK IN THE VEGETABLE GARDEN, SORTED BY MONTH. The horticultural year really begins in August. This is the time when all seasonal crops are planted, and when work begins on those for the following winter, spring and summer. In August, there's still time to collect enough manure and leaves to build up the layers, and to make enough compost to start a regular crop rotation the following spring, with or without layers, and thus considerably increase the production of the vegetable garden. The summary of work I have given here is intended to remind the gardener of the main tasks to be carried out each month of the year, and to make it easier for the owner to direct and supervise this work: AUGUST. All outdoor plants are watered according to their needs; the loose soil, 14 in all crops, is maintained by frequent hoeing. Weeds resulting from hoeing are collected and mixed with the compost heaps in the factory. In August, we sow early white onions for transplanting in October; Yorck and oxheart cabbages for nurseries in September and October; short red Dutch carrots for harvesting before the frosts; leeks for counter-planting in spring; salsify, spinach, turnips and lamb's lettuce for winter, and passion lettuce for late winter. The last plantings of celery, chicory, etc. are made in the open ground. Beans are sown to eat green, and sometimes green beans, if the late season is mild. This is also the month to visit the borders, and replant them if they are in poor condition. Layers are no longer in use; the last melons are harvested, into which cauliflowers have been counter-planted. Unnecessary layers are demolished, and their products mixed into the compost mass. SEPTEMBER. Now that the heat is over and watering has subsided a little, it's time to activate all the field crops and prepare for winter and the following year. Early carrots, turnips, spinach and lamb's lettuce are still sown in the open ground. The previous month's seedlings are transplanted into nurseries. 8 Cauliflower, Yorck cabbage, gotte lettuce, chicory for winter and spring, and leeks for transplanting are also sown. Celery is being ridged, cardoons are being stuffed for blanching, and strawberry plants are being planted. We keep a few old beds on which to transplant lettuces sown in the open ground, and carefully collect all the manure needed to build up the beds as winter approaches. We grind the horse manure and mix the others with the composts. OCTOBER. Everything that can be removed is gradually brought in. Field harvests yield a quantity of leaves and herbaceous matter that are carefully collected to increase the mass of the composts. Never lose sight of the vegetable garden's rotation, and constantly think about what manure and leaves are needed for the beds, what composts are needed for plot no. 1, what potting soil is needed for plot no. 2, and what ashes are needed for plot no. 3. The month of October provides plenty of fertilizer, and nothing should be wasted. We are still sowing lamb's lettuce, spinach, chervil and radishes. All seedlings from the previous two months are transplanted into nurseries. We demolish the useless layers, those that have lost all their heat; some of the manure from these layers, that which has not been excessively consumed, is mixed with the herbaceous matter; the most decomposed is piled 9 separately to make new compost for the layers, and the old compost from the layers is reserved for the composting of square no. 2. Transplanting is carried out on muted layers that have produced melons, chicory, crepe lettuce, romaine, cauliflower, etc. We also sow on muted beds and under cloches: gottes, small black and romaine lettuces, to harvest during the winter. On rainy days, we repair all our shelter equipment, so as not to be surprised by frosts. We also start collecting leaves, and pile them up for diapers and composts. NOVEMBER. All shelters, frames, cloches and straw mats must be in good condition. We must have a certain quantity of bedding ready to cover field crops at the first threat of frost. Asparagus stalks are cut to a height of 40 centimeters, to mark the place of the base, and the mound made in spring is cut down to leave only 10 centimeters of soil on the crown, so that the asparagus can breathe. Carefully cut off any remaining artichoke stems, remove the largest leaves and start buttering. Artichokes grown annually are pulled out and returned to the cellar or vegetable greenhouse. All that's left to do is harvest and bring in the field crops, and protect what's left from frost. 3 Collect leaves in the garden, the park and even the woods, and stock up on broom, gorse and heather to mix into compost. In the woods, you'll also find very fine, fairly long grass that has dried on the ground and is excellent for making litter. Pond and ditch curings and pond grasses are also collected, as well as as ashes from lime kilns, factories, locomotives, etc. All of this should be done while the plants are growing. All this should be done while the field crops are resting; this resting time is used to activate the fertilizer factory. We can start smoking as much as we can a few planks of plot no. h, which will become no. 1 the following year, and put in Yorck and beefheart cabbages. We start to build up a few warm layers to sow peas and beans to eat green, and to set out lettuces, chicory, cauliflower, etc., between which we can place a few tufts of sorrel. As new layers are built up, the old ones are demolished, and the manure from them is distributed as we have indicated. Keep an active watch, and observe the weather more than ever; increase straw mat coverings at night, and apply stoves if frost is felt. DECEMBER. The fertilizer factory should be working as hard as possible, as it does during the whole of time 26, when field crops take up very little space, in order to meet the needs of spring. When the weather is mild, we uncover the artichokes and plow all the free beds. If it's freezing, on the other hand, manure is hauled in to clear the beds, and once everything has been transported, it is buried as soon as the thaw sets in. If the frost persists, we increase our supplies of leaves, broom, gorse and moss, which we fetch from the woods, and we cover whatever remains in the ground. When the weather is mild, early cabbages can be planted in square No. 1. Layering requires a great deal of activity in this month. New warm layers are put up for sowing melons, tomatoes, etc., and a few warm layers for sowing carrots and radishes, and putting peas, beans, etc. into place. We apply stoves wherever needed, depending on the temperature; we cover the frames at night with straw mats, and the bells with litter. If it snows, cover all the layers with straw mats and bedding, and carefully remove any snow remaining on and around the layers as soon as it has stopped falling. Air is given whenever the weather permits, in full sun, and when the thermometer is above zero. If some plants, poorly covered at night, are affected by frost, we must be careful not to uncover them; on the contrary, we double-cover them to let them thaw slowly, in the shade, which is the only way to preserve them. JANUARY. The manure for plot no. 1, the compost for plot no. 2, and the ashes for plot no. 3 should be transported to the site, so that we can take advantage of the mild weather to deep-till the fertilizer for plot no. 1; plow plot no. 2, and throw the compost into the beds as needed after plowing; plot no. 3 should also be plowed and ashed in January, or by February 10 at the latest. The soil is cleared of the last crops left in the ground or under the bedding; artichokes are kept in good condition; asparagus is smoked under cover, without being replanted, and if the weather is mild, early cabbages are planted, which can be counter-planted with leeks. In January, we increase the number of warm and hot layers, to set out melons, and nursery all early seedlings. Air is provided when the weather is mild; stoves and blankets are increased when it's freezing. Asparagus is heated; cucumbers, melons, tomatoes, chicory, etc., etc., are sown under frames. We also sow cauliflower, broad beans, beans, etc., etc., for transplanting in nurseries. Early carrots are sown, early potatoes are planted, and strawberry plants are started. Finally, we organize all the winter crops; we raise the seedlings that will be planted in the open ground two months later, we sow and counter-plant lettuces in the middle of all this, so that we never run out of them. FEBRUARY. We take advantage of the fine weather to sow onions, carrots, leeks, parsnips, etc., etc., in the open ground, in plot no. 2. Spinach can be sown between the early cabbages. The first peas and broad beans are sown in plot 3. The beds still require continuous care throughout February. All the plants sown in warm layers and transplanted in trays are placed in warm layers. Melons, cucumbers, tomatoes, cauliflower, savoy cabbage, celery, lettuces, chicory, romaine, radishes, etc., etc., are constantly sown and transplanted under glass. Air is provided as often as possible, shelter is provided at night, and the layers are kept warm with stoves. MARCH. Work in the open ground resumes in full swing. Carrots, onions, radishes, lettuces, sorrel, spinach, second-season peas, lentils, etc., etc., are sown in the open ground. All plants that can withstand full soil are planted: cabbages, lettuces, etc., and counter-planted as described in the general and special crops section. Strawberries, shallots, garlic, civet, all roots for seed, etc., etc., etc., are planted. 8 The beds require a fair amount of supervision in March; frost is hardly to be feared any more, but in exchange the sun gains strength, and sunburn is to be feared, especially when the plants, raised under glass, have not had all the air and light it was possible to give them. Shade is needed for the first few days, to gradually accustom the plants to very bright light. Melons, cucumbers, etc. are planted on warm, muted beds, and anything that can do without shelter is transplanted into the ground. APRIL. Asparagus is given a good ploughing, and mounded as I have indicated. We demolish the mounds of permanent artichokes, and spread the manure that covered them; we plow deeply and grommet at the same time. Artichokes kept in pots for annual cultivation are planted in plot no. 1, as are those kept in the cellar for the second season. All transplants grown under glass are planted in the open ground; cabbages, lettuces, etc. begin to be sown in the open ground. Pot-raised beans are transplanted, and all the bean varieties you want to grow are sown in the open ground. Layers no longer need heaters, as the sun takes their place; tender plants need to be shaded, and well covered at night. Various vegetables are still sown on the beds, to be transplanted in nurseries or in the open ground. MAY. The first harvests begin; they are immediately replaced by new seedlings and new plantings, so as to have everything throughout the summer. All harvests must be counter-planted to increase yields. We hoe, weed and water as needed. The fertilizer factory, depleted by the spring crops, must also be kept in mind. We demolish the first layers to mix the product with herbaceous debris from the vegetable garden. Carrots, turnips, cabbages, lettuces, romaine, chicory, cardoons, etc., etc., continue to be sown; nurseries are created to counter-plant and replace, as and when necessary, all that is removed. We sow beans to eat green, especially flageolets. The beds are easy to manage; air is given as often as possible; even the frames and bells are completely removed, and covered in the evening and at night. JUNE. We continue sowing in the open ground, so as never to run out of produce. We sow little and often everything, so as to always have well-stocked nurseries to fill the gaps which are frequent at this time of year, when the vegetable garden is in full production. Water as needed. The beds have produced the first melons, and we counter-plant them with cauliflowers, or replace them with other plants. JULY. The harvest is in full swing. Removed crops are replaced by nursery produce. Unnecessary layers are demolished to provide fertilizer for the mass, and everything that can be harvested before the frosts is sown. Clamart peas and beans can still be sown for harvesting in the late season. Cucumbers and melons are counter-planted with cauliflowers. END. Diuee ( 3 BEESGE COOgle FIRST PART.
